The Bridge Born

The Bridge Born

Riarhin VIII

Part I — The people at the door

Chapter 1 — Seven years

“I gave you seven years,” Farieth said.

Riarhin had been trying to press a loose root back beneath the earth. It crossed the grave at an angle, pale where the spade had cut it. Each time she pushed it down, it rose again.

Her grandmother stood beyond the low wall, lean and straight beneath a loose travelling robe. One hand rested near the sabre at her hip. A little of the blade showed at the scabbard’s mouth.

“Seven good years.” Farieth looked at the disturbed earth. “I know how quickly happiness passes among your kind. I thought there was no cruelty in waiting.”

Riarhin rose. Soil adhered to the wet heel of her hand.

The women who had helped carry Ailsa were gone. Down the hill, a man was settling a harness on the mule that had brought the boards. He swore at a buckle. Riarhin could hear him clearly.

“You knew her?”

“I saw you together.”

“When?”

Farieth came through the opening in the wall.

Her face softened.

“I am sorry,” she said. “For her. For you. And for what I have come to do.”

Remorse was in Riarhin’s hand before her grandmother had taken five steps.

Farieth stopped.

Riarhin moved sideways until the grave’s stone headmarker covered the approach to her right leg. The sword lay low, its point towards the opening. She could feel a tear reaching the corner of her mouth. She left it there.

For a moment something almost like approval touched Farieth’s face.

“Today?” Riarhin asked.

Her grandmother looked at the ground between them.

The man below the hill called to ask whether Riarhin wanted the mule brought up. She did not turn. Farieth took a step back, then another, and passed through the opening. Her robe brushed the wall. A burr caught at its hem and travelled with her down the path.

Riarhin stood until she could no longer see her.

Warm wind stirred the grass against the new earth.

The mule came labouring up the slope. Its owner stopped when he saw the sword.

“Something wrong?”

“Yes.”

He looked down the empty path. Riarhin wiped the blade though it was clean, put it away, and tried once more to settle the root. This time she found a stone to hold it.

She had helped wash Ailsa the night before.

There had been a small bruise above the knee, yellow at the edges. Riarhin knew how she had got it. The workroom stool had been left beneath the cutting table, and Ailsa had struck it while carrying folded cloth. She had blamed the stool with a vehemence that made her daughter laugh.

The bruise ought to have finished fading.

Riarhin had held the clean linen while the women worked. When they asked whether she wanted to comb the hair, she said yes and then could not begin. Ailsa’s daughter took the comb, worked through the first tangle, and passed it back without looking at her.

Now there was soil beneath her nails and a threat she could not explain. She stopped at the bottom of the graveyard path and washed her hands at the mule’s bucket. The mule’s owner waited without asking for it back.

At home, Ailsa’s daughter opened the door. She had been crying and had washed her face badly; one cheek was still marked.

“Where have you been?”

“At the grave.”

“You said you were coming straight back.”

“I meant to.”

The young woman stepped aside, saw the soil on her, and reached for a cloth.

Riarhin caught her wrist before she touched the sword belt.

Both looked down.

Riarhin let go.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

The young woman put the cloth on the stair rail.

“Take it off, then.”

Riarhin unbuckled the belt. Without its weight she felt briefly unbalanced. She carried it upstairs, laid the sword on the bed and returned to find a place set for her at the cutting table. The bowls were covered. Someone had remembered she disliked the skin that formed when the food cooled.

There were more visitors than chairs. A woman from the next lane sat on a chest with her knees together and spoke about a pattern Ailsa had once altered for her. Riarhin knew the pattern. She had watched Ailsa draw it on the back of a letter, squinting in the evening light. The visitor remembered a different part: how the altered garment had let her conceal a pregnancy until she was ready to tell her household.

“She would not take the extra,” the woman said. “I left it with the girl.”

“I remember,” Ailsa’s daughter said.

Riarhin lifted the cover from her bowl. The smell made her stomach turn. She had not eaten since morning, and her body seemed to regard this as a reason to reject food entirely.

The visitors left by degrees. Each departure involved an embrace or a word that she could not answer properly. One man shook her hand and held it too long. She wanted to hurt him for preventing her from withdrawing, then saw that he was crying.

When the room was empty, Ailsa’s daughter sat opposite her.

“Was it bad?”

“The grave?”

“Going back.”

“Yes.”

“I couldn’t.”

“You were there this morning.”

“I couldn’t stay.”

Riarhin looked at the place where the young woman had laid her hands on the table. There were little nicks along the first finger, acquired through taking on her mother’s work too quickly.

“She knew you loved her.”

The daughter’s mouth tightened.

“I’m aware.”

Riarhin wished the words back. They had sounded like something offered at every door that day, a thing one person said because another was grieving. She had meant them and still disliked how they fell between them.

“I didn’t want to leave her,” she said instead.

“Then why did you?”

Riarhin could not answer without bringing Farieth into the room. The young woman saw her hesitation and rose.

“I’ll warm that.”

“Leave it.”

“You haven’t eaten any.”

“I will.”

She ate while Ailsa’s daughter stood over her with the bowl’s cover in her hand. The first mouthful was difficult. The second made her realize how hungry she was. By the time she finished, they were both crying, and neither apologized for the way they had spoken.

That night she woke because the bed moved.

For one unguarded instant she reached across it. Her hand found a fold of sheet and the cool place beyond. Downstairs a door had closed. The movement had been her own, turning towards the sound.

She lay with her arm extended until it began to ache. The window was open a little. Warm air entered through the gap, carrying the smell of something fried in the next lane. Ailsa had disliked sleeping with the window shut. Riarhin had closed it before lying down, then opened it because the room felt wrong. Now the gap was large enough for a hand to reach the inner fastening.

She got up and closed it.

The sword was beside the bed. She lifted it, felt the weight settle against her palm, and stood listening. Ailsa’s daughter was moving in the workroom. Riarhin knew the tread even through the floorboards: a quick turn at the end of the table, then the slight drag where a hem or slipper caught. She put Remorse down and went downstairs.

The young woman had spread cloth across the cutting table. She was measuring the same edge again. The lamp stood too close; its light turned the cloth yellow and cast a hard shadow beneath her hand.

“You’ll burn that.”

“I won’t.”

Riarhin moved the lamp. The daughter looked up with a fury so immediate that she stopped.

“Don’t start doing it.”

“Doing what?”

“Everything she did.”

Riarhin set the lamp where she had meant to put it. There was no answer she could give which would not sound like an argument. The young woman drew the measure through her fingers, keeping her eyes on it. Her mouth began to tremble.

“I want her to come down,” she said. “I keep being so stupid about it.”

Riarhin sat on the stool at the end of the table. The daughter held the measure against the cloth once more, saw that she had begun from the wrong mark, and dropped it.

“She’d tell me to go to bed.”

“Yes.”

“She’d say it as if there was somewhere else to go tomorrow.”

Riarhin looked at the pattern beneath the cloth. Ailsa had weighted one corner with a smooth stone brought from the shore. On a windy afternoon years earlier, the girl had insisted that the stone looked like a sleeping animal. Ailsa had agreed and made a face behind her back. Riarhin had caught the face and laughed. The girl had accused them both of laughing at her, correctly.

“It is tomorrow,” Riarhin said.

The daughter gave a wet, unwilling laugh. “That’s worse.”

“Yes.”

They rolled the cloth together. Riarhin followed the daughter’s hand, letting her choose where the edge should meet. The work took longer than it needed to. Neither suggested hurrying. When it was done, the young woman went to the cupboard, brought out a small plate, and broke the last piece of sweet bread in two.

“I hid it,” she said. “Everyone kept eating.”

They ate sitting on the steps. The bread had dried at its broken edge. Riarhin held her half in both hands and thought of Farieth saying seven good years. She had wanted to strike her for counting them. Now she could not stop counting particular afternoons: Ailsa asleep in a chair with a thread stuck to her sleeve, Ailsa calling from the yard because the water jar was too heavy, Ailsa lifting her head from a kiss to ask whether someone had moved the scissors.

The daughter leaned against her shoulder. Riarhin put an arm around her and stayed until the weight grew heavy with sleep.

Chapter 2 — Ailsa’s arrangements

The repairer came for the window on the morning after the burial.

He brought the wrong wood.

Ailsa had wanted the narrow shutter rehung with a strip of dark timber along the bottom, where the rain blew through. She had chosen it from his yard and left a length of red thread around the piece. He remembered the thread. He had sold the timber.

“She wouldn’t have had that,” her daughter said.

“It will take the weather better.”

“She wouldn’t have had it.”

Riarhin stood between them with the old shutter under her arm. There had been a time when a disagreement of this sort would have scarcely reached her. Now she could see the red thread. Ailsa had come home with a splinter in her thumb and made Riarhin get it out before allowing her to laugh.

“Leave the wood,” Riarhin said. “We’ll look at it tomorrow.”

The repairer turned the wood over, exposing the better face. Ailsa’s daughter pushed it back.

“Did you put the thread on another piece?”

“No.”

“Then you knew which one.”

He looked at Riarhin. She disliked his appeal and disliked knowing that she could end the quarrel by making the young woman swallow it. The new wood was sound. It had no fault except that somebody still alive had brought it into the house.

“Who bought it?” Riarhin asked.

“A man wanting a chest.”

“Has he cut it?”

“I imagine so.”

“Ask.”

The repairer ran his thumb over the edge. He had a split nail, carefully bound below the tip. Ailsa had once sent food to his wife after a difficult birth. He had sat in this room and drunk their tea, praising a child whose name Riarhin could no longer summon.

“I’ll ask,” he said.

He named what he was owed. The young woman drew breath.

“I know,” Riarhin said to him. “I’ll pay you.”

After he left they propped the shutter in its opening and argued about whether it would fall on somebody. Her brother came upstairs with two wedges. He worked near the harbour now and had acquired the habit of expecting everyone to get out of the way when he carried tools. His sister did not move. He put one wedge between her foot and the wall.

“That’s where it goes.”

“You might ask.”

“I might.”

Riarhin laughed. It surprised her enough that she did it again, once, and had to sit on the bed.

The ugly yellow glass bird stood on the shelf above her. Ailsa had insisted it had become more beautiful over the years. It had become dusty.

Below, their father was opening the workroom for two women who wanted to finish an order. There were lengths of cloth to deliver and a customer inland who had paid half. The women asked where Ailsa kept the pattern. Her daughter knew. She fetched it, checked the marks and went down to show them.

The bed had been theirs for nearly six years. They had bought it after leaving the estate for the rooms above the workroom, argued over its price, and discovered on the first night that one leg was shorter than the others. Ailsa put a folded scrap beneath it. Riarhin promised to repair the leg. The scrap was still there.

She crouched and took it out.

The frame moved. The son caught the glass bird as it trembled on the shelf above.

“Leave it,” he said.

She put the scrap back.

There had been a spring in which she wanted to go north and Ailsa would not discuss it until the workroom’s new roof was finished. By the time it was finished, the daughter was ill; by the time she recovered, the passage had become expensive. Riarhin had accused Ailsa of finding reasons.

Ailsa had admitted that she liked having her there.

They had quarrelled more honestly after that. Riarhin had still gone on small journeys, had come back with useless presents and useful money, and had sometimes returned to find everyone angry at an absence she had thought brief. Seven years had not passed without effort. She could remember mornings when she woke determined to be less difficult and managed it until breakfast.

Riarhin watched the window.

Below it, the son set his tools in a row along the sill. There were more than he needed for two wedges. He took one out, put it back, and at last said he had meant to ask her something before his mother became ill.

“What?”

“There’s a place on one of the harbour crews. Repair work. They’d take me farther down the coast when they go.”

“For how long?”

“I don’t know yet.”

She almost asked whether it was safe. He would say yes because he wanted to go, and she would begin explaining dangers he had probably heard from his father already. He watched her face while she tried to find a different question.

“What do you want me to do?”

“Nothing. I was telling you.”

“You said ask.”

He looked down at the tools. “I was going to ask whether you knew the place. Where they work. You’ve been everywhere.”

“Not everywhere.”

He gave her the name. She had passed its roadstead years earlier and gone ashore only long enough to pay for water. That was less than he hoped for. She described the landing as she remembered it and added that a landing could change in seven years. He nodded, disappointed but still listening.

“Would you have gone?” he asked.

“At your age?”

“Yes.”

She thought of the people she had followed, the things she had believed about strength because strength was the one thing no one could prevent her trying to acquire. He stood in a room with a sound roof over most of it, carrying tools he had chosen. His mother had wanted him to read a contract before agreeing to serve under it. Riarhin had learned that lesson after paying dearly for the omission.

“I’d read what they put in front of you.”

“I have.”

“Then read what they left out.”

He frowned. “How?”

“What happens if you’re ill? Who pays to bring you back? Whether the sum they name is yours before or after food.”

He considered that. “I can ask.”

“Yes.”

He gathered the tools, then set them down again.

“I don’t know if I should go now.”

Riarhin could hear his sister below, giving directions in a voice she had acquired too quickly. The son looked towards the floor. He wanted someone to tell him that leaving would not be desertion. She could not promise that his sister would never call it that, or that he would never feel it. She asked him to speak to her before giving up the place.

“She’ll tell me to go.”

“Then listen.”

“She won’t mean it.”

“Ask again.”

He nodded reluctantly. It was an unsatisfying answer, and she had no better one. He put the last tool in its wrapping and looked at the window she kept watching.

“What happened yesterday?” the son asked.

She told him about the visitor, sparing him the part that she did not understand herself.

He listened with the wedges still in his hand.

“Does she know this house?”

“Possibly.”

“Then you should have told us yesterday.”

“Yes.”

He began asking which doors to keep closed and who should be warned. His father came up while they were discussing the yard. He listened, rubbed the back of his neck, and asked whether the women below were safe to walk home.

“I’ll go with them,” Riarhin said.

“I can take them. You stay here.”

For a moment she wanted to insist. He had already picked up the basket containing their unfinished work.

After he left, she and the daughter went through the workroom together. There were four orders waiting, all for people who knew Ailsa had died. Three had sent condolences. One had sent a boy to ask whether grief meant he must pay another worker to finish what he had already paid for.

“Show me that one.”

The daughter held up a little coat. The lining hung loose below it; one sleeve was still separate. It was much too small for the man Riarhin had imagined visiting.

“His youngest,” she said. “There’s a ceremony.”

“When?”

“The day after tomorrow.”

Riarhin sat at the table. She could make a wound hold together while someone moved, mend harness in bad light, sew a patch securely enough that it outlasted the cloth around it. She took up the sleeve and examined where it belonged.

“Don’t,” the daughter said.

“I can sew.”

“I’ve seen.”

Riarhin put the sleeve down.

“You could press these.”

She was given folded pieces, a clean cloth to place over them, and instructions about the warmth of the iron. Ailsa’s daughter watched the first one, corrected the direction in which Riarhin pulled, and returned to the coat. The workroom settled into small noises: a needle passing through cloth, the iron set down, somebody upstairs shifting the wedges in the shutter.

At first Riarhin worked too quickly. The young woman took a piece back and made her look at the seam impressed through it.

“It can be made right,” she said, seeing Riarhin’s expression. “It just takes longer.”

“Show me.”

She did. Her hand rested beside Riarhin’s while the steam rose. The skin was young and the thumbnail chewed to the quick. Ailsa had always had good nails except on the hand she used to open parcels.

“Stop looking at me like that.”

Riarhin lowered her eyes to the cloth.

“I’m sorry.”

“I know who I sound like.”

They worked without speaking until the daughter held the coat away from her and checked it against the light. Then she gave Riarhin the smallest of the pieces to press.

“This goes inside. Nobody sees it.”

“I’ll do it properly.”

“I know.”

Their father returned with the empty basket and stayed in the doorway long enough that the daughter asked whether he intended to come in. He put his basket beneath the table. They finished the coat before the light failed, and Riarhin carried it to the customer herself.

The boy who received it tried to open the wrapping. His father stopped him, paid the remainder, and began an apology Riarhin was too tired to help him make. She waited until he finished.

“It’s done,” she said. “Let him see it.”

The boy put his arms through the sleeves while the paper still hung from the back. His father turned him towards the light. The shoulders fitted. Riarhin could not tell whether the child liked the coat, but he had stopped pulling at it.

She returned by the longer road, along the places where she and Ailsa had walked when the evening wind made the room bearable again.

Before the lamp was lit, she went to the physician who had attended Ailsa.

He was grinding something in a shallow stone dish. He wiped his hands and offered her a seat she did not want. A strip of sunlight crossed the floor and caught the dust from his work. She could remember the room from the last visit: the same chair, a different bowl on the shelf, the physician reaching for a clean cloth before answering a question.

“Was there anything else?” she asked.

He did not make her say what she meant.

“There were things I could have tried. We spoke about them.”

“And she said no.”

“Yes.”

“Because of the cost?”

“Because of what they might have done to her, and what they could not promise.”

Riarhin stood with one hand on the back of the chair. Ailsa had sent her out to buy fruit during that conversation. She had returned to find them speaking of the window and had known, without asking, that the important part was over.

“You should have told me.”

“She asked me not to make you decide for her.”

“I wouldn’t have.”

The physician looked down at his hands. There was a little yellow powder caught in the folds of his skin.

Riarhin sat.

They stayed in the quiet for a while. From outside came the sound of a cart being reversed, a driver saying the same three words with increasing despair. Eventually the physician asked whether she was sleeping. She nearly laughed.

He gave her something mild to steep and told her to eat before drinking it. She paid him although he objected. She needed the visit to end in something she knew how to do.

On the way back she bought the fruit Ailsa had liked during the illness. At the door she realized what she was carrying and almost left it outside. Her son took the parcel, opened it and ate one before asking whether it was for anybody in particular.

“No,” Riarhin said.

He handed her another.

That night they left the workroom lamp burning. Riarhin slept badly in the room above it, with her hand in the hollow beside her where Ailsa had slept.

Chapter 3 — Four visitors

The woman at the door asked whether the house could accommodate a stringed instrument out of direct sunlight.

“Possibly,” Riarhin said.

“Thank you. It has been the principal concern of the last three streets.”

Behind her a man shifted a wrapped case from one shoulder to the other.

“It split in the heat once.”

“So you have told us.”

A second woman leaned past them. She had a broad, pleasant face, a travel cloak buttoned wrong at the neck, and a voice that arrived as if they had already met.

“Riarhin? I’m Zemia. We have come a very long way, and this is an unfortunate introduction.”

The fourth traveller waited beside the drain. She was examining the gutter above the door.

“Your water comes down the wall,” she said.

“It does,” Riarhin said. “Who are you?”

“Coryd.”

The first woman smiled. “Harissa. The man with the delicate cargo is Linias. May we come in before she tells you about your foundations?”

Riarhin asked them to wait.

Harissa lowered her travelling chest with an audible release of breath. Linias immediately asked her to move it out of the strip of sunlight, though his own case was still on his shoulder. They began arguing over whether the sun would reach it before they went inside. Coryd crouched by the drain and lifted a broken tile out of its mouth.

Riarhin went upstairs. Through the propped shutter she saw part of the lane: two porters returning with an empty handcart, a woman she knew bargaining over fruit, four strangers becoming impatient on her doorstep. Nobody waited farther down the lane. Nobody had stopped work to watch them except the neighbour opposite, who watched everyone.

The sword lay where she had put it. She threaded the belt slowly, trying to distinguish what had frightened her from what had hurt. A woman had said her name with recognition and expected welcome. Until this week she might have complained about visitors and made room for them.

Downstairs, the daughter had already opened the door.

“They were standing in the way,” she said when Riarhin returned.

Riarhin almost reproved her. Harissa was carrying the chest around a corner without touching the wall. She lifted it over a stool she could not have seen until the moment she moved. Linias followed, bent beneath the lintel, and straightened into the place she had cleared without looking.

Riarhin told them where to sit. When she returned, Zemia was holding one end of a bolt of cloth for Ailsa’s daughter. Nobody had asked her to. She had seen what was required and taken it.

They sat among the cutting tables.

Zemia began with their father.

She did not use a name that meant anything certain to Riarhin. Names had been a difficulty with him, as with everything that reached her second-hand. But she described the small scar where one finger had been broken and badly set. Bea had spoken of that finger. She had put her own straight one against Riarhin’s nose to demonstrate how crooked it was.

Linias remembered a song that ended on a note his mother said was wrong. He hummed it. Riarhin knew the melody and had never known its last phrase.

Harissa described a habit of turning a cup before drinking, looking for a chip.

“That proves very little,” Coryd said.

“It proves he disliked cutting his mouth,” Harissa answered.

Riarhin looked from one to another. Their ears told her less than their stillness. Linias balanced his cup on his knee without attending to it. Harissa shifted when a cart passed outside, already making room for someone who had not entered. Zemia’s eyes moved over a pattern on the table and returned to its maker.

“How did you find me?”

Zemia let the cloth settle.

“Eventually? By asking.”

“Whom?”

Zemia named the cloth buyer first. Riarhin knew him. He was a talkative man who could remember the width of a room he had seen once, and who regularly forgot that a person might not want to be discussed in front of customers.

“What did you tell him?”

“That we were looking for our sister.”

“What did you tell him about her?”

“A woman with pale hair, who had lived in Ubral. Who had travelled with a man from Kassamira.”

Riarhin’s fingers tightened around the cup.

“There are a good many women with pale hair.”

“He said there were not so many with that sword.”

All four looked at Remorse. She had made the recognition easier by fetching it. Harissa, seeing where her eyes went, put her own hands flat on the table.

“Are we frightening you?”

“No.”

“Good. You’re frightening me.”

There was a pause.

“We hoped,” Linias said, “that you might be pleased.”

She was. The feeling was so unwelcome in its timing that she had scarcely recognized it.

Linias looked younger when disappointed. He was not young; there was a fine crease at each side of his mouth, and his travelling coat had been altered for his changing shoulders by someone who knew him well. He could have been a stranger she would pass in an inn without turning. The thought that he might not be made her want to touch his face, an impulse so abrupt that she tucked her free hand beneath her knee.

“Give me a moment,” she said.

The room had become too close. She went to the yard and stood beside the water jar, listening to the visitors lower their voices when they realized she could still hear. The neighbour’s shutter opened. Riarhin looked up and received the sort of glance that meant questions would be asked later whether she encouraged them now or not.

She had spent much of her life wondering who might come for her. Men sent by someone she had harmed, women who knew a name she had stopped using, a captain who believed she still owed him work. She had not imagined four people carrying their own possessions and asking for a place to sleep because they thought they belonged to her.

Bea had once brought a visitor into a room where Riarhin was pretending to sleep. He had put something on the table, spoken softly, and gone away before morning. For years she had tried to remember whether he looked at her. She remembered the sound of his cup being set down instead, the small complaint Bea made when he left a mark on the wood. It might have been her father. It might have been any man her mother knew. Memory had kept the mark and withheld the face because the child beneath the blanket had been afraid to look.

Now strangers had brought her a crooked finger, a tune, a cup turned before drinking. She could make those things into a man if she wanted badly enough. She knew how dangerous wanting could be.

Coryd came to the yard door. She did not step out.

“Do you want us gone?”

“No.”

“Then come in when you’re ready. Zemia is trying not to talk. It’s making everyone uncomfortable.”

Riarhin laughed once. Coryd’s mouth softened, then she went back inside without asking to see more of it.

Riarhin washed her hands though they were clean. The water was warm from the day. She dried them on the cloth hung beside the jar and returned to her place.

Linias nodded. Harissa looked away, granting her the same thing less graciously. Zemia began to straighten a folded cloth, then left it alone.

Riarhin took a breath.

“How many people know you came here?”

Zemia began naming them. A merchant at one port; a performer at another; a man who had heard Riarhin lived with a woman from Ubral; a cloth buyer who knew the workroom. Riarhin asked which had given them an address. The list shortened.

Harissa took off one boot and tipped a pebble into her palm.

“Do we pass?”

“This isn’t a test.”

“Then why do I feel I’m doing badly?”

The daughter brought a pot and more cups. Zemia rose to help, then waited when the young woman shook her head.

“Was your mother called Bea?” Linias asked.

Riarhin looked at him.

“Our father spoke of someone with that name. Mine did not care to hear it.”

“What did he say?”

Linias’s expression changed. He had expected recognition, perhaps pleasure, and now found himself responsible for something larger.

“That she sang badly when she was happy. That she knew she sang badly.”

Riarhin remembered Bea walking across a room with a ribbon between her teeth, singing around it. She had not thought of the ribbon in years.

Harissa put the pebble on the table instead of dropping it on the floor.

Riarhin poured the tea too high and splashed her wrist. She set the pot down.

“You can stay tonight,” she said.

Harissa pulled her second boot off with relief. Linias began to ask where he should put the case, caught her expression and carried it where she pointed. Coryd went outside to wash without requesting directions. Zemia waited until the others were occupied.

“We didn’t know about Ailsa.”

Riarhin nodded.

“If we’d known, we would have—”

“Done what?”

Zemia stopped. A blush climbed from her collar.

“I don’t know.”

That answer made it easier to bear her. Riarhin brought bedding from the chest upstairs, including a heavy coverlet bought for weather the coast seldom provided. Ailsa had liked its colours. Harissa admired it and asked who had woven it.

“A woman inland. She died three years ago.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be. She lived to be old and disliked nearly everyone.”

“What a luxury,” Harissa said, running her palm over the work.

Linias asked whether he might tune the instrument before the room filled with bedding. Riarhin said yes. He unwrapped it carefully, laying the cloth aside before opening the case. Its wood was darker than she had expected, polished where an arm rested against it, scarred along one edge. There was a repaired split near the base.

“Is that the heat?” she asked.

“That’s a chair.”

“You hit someone?”

“Someone sat down.”

Harissa laughed from the other side of the table. Linias looked pained.

“It was under the chair. I thought it was safe there.”

He plucked one string, adjusted it, then another. Riarhin stood close enough to see how little his fingers moved. He played the phrase he had hummed earlier. The ending was strange to her, a rise where Bea’s voice had fallen, and for a moment she disliked him for knowing it.

“My mother did that differently.”

“Show me.”

“I don’t sing.”

“You can hum.”

She tried. Her voice was rough with the day. He waited through the places where she stopped, then played what she had given him without correcting it. She heard Bea in the altered phrase so clearly that she had to sit down.

Linias rested his hand across the strings.

“Was that right?”

“Yes.”

He nodded and looked at his hands. He did not ask whether she wanted it again. After a while she did ask, and he played it more quietly.

Harissa had ceased unfolding the coverlet. Coryd stood in the doorway with water dripping from her wrists. Riarhin could feel them listening. It was uncomfortable, and she found she did not want them to stop.

When Linias finished, Harissa said their father must have been a poor judge of music if he preferred the other ending. Coryd said there was nothing wrong with the other ending. They argued, and the room became easier to inhabit.

They arranged themselves beneath the tables, leaving the aisle clear for the morning’s work. Linias and Zemia had the ease of people who knew which possessions could be moved without asking. Harissa put a comb on the chest beside her and told everyone it was not communal. Coryd offered to sleep by the yard door.

Riarhin said she would take it.

“We didn’t come to put you on a doorstep,” Coryd said.

“I’ve slept in worse places.”

“So have I. It isn’t a competition.”

From the stairs Ailsa’s daughter watched them with an expression Riarhin could not read. Later, when the visitors were washing, she asked whether they were truly related.

“I think so.”

“All of them?”

“Yes.”

“Will they want things?”

“Probably supper.”

The daughter did not smile. Riarhin stopped trying to make the question smaller.

“I don’t know.”

Her father came in from the yard carrying the empty water jar. He set it down and looked towards the workroom, where Linias had begun explaining how the strings were made. Harissa interrupted to say that no one had asked. Linias continued with more detail.

“Do you want me to ask them to leave?” the father said.

“No.”

“Then there’s room.”

His daughter turned to him. “You can’t just—”

“There’s room for tonight. We can discuss tomorrow tomorrow.”

He went to fill the jar. Riarhin watched him through the doorway. He had known the household before she arrived and had made room for her without becoming smaller in his children’s lives. She had found that generosity difficult to accept at first. It had left her nothing to fight for which was not already offered.

The young woman sat on the bottom step. The day’s work had left a crease across her forehead where she rubbed it with the back of her hand.

“You’ve never spoken of them.”

“I didn’t know.”

“How do you not know you have four brothers and sisters?”

Riarhin looked into the workroom. Zemia had found Linias’s missing string wrapped inside a spare shirt. He received it with the gratitude of a man who expected to be mocked and was being spared.

“I wish I could tell you.”

The daughter glanced towards the visitors.

“Does it make you happy?”

“I don’t know yet.”

“You smiled at him.”

“Did I?”

“When he sang.”

Riarhin had not meant to. She pressed a fingertip against the little burn on her wrist, then stopped when the daughter noticed.

“They need somewhere to stay,” she said.

“I can see that.”

“I should have asked you.”

“Yes.”

The young woman rose and took the remaining cups into the yard. Riarhin followed with the pot. They washed without more discussion, passing each thing from one pair of hands to the other. When they returned, Coryd had cleared the drain and was holding the broken tile up to Harissa as evidence in some continuing disagreement.

Harissa caught Riarhin watching.

“She thinks I threw it there.”

“She throws things when they cease to please her,” Coryd said.

“They were my things.”

“The man wanted his buckle back.”

“He should have left it attached to his trousers.”

Linias dropped a string. Zemia bent to pick it up and stayed bent a little longer than was necessary. Ailsa’s daughter made a sound and covered it with the back of her hand. Riarhin laughed before she could consider whether she wanted to.

Chapter 4 — The ones who did not come

Coryd spread a woman’s waistcoat on the table. The fastenings were horn, carved into little flat fish. One was missing.

“She made these,” Coryd said. “She had no patience for needlework, but she would spend a day making a button.”

Dovra had been their sister. She had lived with a potter who disliked strangers and disliked questions more. When Coryd found him, he had given her the waistcoat and told her to stop bringing the dead into his kitchen.

“Did he see who killed her?”

“He saw the visitor. Afterwards he found her by the kiln.”

“Describe the visitor.”

Coryd did. The robe had been different. The sabre had not.

“She had mud on her boots,” Coryd said. “That’s what he kept telling me. She stopped at the mat and scraped it off. He saw her do it. He said there was room to put the horse where the rain wouldn’t reach it, and she thanked him.”

Riarhin waited for more.

“He had made a firing. He couldn’t go with them. The kiln was too hot to leave. She asked how long it would take before he could close it and come inside.”

“Did he tell her?”

“Yes.”

“How long?”

“Past dark.”

Harissa pushed her cup away.

“He was still there when he heard something fall. He thought a stack had gone over. She had been sorting the spoiled ones to break for the path.”

Coryd rubbed the flat fish between her fingers until its tail pressed a pale mark into her skin. Ailsa’s house felt abruptly too small for the five of them.

“What happened when you arrived?”

“He thought I was coming to take her things. He had put them in a chest. He wouldn’t open it while I was there.”

“Why did he give you this?”

“Because I asked how her hands were. I couldn’t think of anything else. She used to burn herself when she worked with him. She always reached too quickly.”

Riarhin looked at Coryd’s hands. One thumbnail was thickened where it had once been crushed. She had noticed it when Coryd cleared the drain, without thinking of a history behind it.

“He fetched the waistcoat,” Coryd said. “It was hanging on a peg. He said I could take it or leave his house.”

“Was he hurt?”

“Not where I could see.”

Zemia sat with both hands under the table.

“What did the potter call her?” Riarhin asked.

“Dovra. I called her Dovi when we were young.”

“Which did she prefer?”

“The first, later.”

“Did she have children?”

“One. Grown. He wouldn’t tell me where.”

“That was sensible.”

Coryd’s mouth tightened.

“I thought so afterwards.”

Riarhin turned the little horn fish between finger and thumb. Its tail had been worn nearly smooth by handling. The woman who made it had known where to leave the thicker part so it would not snap when forced through cloth. She turned the fish again. She had never seen her sister wear it.

She handed it back before she began pretending.

Coryd folded it along existing creases. She had done this often enough that the missing fastening no longer interrupted the movement.

Another sibling, Telvan, had vanished after a performance in a northern town. Linias had gone there because someone told him the man played a phrase as he did. He found a landlady waiting to recover the cost of a broken chair. The traveller who had come for her lodger had paid for supper and asked that he be allowed to finish it.

“Perhaps he went with her,” Harissa said.

“His bag was there.”

“People leave bags.”

“And his shoes?”

Harissa looked away.

Linias had brought a small packet of letters. Most concerned performances, money due, invitations which had reached people too late. He untied them and found a folded scrap without a seal.

“The landlady sent this after I left.”

Riarhin read it. The woman wanted to know whether he had found the lodger and whether someone could collect the things before the room was needed again. She had written that the shoes had been mended and the cobbler still expected payment.

Linias watched Riarhin fold the scrap.

“I paid it.”

“Do you have them?”

“No. I asked her to keep them.”

“For him?”

He nodded. Harissa turned her cup slowly on the table.

“What did he play?” Riarhin asked.

Linias looked surprised. “Several things. That’s why I went. A man told me about a player who could listen once and give a tune back with the mistakes left in. He used to make people laugh doing it.”

“Did you hear him?”

“No.”

He drew one finger along the edge of the letter, opened his mouth, and closed it.

“I was going to catch him out,” he said. “I had a piece ready. Something awkward. I thought we’d play it together afterwards.”

“Would it have caught him?” Harissa asked.

“Probably not.”

He smiled briefly. Then he looked down at the letter again.

The woman had described the night badly, because nothing had seemed important until afterwards. Telvan had eaten in the kitchen rather than the common room. He had complained about a string. The visitor arrived while he was scraping his bowl. She offered to wait. Later the landlady found the kitchen empty and assumed the lodger had gone to fetch water, though the jar was full. A neighbour remembered a woman walking beside him near the lane. The neighbour could not say whether they were talking.

“Did the visitor pay for the room?” Riarhin asked.

“Only supper.”

“Did he take his instrument?”

“The small one. The other was left.”

Riarhin looked towards Linias’s case. He followed her glance and put his foot against it.

“I thought of that too.”

Zemia reached for the letter as if to help fold it. Linias had already done so. She withdrew her hand and asked whether the landlady had answered his last message.

“Not yet.”

The papers made a faint crackling sound as he tied them again. Harissa drew the discarded cup back towards her and drank what had gone cold.

Riarhin asked the dates. They answered in festivals, journeys, a woman’s pregnancy, a season in which the river had flooded. She made them go through it again. Zemia helped reconcile two accounts, then stopped when Riarhin asked when she had first heard about the woman at the kiln.

“Later,” she said. “I wasn’t there.”

Coryd drew a folded piece of bark from inside the waistcoat. A route had been scratched on its smooth face. Beneath it were marks Riarhin understood separately and could not yet make sense of together.

“She had been to the Valewood,” Coryd said. “She was going back. There’s someone at the edge who knew her.”

Riarhin touched a turn in the scratched line.

Her finger wanted to continue past it.

“Have you seen this before?” Zemia asked.

“Not this.”

She took her hand away.

Below the window, Ailsa’s daughter was telling a customer that the work would be finished when it was finished. She sounded so much like her mother that Riarhin had to wait before asking her next question.

“Did any of them call the woman their grandmother?”

Coryd shook her head. Linias said he had not been told. Harissa remembered a warning passed to her through a woman she had once performed with: an older Lethai was looking for people whose fathers had come and gone. She had thought it an objection from someone’s family to a liaison long finished.

Zemia looked at the waistcoat.

“She told me she was mine.”

Riarhin waited.

“Years ago. I thought it explained things.”

“What things?”

“The way I remembered. How quickly I could do what people showed me. How impatient they became when I didn’t understand why I should want to.”

Linias leaned forward.

“You met her?”

“Yes.”

“You never said.”

“I didn’t know then that she was looking for us like this.”

Riarhin heard the word then and kept it. She did not yet know what it contained.

“What did she tell you about our father?”

“That he had been foolish.”

Harissa snorted.

“Several women agreed with her, apparently.”

Zemia smiled too quickly and lost the smile when nobody joined her. She described the meeting as best she could: a lodging house, a woman waiting when she returned, questions about her work. Nothing in the account explained the killings. Nothing required it to.

Coryd folded the waistcoat away.

“I want to find out whether her child is safe,” she said.

“We can send a warning,” Riarhin said.

“The potter told me not to return.”

“Then ask someone he knows.”

“And tell them what?”

They spent the next hour making a message that did not give the dead woman’s child to another searcher. Riarhin watched Zemia choose each word carefully. She was good at it. When they were done, Coryd copied the message herself.

Outside, the customer finally accepted that the cloth would not be ready that afternoon. Ailsa’s daughter came in, saw the cups untouched on the table and asked whether anyone intended to help clear them.

Harissa stood at once.

While the others cleared the table, Riarhin took Coryd upstairs with the waistcoat. She wanted better light on the route fragment. Coryd followed reluctantly, carrying the bundle as if it might be taken from her.

“I’m not keeping it.”

“I didn’t say you were.”

At the window they unfolded it again. The underside of the bark was darker where it had been handled. One line ended at an old split; on the other side of the split another began, at a different angle.

“Did she draw this?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did the potter?”

“He wouldn’t tell me.”

Riarhin brought a piece of thin cloth and laid it over the fragment to keep the lifted edge from catching. Coryd watched every movement.

“We quarrelled,” she said.

Riarhin left her hand on the cloth.

“She wanted me to stay. I had work. I said I would come back when the work was done.”

“How long before?”

“A while.”

Riarhin did not ask again. Below them the daughter laughed at something Harissa had said, and Coryd shut her eyes.

“She was always asking people to stay. She asked me as if it was easy. I said something about the potter being able to keep her.”

“Did you write afterwards?”

“Yes. About whether she could mend something for a woman I knew. Nothing about what I’d said.”

Coryd opened her eyes and reached for the bark.

Riarhin wanted to tell her that sisters quarrelled, that people left one another, that a woman could remember an injury and continue loving the person who inflicted it. Coryd had not asked. She held the cloth steady until the wrapping was secure, then went downstairs with her.

At the table Harissa had separated the cups into pairs according to which would sit inside which without chipping. She was explaining the arrangement to the daughter, who listened with a growing expression of incredulity.

“It’s my house,” the daughter said.

“And they’re your cups. You may break them any way you choose.”

Coryd put the waistcoat away and took two cups to the shelf. She placed them side by side, handles out, leaving Harissa’s system unfulfilled.

Chapter 5 — Under one roof

Linias snored only when he lay on his back. Zemia said this with the confidence of long experience, then spent half the night turning him over with her foot.

In the morning he accused her of kicking him.

“You were keeping everyone awake.”

“I was asleep. I couldn’t know.”

“You know now.”

Harissa took the bread before he could. Coryd was on the roof repairing the gutter with a strip of lead somebody had left in the yard. Riarhin heard the light scrape of her boots and went outside.

“You might have asked.”

Coryd looked down.

“Would you have said yes?”

“Probably.”

“Then we’ve saved time.”

She moved across the tiles with a bucket between her ankles. Riarhin had prepared to tell her where the weak section lay. Coryd felt it, shifted her weight and passed over without looking.

Linias came into the yard with two cups. He looked up at Coryd and set one on the low wall where she could reach it when she came down.

“Does she always do this?” Riarhin asked.

“Improve houses she has no intention of living in? Frequently.”

“I can hear,” Coryd said.

“You were meant to.”

He gave Riarhin the other cup. She looked at it and then at him.

“What will you drink?”

“I’ve had mine.”

There was a wet mark on his upper lip. He wiped it when she continued looking and seemed faintly embarrassed by so much attention being given to a cup.

Inside, Zemia had begun arranging a shopping expedition. There were more people than food in the house, and Harissa distrusted the quality of the bread they had been given at the harbour. Ailsa’s daughter gave them directions, repeated them when Zemia asked where she bought lamp oil, and finally took a basket to come herself.

“I’ll go,” Riarhin said.

“You can come too.”

They went out together, leaving Linias to help move a table and Coryd on the roof. Riarhin walked behind them. The daughter knew which side of the lane to use when the water carriers passed and where to pause so a cart could turn without catching the basket. Zemia followed her advice without making her repeat it. Harissa seemed to be looking at everything else and still missed none of the places where her hem might drag.

At the bread stall, the seller offered Riarhin the flatter loaves Ailsa preferred. She took them before thinking. The daughter asked for two of the round ones as well.

“He likes those,” she said.

Her brother. Riarhin knew that. She had been buying the wrong bread for him for years because he ate it without complaint. She added another round loaf and received a look of brief, painful gratitude that she would rather have earned through something less small.

Zemia was examining a row of clay lamps. She asked the seller about the marks on their bases and listened to an account of his quarrel with a man who copied his shapes. By the time she bought one, she knew where his daughter lived and why he had stopped supplying a particular house. Harissa stood beside her making an increasingly desperate face.

“Do you need the lamp?” Riarhin asked.

“No. They need one upstairs.”

“Who told you?”

“It smoked last night.”

Riarhin had noticed the smoke. She had not thought of buying a lamp. She watched Zemia wrap it in a piece of cloth and felt a sharp irritation at being helped so readily in a house whose needs she ought to know.

Harissa caught her looking.

“She does that. You can object. It won’t improve the lamp you’ve got.”

At the next stall a man offered to carry Harissa’s basket. She let him, took it back once they had crossed a patch of mud, and thanked him with a smile that left him standing still while she walked on. The daughter laughed.

“Was he handsome?” Zemia asked.

“He was useful.”

“Harissa.”

“He was both. Will you let me have a pleasant morning?”

They bought fruit, oil, and something wrapped in leaves which Zemia assured them was excellent. Harissa said the word had been used of breakfast and must now be supported by evidence. They opened the wrapping by the water. It was too hot to eat with dignity. Riarhin burnt the roof of her mouth and laughed at Zemia doing the same.

On the way home, the daughter carried the new lamp. Riarhin reached to take it when the basket grew heavy, but the young woman moved it to her other hand and gave her the bread instead.

At the house Coryd had finished the gutter. Linias had shifted the table, put it back when it blocked the cupboard, and found a third position which satisfied neither himself nor the women returning from market. He asked which arrangement they preferred.

The daughter put down the lamp and moved the table a hand’s breadth.

“There.”

Linias looked at the space it opened. “I was about to try that.”

“You should have.”

She went to fetch the oil. Zemia unwrapped the lamp and began cleaning away the bits of straw stuck to its base.

Linias volunteered to cook the fish. The daughter asked whether he knew how. He said he knew how to cook fish. Harissa said she had heard a similar distinction made over a horse he claimed to know how to ride.

“It was a mule.”

“Did the mule know?”

Coryd took the knife from him before the argument reached the fish. She opened one with a single practised movement and set the entrails aside. Linias watched closely, then asked for the knife back. He copied the movement on the second fish, quickly enough that the daughter looked up from the oil.

“Do it slower,” Coryd said.

“Why?”

“So you see the bones.”

“I saw them.”

“Then don’t leave them in my supper.”

He did the third more slowly. He adjusted the pressure through the blade exactly where Coryd had. He argued through the next one too. Riarhin found herself smiling.

Zemia built the small cooking fire. She chose the wrong kindling from two baskets and had to take it out again because it was for lighting the workroom lamps. The daughter corrected her sharply. Zemia apologized and asked where the other pieces belonged. Harissa, sitting with her skirt held away from the wet floor, observed that they had improved the household’s speed by doubling the number of instructions required.

“Come and do something,” the daughter said.

Harissa came. She could slice greens so finely that the daughter accused her of showing off. She agreed and did the next handful finer. Linias began trying to turn a fish in the pan without breaking it. Riarhin caught the pan’s handle before it slipped off the edge of the stove.

For a little while they were all too busy to speak about who they were to one another. Oil spat. Zemia burned one fingertip and put it in her mouth. Coryd opened the door because the smoke would not draw. Linias announced that the first fish was perfectly cooked on one side and could be admired from that direction.

They ate that one while waiting for the rest. It was better than Harissa intended to admit. She asked for salt, found there was enough already, and took another piece. The daughter stood beside Riarhin with hot food balanced on a scrap of bread. Her shoulders had loosened.

When the next fish was ready, Linias offered it to her first. She accepted with the grave courtesy of a host much older than she was. Then he burnt his tongue and she laughed until she had to put her own food down.

Riarhin found herself watching the four strangers use the room. She wanted them to go before they touched anything else. She wanted to set out every cup in the cupboard and keep them there until the light failed. Harissa asked where the fruit belonged, and she managed to give an answer which put it somewhere Ailsa would not have put it.

At supper Zemia persuaded the young woman to show Harissa the workroom’s new patterns. Harissa had strong views about how cloth should hang when a body turned. They disagreed for twenty minutes and came back with a pinned length that interested them both.

Linias played while they cleared the table. Harissa took Riarhin’s hand.

“No.”

“Then stand there looking miserable. I’ll go round you.”

Riarhin let herself be moved. The step changed on the third turn. She caught the change, and Harissa laughed aloud.

“There you are.”

They went through it faster. Riarhin felt the table behind her, the loose board under one heel, the hand that asked without pulling. When the tune ended she was breathing harder than she expected. Ailsa’s son applauded by striking two spoons together until his sister took one away.

After the dance, Harissa asked about a particular turn Riarhin had made.

“You expected the floor to be farther away.”

“It usually is.”

“That’s a very tall opinion of yourself.”

Riarhin tried to show her. Harissa imitated the movement, failed deliberately, and fell into Linias’s lap. He caught her without interrupting the phrase he was playing, then objected because her elbow was in his ribs.

Coryd took over the tapping of the spoons. The son, deprived of his second one, used a knuckle against the table. They built a rhythm that wandered away from the tune and returned at a place nobody had agreed upon.

Riarhin had known companions who could move together like this after years of practice. Here the smallest invitation was caught at once. She found herself making a movement simply to see Harissa answer it, and then another because the answer had surprised her. Sweat cooled at the back of her neck. She had not thought of the grave for several minutes when the knowledge of that absence struck her.

She stopped.

Harissa let the next turn pass without trying to catch her.

“Water?”

Riarhin nodded.

She went into the yard. The repaired gutter made a straight dark line above the door. Coryd had done a good job. Ailsa had meant to have it repaired before the rains; Riarhin had offered three times and never found the right afternoon.

Zemia brought water but did not follow her into the corner where she stood. She left the cup on a barrel and went back inside, where Linias had begun a tune too difficult for anyone to dance to with dignity.

Later Riarhin asked Zemia why she had collected them all.

“Because I found Linias sleeping in a warehouse with his case under his head. Because Harissa was pretending she had hired the man who followed her. Because Coryd said she would come only as far as the next town and has been saying it ever since.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It is mine.”

Riarhin began to explain what one determined attacker could do to a gathered family.

Zemia interrupted.

“We couldn’t keep leaving people alone.”

Linias was already asleep in the corner. His head rested against the wall, his mouth open. Riarhin went to close the yard door. She returned for a blanket and laid it over his knees.

Chapter 6 — The visit to the landing

The boatman remembered the sabre because he had wanted to ask about its maker.

“I didn’t,” he said. “She wasn’t encouraging.”

“What did she ask?”

“When the northbound vessels called. Whether families took passage together. Whether a woman with pale hair had been making arrangements.”

“Did you answer?”

He looked offended.

“I sell passage.”

Riarhin asked him to describe where Farieth had stood. He pointed to a place beside a stack of baskets, clear of the men loading the boat and close enough to hear them without asking anyone to raise his voice.

“She waited?”

“Until I was finished.”

“Did she say where she came from?”

“Do most people?”

He had begun to tire of her. Riarhin put money on the bench, enough to make the next question worth answering without making him wonder what answer she wanted.

“Was she alone?”

“When she spoke to me.”

“Before?”

He looked across the water. A boy was trying to pull a float out from beneath the stern of a moored boat. The boatman told him to leave it until the tide moved. The boy pretended not to hear.

“There was a porter,” the man said. “With a bundle. I don’t know whether it was hers.”

“What bundle?”

“Wrapped. Grey. Like bundles are.”

She thanked him. As she turned away he said the woman had asked another question: whether the small coast boats could put passengers ashore without using the main quay.

“What did you tell her?”

“That it depends on the boat.”

He was right. Riarhin disliked him for being right at no cost to himself and made herself stop before the feeling became an accusation.

The porter with the grey bundle was easier to find than she expected. Three men sat in shade beside a cart, arguing over who had carried more before the sun reached the wall. One had a strip of grey cloth tied around his wrist to keep a bandage in place. He looked at the money she offered and said he had carried the woman’s bundle from a lodging near the outer gate. She paid him fairly. He had brought it to a boat going along the coast, not to the vessel Zemia was considering.

“Did she board?”

“I left before it went.”

“Did she give a name?”

“To me?”

He laughed and held out his hand for the coin. Riarhin gave it to him.

The lodging was up a lane full of hanging mats. A woman beating one with a stick told her to stand aside. Dust rolled over her boots. When it settled, Riarhin asked after the guest. The woman looked at the sword wrapping, then towards the door.

“She paid.”

“I’m not collecting money.”

“That’s what the last one said.”

Riarhin described Farieth again. The woman listened with the stick resting against her shoulder. She remembered a quiet guest who asked for a room at the back and took her own water upstairs. No, she had not complained. No, she had left nothing except a folded cloth which the woman had already washed. Yes, she knew the dead woman’s house; someone had brought mourning bread from that lane.

Riarhin felt the question she had meant to ask change in her mouth.

“Did she ask you about Ailsa?”

“She asked when the burial was.”

The stick shifted on the woman’s shoulder. “Are you the one?”

Riarhin nodded.

“I’m sorry.”

“What did she say when you told her?”

“She thanked me.”

There was no more. Farieth had slept in a room, carried water, paid for what she used, and asked when a body would be buried. Riarhin could have gone upstairs and inspected the window. She could have stood where her grandmother stood and tried to make the room confess something. Instead she asked the woman to send word if the guest returned. She gave the workroom’s direction and paid for a messenger who might never be needed.

On the way back she stopped beside the little court where the mourning bread had been baked. The oven was cold now. Two women were sorting trays. One recognized her and came forward with flour on her sleeve.

“Did you get enough?”

“Yes. Thank you.”

“There was another loaf. It went with your neighbour.”

Riarhin remembered the neighbour carrying something wrapped. She had not known where it came from. She thanked the woman again and found herself unable to leave at once. The woman took her hand, held it briefly, and let go before she had to pull away.

Riarhin walked back along the water. Nets lay over the stones. A child was picking shells from the mesh and dropping them into a broken pot. The child looked up when Riarhin’s shadow crossed her hands, then returned to work.

Farieth could ask questions here as easily as anyone.

Before returning home she went back to the grave.

She had meant to pass the lower turn without climbing. From the road she could see the wall and the place where the path entered it. There was nobody there. She climbed far enough to look over, then went the rest of the way because stopping short had become more difficult than going on.

The stone she had put over the root remained in place. Wind had dried the top of the earth. Little cracks followed the shape of the clods where water had settled them. Someone had left a handful of flowers, already wilting. Riarhin crouched and turned one stem so its cut end lay in the shade of the marker.

She listened to the path while she worked. That made her angry. She had come to a grave and was using half her attention to imagine where a sword might appear. She put both hands flat on her knees and made herself look at the earth.

There was nothing to tell Ailsa which Ailsa would answer. Riarhin had known people who spoke to graves without expecting an answer and had never considered them foolish. Now every sentence she tried sounded like something she was preparing for another listener.

“I don’t know them,” she said at last.

The wind moved the flowers.

“I want to.”

She stayed until the shadow of the marker had moved across her hand. On the way down she stopped at the place where Farieth had left the path and looked at the ground. A burr lay against the wall. It might have been the one caught in her robe. Riarhin left it where it was.

At home, Zemia had acquired three offers of passage and rejected two. The third involved a transfer and a wait whose length the captain would not guarantee.

“He says we can sleep aboard until the other vessel comes.”

“And if it doesn’t?”

“He says it always does.”

Riarhin waited.

“I asked that too,” Zemia said. “He became less agreeable.”

She had written to two people who might know better. Her letters lay open, drying on the table. Riarhin read the addresses when Zemia turned them for her.

“You have friends everywhere.”

“Acquaintances. Friends expect to be entertained.”

Riarhin told her about the landing.

The expression went out of Zemia’s face. She sat down slowly.

“How soon can we go?”

“When the household is ready for us to leave.”

“She’s looking here.”

“I know.”

That afternoon Riarhin walked the women from the workroom to their homes and spoke to the neighbours who watched the lane. She described a woman, a robe, a sword. She asked them to send word if they saw her.

The neighbour opposite wanted to know whether this would affect deliveries.

“It might.”

“Then tell your visitors not to block the gate.”

Riarhin promised.

The neighbour asked whether she should tell the watch. Riarhin considered the men who came through the lane collecting complaints and the officer who decided which of those complaints justified an interruption to his meal.

“Tell them there has been a threat. Give them the description. Don’t tell them you know what she is.”

“What is she?”

“I don’t know enough.”

The neighbour folded her arms.

“You’re not very reassuring.”

“No.”

That evening the watch came. Two men looked at the workroom entrances while a third asked questions. The youngest wanted to see the sword Riarhin carried. His companion told him to stop being an idiot and inspect the latch.

Riarhin gave them the information she had. The questioning man wrote down grandmother, paused, and looked at her again.

“Your father’s mother?”

“Yes.”

“And she has threatened you.”

“Yes.”

He asked whether there was property in dispute. Riarhin told him that she knew of none. He asked whether the visitors would leave if the household requested it. She said they were here by invitation.

When they had gone, Harissa sat at the table and repeated the youngest man’s expression on seeing Remorse. Her imitation was merciless. Linias laughed until Zemia told them the street could hear.

The older watchman returned a little later without the other two. He had left his staff outside the door and stood with his hands empty.

“I ought to ask what you intend,” he said.

“To leave.”

“With them?”

“Yes.”

He looked towards the table, where Harissa had made Linias laugh again. He waited until the sound diminished before continuing.

“If she comes here, send for us.”

Riarhin began to say that he did not understand. He lifted one hand, mildly enough to stop her without seeming to give an order.

“I’m not asking whether we can beat her in a yard. I’m asking you not to let my men find out from someone bleeding in the lane.”

She nodded.

He asked where the neighbours could send a child safely if trouble came. She showed him the rear way and the house whose owner knew the family. He asked whether that owner had agreed. Riarhin went across with him to ask. The woman opposite was displeased at the prospect and more displeased at having been taken for granted. In the end she agreed, provided nobody blocked her own stair and somebody told her husband.

The watchman waited while Riarhin told him. Then he said goodnight and went back to his men.

Riarhin remained by the open door. She had been glad to see ordinary men ask ordinary questions. They could not help her much, but they had come. Ailsa’s daughter stood beside her.

“Would it have helped if you had told them more?”

“Perhaps. It might also have made them put somebody outside all night.”

“Wouldn’t that be good?”

Riarhin looked at the empty place where a watchman might have sat.

The daughter followed her gaze, and her face changed.

“Then what do we do?”

“We make sure you can close the yard from upstairs. We keep the neighbours informed. And I leave when we can leave properly.”

“People keep saying properly as if it tells you when a thing is bearable.”

Riarhin put an arm around her. The young woman leaned against it for a moment, then went inside to complain that Harissa had moved all the cups.

Chapter 7 — What they agree to

They sat with the scratched bark between them and considered splitting up.

Harissa proposed it first. She could travel with a troupe. Linias could find another. Zemia knew enough people to disappear among them.

“For how long?” Coryd asked.

Harissa leaned back.

“I was hoping someone else would answer that.”

Riarhin had spent the morning speaking to a man who hired out guards for warehouses and caravans. She had taken Coryd because she wanted somebody beside her who would remember what was actually offered.

The man received them in a room above a yard full of men pretending not to wait for work. His desk had a broken leg propped on a block. When Riarhin sat down, he asked whether she had come to offer her own services.

“No.”

He looked disappointed. Then she described the work she wanted done.

“One person,” he said.

“That we know of.”

“Following five of you.”

“Yes.”

“And you would like an escort.”

“Would you take the work?”

He leaned back. “Where?”

“Here, for now. On the road, perhaps.”

He asked ordinary questions. Had the pursuer attacked? Had anyone reported it? Was there a dispute which might make his employees participants in an offence rather than protection against one? Riarhin answered each as carefully as she could. Coryd supplied what she knew of Dovra. The man stopped interrupting once she reached the kiln.

He called someone up from the yard. The guard who entered was broad through the shoulders, with a patched cuff and a courteous manner. He listened to the description, then asked whether the woman used the sword in her right hand.

“Either,” Riarhin said. She had not seen enough to swear which was preferred.

The guard nodded. He asked about her reach, her speed, the way she had approached at the grave. When Riarhin finished he looked at the man behind the desk.

“Six. Eight if they need to move.”

The man named the price.

Coryd made no sound. Riarhin could feel her calculating beside her.

“And if she waits?” Riarhin asked.

“We keep being paid.”

“If she follows one of us separately?”

“You don’t go separately.”

“For how long?”

The man’s expression changed. He had begun to understand that she was asking for an answer his business did not sell.

“We can keep people at the house. Get you to a ship. If you have a place to be secure at the other end, we can talk about taking you there.”

“And the people left here?”

“We can talk about that too.”

The guard stood quietly while they spoke. Riarhin liked him. That made it harder to imagine putting him in Farieth’s path without being able to tell him what she was. She asked whether he would do the work after hearing everything.

He glanced towards the yard.

“I’ve stood outside worse doors.”

“You don’t know this one.”

“No. I’d want to see it first.”

Riarhin left money for a visit to the workroom, with the household’s agreement. She made no arrangement for an escort yet.

Outside, Coryd walked beside her without speaking until they reached the corner.

“That’s a way to leave,” she said.

“Yes.”

“It could be a way to stay.”

“For a while.”

Coryd stopped. “A while is something.”

Riarhin turned back to her.

“I know.”

“Do you? You looked as if you’d already decided he couldn’t help.”

Riarhin looked towards the doorway they had left. The guard was coming down to the yard. He paused to let another man pass, then went to examine a strap someone held out to him. He could help. She had wanted him to promise something else.

At the table that evening she gave the family his terms without dismissing them. Harissa asked how long they could afford it. Linias asked whether the arrangement could be made in another city. Zemia began naming people who might offer shelter, then stopped when Coryd asked what they would tell them about the woman following.

They discussed an appeal to the local authorities. Riarhin described the watch’s visit. Coryd could testify to finding the potter and the waistcoat; Linias could describe the abandoned room. Neither could produce a person who had watched Farieth kill. The threat at the grave had no witness except Riarhin and the man below, who had seen two women speaking and a sword drawn by the one still living in the town.

“Mine,” she said when Harissa asked which sword.

Harissa closed her eyes.

Linias asked whether Riarhin could write to a queen.

“No.”

He seemed about to make a joke and thought better of it.

The woman at the Valewood’s edge had known their dead sister and had once corresponded with Farieth. That was more than they had here. Coryd knew how to reach her, though not what she would give them.

“I want to ask why Dovra went,” she said.

“Would an answer change what we do?” Harissa asked.

“It might.”

“And if it doesn’t?”

Coryd drew the wrapped waistcoat closer. “I still want to ask.”

Riarhin looked at the bark between them. She could almost place the turn she had recognized, but the familiarity might have come from any of a hundred walks. She would not take them there because a line made her finger want to continue. She described what she remembered of the Valewood instead: houses in which guests were fed, paths that needed someone to tell you when they could be used, ordinary work beside things she had never understood. She told them she had trained there and that leaving it once had not made it safe to return.

“Can you fight there?” Harissa asked.

“Yes.”

It was the first answer she had given that evening without needing to qualify it.

Harissa examined her. “And can we?”

“You can learn enough to get out of my way. Sometimes enough to help.”

“I already know how to get out of a person’s way.”

“Not while watching the person trying to kill you.”

Harissa looked away first. Linias picked up the cup he had not touched since the discussion began. Zemia watched Riarhin with an expression too intent to be simple relief.

They did not decide all at once. Coryd said she meant to follow Dovra’s route. Linias said he would go with her. Harissa wanted the terms of passage settled before she promised anything. Zemia said she had come to find them because she did not want to keep choosing which person to leave behind.

Riarhin sat with her hands on the table. There was a mark in the wood where Ailsa had cut too deep into a pattern. She remembered teasing her for it and being told to find something useful to do with her own hands.

“I’ll come,” she said.

“If we go,” Riarhin said, “you tell me when you leave the group. Even to fetch water.”

“Do you?” Coryd asked.

“Yes.”

“And if we disagree with an order?”

“Disagree before someone is trying to kill us.”

Harissa put a hand on her travelling chest.

“What did they tell you about me?” Riarhin asked.

The question caught all four of them looking elsewhere. Zemia was first to look back.

“Who?”

“The people you asked. Before you came.”

Linias shifted his cup. Harissa withdrew her hand from the chest.

“Different things,” Zemia said.

“Tell me one.”

“That you knew how to live through trouble.”

“And another.”

Zemia looked at the sword. Riarhin waited until Linias answered.

“That you killed a queen.”

The words reached the workroom below through the open stair. Riarhin heard a chair move and knew someone had stopped to listen. She got up and closed the door. When she returned, nobody was smiling.

“Did you believe it?”

“Yes,” Linias said.

Harissa looked at him, perhaps surprised by how quickly he had answered. He lifted his hands and let them fall.

“I thought you might have had a reason.”

Riarhin sat down. She could have given him reasons. She had carried them across seas, taken them into a mountain, brought them home to a woman who knew enough to hold her without requiring the account. None of that belonged in a promise made over Ailsa’s cutting table.

“People died with me,” she said.

“We know,” Zemia answered.

“No. You know that people died. You haven’t asked which people, or what I told them before they went.”

Coryd watched her steadily. Harissa had put her hand back on the chest, no longer using it as an argument.

“What are you telling us?” Coryd asked.

“That being with me hasn’t always kept people alive.”

Linias looked down. For a moment Riarhin thought she had frightened him enough to undo the agreement. Then he looked up again.

“Being without you didn’t keep Telvan alive.”

“We don’t know he’s dead.”

“We don’t know he isn’t.”

Riarhin wanted to object to the way fear made the unknown useful. She had done the same when choosing a road around an army. She let him finish.

“I didn’t come because I thought you would make it easy,” he said. “I came because I didn’t want to keep hearing afterwards.”

He stopped there. Zemia reached towards him, saw Riarhin looking, and put her hand on her own knee instead. Harissa opened the chest and took out a folded length of cloth.

“This,” she said, laying it across the table, “is why I don’t want you deciding everything is lighter when thrown away.”

It was worn at the fastening and carefully repaired at the hem. An altered seam let it open around a turning body.

“I can work in this. I can eat next month because I have it. If you tell me to run without it, I’ll run. But don’t tell me it was nothing.”

Riarhin touched the folded edge, then took her hand away.

“I won’t.”

Harissa put it back.

“Show me how to carry the chest quickly,” Riarhin said.

They spent an hour in the yard, reducing bundles and discovering that Linias had packed a second pair of shoes which hurt him.

“They were expensive.”

“Do they hurt expensively?” Harissa asked.

Riarhin taught them two signals. One sent them through the nearest prepared exit. The other told them to stop moving. They learned both at once. Harissa demonstrated the second with an imitation of Riarhin’s expression which made Linias lose his footing against a basket.

“Now do it while carrying something,” Riarhin said.

Linias took his case. Coryd lifted a bundle. Harissa folded her arms until Riarhin nodded towards the chest, then picked up one end and waited for somebody to take the other. Zemia went to help her.

Riarhin gave the first signal.

Linias went through the passage so quickly that he reached the turn before remembering he was meant to wait beyond it. Coryd followed. The chest would not fit at the angle Harissa and Zemia brought it. They set it down, changed their grip, and lifted again. Riarhin stepped into the opening and held up the hand that meant stop.

Both stopped. The corner of the chest struck her shin.

“Put it down.”

They did. Riarhin rubbed the shin while Harissa tried unsuccessfully not to smile.

“You moved into it.”

“Yes.”

“Was that part of the instruction?”

“No.”

That finished her restraint. Harissa laughed, and Riarhin laughed with her, briefly and despite the pain. Upstairs the son put his head out of the window to ask what they were doing. Harissa told him they were receiving instruction from a formidable warrior. He said they were blocking the route to the privy and went away before Riarhin could defend herself.

They moved the chest back. This time Riarhin let them finish the turn before giving the second signal. They stopped cleanly. Zemia had already transferred the weight to a place from which she could lower it without trapping Harissa’s fingers. She had changed her grip to match Harissa’s.

“Good. Again.”

Harissa rolled her eyes, but she lifted her end.

By the fourth attempt they had found a way to carry it along the narrow stretch. Linias came back to watch. He had left the instrument in the passage beyond, where it would not be struck if someone fell. Riarhin approved of that and was about to say so when Coryd asked what happened if the way out was occupied.

“By her?”

“By anyone.”

“We’ll choose another.”

“There’s one gate.”

Riarhin looked towards the wall. Its upper edge was broad enough for all of them. Beyond it lay the neighbour’s yard, reached by lowering onto the roof of a small shed. She had considered that way without describing it to them.

“Can you get up there?”

Coryd looked once and went. A foot against the projecting stone, a hand on the coping, and she was sitting on top. Her balance scarcely changed when she turned to see what lay beyond. Linias followed, using a different hold. He was delighted with himself until Riarhin told him not to stand upright against the sky.

Harissa climbed without the chest and looked down at it.

“Well?”

“We leave it.”

“I know. I was asking where you want me now.”
