Riarhin went up last. The four of them sat along the wall like children waiting to be told to get down. In the next yard the neighbour stood holding a pan.

“What?” she said.

Riarhin had not heard her door open. She looked at the others. Harissa was biting the inside of her cheek.

“An escape,” Riarhin said.

“From your manners?”

Linias covered his face. Riarhin apologized and asked whether they might try the descent onto the shed. The neighbour considered the roof, then the four bodies poised above her washing.

“One at a time. Not on that corner.”

Coryd went first. She tested the roof before taking her full weight, climbed down a post, and moved out of the next person’s way. Harissa followed, then Linias. Zemia had remained below with the chest. When Riarhin returned over the wall, she was waiting beside it.

“You can make it,” Riarhin said.

“I know.”

“Then show me.”

Zemia looked up at the edge. She could have made the climb easily; Riarhin had watched her move aboard carts with both arms full. She lifted a hand towards the projecting stone and put it down again.

“What?” Riarhin asked.

“If you’re still here.”

“You keep going.”

Zemia’s gaze moved to the passage where the others would return. Riarhin heard them thanking the neighbour, heard the gate catch rise. There would be no use telling this woman she could not help. She would look for something to carry, someone to fetch, and remain until she found it.

“Count them when you’re over,” Riarhin said. “Know where they’ve gone. If you have to leave the yard, make sure the others can see you.”

“And you?”

“Then I know where to come.”

Zemia climbed. She paused at the top, looking back. Riarhin pointed towards the shed. Her sister went down.

By the time they returned, their father had brought a board to put beneath the chest so its bottom would stop marking the paving. He asked whether they had finished. Riarhin looked at the sunlight leaving the wall, the dirt on Harissa’s palms, the way Linias kept glancing towards the height he had crossed.

“For today.”

He nodded and went to wash. Riarhin moved the chest onto the board herself. Her shin had begun to bruise.

Coryd brought out the money they had left. It was less than Riarhin expected and more than Harissa wanted discussed in front of everyone. She took a smaller purse from her chest and added it without speaking.

“You said you had none,” Linias said.

“I said I had none for your shoes.”

Riarhin put her own money beside theirs. They could travel, eat, and replace some things. They could not hire a private army. She asked Coryd to keep the account.

“Who do we tell?” Zemia asked.

“Our hosts. Those who need to receive us. Nobody for the pleasure of announcing a journey.”

“I owe people letters.”

“Write them.”

“With no address?”

“With an address where a letter can wait without telling anyone where you sleep.”

Zemia considered this, then nodded. Coryd named a merchant at the Thin Coast who accepted packets for a fee. Harissa knew somebody who had used him and complained about the fee. They argued over whether his nephew still worked there.

Riarhin listened. Their lives had already crossed in ways neither she nor they had understood. Harissa had performed in a courtyard where Coryd repaired the drainage two seasons later. Linias knew the brother of a woman Zemia had stayed with. Linias tried to work out whether he had played there that summer. Harissa said she would have remembered.

When the argument became loud enough to bring the daughter downstairs, Riarhin asked them to lower their voices.

They did, for perhaps a minute.

As the light went, Zemia returned to the table and folded the bark in its wrapping.

“Our sister went there,” she said.

“That doesn’t make it safe,” Riarhin answered.

“No. But somebody there knew her.”

Chapter 8 — Leaving things in place

Riarhin left the yellow bird on the shelf.

She moved it twice before deciding where. The first place caught the sun too strongly. The second was where Ailsa had always kept it.

Her daughter watched from the door.

“I can dust it.”

“I know.”

“You don’t have to take everything she gave you.”

Riarhin sat on the bed. The girl—no longer a girl, and capable of reminding her—came and sat beside her.

“Will you come back?”

“I mean to.”

“That’s a horrible answer.”

“Yes.”

The young woman got up first. She opened the small chest at the foot of the bed and took out a folded garment which Riarhin had not known was there. Ailsa’s blue work dress, washed and mended at the elbow. The daughter held it against her own body, looking at the length.

“I was going to change it.”

“You should.”

“I know I should. I’m telling you.”

Riarhin nodded. The dress would need to be taken in at the waist and let out somewhere else. She had watched Ailsa wear it until the cuffs softened, watched her lift the skirt over a puddle, watched the blue turn almost black in rain. It was cloth in the daughter’s hands. She knew how to make it fit.

“There’s a pocket inside,” Riarhin said.

“I found it.”

“On the other side.”

The daughter looked. Her fingers entered the narrow opening and came out with a piece of chalk wrapped in thread. She stared at it, then laughed and laid it on the bed.

“She was always losing them.”

“She was always hiding them from you.”

“That too.”

They folded the dress again. The daughter put it in a different place, among the things she meant to work on. Riarhin watched her close the chest without returning it.

When the cart arrived, neither had said everything she had come upstairs to say.

The guard from the warehouse yard had come the previous afternoon. He had looked at the entrances, spoken to the neighbours, and moved a stack of empty frames away from the wall because someone could use them to reach the upper window. The children’s father had gone around with him, asking questions Riarhin would not have thought to ask about deliveries and the night use of the lane.

They had agreed on visits, company for late work, a signal to the house opposite. It was less than a fortress and more than the family had possessed when Riarhin first brought a sword downstairs. She paid the first part from the chest. Their father kept the direction of the guard’s lodging and would decide whether the arrangement needed to continue.

“Don’t send money we haven’t asked for,” he told her when she began to explain how more could be obtained.

“I can—”

“I know. We can ask.”

She stopped. He had folded the direction carefully and placed it with the workroom’s other papers, where Ailsa’s daughter could find it too.

Downstairs, the children’s father checked the straps with Coryd. The son had found a better wedge for the shutter and wanted Riarhin to look at it before she went. She went upstairs again. It held cleanly, without marking the wall. The son put his hand against the shutter and pushed, harder than necessary.

“It won’t come loose.”

“I can see.”

“I tried it from outside.”

“You shouldn’t have—”

He looked at her. She stopped.

“The guard was there,” he said. “He held the ladder.”

She examined the wedge again. He had cut it to follow the uneven wall, taking more trouble than she would have taken with a temporary repair. There was a smear of blood on the edge where a tool had slipped. He saw her looking and put the hand behind his back.

“It’s nothing.”

“I wasn’t going to say anything.”

“You looked.”

“Yes.”

For a moment they stood in the narrow space between the bed and window, each waiting for the other to find a better way to leave the conversation. Then he took a small packet from his coat.

“For the journey.”

Dried fruit, tied in a piece of scrap cloth. Ailsa had sent him to school with packets like it. He had complained that the fruit made his books sticky.

Riarhin put it in her pocket without opening it.

“Thank you.”

He nodded, looking at the shutter. She touched his shoulder. He came towards her so abruptly that their foreheads nearly struck. She held him until he pulled away, then let him go without straightening his collar.

Ailsa would have admired the work and objected to the wood. Riarhin said so. He smiled.

Their father walked with Riarhin to the corner while the cart was loaded. He carried a roll of cloth under one arm to deliver after seeing them aboard.

“She asked me to look after you,” he said.

Riarhin stopped.

“When?”

“Before it was bad. When she still thought telling people what to do would settle things.”

Riarhin laughed once and had to look away.

“What did you say?”

“That you would make it difficult.”

“She knew that.”

“Yes.”

He shifted the cloth and looked back towards the house. Their daughter was telling Linias how to lift his case without dragging it against the newly painted door.

“I can keep the work going for a while,” he said. “She knows most of it. He’ll help when he stops thinking harbour work is the only work anybody does.”

“Take money from the chest if you need it.”

“I will. And I’ll write it down, because you’ll forget that you told me.”

“I won’t forget.”

“You’ll remember telling me and forget what it meant.”

She looked at him, then nodded. He had known her seven years. He was entitled to that much accuracy.

He offered his hand. She took it, then drew him into an embrace that surprised them both. The cloth slipped between them. He swore softly as it began to unroll, and they separated to catch it.

At the end of the lane their father asked her to wait while he delivered the cloth. Riarhin stood beneath an awning whose edge had dripped on her for seven winters. Someone had finally mended the hole. She looked up at the patch and found herself annoyed that she had not been there to complain about the work.

He returned without the roll. His hands looked oddly empty.

“She wanted the blue,” he said.

“Who?”

“The woman I took that to. Asked whether there was any of the blue left. I told her to ask at the house.”

There was blue left. Ailsa had put it aside for somebody who had changed her mind, and the daughter had spent an afternoon finding room for it. Riarhin almost explained where it was. He knew. He had carried it upstairs.

He leaned against the post beside her.

“I keep doing that,” he said. “Sending people to ask.”

“She knows the work.”

“Our daughter? Yes. I didn’t mean her.”

Riarhin looked at him. He had stood at the bedside without making a difficulty of her being there, had fetched clean linen and opened the window when Ailsa asked. She had been grateful for the lack of a scene and had mistaken it, at times, for ease.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

He shook his head, impatiently rather than in forgiveness. “It was years ago. We made our arrangements.”

“I know.”

“No, you don’t. That’s all right.”

They stood beneath the repaired awning. A carrier pushed past with a board too wide for the lane. Riarhin stepped aside, putting her hand against the post behind the man’s head to keep him from striking it as he turned. The carrier thanked her without looking up.

“I’d like you to come back,” their father said.

She could not answer at once. The permission she had been seeking from the children had arrived from someone whose permission she had forgotten she might need.

“If I can.”

“Yes. We all heard you upstairs.”

She rubbed her thumb along the post. There was a groove where a cord had worn the wood.

“She wanted to see the mountains again,” she said.

“She told me.”

“I thought we had time.”

“So did I.”

He pushed away from the post. The cart had begun to move at the end of the lane. Harissa called to somebody to lift the other corner, and Linias replied that he had only the usual number of arms.

Their father glanced towards the sound, then back at her.

“I used to think she was saving the best of herself for somewhere else,” he said. “When we were young. If we got to the right place, I’d see it.”

Riarhin could remember that hope in herself so vividly that she felt ashamed on his behalf before she could stop herself. He saw something in her face and smiled without much pleasure.

“I got older.”

She put her hand on his arm. He covered it briefly with his own.

“Tell them about her,” he said, looking towards the cart. “The things that would have made her cross too. They might as well know who you’re missing.”

Then he went to help Coryd with the wheel, leaving Riarhin under the awning until she could follow.

At the harbour Zemia took her letters to a man who carried packets between vessels. She waited for him to put them in the leather bag rather than leaving them on his stool. Then she bought bread and a little jar of something sour for the crossing.

Harissa was already arguing with a sailor about where her chest would go. Linias stood beside it, unhelpfully explaining that the instrument was more vulnerable than the chest. Coryd had found the captain and was asking where they could sleep without being stepped on.

Riarhin bought a plain knife from a man with a tray outside the harbour gate. She tried the grip in each hand and asked him to fetch a sheath that did not split when bent. He offered her a second blade for less money. She shook her head, wrapped the one she had chosen, and put it inside her pack. The man wished her a safe journey with the enthusiasm of somebody who hoped she would buy another on her return.

Riarhin turned back.

Ailsa’s daughter lifted a hand. Her father stood behind her, one hand on the cart’s rail. The son had gone to work; he had said goodbye badly and fiercely at the house.

Riarhin lifted hers.

When the vessel moved, she watched the gap grow between the hull and the quay. Zemia came to stand beside her but did not begin a conversation.

After a while she opened the jar and offered it.

Riarhin took a piece with her fingers.

It was so sour that her eyes watered. Zemia laughed, then tasted it herself and made a worse face.

They finished the jar before the harbour disappeared.

Part II — What the body learns

Chapter 9 — Passage

On the ninth morning Linias attempted to help a sailor and was told, with considerable feeling, to stop.

He stood holding the rope he had caught.

“I had it.”

“I could see that. Let it go.”

The rope ran through the man’s hands, a spar moved, and the sail filled with a noise like a door slamming in a large empty room.

Linias looked up.

“Oh.”

“Yes. Oh.”

By evening he and the sailor were playing for the same audience. The man knew three songs and insisted they were all different despite sharing nearly every note. Harissa sang the second verse of one over the third verse of another and demonstrated the problem. Nobody thanked her.

Before the music there had been a dispute about drinking water. A passenger had filled a basin to wash a stain from his coat. The cook saw him and demanded it back. The man pointed out that the water was already in the basin, which the cook appeared to regard as an additional offence rather than an argument.

Harissa stepped between them carrying a cup. She asked the passenger whether he knew what had stained the coat. He did not. She examined it, rubbed a corner between her fingers and pronounced that water would set it more firmly. Riarhin had no idea whether this was true. The man surrendered the basin and accepted advice about leaving the stain alone until they reached land.

When he had gone, the cook asked Harissa whether she wanted a job.

“Yours looks dreadful.”

“It is.”

“Then no.”

She drank the cup of water she had been carrying and returned to her place. Coryd asked what the stain was.

“Something he has now stopped washing.”

The passenger held his coat away from the spray. When Harissa passed him later, he thanked her.

Riarhin sat on a coil of spare line with her feet braced against a timber. The air smelt of tar and cooking onions. A pot moved on its hook with the ship. Each time it swung, the cook steadied it without looking.

She had written a letter that morning and could not decide whether it was worth sending. It described weather and food. Ailsa would have read both with interest, then asked what she was leaving out. Her daughter might do the same.

Zemia sat beside Linias and corrected a word he sang.

“That doesn’t mean what you think it means.”

“It means exactly what I think it means.”

“Then you have greatly offended the governor’s wife.”

“The governor’s wife was never going to hear it.”

A sailor asked for the word again.

Riarhin laughed and turned to her right.

There was only the folded sailcloth beside her.

She kept looking at it until the tune changed. Then Coryd put a cup where her hand could reach it and sat on the other side of the coil.

The weather changed during the night. By morning the water beyond the rail looked like hammered metal, each ridge breaking in a different direction. They ate little. Zemia, who had been steady through the first days, became ill and furious with anyone who suggested lying down.

Linias held her hair. Harissa held the cup she could not yet drink. Coryd secured the belongings that slid towards the lee side. Riarhin sat where Zemia could lean against her without having to ask.

“I have crossed before,” Zemia said between bouts.

“So has the ship,” Linias answered. “It appears to be making a fuss anyway.”

She tried to laugh and was sick again.

The sea kept them there all afternoon. Riarhin’s back ached against the timber. Zemia grew heavier against her. When at last she slept, Riarhin did not move to ease the pressure.

Linias sat opposite with his hands loose between his knees.

“How long have you known her?” Riarhin asked.

“Long enough to have seen her ill in more flattering ways.”

“How long?”

He named a summer and a town, then gave a number of years that seemed too short for their ease together.

“She found me with a debt and a broken instrument. She found someone to mend it. I paid her back.”

“Did she ask?”

“No. That made it worse.”

He watched Zemia sleep.

“She never let me forget the song I played to thank her, though. It was a terrible song.”

“Was it?”

“It was very sincere.”

Later, when Riarhin’s leg began to go numb, he moved Zemia carefully against his own shoulder. She woke only enough to complain that he was bony.

Riarhin went to loosen the stiffness in her back. The sailor who had rebuked Linias stood near the mast, looking up. She followed his gaze and saw a loose length of canvas beating against a line. He called to another man. The other shook his head and pointed towards the captain.

Riarhin kept out of their way. On a road, knowing what a man meant to do often gave her enough to help him do it. Aboard ship she could see the movements without knowing which load must be allowed to move and which would tear something loose. She found a place where she could watch without being called upon and disliked how much it resembled waiting to be told where to sit.

The vessel rolled. A passenger who had been crossing the deck lost his footing, recovered, and laughed too loudly. The next roll came before the laugh ended. He struck the rail with his hip. Riarhin caught the back of his coat and pulled him down beside her.

“Stay low.”

“I’m quite capable—”

A wave burst against the bow and sent water along the deck. He stopped speaking. Riarhin held him until he had found a grip, then released him. He crawled towards the covered place without thanking her.

Coryd had come to secure one of their bundles. She looked at the water moving around Riarhin’s boots and pointed towards the lee rail. Harissa’s chest was no longer where they had lashed it. One strap had loosened. The chest shifted a little with each roll, turning its heavier end towards the open deck.

Riarhin reached it first. She put a knee against the lid and caught the loose strap. Harissa shouted from the covered place. Riarhin could not hear the words. Coryd came down beside her, took the strap, and tried to pass it beneath the timber it had been tied to.

The ship lifted. The chest pressed hard against Riarhin’s thigh. Something inside knocked against the lid.

“Let it settle,” Coryd said.

Riarhin eased her weight. The chest settled. Coryd got the strap under, drew it through, and found the torn place where it would not hold a knot under strain.

“Another.”

Riarhin looked towards their bundles. Harissa was already coming, holding the sash she had taken from her waist. She moved in the pauses between the vessel’s rolls, so neatly that a sailor reaching to stop her missed her arm. At the chest she went to her knees and passed the sash beneath it.

“Not that side,” Coryd said.

“It will catch the hinge.”

“It has to.”

Harissa hesitated. The next roll drove all three of them against the timber. Riarhin’s hand slipped. She caught the edge of the lid and felt the nail of one finger bend back. Coryd hauled the sash through the wrong place for preserving a hinge and the right place for holding a chest.

“Now.”

Harissa pulled. The knot held. The hinge creaked. They remained over it through another roll, waiting to see what moved.

Nothing did.

A sailor swore at them for obstructing the deck. Riarhin began an answer and then saw the line in his hands, the place where it needed to pass. She got Harissa up. Coryd went after them backwards until there was room to turn.

In shelter, Harissa looked at Riarhin’s hand. A little blood showed beneath the raised nail. She took a strip of cloth from her pocket and gave it to her. Then she looked back at the chest.

“I could have put the sash around the other end.”

“It’s holding,” Coryd said.

“I saw another way.”

“So did the sea.”

Harissa turned on her, ready to answer. Coryd was wiping salt water out of her eyes with the heel of one hand. The anger went out of Harissa’s shoulders. She sat down and began wringing her skirt onto a place the cook immediately told her not to use.

Linias, still holding Zemia, watched them return.

“Have we saved all our irreplaceable possessions?”

“Except your wit,” Harissa said. “That went over early.”

He looked towards the chest. “I’d have helped.”

Zemia’s head lay heavy against his shoulder. Riarhin nodded towards her. “You are.”

He glanced down as if he had forgotten she was there. She stirred, opened her eyes, and asked who had been shouting. Harissa said everyone. Zemia shut her eyes again.

When the weather eased, the damaged hinge would not open cleanly. Coryd worked at it while Harissa watched, making suggestions until Coryd gave her the tool. Harissa tried, discovered the resistance was elsewhere than she thought, and handed it back without speaking. Riarhin sat with her aching finger in her lap. She had offered the knife she bought at the harbour and been told its point was too thick.

The chest opened at last. Some damp had entered. Harissa unfolded her clothes one at a time, laying them over every surface the crew would permit. Linias held two lengths above the deck while she examined the hems. A strand of gold-coloured thread had pulled loose from one. She touched it with an expression Riarhin had seen on men examining a damaged sword.

“Can it be mended?”

“Yes. It’s only thread.”

Harissa continued looking at it. Riarhin stayed beside her long enough to understand that the answer did not end the disappointment.

They travelled west through successive calls, taking on cargo that filled the spaces in which they had become comfortable. At one harbour their captain put letters aboard a faster vessel sailing ahead. Zemia recognized the packet-man’s bag and asked whether theirs had gone. He told her they had.

Riarhin gave him her own letter.

The captain kept them aboard through that call. Cargo had to be moved, the quay was crowded, and he had no wish to send men through the town looking for passengers who thought a tide would wait because they had paid in advance.

Coryd asked whether there was work for somebody who understood a line. The captain looked at her hands, then gave her to a sailor who did not welcome the gift. Riarhin watched them lower a bale towards the quay. Coryd kept out of the man’s way until he told her where to stand, then held the line exactly as he asked.

On the second bale he stopped checking her.

Linias leaned on the rail beside Riarhin.

“She’ll be captain before we leave.”

“She’ll be tired.”

“She likes that better than waiting.”

A man below called for the next load. The sailor moved too soon, or the man moved too late; Riarhin saw their intentions separate before she saw the danger. The bale swung over an empty patch of quay and began to turn.

Coryd let the line run.

The sailor shouted at her. She ignored him for one breath and then took the strain again, easing the load until it struck the quay on its broad side. Cloth burst from one seam. The man who had been beneath it jumped clear.

Everyone spoke at once.

Riarhin had a hand on the rail, ready to jump down. Coryd looked up at her and shook her head.

The sailor demanded to know why she had let go. She showed him the burned place across her palm and pointed to the man on the quay. He repeated his question. She gave him the same answer, louder. The captain came over before either grew inventive.

Coryd listened to the captain, lifted the line and demonstrated the moment at which it had twisted. He examined the block overhead. Then he sent the sailor to fetch a replacement and told Coryd to wash her hand.

The sailor passed Riarhin with his jaw tight. She could have said something. She moved aside instead.

Coryd came to the water vessel and held her hand beneath a little stream poured by Zemia. The skin was reddened but whole. She flexed her fingers, judging what she would be able to do, then accepted the clean cloth Harissa brought.

“You could have been pulled over,” Riarhin said.

“I let it run before that.”

“I saw.”

“Then why are you telling me?”

Riarhin looked down at the quay. The man who had nearly been struck was gathering cloth and saying something to an officer of the port.

“Is it bad?” she asked, indicating the hand.

“No.”

“Let me know if it becomes bad.”

Coryd nodded, accepting that much without an argument.

Later the captain paid her for the morning. She took the coins and went back to the sailor to ask whether he still needed help with the line. He grunted. She took her place beside him. By the time the quay began slipping astern, they were complaining about the men ashore with the easy contempt of people who had survived working together.

Riarhin went to the place where she slept. Her first letter had already gone. She began another on the back of a scrap that had wrapped a purchase, telling Ailsa’s daughter about Coryd and the damaged bale. She left out the part about her own hand on the rail, then put it in. At the bottom she asked whether the new lamp worked and whether the shutter had been painted.

There was no place for a reply to catch them until the next agreed address. She folded the letter anyway.

At a later call they changed vessels. The new captain would take them only as far as the next connection and refused to include waiting time in the price. Zemia argued until he began dealing with somebody else. Harissa took her sleeve and brought her away.

“You’ve got what he’ll give.”

“He gave that man a better place.”

“That man owns half the cargo.”

Zemia looked at the bales being loaded. Her mouth moved once before she turned away.

They moved their possessions across two planks and through a crowd which had no reason to make their journey easier. Linias took the instrument himself. Coryd and Riarhin lifted Harissa’s chest while Harissa carried the smaller bags. Zemia kept the papers dry against her body and turned back three times to check that all of them had crossed.

The space assigned to them was lower, hotter, and noisier. A family nearby had a child who kicked in sleep. The father apologized before the first night began. Harissa said she had travelled with worse. By morning she withdrew the assurance.

Riarhin woke to a small heel against her calf and the child staring at her hair. She raised a hand slowly so the child could see she meant no harm. The child asked whether it had always been that colour.

“For as long as I remember.”

“Mine will be brown.”

“It is brown.”

“When I’m old.”

The father caught the child’s arm and began to apologize. Riarhin told him it was all right. It was pleasant to be questioned about something she could answer without hiding a body inside it.

On the third evening Linias let the child touch the unstrung length he kept for repairs. He wound it around a piece of wood and made a faint note by pulling it taut. The child tried to reproduce the sound, failed, and became angry. Linias changed the position of the child’s hand, waited, and changed it again when the next attempt hurt.

Harissa watched him without her usual comment. When the father came to take the child, Linias gave him the little piece of wood and kept the string. The child objected. He said the string was his and the wood could have another. Riarhin liked him for keeping something he needed.

That night the cook let them eat near his fire because the deck had become too wet. Harissa described a patron who had paid her to dance and then spent the performance explaining how it should be done. Linias claimed to have met the same man in every town he had visited.

Riarhin listened with her bowl held in both hands. She was warm. For a little while she was warm enough.

Chapter 10 — Where to stand

Coryd put her blanket in the wrong place because she had obeyed the instruction she was given.

The sailor had told her to keep clear of the hatch. She had measured the hatch’s sweep and left exactly enough room. Now the sailor wanted the space beside it too, because a man carrying a sack could not turn there.

“You said the hatch.”

“I meant the hatch.”

They stared at each other.

Riarhin began to intervene and remembered Ghere standing in a doorway with a bowl in his hands.

She had been fourteen, more or less. She had believed she was hungry in a way nobody in the Valewood could understand.

“Leave it there,” he had said.

She had left the bowl on the ledge beside her. Ghere looked at it, then at her, then carried it to the other side of the room.

“You said there.”

“Yes.”

“That was where I was.”

A woman working at the low hearth looked up. Riarhin knew every word of what she said. Together they seemed to mean that bowls belonged where the people who needed them would be.

“How am I meant to know where they will be?”

Ghere set his bowl down.

“You have been in this room.”

“For a day.”

He looked genuinely puzzled by the distinction.

At supper she sat where Ghere put her and discovered halfway through that she was expected to move. The person arriving was wet from the rain. Her place was nearest the warm stone. Nobody said that this was why. They looked at her until she understood that they wanted something and asked, in the language she knew, why everyone was looking.

Ghere touched her arm.

She stood too quickly. The bowl overturned. She caught it before it struck the floor, but the food was gone.

Somebody smiled at the catch. It made her angrier than laughter would have.

Outside she crouched beneath a tree and watched rain run from a leaf onto the back of her hand. Ghere came with more food. She refused it because she wanted him to know how much she was hurt. Then she took it because she was hungry.

“You know the words,” he said.

“Yes.”

He waited, as if that ought to lead somewhere.

“Tell me when you want me to move.”

“You could see she was wet.”

“So am I.”

He looked at her wet sleeves. At last he sat beside her, allowing the rain to reach him too.

For some time neither spoke. She ate. The food tasted of something she had never had in Silkstrand, sharp at first and then warm in the mouth. She wanted to ask what it was and refused herself the pleasure of asking until she had finished being angry.

Ghere told her anyway, touching a leaf near his foot. She looked at it. He showed her the difference between it and another growing beside it. She retained the distinction immediately, and his expression brightened with a pride she had not yet learned to distrust.

Later he took her to a place where water fell through the roof into a stone basin. Ferns grew from the broken rim. She knelt and drank, then put her whole face under the thin stream. When she sat back, he was smiling.

She threw water at him.

He moved just enough. She tried again. The third time he let it strike his sleeve, and she knew he had let it. She was angry for a moment, then laughed anyway.

She had wanted very badly to live there.

On the walk back, Ghere took a different path. She followed his feet through a patch of low fern and came out above a cistern whose sides fell farther than she could judge. Water lay so still beneath them that at first she mistook it for a dark floor.

He set down his empty bowl and began clearing leaves from a narrow channel. She crouched to help. He caught her wrist before she reached into the channel’s mouth, moved her fingers to the safe edge, and released her. Something below the rim closed with a soft click.

She pulled back.

“Tell me first.”

He looked at the place where the leaves had been. She expected him to say she should have seen it. Instead he touched her wrist again, lightly, checking that the skin was whole.

“Here,” he said, showing the edge. “Only here.”

They worked from opposite ends. It was easier to do something with him when neither expected conversation. She collected the leaves in her skirt, he opened a second channel, and the water beneath them shivered. A pale disk moved across it.

Riarhin looked up. The sky above the cistern was a pale evening blue. Nothing stood where the reflected moon should have been.

“Ghere.”

He came beside her.

In the water a row of white columns stretched around the cistern. The broken stone beneath her feet formed an unbroken walk. Shadows crossed it, too quickly to be clouds. One paused on the farther side. A sleeved arm rested on a low wall, something bright held in the hand.

She leaned forward.

Ghere put his arm across her chest and brought her back. He did not hurt her. That made it harder to object.

“I can stand.”

“I know.”

“What is it?”

“Water.”

She turned on him. “I know what water is.”

He knelt, took a leaf from her skirt, and placed it on the surface close to the rim. It floated across the reflected colonnade. The shadow did not move out of its way. Riarhin watched until the leaf reached a seam in the stone and stopped.

“Was it like that?” she asked.

“Perhaps.”

“When you came?”

“No.”

“Before?”

“I don’t know.”

She disliked that answer almost as much as the ones whose meanings he expected her to supply. She had come to a place where everything seemed old enough to know. Ghere stood beside a city in the water and told her he did not.

The figure at the far side bent its head. Riarhin moved to see past the reflection of her own shoulder. A hand rose in the water, palm turned towards her.

She lifted hers.

Ghere lowered it.

“Was it asking?” she said.

“I don’t know.”

He was watching the water with an expression she had not seen on him before. The reflected hand remained lifted after her own had been lowered. Then a little wind reached the cistern and broke it into light.

They left the channel clear. Ghere took her bundle of wet leaves when it grew too heavy and carried it until they found a place to empty it. She asked him whether the walk had belonged to people like them.

“People lived there.”

“Like you.”

He glanced at her. “Some.”

“Then why is it broken?”

He waited while she stepped over a root. She could feel him deciding how much she could be told, and was ready to resent it before he spoke.

“What have you heard?”

She gave him the account Bea had once repeated after a visitor left: people reaching too high, a gift divided so they could never reach so high again. Bea had made it sound like a warning told to children who climbed where somebody might have to fetch them down. Riarhin had liked the part about reaching.

Ghere listened until she finished.

“Who did it?” she asked.

“I wasn’t there.”

She stopped walking. This seemed to her a deliberate refusal of the question.

“Then who told you?”

He named someone whose name she would hear used for other people, and for a place, over the following years. At that moment she understood it as an old teacher. It was enough to let her continue beside him without being angry all the way home.

At the door he gave her back the empty bowl. She remembered where he had put it earlier and carried it there. The woman at the hearth smiled.

Outside, Ghere shook the rain from his sleeves. He did not see Riarhin watching to find out whether he was pleased.

Later the woman at the hearth asked whether Riarhin understood what had been said about the sleeping room. Riarhin had understood the door and something about the morning. Ghere looked up from his bowl.

She nodded.

The woman smiled and returned to her work. Nobody moved her chair or began the explanation again in the slower voice which made everyone else stop talking. Riarhin waited until Ghere went out, then followed another person carrying bedding. She put hers where that person put his and watched which door he used.

Before they slept, Ghere said she was learning. She smiled at the right moment.

The following morning she woke before anyone called her. She had slept in a room whose far side was open to leaves. The bed was narrow, the blanket heavier than the air required. At first she could not remember where her boots were. Someone had moved them out of the damp and put them beneath the bed.

She went looking for Ghere and found three people taking a fallen branch apart with small blades. One held the branch under tension while another cut. The third stripped bark from a section already freed. Riarhin stood at the edge of the work until the woman with the blade looked up and said something about the far end.

She took it.

The woman made a sharper sound. Riarhin held still. The branch was straining against her palm. She could feel where it would go when released and shifted her hand to keep it from striking the worker’s face.

That produced another objection.

“I’m holding it,” Riarhin said.

The woman pointed to a fork beyond her elbow. Riarhin changed her grip. The tension eased. The cut finished, and the branch swung harmlessly down between them.

The woman said the same words again, more slowly. Riarhin heard that she had been asked to hold the far end after it fell. The place in the sentence where she expected the distinction contained a word for the person who had held it yesterday. She had not been there yesterday. She could not make the explanation improve itself by being remembered.

When Ghere arrived, she was stripping bark with the third worker. He crouched to watch. The blade fitted her hand badly, so she had turned it and was drawing the bark towards herself with the edge held almost flat. The worker beside her showed a better angle. She used it at once.

Ghere smiled. Riarhin bent her head to keep him from seeing how much the smile mattered.

They made hoops from the thin strips. Riarhin thought they were for baskets. A child came to take two and began rolling them along the path. The worker who had cut the branch called after him. He returned one, kept the other, and vanished into the ferns.

“Was that allowed?” Riarhin asked.

Ghere looked towards the path.

“That one.”

“Why that one?”

He pointed at a split in the remaining hoop. It was to be rebound before someone put weight on it. The child’s hoop would hold its shape as it rolled. Riarhin waited for the rest of the explanation. There was none.

She worked until her fingers hurt. Nobody praised her for continuing. They asked her to move once because her knees blocked a strip they wanted, gave her water, and eventually took the blade away because there was nothing left to cut. The hoops she had made lay among the others.

Ghere asked whether she was tired.

“No.”

He looked at her hands. “Come.”

He brought her to a path which climbed through trees so large she could not see their tops. The people she had worked beside went ahead, talking without waiting for her. Ghere followed her this time. When she reached a place where two roots crossed, he let her choose and corrected her only after she had committed to the harder one.

“Why didn’t you tell me?”

“You can cross there.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

He waited while she crossed. The root curved out over a hollow and returned to ground beyond a stand of pale stems. She put one hand against the trunk, felt the living surface give beneath her palm, and moved along it. There was a moment halfway when the hollow seemed to deepen without the ground moving. She kept her eyes on the far end.

On the other side she turned, breathless and pleased despite herself.

Ghere had come by the easy way.

She laughed at him, surprised by how much she wanted to. He lifted one eyebrow. The expression was so like a man enjoying a private joke that she forgot, briefly, to resent being the subject of it.

The clearing beyond held people she had not seen before. A woman was binding another’s ankle. Someone slept with an arm over his face. Two children argued over a length of cord and were told to take the argument somewhere less audible. Riarhin tried to decide who was important. Ghere sat beside the person with the injured ankle and began helping with the binding. She remained standing until a woman pushed a folded cloth towards her with one foot.

She sat on it. No one asked her to explain why she had come.

When they returned at evening, the hoop she had made had been bound onto a basket. A different person carried it past her without knowing whose work it was. She watched it go until Ghere asked what she was looking at.

“Nothing.”

He did not believe her. He did not ask again.

On the ship Coryd was rolling her blanket with short, furious movements.

“He meant the space for the people using it,” Riarhin said.

“Then he could have said so.”

“Yes.”

Coryd glanced at her, expecting more.

Coryd sat on the rolled blanket while the cook argued with a sailor about his missing knife.

“Ghere was your brother?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Did he know about us?”

“I don’t know.”

“Would he have told you?”

Riarhin watched the cook recover the knife from exactly where the sailor said it had been.

“I would once have said yes.”

Coryd nodded as though this answered more than it did.

“He got me out of Silkstrand,” Riarhin said. “I owe him that.”

“Is he dead?”

“I don’t know that either.”

She disliked how much of her life became uncertain when somebody asked ordinary questions about it. Dates blurred because she had stopped keeping them, but she remembered a man’s thumb against a practice blade and the shape of a wet bowl on stone. Ghere would have understood the difference. He might have found her inability to answer entirely reasonable, which irritated her more.

Coryd stood and picked up the blanket.

“If you find him, ask.”

Riarhin helped move the blanket. They chose another place together, asked the cook whether it would obstruct him, and were told that everywhere obstructed him eventually.

“Then we’ll move eventually,” Coryd said.

Chapter 11 — The changed landing

By the time they reached the Dolmis connection, the house in which Zemia had booked rooms had lost half its roof to a fire.

The owner showed them the rooms anyway.

“It doesn’t rain often.”

Water dripped from a beam into a basin.

“That came from putting it out,” he explained.

He had brought them upstairs past a blackened cupboard and a pair of doors laid flat upon trestles. On the lower landing a girl was scraping soot out of the grooves of a bedstead. She did not look up when her father said the house would be back in order by the end of the week. Her scraper stopped moving. Riarhin looked through the space where the upper room’s ceiling had been and saw a gull settle on a rafter.

“We paid for three nights,” Zemia said.

“And I’ve kept three nights.”

“For five people.”

“There’s room.”

He showed them where another bed could go. The boards dipped beneath his shoe. He moved his foot before continuing, but Coryd had seen it. She put Harissa’s chest down beside the door and went back to the stairs.

“Don’t bring it in,” she told Linias.

He had already tilted the instrument case to get it round the turn. He tilted it back with a soft, elaborate sigh. Harissa watched the gull scratch itself above the bed.

“Does that one pay?”

The owner tried to smile.

Riarhin asked for their money back. He began to explain what had happened to the money. The roofers wanted half their charge in advance. The wet plaster had come down into the kitchen. His brother had promised to help and had sent a boy with a sack of onions. Riarhin could smell the onions beneath the burned wood. They had begun to soften in the damp.

“I’m sorry about your house,” Zemia said.

He turned towards her at once. “Then you understand.”

“I understand. We can’t sleep here.”

The girl on the landing resumed scraping. Riarhin waited until the man stopped promising things and told them what he could return. They went down to his remaining table to count it. He had to ask his daughter where she had put the bag. She reached beneath her apron without getting up.

Zemia sat beside the table, looking at the wet cuffs of her travelling dress. She had been pleased with the rooms when she arranged them. There would have been a courtyard, a kitchen they could use, and a door which could be barred from within. Riarhin remembered her describing the courtyard aboard ship. There was no pleasure in catching her at a mistake which had happened after she left.

“Your cousin has a store,” the daughter said.

Her father looked round.

“It’s full.”

“They took the mast out yesterday.”

“It’s a boatyard.”

“So is half the harbour.”

The girl wiped the scraper on a rag and told them how to find it. Her father added directions until she told him he was sending them to the wrong gate.

Outside, Linias lowered the case onto a dry stone and flexed his fingers. A porter had followed them from the landing in the hope that they would tire of their luggage. He leaned on the shaft of his little cart and watched without troubling to conceal his satisfaction.

“How much to the boatyard?” Riarhin asked.

He named more than she liked. Coryd looked back at the steep, slippery lane they had just climbed and said she would pay her share. They loaded the cart.

The cousin had fewer opinions and more roof. He was standing in the ribs of a boat when they found him. A strip of wood lay along his thigh, held there with one hand while he marked it with the other. He finished marking before he answered them. Riarhin liked that until she saw how narrow the stair to the loft was.

“You’d have to come in before we close.”

“When do you close?”

“When I’ve finished.”

“Some indication would help,” Harissa said.

He looked at the open sky, considered the boat, and said supper. His wife, arriving behind him, asked whose supper he meant.

She had a broom in her hand and a little sawdust caught in the dark hair at her temple. She listened to the proposal from the bottom of the stairs, then climbed ahead of them to see what her husband had offered. The loft smelled of resin, rope, and the old damp of things stored too near the sea. One wall was hung with strips of timber. Coils of line lay beneath the slope of the roof. At the far end an open loading door admitted enough light to show where a mast had scraped the floor on its way out.

“No cooking,” the woman said.

Zemia held up her hand. “No cooking.”

“And no washing thrown into the yard.”

“Yes.”

“And if you hear the dog, you don’t feed it. It belongs next door. They send it here because they think I’m soft.”

Harissa looked at the dog behind her. It wagged its tail once, cautiously.

The woman pointed the broom at it.

“Out.”

The dog went as far as the landing and lay down.

They paid for the loft and a key to the yard gate. Riarhin asked for a second way out. The woman looked at her husband with the triumphant expression of someone whose doubts had just been vindicated.

“What business are you in?”

“Travelling.”

“Everybody’s in that business until something goes missing.”

Coryd opened their bags far enough to show tools, clothes, the instrument, Harissa’s folded costumes. Riarhin kept her own pack closed. The woman noticed.

“There’s a sword in that wrapping.”

“Yes.”

“Do you intend to use it here?”

“No.”

The woman continued looking at her. Riarhin wished she had answered differently. She could promise where they would put the washing. She could not make the other promise with the same ease, and the woman knew something of that even without knowing why.

“We won’t touch your stores,” Riarhin said. “We’ll pay for anything we damage.”

“You’ll pay first.”

They paid more. Zemia took out her own purse before Riarhin could open hers again. The woman swept a section of floor clear for their bedding and told them where a jar could be filled. On the way down she lifted the dog by the loose skin of its neck and rump and deposited it in the yard. It seemed accustomed to being moved in this manner. It shook itself and went to investigate the cart.

The loading opening faced a second building across a gap wide enough to discourage a thief and narrow enough to interest Riarhin. Both buildings belonged to their host, though he became uneasy when she asked to see the second roof.

“I’m not responsible if you fall.”

“No.”

“You can reach the store from below.”

“I’d like to see it from here.”

He looked at her boots, then at her face, and stepped aside.

The jump was easy with nothing in her hands. A roof tile shifted beneath the ball of her foot on landing. She moved to the ridge, tested the next two tiles, and looked down into the yards beyond. A fish merchant had stretched pale cloth above his cleaning tables. Farther on, two women were hauling a net across a court so small that they had to fold each section as it came free of the basket. Riarhin watched their route to the alley. One saw her and shouted something about thieves. She raised an empty hand and climbed down where the boatbuilder had shown her.

When she returned to the loft, Harissa was sitting on her chest with one shoe off. The blister at her heel had opened on the walk from the landing.

“You’ve found us another road?”

“If we need it.”

“Do we have to try it now?”

“Just once.”

Harissa put her shoe on without fastening it and followed her.

Coryd went across first. She chose the ridge without being told and turned to offer Harissa a hand. Harissa ignored the hand until she landed, then took it to steady the loose shoe. Zemia crossed lightly. Linias looked down, secured his case more tightly, and drew back to jump.

“Leave that here,” Riarhin said.

“I want to know if I can do it with it.”

“First without.”

He unfastened the strap. Without the case he crossed so easily that he looked annoyed when he turned back. Riarhin made him return by the same way and showed him where the tile had moved. He crouched to test it. His fingers were long and careful, the nails kept short on one hand and a little longer on the other.

“It would hold if I put my weight there.”

“It might.”

He looked up at her tone.

“Someone else might be standing there,” she said. “Or you might be looking behind you.”

He nodded. She was uncertain whether she had been understood or merely heard. The boatbuilder called from below that he wanted to shut the store, and the question went no farther.

Zemia went to fetch bedding. Coryd bought food. Harissa remained with the luggage and took her shoe off again. Linias asked where he could safely leave the instrument while he helped carry water. They chose a place near the stairs, away from the damp at the loading opening. Riarhin watched him lay it down and knew she ought to make him put it nearer the roof exit instead. Then their host called her to look at the gate key, which stuck, and the case remained where it was.

There was a platform hung from ropes for moving timber to the upper store. Its locking peg had worn thin. She found another piece of wood and fitted it beside the old one, leaving a loop she could reach from above. She asked the boatbuilder whether anyone would need the platform that night.

“Are you going to sleep on it?”

“No.”

“Then leave it alone.”

She took her hand away. He examined the extra peg, grunted, and went back to the boat. On her way round the yard she noticed a strip of iron tapping against a pile whenever the wind moved it. The sound was irregular enough to become irritating once she attended to it. She made herself listen to the water instead.

Zemia came back late with blankets over both arms.

“The first woman had none to spare.”

“Which woman?”

“The one at the blue door. She sent me to the stalls.”

The blankets were clean and smelt of dried peel. Riarhin helped shake them out. Under one was a little paper parcel of fried beans, still warm. Zemia gave it to Harissa, who said she had been hoping for something less nourishing and ate them anyway.

They supped sitting along the open loft door, their feet inside the sill. Coryd had bought too much bread because the baker was closing and offered the second loaf cheaply. Linias broke pieces off it and pretended to consider throwing one to the dog.

“Don’t,” said Harissa.

“I wasn’t going to.”

The dog stared up at him. Harissa took the bread from his hand and ate it.

Below them the boatbuilder covered his tools. His wife called from the street. He answered without looking up, then looked up when she called again. Riarhin watched him stop to move a piece of wood out of the weather before joining her. Ailsa had done that with work she meant to resume in the morning: one last fold, one last thing set beyond a child’s reach. For a little while Riarhin could have been waiting upstairs for her.

Coryd nudged the jar towards her. She drank. The water had acquired a taste of the jar’s former contents, something sweet and faintly burned.

After supper the yard closed. Their host secured the gate and went home. Harissa unrolled the bedding and complained that Zemia had chosen the blanket with the best edge. Zemia offered to exchange. Harissa said she did not want that one now that everyone would think her covetous.

“You are covetous,” Linias said.

“Of better company.”

Riarhin heard the normal movements of settling boats, water against piles, the loose piece of iron tapping in the wind.

Then the iron stopped.

She rose without putting on her boots.

Below the loading opening a figure stood in the yard, looking up. The robe was dark in this light. One pale hand rested beside the sabre.

Farieth had not gone to the burned house.

Riarhin touched Harissa’s shoulder and gave the withdrawal signal.

Chapter 12 — Through the loft

Coryd reached the roof first. She went low across the gap, turned and caught Harissa’s hand.

For a moment everything happened as they had practised. Harissa’s blanket slid off her shoulders and lay across the floor. Zemia stepped over it. Linias rose with the water jar still in his hand, set it down carefully, and turned towards the loading door. Riarhin saw him look at his empty shoulder.

He had left his instrument beside the stairs.

He went for it.

Riarhin heard the tread below change as Farieth began to climb. She drew Remorse and put herself across the narrow head of the stair.

“Leave it.”

He had heard. His head turned towards her voice. He was already close enough to touch the case, and for a fraction of a breath Riarhin expected him to come away from it empty-handed. Then his fingers closed around the strap.

Farieth’s first step had creaked. The following steps made no sound she could distinguish from the water. Riarhin had expected to see the sabre before her grandmother’s face. Instead Farieth looked up through the gap beneath the rail, her expression attentive, almost enquiring. Her right hand was still low.

Riarhin put her left foot forward. A nail head pressed beneath her heel. She moved half an inch away from it. The stair was wide enough for a person carrying a bundle, too narrow for her to pass a blade safely at her side. Above Farieth’s shoulder the yard was dark. No one stood there to stop her leaving the way she had come.

“Let him go,” Riarhin said.

Farieth raised the sabre.

The sabre rose beneath Riarhin’s blade. She gave ground a hand’s breadth, turned her wrist and sent its edge into the stairpost. Wood split. Farieth withdrew without wrenching. Her next cut came lower.

Riarhin could not retreat far enough without giving her the landing. She lifted her foot, let the blade pass under it and stamped against the broken rail. It tore away from its lower fastening. Farieth caught the falling length with her free hand and pushed it aside. Riarhin had already withdrawn her foot. The nail at her heel scraped her skin as she turned.

There was no room for a broad cut. She kept Remorse’s point close to the line of Farieth’s throat and made her grandmother bring the sabre across it before taking another step. Their blades touched lightly. Farieth let the touch run towards the stronger part of her own blade. Riarhin loosened it at once, withdrawing the point and returning along the other side. The movement bought nothing she could see except another breath.

Behind her Linias struck the doorframe with the case. He swore, turned it, and passed through. Riarhin did not look. Her grandmother’s eyes moved towards the sound. Riarhin made a small thrust and felt cloth part before Farieth’s shoulder had finished turning away.

“Now,” she said.

Linias ran.

Farieth looked past Riarhin towards him. Riarhin cut at her face. Her grandmother leaned away, the edge missing by less than a finger, and came up another step.

At the opening Harissa slipped on a loose tile. Her leg struck the metal corner of a gutter. She made a short angry sound and hauled herself across with Coryd pulling from the other side.

Below, the cousin shouted from the yard. He had returned for a tool and found the gate open. The dog barked once, then made a noise Riarhin had never heard it make. A board fell against the stair rail. Farieth did not look towards any of it.

Riarhin had not fought in a place this narrow for years. The old impulse was to crowd her opponent, take the hand, make the next movement short and final. There was no knife in her other hand that she trusted for it, and Farieth’s balance did not offer the weight she expected. She would have to keep the point between them long enough for the others to leave.

The sabre caught a strand of her hair and cut it free.

She felt it fall against her cheek. The smallness of the contact steadied her. Farieth was close enough to kill; so was she, if she could force a mistake. She threatened the face again and let her grandmother spend the next moment answering it.

Zemia was last. She stood staring at the stairs.

Riarhin could see her in the edge of her vision, pale hands empty, the clean blankets at her feet. She had stopped. Farieth glanced towards her, and Riarhin struck hard enough to force the sabre up between them.

“Go.”

Zemia flinched. She took one step backwards, found the sill with her heel, and turned. Coryd’s hand appeared beyond her.

Farieth changed the pressure on their blades. Riarhin released the contact before it could pull her sideways. Her grandmother’s free hand passed close enough to brush her sleeve. If it had closed upon her wrist, the stair would have left her nowhere to turn.

She retreated one pace. The landing was theirs now, both of them upon it, and the loading opening was behind her right shoulder. She had to get there without giving Farieth a straight line to the roof.

Farieth’s sabre struck the post beside her head. Riarhin felt the vibration in her teeth. She moved sideways through the loading opening, caught the prepared loop and pulled.

The platform dropped between the stairs and the yard with a crash that shook the building. Ropes whipped upwards. One struck Riarhin’s shoulder. She let herself fall towards the neighbouring roof.

Hands caught her coat.

For a moment all her weight hung from her throat. Then Linias and Coryd dragged her over the ridge and she could breathe.

They crossed two roofs. Riarhin tried to breathe without coughing and could not. Each breath caught at the place where her coat had dragged across her throat. Coryd looked back once. Riarhin waved her on and found the ridge with her feet.

The roof tiles had been warm when she tested them that afternoon. Now a film of wet made them treacherous. Harissa kept her injured leg straight and moved sideways. Linias reached for her. She snapped that he should watch where he put his own feet. He obeyed, his hand still held uselessly near her elbow.

A shout rose behind them. Riarhin looked over her shoulder. The loft opening was empty. That did not tell her where Farieth had gone. She could take the street while they crossed roofs, reach the lane first, stand below the wall where they meant to descend.

Riarhin touched Coryd’s back and pointed to the fish merchant’s awning.

“There?”

“Down there. Now.”

Coryd lay along the roof edge, felt beneath it, and lowered herself by both hands. The awning’s frame took her weight with a noise that brought the merchant out of his room. He shouted before he had found his shoes. Coryd dropped beside his tables, turned, and held up both arms for Harissa.

Harissa looked down at her, mouth tight. Blood had made the sole of her shoe slippery.

“Hold the gutter,” Riarhin said. “Turn your hip. She has you.”

The merchant came out with a pole. Coryd took it from him long enough to lay it across the table beneath Harissa’s foot. Harissa descended along it, swearing with every movement. Coryd gave the pole back.

“We’re leaving,” she said.

He stared at the blood on his stones.

“Out,” he said. “Whatever this is, out.”

Riarhin came down last, landed badly on the scraped heel, and stumbled against a table. The merchant recoiled from the sword in her hand. She sheathed it as soon as she had room and asked where the rear gate led.

“You came into my yard.”

“Yes. Where does it lead?”

He pointed with the pole. His hand was shaking. She left money on the nearest table, beneath the weight he used for holding cloth down. He glanced at it, then looked at the roof above her. He was still looking there when she followed the others out.

Behind them someone began shouting about the platform. Riarhin took the lead through a lane she had walked that afternoon. Harissa kept up until the harbour lights were behind them. Then she stopped, pressed both hands over her shin, and swore at Linias with extraordinary precision.

He stood holding the case.

“I’m sorry.”

“Carry something useful.”

Linias crouched awkwardly. Harissa put a hand against his chest.

“Not like that. You’ll drop me.”

“Then tell me how.”

She did. He listened this time, sliding one arm beneath her knees and waiting until she had her own grip. Zemia took the case without being asked. Its strap had cut into his neck; a red line ran beneath his ear.

At the first well they stopped beneath an arch where a faded figure had been painted above the water. Someone had left flowers in a cracked bowl. Riarhin moved the bowl before Linias put Harissa down, then regretted the care with which she had done it. Harissa had both hands over her shin. Blood was running between her fingers.

“Let me see.”

“Don’t pull.”

“I won’t.”

The stocking had stuck at one edge. Riarhin wet it and waited while Coryd filled another cup. She had no clean cloth long enough to bind the cut. Zemia took off the light wrap she wore around her shoulders and held it out.

“Use this.”

It was embroidered at the ends. Harissa looked at it and then away. Riarhin folded an unworked section and pressed it against the wound. The opening was longer than she had thought, angled over the bone, with a little dark scrap at its lower end. She left the scrap for someone who could see properly and sent Coryd to find a healer.

Linias held the cup. Riarhin told him to wash his hands and give Harissa something to drink. He went to the well, poured water over his fingers, forgot what he had been sent to do, and stood there until Zemia brought him back.

From the harbour came a bell. Riarhin did not know what it meant. She listened for running feet beneath it and heard only a man arguing with someone who wanted to close a gate.

Harissa looked over Riarhin’s shoulder at Linias.

“Will it play?”

He stared at her.

“I don’t know.”

“Find out when I’m not bleeding.”

Zemia knelt beside him and touched the seam of the case. He pulled it away, then gave it back a moment later without meeting her eyes.

When Coryd returned with directions, Linias lifted Harissa again, waiting for her grip before he stood.

Chapter 13 — Again

The woman who stitched Harissa’s leg had a sleeping child on her back and no interest in hearing how it happened.

“Hold still.”

“I am.”

“More still.”

Riarhin held the lamp. Linias sat outside, unable to bear watching and ashamed of leaving. The healer had sent Zemia for boiled water and ordered Coryd to move a stool beneath Harissa’s foot. Neither instruction had left room for discussion. Her child slept against her back with its cheek flattened on the cloth that held it.

“How long since?”

“Less than an hour,” Riarhin said.

Harissa gripped the sides of the chair. The healer loosened the cloth and waited for the slow flow to show her where the wound was deepest. She asked Harissa to move her toes, then to turn her foot a little. Harissa did both and looked at Riarhin as if demanding a verdict.

The healer found the scrap of gutter dirt. She took it out. Harissa’s hands tightened on the chair until the joints of the wood creaked.

“That’s out,” the woman said. “There’s more washing.”

“Is there something I can bite?”

Coryd folded a cloth. Harissa took it but did not put it in her mouth. She held it between both hands while the wound was washed, drew one breath through her nose, and swore so fiercely that the child stirred. The healer reached behind her to pat its back without lifting her eyes from the shin.

Riarhin raised the lamp when asked. She wanted something else to do. Holding the light let her see every moment when Harissa expected the pain to stop and it began again. On a field there would have been another body beside this one, someone calling, hands needed elsewhere. Here she had to stand still and keep the shadow of her wrist out of the wound.

The healer stitched it and wrapped it. She asked how far they meant to travel the following day.

“Nowhere,” Riarhin said before Harissa could answer.

Harissa shut her eyes.

“Good. Bring her back in the morning.”

When they asked for rooms, the woman directed them to a stable whose owner let the upper floor. She knew the floor was dry because she had dressed his wife’s foot there the week before. Riarhin paid. The woman counted the money one-handed. Her child had woken and was pulling at the loosened cloth across her shoulder.

At the door Linias stood so suddenly that he struck his head on the lintel. Harissa laughed. It hurt enough to stop the laugh at once, but she put out her arms and let him lift her.

The cousin found them the next morning. Riarhin met him in the stable yard, where the smell of wet straw kept him from standing too close to the wall.

“My platform,” he said.

“I know.”

“The gate. The stair rail.”

“I know.”

“My wife thinks I brought trouble into the yard deliberately.”

Riarhin gave him money for the damaged wood. It was not enough for the whole platform. She told him where a letter could be left and promised the rest when she could obtain it. He looked as if he had heard such promises before.

“Did anyone get hurt?”

“The dog ran away. My wife says he has more sense than me.”

“Anyone else?”

He shook his head.

The relief made her knees loosen. She had not realized how much she had been waiting for the answer.

He tucked the money inside his coat.

“She asked about you before,” he said.

Riarhin looked up.

“Who?”

“The woman. A message came to the burned house. I heard about it when I went there for the bedding money. They sent the messenger to the yard.”

“When?”

“Before dark.”

“Who carried it?”

“A boy, I think. I didn’t see him.”

Riarhin asked whether the message remained. It had been delivered; nobody thought to keep a wrapper. She thanked him. He seemed surprised to receive thanks after the account of his losses.

They stayed three days in rooms over a stable.

The healer came to examine the wound on the second morning because Harissa could not bear the stairs again. She made them lift the bedding near the window and asked for the room to be cleared. Riarhin began to remain. Harissa told her to go with the others.

Outside, the stable owner was harnessing a cart horse which disliked the girth. Coryd stood out of reach of its teeth and held the loose end while he adjusted it. Linias watched the window above them. Zemia had gone for breakfast, leaving enough money on the table that nobody could accuse her of choosing only what she liked.

“He’ll have to carry her again,” Coryd said when the horse moved away.

“I can carry her,” Riarhin said.

“I meant him.” She nodded at Linias. “She asked him.”

Riarhin stopped preparing an argument. Above them Harissa said something too indistinct to hear, followed by the healer’s shorter answer. Linias rubbed the back of his neck where the instrument strap had cut it.

“I could sell it,” he said.

Neither woman answered quickly enough.

“The case. If we need money for a cart.”

“How would you carry the instrument?” Coryd asked.

“Wrapped.”

“Across water?”

He turned away. “I’m trying to think.”

Riarhin went to stand beside him. The stable’s outer gate framed a section of street, wheels passing, a woman with a tray, a boy dragging a stick along the wall. She envied them as they passed.

“We can pay for a cart,” she said.

“You keep saying we.”

“Yes.”

“You mean you.”

“Harissa put money in. So did Coryd. Zemia paid for the blankets.”

“And I brought the thing that got her hurt.”

Riarhin heard the door upstairs open. She waited until the healer had passed them and gone into the stable owner’s room to wash her hands.

“I let you put it by the stairs,” she said.

Linias looked at her.

“I knew it ought to be near the other way out. I meant to move it.”

“I still went back.”

“Yes.”

He leaned against the wall, his head tipped back. She had no wish to divide the blame until each had a comfortable portion. She wanted him to know what had happened before his part in it began.

When Zemia returned, she brought a little jar for Harissa because the healer had said she should eat. Harissa objected to the jar, the food, and the way everyone watched her use the spoon. Then she ate half of it and slept.

Riarhin took Linias to a repairer recommended by the stable owner. The man worked in a narrow shop with two instruments suspended above a bench and a kettle balanced in the coals. He listened to the damaged note and asked who had made the last repair. Linias admitted doing it himself.

“I can see.”

“Then why ask?”

“To see whether you knew.”

He named a charge and a waiting time they could not afford. Linias asked for materials instead. The man would not sell the first piece he chose; it had been seasoned for another job. He offered a thinner strip and a small quantity of glue, then made Linias demonstrate how he meant to use them. Riarhin waited by the door while the two men argued. The repairer’s tone became less contemptuous as Linias answered him. At last he laid the materials on the bench and said they would have to do until someone could open the whole seam.

Linias paid from his own purse. On the way back he stopped to buy Harissa something wrapped in grease-dark paper. He would not tell Riarhin what it was. Harissa opened it, looked at him, and said he had remembered the wrong kind.

“I know,” he said. “It’s what they had.”

She ate it slowly and gave him half.

That night Riarhin slept on the floor beside the door and woke to Harissa trying to stand.

“Where?”

“The pot.”

Riarhin got up. Harissa held the wall with both hands, looking furious enough to strike anyone who mentioned the inconvenience. She had wrapped her skirts high above the bandage. The bare foot beneath looked vulnerable in a way Riarhin had not expected after watching her dance.

“I can help.”

“Then help.”

Riarhin brought the pot closer. Harissa looked at her.

“Go away.”

She went to the far end of the room and waited. The stable beneath them smelt strongly in the warmth. A horse shifted, then struck the wall with a hoof. Harissa swore under her breath. When she was done, she allowed Riarhin to bring her back to the bedding.

Linias had woken. He sat up, prepared to be useful and afraid of being told what his usefulness was worth.

“Water?” he asked.

“Yes.”

He brought it. Harissa drank and lay down. He remained kneeling with the cup in his hands.

“I heard you,” he said to Riarhin.

“When?”

“When you said to leave it.”

She waited. Harissa’s eyes were open.

“I thought I could reach it and still go. I thought you meant I might be slow.”

“You were slow.”

“No. I mean—”

He rubbed his face with his free hand.

“It was a movement I could do. I knew I could do it. I didn’t think about what she could do while I was doing it.”

Harissa turned her head towards the wall.

“Neither did I,” she said. “I thought you were right behind me. I hurried.”

Riarhin sat back on her heels. She had been prepared to explain the difference between practising a movement and choosing it while an enemy chose another. Linias had already found it. Harissa had given him the rest. There was no lecture left to deliver without enjoying their shame.

“I’ll show you where to leave the case tomorrow,” she said. “If you have to leave it.”

“What if there isn’t anywhere?”

“Then you leave it anyway.”

He nodded, staring into the empty cup.

Harissa put out a hand. For a moment Riarhin thought she meant to touch him. She took the cup, set it on the floor where it could not be knocked over, and told them both to lie down.

Riarhin returned to the door. Behind her Linias whispered something to Zemia. Zemia answered, too quietly for Riarhin to hear without choosing to listen. She let the stable’s noises cover them. Harissa learned exactly how far she could bend her ankle before pulling the wound. On the third day Riarhin made the others practise leaving through the rear door without crowding it.

Zemia turned too soon. Riarhin lifted her hand to move her shoulder.

Zemia braced.

Riarhin saw the old practice yard, the white patch of sky beyond Ghere’s head, and knew before he moved which side she would fall on.

She fell on the other.

“Again.”

She rose. Dirt adhered to the sweat along her ribs. He gave her the opening she had wanted and took it away as she entered.

This time she struck the ground hard enough to lose her breath.

“What?” she asked when she could speak. “What am I doing?”

He put his hand where hers had been.

She knew the placement. She could reproduce it. She could reproduce his entire movement, slowly or quickly, in either direction. She could not find the thing he wanted her to notice before he struck.

“Again.”

By evening she had learned to keep her mouth shut when she fell.

He brought a bowl to her sleeping place later. The food was cut small because he had seen her split lip. He sat while she ate. She wanted him to leave, and when he stood she wanted him to stay.

In the stable yard Riarhin lowered her hand.

“Turn after the person ahead has cleared the frame,” she said. “If you turn here, your shoulder blocks the next one.”

Zemia tried it. So did Coryd. They had it immediately.

“And if the person ahead falls?” Linias asked.

“Keep the opening clear.”

“You mean step over them.”

“If you stop in the doorway, nobody can help.”

He looked towards the stairs where Harissa sat with her leg raised.

“I won’t leave her.”

“I’m asking you to leave room.”

He tried it again. This time he cleared the door before turning back.

Chapter 14 — The road from the water

They reached the Thin Coast on a smaller boat whose owner put Harissa under an awning and charged for the space her raised leg occupied.

Coryd argued until he reduced the charge. Harissa paid the rest herself.

“I have money,” she said when Coryd began again. “And I want him to stop speaking to me.”

Beyond the landing the road followed fields that narrowed as the trees drew closer. Timber houses leaned away from the damp. In one village a bell hung between two poles, though there was no building around it. Nobody rang it while they passed.

They slept in a wool shed belonging to a woman Coryd had worked for years before. The woman remembered her with affection and offered no free meals.

Coryd chopped wood before breakfast. Riarhin found her at the block.

“How long were you away in the port?”

“Buying food?”

“Yes.”

Coryd rested the axe.

“I bought food. I asked where it was cheaper. I went there. Then I came back.”

“Did you tell anyone where we were staying?”

“I asked a man how to get back. I had not memorized a city I arrived in that afternoon.”

Riarhin looked at the split wood.

“Which man?”

Coryd gave her a description, then struck the next log so hard it flew off the block.

Before breakfast Riarhin found Harissa sitting on the edge of the bed with one of her dancing shoes in her hand. It was a soft thing, repaired along the side so carefully that the newer thread had become a pattern.

“I thought you were resting.”

“I am.”

“You’re dressed.”

“I find I rest better clothed.”

Riarhin sat on the other bed. Harissa turned the shoe over and pressed her thumb into the sole.

“You’re going to tell me to be patient.”

“No.”

“Good. I’ve been patient in four ports and a stable. It has been very educational.”

She put the shoe on the bed between them.

“I was meant to be in a town farther south now. Six performances. A woman who pays without making you ask twice. I had a new piece.”

“Can it wait?”

“It can. The woman may not.”

Riarhin had been paid to arrive at particular moments too. She kept the comparison to herself.

“Show me,” she said.

“My leg—”

“What you can.”

Harissa studied her to see whether she was being indulged. Then she stood, kept most of her weight over the good leg, and began with her hands.

Riarhin saw at once how much she had missed when watching the dances merely as skilled movement. A pause changed who seemed to be speaking. A turn of the head made an answer impatient. Harissa shifted one hand slightly and the person she had made became older, or weary, or unwilling to admit affection. Riarhin could copy the movements. She would not have known to make them.

“What happens then?”

“The husband returns.”

“Is that unfortunate?”

“Depends who you ask.”

Harissa smiled and sat before the wound began to pull.

“That’s why I want my chest.”

“I know.”

“I don’t think you did.”

Riarhin picked up the shoe and gave it to her.

At breakfast Zemia rubbed Harissa’s foot below the bandage while Linias tuned the damaged instrument. A seam had opened during the escape. He plucked a string, winced and loosened it.

Riarhin asked Zemia about the bedding again. She answered without irritation, repeating the colours of the doors, the amount paid, the woman who had offered an extra blanket.

Every account could have been true.

Their hostess offered work for another day. Harissa needed the rest, and the passage had left their purses thinner than they liked. Coryd accepted before Riarhin could decide for them.
