Before the work began, the woman took Coryd into the smaller shed to look at a broken frame. Riarhin followed as far as the door with the water she had been asked to carry. Inside, Coryd had set one hand on a beam and was laughing at something the woman said. It was an easy laugh, with none of the caution Riarhin had begun to expect from her.

“You left it like that,” the woman said.

“It wasn’t split when I left.”

“It was thinking about it.”

Coryd examined the grain. She asked how long it had been taking more weight. The woman pointed to a newer arrangement beyond it. Coryd went to look and made the same disapproving sound Riarhin had heard from her own mouth at the loft.

“I said you’d do that,” the woman said.

“Then you might have waited.”

“You were gone.”

Riarhin set the water inside the door. Coryd turned, saw her, and moved aside. The ease did not vanish, but it became something Riarhin had entered rather than something offered to her.

The woman asked whether she was another of the sisters. Riarhin said yes.

“Dovra came once,” the woman said.

Coryd’s hand stopped on the beam.

“When?”

“After you. She had two little tools she wanted somebody here to mend. She said you’d told her we did things properly.”

Coryd looked at the floor. Riarhin could see that this was new to her.

“Did she stay?”

“One night. She ate as much as a carter and apologized for it. There was no need.”

The woman went to a shelf and brought down a small horn scoop. Its edge had been worked smooth by use. “She made this because she said she’d used up all our spoons.”

Coryd took it. She turned it once, looked at the place where the handle joined the bowl, and gave it back.

“She’s dead,” she said.

The woman sat on a bale. Her expression changed slowly, as if she had first to let go of the visitor she remembered before admitting what she had heard. She asked how. Coryd answered only that she had been killed.

“Her man?”

“No.”

The woman exhaled. “He was cross enough, from what she said.”

“He didn’t hurt her.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

They stood in the shed with the broken frame between them. Riarhin had nothing to add. She picked up a fallen length of cord and put it where someone could find it, then wished she had not made a task of the silence.

At last the woman rose. She said Coryd could mend the frame after the wool was sorted if she wanted another day’s pay. Coryd accepted. The scoop went back on its peg. It was still needed for the work it had been made to do.

They spent the morning sorting wool. Harissa knew less about it than she claimed and more about what a buyer would tolerate than the hostess expected. Linias developed an elaborate dislike of one particularly filthy fleece. Zemia coaxed the woman’s youngest son into helping by asking him to demonstrate a knot none of the visitors could apparently manage.

Riarhin worked beside the window. Her hands learned the task quickly; choosing what to discard took longer. The hostess corrected her twice, then put two handfuls side by side and let her feel the difference.

At noon the young son asked whether Linias and Zemia were married.

Harissa nearly choked.

“No,” Linias said.

“Why not?”

“Because she is my sister.”

The boy examined them with renewed interest.

“You don’t look the same.”

“Thank you,” Zemia said.

Later Coryd asked Riarhin whether she had been satisfied by the work, or whether she still thought the morning should have been spent practising escape.

“They needed food,” she said.

“We needed money.”

“Yes.”

Coryd rolled the paid coins in her palm and gave Riarhin half without making her ask. They were back to speaking easily for the length of that exchange. Riarhin did not attempt to prolong it with an apology she had already made.

They walked until the fields ended. Their hostess’s bread grew hard in the bag. Harissa stopped pretending her leg did not hurt and accepted a slower pace. Riarhin had begun to recognize the smell of the wet leaves.

At the last house she asked where the stream could be crossed. The woman at the door gave her a direction with the hand in which she held a knife. She was peeling something fibrous into a bowl. Riarhin waited for a place-name, a distance, anything that could be repeated to the others.

The woman pointed again, less patiently.

Riarhin thanked her. They followed the track until a fallen tree blocked it and a lighter strip turned towards the sound of water.

“There,” Coryd said.

Riarhin looked above the strip. A forked branch had been wedged into a living trunk, the pale cut end turned inwards. Below it three stems had been bent together without breaking. She stepped off the track and crouched, keeping her weight on a root.

Harissa lowered herself onto the fallen tree.

“If this is somewhere to sit, I approve.”

“Stay there.”

“You needn’t sound so doubtful about my willingness.”

Riarhin touched one of the bent stems. The bark was unbroken. The ground beneath looked firm, covered in the same damp leaves as the track. Farther down, the shallowest impression in the leaf mould followed the lighter strip towards the stream.

She had been shown something like this by Ghere. He had made her walk the upper root and then return, angry because she had been brought back before reaching the water. She remembered the exact shape of the branch he had used. This was a different tree. The difference mattered, but she did not know how much.

“Someone keeps this closed,” she said.

Linias looked at the open way. “With that?”

“With everybody knowing what it means.”

“Do you?”

“Enough not to walk there.”

She backed onto the track.

The man was above Harissa’s shoulder when Riarhin first saw him. His grey sleeve lay along a branch with such ease that it appeared to be part of the bark. One foot rested on the far side of the trunk; the other was hidden by leaves. Riarhin could see no weapon, which told her nothing.

Harissa saw Riarhin look and began to turn.

“Stay where you are,” Riarhin said quietly.

The man moved. He came down a sloping limb and passed to another trunk without appearing to hurry. Behind him a woman stepped across a narrow gap, carrying a wrapped bundle against one hip. A child followed on the same branch, placing a hand where hers had been. Riarhin had been looking into those trees for several minutes. There were at least six people in them now.

Linias opened his mouth. Zemia laid her hand on his sleeve.

Riarhin spoke in the language she had learned here.

“We need the crossing.”

The man’s head turned a little. His gaze passed over her face, Remorse, the four people behind her. He said something concerning the lower water and continued along the trunk.

“Is that permission?” Coryd whispered.

“No.”

Riarhin stepped to where he would still be able to hear her.

“The crossing for five. Today.”

He made a small motion with his fingers, towards something above her rather than towards the stream. The woman with the bundle had gone. The child paused long enough to examine the case on Linias’s back, then followed her.

One of the people farther in the trees spoke. Riarhin heard a word she knew as ours in some constructions and within in others. The rest concerned water, a fallen thing, and feet that had already passed. She retained it perfectly. It might have been an instruction about where they should cross, or an explanation of why they should not.

“The fallen tree?” she asked.

Nobody answered.

She could hear their movement now because she knew which small sounds to attend to. A leaf folded under a palm. Bark rubbed by cloth. The brief clack of something carried in the bundle against a living branch. They went through the trees at the speed she might have walked an open road, choosing supports which did not bend far enough to show from below.

Harissa had turned despite the warning. She stared into a space between two trunks.

“Where did they go?”

“On.”

“Were they speaking to us?”

“Some of it.”

The answer angered Riarhin as she gave it. She had sounded like Ghere.

She called once more, asking whether the upper way still reached the crossing. A shoulder stopped among the leaves. A hand appeared, palm down, then turned slightly towards her.

Before she could ask what that meant, it was gone.

They waited. Linias moved first, setting his case against the trunk and rolling the tension out of one shoulder.

“Well,” he said. “I hope we made a good impression.”

Coryd went to the tree where the first man had stood. She examined the ground beneath it. “Did they come past the house?”

“Probably not.”

“Then they’ve been beside us.”

Riarhin looked along the line of bent stems. There were other marks farther in, placed for a traveller coming from the opposite direction. She had seen none of them on the approach because she had been looking for an ambush at the height a human would place one.

“They’re a household,” she said. “Or several travelling together. The mark is theirs.”

“How do you know?”

“The same cut on the carrying frame. I’ve seen that used here.”

Harissa glanced at the branches overhead. “Did you know them?”

“No.”

“Would it have helped?”

Riarhin nearly laughed. “I would have known what they were talking about.”

She asked Coryd to wait with the others and went a little farther along the upper ground. She tested each place before putting her full weight on it. At the next tree the root she had been following divided, one fork buried beneath moss, the other raised towards a ledge. She took the buried fork until it curved back, then stopped.

Ghere had made her come back because the root returned towards its own trunk. She remembered that now: his hand turning, palm down, the impatience she had felt at being asked to notice the direction of a root instead of being permitted to reach the water. She went back to the division and climbed to the ledge.

Below it, between two rocks, the crossing remained.

Water ran over flat stepping stones. Beyond them was a cut bank with handholds placed too high for a child but within her reach. The lower strip where Coryd had meant to go ended in a spread of leaves over brown water. Riarhin could see its depth where the surface moved around a fallen limb. The current there was stronger than its stillness suggested.

She returned for the others.

“The upper way.”

“Did they say that?” Zemia asked.

“They indicated it. I didn’t understand.”

Coryd lifted the chest with Linias. Riarhin went ahead to show them the roots that would bear weight. Harissa followed slowly, annoyed by every offer of a hand until she needed one. At the ledge Riarhin lay down and let her use a shoulder to reach the first stone.

Halfway across, Harissa stopped. The water had covered the place where she meant to put her bad foot. Her grip tightened on Riarhin’s hand.

“On my foot,” she said.

“What?”

“Put yours on mine. There’s room behind the toes.”

Harissa did. They stood together while the water pressed their ankles. Riarhin moved her other foot to the next stone and let her sister follow, maintaining the same distance until they reached the bank.

At the top Harissa leaned against a tree and laughed without pleasure.

“I could dance on a table half that width.”

“The table wouldn’t move.”

“People do move tables. You haven’t worked in the worst rooms.”

Linias, from below, asked whether they intended to have their conversation before or after helping with the chest. They turned to take the straps.

Only when everyone stood on the bank did Riarhin look for the clan again. She saw a flash of lighter cloth far upslope, nothing more. They had not waited to see whether she understood. Nor had anyone returned to demand something for the direction.

Zemia began rehearsing the words they had heard.

Riarhin stopped her on the second phrase.

“Don’t call after them.”

“I’m trying to understand.”

“Then do it softly.”

They went on along the upper path. For the rest of the afternoon Riarhin looked above the place where her eyes naturally wanted to rest. Once she saw a person watching them from a fork in a tree. By the time Coryd turned, there was only the fork.

They stopped before the light failed, at a place where three broad trunks enclosed a patch of raised earth. Riarhin walked around it once, then again by a different line. There was no hollow beneath the leaf mould and no open water close enough to hear. She found a fallen length of bark to put under Harissa’s bedding and showed the others where rain would run if it came.

“Have you slept here?” Linias asked.

“Somewhere like it.”

“Was it pleasant?”

“I don’t remember sleeping badly.”

Harissa set down her chest. “A glowing recommendation.”

Riarhin smiled. The place felt familiar without trying to become a memory. That was a relief after the clan’s words, which remained complete in her head and incomplete everywhere else. She could feel the rising ground beneath her feet, the wind moving above rather than through the trunks. Ghere had made her find such places until she could do it without needing him to stand beside her.

Coryd asked where the water was. Riarhin pointed downhill and said she would come. They took two vessels and went far enough for the others’ voices to become indistinct. At the narrow run Riarhin looked for the things Ghere had taught her to look for: leaves resting where water ought to move, a surface too still beneath a falling drop, growth which stopped at an edge no stone explained. Here the water carried ordinary dirt between ordinary roots. She tasted a little and waited before filling the vessels.

Coryd watched without interruption.

“Do you miss it?” she asked.

“Which part?”

“All this.”

Riarhin held the vessel beneath the run. Cold water spilled over her thumb. “I missed being good at it.”

Coryd nodded as though that was an answer she understood. She took the filled vessel and waited while Riarhin filled the other.

“Was it better when you first came?”

“No. I was hungry, and I kept being told to put things somewhere else.”

“That sounds familiar.”

They carried the water up together. Linias had cleared a place for the food and Harissa had begun to argue that a fire would make the camp less miserable. Zemia looked to Riarhin before supporting either side.

“A small one,” Riarhin said. “There.”

She chose a patch of bare earth away from the bark. Coryd gathered dry wood from beneath a fallen trunk. Linias made a structure which collapsed when he tried to put the smallest pieces beneath it. Harissa corrected him from her seat. He told her she was welcome to demonstrate. She held out her hands for the wood.

Her little fire caught first. She accepted this victory with an expression so insufferable that even Riarhin laughed. Linias said it was a dancer’s fire, all display and no useful heat. Harissa put the pot over it and invited him to wait.

While the water warmed, Zemia opened the food bag. The bread had hardened. She softened slices in the steam and handed them out. Coryd found a piece of cheese which had acquired the shape of the corner it travelled in. Linias examined it and asked whether the corner had been clean. Coryd ate a piece before answering.

“I see,” he said, and took the next.

The fire was small enough that they had to sit close. Riarhin could feel Harissa’s shoulder against hers when either moved. Far above them a branch flexed under something heavier than a bird. Everyone looked up. Nothing showed between the leaves. After a while Coryd returned to cutting the cheese.

Harissa asked whether the clan would know they were there.

“Probably.”

“Would they care?”

“I don’t know.”

“I begin to understand why you left.”

Riarhin stirred the pot. “That wasn’t all of it.”

No one asked for the rest immediately. She was grateful until Linias said he had once been told he could stay in a house indefinitely, then discovered that the family meant until the older daughter returned. Nobody had thought to mention her.

“Did you go?” Coryd asked.

“I slept in the loft.”

“With the daughter?” Harissa asked.

“With the oats. Try to attend.”

Zemia laughed, then said he had told her the story differently before. Linias looked affronted.

“The oats weren’t important before.”

“What was?”

“The daughter’s opinion of my playing.”

“Favourable?” Riarhin asked.

“She wanted me farther from the stair.”

The pot began to tremble on its support. Harissa steadied it with a stick and gave Linias a look. He conceded that the fire had heat. The concession was so grudging that she insisted he repeat it without sounding ill.

They ate from the same pot because washing every bowl would use more water than they wished to fetch. Riarhin let the spoon go round, watching the small adjustments made before each hand received it. Harissa turned it for the next person. Coryd wiped the handle against a cloth. Linias forgot whose turn came after his and was corrected at once by Zemia. They were people who had lived alone often enough to carry solitude into company in little, inconvenient ways.

“Did your mothers know?” Riarhin asked.

The question made the spoon pause.

“About him?” Harissa said.

“About this. How you learned.”

Harissa looked at her hands. “Mine said I was doing it to make other children look stupid.”

“Were you?”

“Sometimes.”

Linias laughed. She did not join him at once.

“She stopped asking me to do things in front of people. I thought she was ashamed of me. Later she told me what they said when I wasn’t there.”

“What did they say?”

Harissa took the spoon back from Zemia and ate before answering. “Enough that she wished she had waited.”

Riarhin knew that evasion and let it stand.

“Mine needed help,” Coryd said. “I could finish things. She liked that.”

She turned the cheese to cut from its softer edge.

“Later I worked for a man making hurdles. He watched me the first morning. Asked how many I could do before dark.”

“How many?” Linias said.

Coryd smiled at the fire. He looked from her to the knife and began to say something else.

“I told him,” she said. “I was pleased he’d asked. Next day he sent one of the others home. Same work for me, same pay. He said I’d told him it was easy.”

“Had you?”

“No. I’d shown him.”

Harissa took the cheese from her so she could put the knife away.

“He asked about home too,” Coryd said. “Whether I’d people to keep. I thought he meant to offer more. When I said no, he told me I was fortunate to need so little.”

“What did you do?” Riarhin asked.

“Finished the week. The other man came back after I went. He wouldn’t speak to me.”

Linias drew breath. Harissa nudged him with her good foot. He closed his mouth and reached for the pot instead.

“Did your mother want you to leave home?” he asked after a while.

“No.”

“Did you write?”

“Yes.”

Coryd put another stick on the fire. The small flame turned blue along one edge, then yellow again. Nobody asked whether a letter had been enough.

Zemia said her mother had taught her to say she had been shown a thing before.

“Even when you hadn’t?” Riarhin asked.

“Especially then.”

“That would be difficult to keep straight.”

“No. I remembered whom I’d told.”

She pulled the food bag’s opening closed.

“There was a woman who let me help with her letters,” she said. “I was small enough to be delighted when she asked me to stay. She’d read them to me, then ask for a date or a phrase. I knew which person she meant before she did.”

“Did she pay?” Harissa asked.

“She fed me. I liked going.”

Zemia rubbed a crumb from the bag’s seam.

“Once she asked why I hadn’t been there the day before. I told her my mother was ill. She was kind about it. Then she asked whether I’d been to another house.”

“Why?” Linias said.

“She thought I must have repeated a letter. Someone had heard something she’d written.”

“Had you?”

“No.”

Her answer came so quickly that he looked down.

“I tried to tell her all the places I’d been. There were more than she expected. More people I knew.” Zemia looked towards the dark beyond the fire. “When my mother was better, I told her I didn’t like the food there.”

Riarhin had begun a question about whether the woman apologized. She picked a crumb from her own sleeve instead.

Zemia asked Linias about his mother.

“She thought it was splendid,” he said.

They all looked at him.

“She did. She told everyone. I was unbearable.”

“Were?” Harissa asked.

He ignored her. “She made me play for visitors who couldn’t hear when I did it badly. Once I played the same wrong turn four times and she told me I was improving.”

“Perhaps you were,” Coryd said.

“I was improving the wrong turn.”

He tilted the water vessel to judge how much was left, then took a drink.

“She once told a visitor I could play anything I’d heard. He asked for something from the forest. I said I hadn’t been. He said surely my father had sung to me.”

Harissa leaned forward. “What did you play?”

“A washing song.”

“An old one?”

“Older than me. My mother sang it. The woman in it finds her husband’s shirt in somebody else’s basket.”

Zemia laughed. Linias sang a little, softly enough that Riarhin had to lean closer for the words. They concerned the position of a stain. Harissa supplied the next line and he shook his head.

“Another husband.”

“Same shirt.”

The argument lasted until Coryd asked whether the visitor had liked it.

“He said it was haunting. My mother went into the yard.”

“To laugh?”

“To fetch her basket. She brought it in and asked whether he’d like the verse about the stockings.”

Riarhin laughed with him. When Harissa asked for that verse, he held up the cup to show that he meant to drink first.

He smiled into the fire, remembering with an affection Riarhin found harder to witness than complaint. Then he looked up at her.

“And yours?”

Riarhin could have answered with Bea’s worry, her evasions, the names she chose not to explain. Instead she remembered a game in which her mother turned her back while Riarhin moved an object in the room. Bea always found it. Years later Riarhin realized she had been watching the reflection in a small polished plate on the wall. As a child she thought her mother could see through doors.

“She cheated at games,” Riarhin said.

Harissa laughed. “Good.”

Riarhin told them about the plate. Linias wanted to know how old she was when she caught her. She had to admit she had not caught her at all; she worked it out after Bea died. Zemia asked whether that made the game less pleasant to remember.

“No.”

It surprised her, the firmness of the answer. Bea had turned from the wall smiling, named the displaced object, and accepted her daughter’s fury with delight. Riarhin could see the ribbon in her hair, the quick movement with which she kept a sleeve out of spilled water. Harissa wanted to know whether the polished plate had been for looking at herself. Riarhin said Bea had used anything which gave her back her own face, including somebody else’s spoon.

The pot emptied. Coryd carried it down to wash while Riarhin watched the upper paths. Linias asked Harissa to show him the hand movement she used before the difficult turn. She refused twice, then showed him so he would stop doing it wrong. He copied it and made her admit he could.

“Now mean something by it,” she said.

He attempted a solemn expression. She hit him with a rolled cloth. Zemia laughed so suddenly that water came out of her mouth, and then all of them were laughing, trying to keep quiet because the trees around them remained dark.

The laugh hurt beneath Riarhin’s ribs. She pressed a hand there and laughed again when Linias tried the solemn face a second time.

Later they banked the fire low. Riarhin offered to take the first watch; Coryd took the next without being asked. Zemia lay down beside Linias, close enough that her shoulder touched his roll. Harissa put her good foot outside the blanket to cool.

Riarhin watched the glow die in the wood. Somewhere above, the weight moved along a branch and stopped. She looked up until she found the small difference between a sleeve and the bark beside it. The watcher turned away. No greeting came down.

She remained awake until Coryd touched her shoulder. When she lay down, the ground she had chosen held dry beneath her.

Chapter 15 — What they earn

The amphitheater had a fig tree growing through its lowest benches and washing hung across the highest.

Children carried bowls between the seats. A woman at the entrance told them where to put their packs, then asked whether the instrument could be played.

“Badly at present,” Linias said.

“Is that different from usual?” Harissa asked.

They were given food and invited to contribute after the local singers. Harissa asked who was paying. The woman at the entrance looked from the bowls to her and back again.

“For more than supper,” Harissa said.

“There are places to sleep.”

“Under cover?”

“Mostly.”

“Then we’ll mostly perform.”

The woman laughed, but Harissa waited. Eventually they settled on supper, dry sleeping places, and a portion of whatever the audience put into the basket. Harissa insisted that the basket pass before the wine jug had gone round twice. Riarhin had not thought of that. She had been looking at the narrow stairs which climbed behind the seats.

Coryd came back from the top carrying a bucket which belonged to somebody else. She had been asked to fetch water, had asked where from, and had understood the reply badly enough to reach the washing trough instead. A boy had brought her to the right place and now followed her, carrying a little empty cup. Coryd filled it from the bucket. He drank, still following her.

Riarhin helped set their possessions beneath an overhanging bench. Harissa opened the chest. Folded cloth lay between thin boards; small packets of mending thread fitted into its corners. She chose a plain skirt with enough weight in the hem to show a turn, then laid it down and examined her leg.

“You don’t have to,” Riarhin said.

Harissa kept looking at the bandage.

“I know.”

“I mean it.”

“I heard you.”

She took a different skirt, lighter, with a worn fastening. Zemia knelt to help with the fastening. Harissa allowed her that and refused when she reached towards the bandage.

“I’ve already done it.”

Zemia withdrew her hands. She settled beside Linias and began arguing with him over what he meant to play. He wanted something quick enough to hide the damaged note. Harissa said she wanted something slow enough to use her feet properly, and if he hurried her she would sit down in the middle and make him finish alone.

“You used to like that one.”

“I used to have two legs.”

“You still have two.”

“Both are listening.”

Riarhin left them to it and climbed far enough to see the entrances. At the top, a woman was taking washing off a line. She asked Riarhin to hold one end of a sheet. The cloth was longer than Riarhin expected; its far edge dragged through dust before she caught it. The woman made an irritated sound, shook it out, and showed her where to hold. Together they folded it along seams which had been repaired in at least three different colours. Down below, Linias found a phrase Harissa liked and played it again.

Nobody waited reverently for strangers to begin. A child dropped a bowl. Two men disputed the position of a basket. Linias played over them, choosing something with a refrain they could catch.

By the third repetition the child was singing the wrong words loudly.

Harissa danced barefoot on the dry stone. At first she barely moved from the place where she had set her shoes. One hand held her skirt clear of the bad leg; the other answered a turn in the music. Riarhin had watched her practising in yards and on a pitching deck. Here the same lifted wrist drew a boy away from his quarrel with another child. A man who had been talking over Linias lowered his voice.

Harissa let them wait. When she turned, she kept her injured leg from taking the hardest landing and made the restraint look deliberate. Her weight travelled through the good foot, the movement continuing through her shoulders after the foot had stopped. Riarhin felt the turn in her own body. She could have reproduced it. She had no idea how Harissa had made half the people on the benches lean towards her before she began.

Linias lengthened a phrase. Harissa gave him a look which might have been pleasure or warning. He smiled into the instrument and lengthened it again. She accepted the challenge, rising onto the good foot and bringing the other across so slowly that Riarhin held her own breath. The hem settled. Harissa’s heel touched the stone without a sound.

The man by the fig tree said something to his neighbour. Harissa heard him. She looked directly at him, smiled, and used the next phrase to turn away. His neighbour laughed and struck his knee.

Riarhin had been about to warn the man with her eyes. She looked down at her hands instead. Harissa was enjoying herself. The bandage was still beneath the cloth; the man had not ceased being a stranger. Neither had asked Riarhin to do anything.

At the end, Linias gave her a longer pause than she needed. Harissa stood in it with her chin high, looking at the people looking at her. Then she bent for her shoes. The applause began before she had straightened. It startled her into a grin which had none of the dance’s calculation.

Riarhin clapped with the others.

When Harissa sat down, she took Riarhin’s cup without asking and drank most of it. Her hand was shaking a little. She put the cup down carefully and leaned her head back against the stone.

“Did you like it?”

“Yes.”

“You look cross.”

“I wasn’t.”

“You were at the beginning.”

Riarhin glanced towards the man by the tree. Harissa followed the glance and laughed.

“Oh, him. He thinks he knows me.”

“Does he?”

“I don’t think so. I’ve met a great many men who are sure they’ve seen me before.”

She closed her eyes. Riarhin shifted so her shoulder kept the harder edge of the bench out of Harissa’s back. Harissa let her weight rest there until Zemia brought the basket.

Zemia introduced Ailsa’s cloth work into a conversation with a woman who dyed thread. She asked Riarhin a question about the workroom, and for a little while Riarhin could speak of Ailsa without having to say she was dead.

The dyer wanted to know how the yellow held in sea air. Riarhin described the cloth Ailsa had kept hanging in the yard for half a season to find out. It had annoyed the neighbour whose window faced it. Ailsa had moved it twice and then offered to make the neighbour curtains if he would stop advising her about the colour.

“Did he?” the woman asked.

“He asked for blue.”

The woman laughed. She showed Riarhin a thread wound around a bit of bone and asked whether it resembled the yellow. Riarhin took it into the light. The colour was warmer, closer to the inside of a cut root. She said so. The woman nodded and began explaining what had gone wrong with the last batch.

Zemia had turned away to answer someone else. Riarhin held the thread while the woman talked. Her own knowledge ran out sooner than she wanted. She could remember where the dye jars had stood, which ones Ailsa opened with a rag around her hand, the mark left on her thumb by a broken lid. She could not answer a question about the time the thread ought to spend in the bath. She said she did not know.

“Ask her,” the woman said. “If you come back this way.”

Riarhin looked at the yellow wound around the bone.

“She’s dead.”

The woman took it gently from her. For a little while they watched a child dragging the collection basket down the steps by its handle. The basket was almost as wide as the space between two benches. Every time it caught, the child pulled harder.

“I’m sorry,” the woman said.

Riarhin nodded. When Zemia returned, the dyer asked her about the song instead. Riarhin stayed beside them. She was grateful not to be sent away by a kindness.

After the performance the children tried to imitate Harissa on the low bench. She watched until one attempted the turn on an unstable stone, then moved the stone without announcing a lesson. The child tried again and fell into a friend. They both laughed.

Linias was approached by a local player who wanted to inspect the repair. He surrendered the instrument reluctantly. The man played a short phrase, listened to the damaged note, and made a face which Linias recognized at once.

“I know.”

“There’s too much tension here.”

“I know.”

“You could—”

“I haven’t got what I need.”

The man looked at him, then fetched a strip of thin wood from a bag. They spent the next hour disagreeing over it. The man wanted to ease the repaired seam before fitting the strip. Linias would not let him loosen it until he had seen where the wood was going to sit. The argument used words Riarhin did not know and gestures she understood: a thumb at the weak point, pressure applied and released, an offer of the object withdrawn because the other person had moved too quickly.

At last the man took a damaged practice instrument from his own bag. He pressed its side until the seam opened and let Linias see what he meant. Linias’s expression changed. He asked a question. The man answered without triumph, and they bent over the repair together.

Zemia brought them food and was told to put it somewhere it could not touch the glue. She set the bowl on the next bench and stayed to listen. Linias asked her to hold the strip while he tested its position. She held it without offering advice. Riarhin saw how readily he gave her something delicate, and how still her hands became when he did.

The woman who had negotiated their supper came to Harissa with a second basket. It held a folded cloth and two small measures of oil, and she placed it beside the chest as if it had nothing to do with payment.

“For the next gathering,” she said.

Harissa looked at the basket, then at her. “How many evenings?”

The woman held up four fingers.

Linias raised his head from the repair. The borrowed strip was held in place between his hands; he could not release it yet. Riarhin saw his attention go from the basket to the sky beyond the seats, calculating days they had never agreed to spend.

Harissa asked about a floor. The woman pointed to the place where she had danced.

“A floor out of the rain.”

They went to look. Riarhin followed at a distance that she hoped would not turn the conversation into an escort. Beneath the upper benches a room had been cleared for drying and storage. Two people were carrying sacks from it. A third held the door open with her foot while arguing that the sacks had not been there long enough to be dry.

The woman showed Harissa the sheltered stone. It was uneven near the entrance but sound farther in. Harissa tested it with the sole of her good foot.

“Move those.” She pointed to a stack of shallow baskets. “And that needs covering. Someone will put a heel into it.”

The woman agreed. Riarhin watched them begin making an evening that did not yet exist: where the musicians would sit, who would carry the bowls, how the light could be kept away from the audience’s eyes. Harissa asked for a stool of a particular height and rejected the first one offered. She had stopped touching her bandage.

Riarhin waited until they had returned to the chest before speaking.

“We won’t be here.”

Harissa kept folding the cloth from the basket.

“I know.”

“Does she?”

“I haven’t said yes.”

“Then why—”

Harissa looked up. Riarhin stopped.

The cloth was coarse, useful for protecting the finer pieces in the chest. Harissa ran its edge between her fingers. “Because she asked about the work.”

Riarhin sat on the next bench. Below them Linias called for someone to bring the bowl nearer. Zemia moved it and began feeding him little pieces while he held the strip. He complained about the size of one. She put it back in the bowl and took a smaller piece with exaggerated care.

“They’ve got room for teaching too,” Harissa said. “Mornings. I wouldn’t have to dance every night.”

Riarhin looked towards the room. The people moving sacks were still arguing. One had found a wet patch and was showing it triumphantly to the others.

“Would you want to stay?”

“I’d want to consider it.”

“I’m asking.”

“I’m answering.”

Harissa put the cloth back in the basket. Her leg had begun to hurt again; Riarhin could tell from the way she straightened it before bending over the chest. She did not offer to finish the folding.

“Where I used to work,” Harissa said, “there was a woman who always made me wait in the passage. She’d paid for a performance. Then she’d keep me outside her room so the people arriving could see what she’d bought.”

“What did you do?”

“What I’d been paid to do. Eventually.”

She shut the chest, then opened it because a corner of the skirt was caught.

“This woman asked whether the leg would be better in a few days. She said one of the children could bring food up if it wasn’t. I don’t think she was trying to buy the right to make me grateful. I think she wants somebody to teach the children.”

Riarhin said nothing. Harissa pulled the skirt clear and laid it flat.

“I’m telling you why I looked.”

“I understand.”

“Do you?”

Riarhin thought of the bed above the workroom, the mark on its frame where Ailsa had struck it moving a chest, the evening she had first been asked to stay because leaving would have meant going out into rain. She had stayed through dry mornings afterwards.

“Yes.”

Harissa glanced at her and softened a little. “Good.”

The woman returned for her answer. Harissa gave it in the language of the coast, speaking slowly enough for both of them to be certain. She could not promise four evenings. She might come back. The basket should remain with its owner.

The woman took the oil and left the cloth.

“For tonight,” she said. “You put more in the basket than supper.”

Harissa accepted it. When the woman had gone, she opened the chest once more and fitted the cloth between two boards. Riarhin held the lid out of her way.

Riarhin sat near the top of the benches. She remembered an amphitheater like this, or perhaps this one with fewer roots. She did not trust the memory enough to give it a place. The carved steps bore shallow hollows where feet had passed for longer than she could imagine. Above the washing, a fragment of blue crystal caught the last light and sent it down onto the crowd. Nobody looked up.

A man sat beside her and asked whether she had come to see the old works. She said she had come to find somebody who knew her sister.

“Living?”

“No.”

He nodded and did not ask the next question. After a while he offered her a piece of fruit. She looked at it, then at the tree from which it had come. He laughed gently and showed her the cut edge where the pit had been removed.

“From the market.”

She ate it. The juice ran onto her thumb, and for a moment she was fourteen again, embarrassed by how much pleasure a mouthful could give her.

Later their dead sister’s contact came to find them.

She was small, grey-haired, and carried a cord with knots spaced unevenly along it. Zemia addressed her before Coryd could make the introduction.

The woman stopped.

“Do not call me that.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Use the name you were given.”

Zemia did. Riarhin looked at her.

“Have you met?”

“I thought—” Zemia began, and stopped.

The pathweaver listened to Coryd’s account.

When Coryd described the waistcoat, the woman asked whether the fish had been carved from dark horn or pale. Coryd answered. The pathweaver looked at the wrapping and did not reach for it.

“She offered to make me a fastening for this,” she said, touching her cloak. “I told her it would catch in the cord. She said she could make one that wouldn’t.”

“Did she?”

“She left before I changed my mind.”

Coryd opened the wrapping. The woman studied the remaining fastenings and at last touched one with the edge of her nail.

“That’s the way she held the knife. I remember.”

Riarhin watched her sister lower her head. Until then the dead woman had existed between them through stories and objects Coryd supplied. Here was somebody else’s memory, specific enough to resist being made convenient.

The pathweaver asked whether the potter had kept the larger knife. Coryd said she had not seen it. The woman nodded and began putting the wrapping back as it had been. She tucked one loose edge with a practised care that made Coryd look away.

Riarhin asked about the last journey. The woman gave the route without turning it into an invitation. The passages had changed since then. The people at the baths might refuse them. Farieth had once been received there, and some would find the accusation difficult to believe.

“We have been finding it difficult ourselves,” Coryd said.

The pathweaver looked at her and nodded. At the mention of the waistcoat she sat down on the edge of a bench.

“She stayed three nights,” she said. “She wanted a way to the old baths. I told her who could take her.”

“Will you tell us?”

“In the morning.”

Zemia placed a cup beside her, with its handle turned away from the frayed cord. The woman noticed.

“Who taught you that?”

Zemia looked at her own hand.

“Someone who was particular.”

Before they left the next morning, Zemia went back to settle what they owed for food. Riarhin watched her stop beside the hollow behind the lower steps, where travellers left messages for those coming after. She was still there when the pathweaver called her.

Chapter 16 — The narrow crossing

Riarhin saw the crossing before she saw Farieth.

A narrow span joined two old foundations above running water. Its boards had been replaced recently; the supports beneath them had not. On the farther bank a lever raised the last section clear for floodwater. The lever had been painted once. Only the underside retained the colour, a green so dark that the wet wood looked newer than it was.

Coryd crossed first without her bag. She kept her weight above the supports, turning one foot where a board had split around its peg. On the far side she put a hand to the lever and looked back.

Riarhin lifted two fingers. Try it.

The last section rose, counterweighted by a basket of stone. Water shone in the opened gap. Coryd lowered it again slowly, feeling where the mechanism caught. Then she crossed back for the chest.

“Will it stay?” Riarhin asked.

“If somebody holds this end.”

“The foot?”

“Hand or foot. It wants to come down.”

Riarhin walked onto the first part of the span. The boards gave beneath her. She could feel the difference between a loose fastening and timber that had begun to rot, and trusted neither with a sudden turn. Below, the water was deep enough to hide the bottom except where it broke white over something hard.

The pathweaver waited on the bank.

“People use it,” she said.

“We’ll use it.”

“You are looking at it as if you mean something else.”

Riarhin came back. A twig lay across the path that had not been there when Coryd stepped onto the bridge. It might have fallen. She looked into the lower branches, then above them.

“Across,” she said.

The pathweaver went first. Harissa followed with Zemia close enough to help if the boards moved. Coryd took one end of the chest and waited for Linias to lift the other. He adjusted the case on his back, freeing both hands.

The whole movement should have taken moments. It took longer because Harissa found the first uncertain board with her bad leg and stopped. Zemia put her hand beneath her elbow. Harissa breathed through her teeth, then went on. Riarhin watched the branches beyond the bank.

A spray of leaves trembled. The others had already settled after the wind.

She moved towards Linias just as his case strap caught on a bolt.

“Duck.”

He bent, thinking she meant to release it. She pushed his shoulder lower and Remorse came free in her left hand.

Farieth’s cut passed where the back of his neck had been. Steel met steel below it with a shock that drove Riarhin’s wrist towards her own ribs. She let the force turn her, using the strong part of the blade to carry the sabre past rather than hold it stopped.

Linias went down on one knee. The case held him against the bolt.

Riarhin cut towards Farieth’s face. Her grandmother moved her head, gave a little ground, and put the sabre between them again. The robe had caught a leaf at the shoulder. That was all Riarhin could see of the passage through the trees.

Coryd had set the chest down on the far bank. She came back far enough to catch the strap while Riarhin held the approach.

Farieth looked beyond the point of Remorse. Harissa and Zemia were still on the movable section. The woman had chosen a place where the family could not scatter.

Riarhin struck the withdrawal signal against the side of her own thigh.

Farieth’s eyes went to the rear position before Linias moved.

Then she cut at his retreating shoulder.

Riarhin closed. It was less space than she wanted, but she could keep the sabre above his body if she entered before the cut gathered its full speed. Remorse slid along the other blade. Her right palm struck Farieth’s elbow, not hard enough to hurt, enough to turn the line. Linias crawled clear behind her.

Farieth stepped around the pressure and struck with the guard. Riarhin took it across the mouth. She tasted blood and bit her own tongue before she could say something foolish. Her grandmother’s next movement was already beginning.

Riarhin went onto the bridge.

The boards bent under her weight. Farieth followed without hurrying, keeping her leading foot clear of the split. She had seen Coryd test it.

Riarhin gave her the width of two boards and no more. Remorse’s point moved towards the eye, withdrew before the sabre could close it off, and returned lower. She did not mean to land either threat. She needed Farieth’s attention forward while the others reached the bank.

Behind her, the timber gave a small upward kick. Coryd had put her foot against the lever and was feeling its weight.

Not yet.

Riarhin could not turn to say it. She held her right hand down, palm flat, for an instant. The boards settled.

Farieth came forward into the space she had almost surrendered. Riarhin let her have it. The next place was narrower, where a root had grown across the lip of the old foundation. She put her heel on the root. Ghere had taught her to do that with nothing in her hands except a bowl; she had fallen often enough to remember the difference between resting on it and committing weight to it.

The sabre came across her body.

Riarhin dropped behind the root’s height, turning on the foot that remained on timber. Remorse opened the travelling robe and the flesh beneath it. Farieth’s breath came sharply through her nose.

She did not stop.

Her return cut drove Riarhin backwards before she could exploit the wound. Riarhin felt the point pass close to her stomach and drew in without meaning to. The step behind her was the movable section. She had reached it too fast.

“Lift.”

Coryd pulled the lever.

The board rose beneath Riarhin’s heel. She used the movement, springing towards the bank rather than fighting to keep it flat. Her free hand struck the stone beside Coryd. A moment later her knees followed. She came up with Remorse across her body.

The rising section hit Farieth’s leading foot. She caught herself on the fixed span. It was an exact, ugly recovery, made with the leg Riarhin had just opened. Water shone between them.

For the first time Riarhin saw blood run down her grandmother’s leg.

Coryd braced both hands on the lever. Harissa had the chest by a strap and was dragging it away from the edge. Linias reached to help her, saw blood on Riarhin’s mouth, and stopped.

“Take it,” Riarhin said.

He took it.

Farieth pressed a hand against her thigh. She looked at Coryd, at the lever, at the stone basket. Her gaze went next to the branch overhanging the water. Riarhin followed it without turning her head. The branch would carry one person. Its outer end would not reach this bank unless it bent almost to breaking.

Riarhin moved beneath the place where that person would land.

Farieth’s eyes came back to her.

“Go,” Riarhin told the others.

They followed the pathweaver into the trees. Coryd remained until the chest was clear, then wedged the lever with a fallen piece of stone. It slipped. She set it again and pressed until it held.

“You too,” Riarhin said.

Her sister left. Riarhin backed between two trunks, keeping Farieth in sight until the leaves closed the gap. Then she turned and ran along the higher ground.

A strip of loose earth lay across the obvious path. She went above it, stepping from root to root, and found the family where the pathweaver had halted them. The woman looked at Riarhin’s feet and nodded once.

“Down there is bad today.”

“I saw.”

They moved on without another explanation.

At the next rise Riarhin climbed far enough to look back through the leaves. Farieth had sat beside the span to bind her wound. The robe was pulled high, the injured leg extended. She worked without haste.

Riarhin watched long enough to be sure, then caught up with the others.

They did not stop until the pathweaver reached a place where she was willing to untie one knot and make another. She said the way behind would not be the same at night. Riarhin asked whether that meant Farieth could not follow.

“It means I would not take you back by it.”

That was all the certainty she would give.

Harissa sat on a fallen trunk. The bandage had spotted through. When Riarhin knelt to examine it, Harissa caught her hand.

“Tell me before you touch.”

“I need to see whether it has opened.”

“Then say that.”

Riarhin did. Harissa let go.

The stitches had held. One edge had begun to bleed again where she had pulled against them. Riarhin cleaned it as she had been shown and replaced the outer cloth. Harissa let her leg relax beneath Riarhin’s hands. Linias stood on the other side of the fallen trunk and watched.

“Sit,” Harissa told him.

“I’m all right.”

“You’re making me tired.”

He sat. Coryd counted the bags, found them all, and went back through them because she had forgotten why she began. Zemia stayed a little apart. Her eyes kept returning to the direction from which they had come.

“She can bleed,” Coryd said to her.

Zemia looked startled.

“You saw it.”

“Yes.”

“Then remember it.”

Riarhin washed her hands with the smallest amount of water she could use. There had been blood on Remorse from the crossing. She cleaned that too, checking the edge by touch and light. Her mouth had swollen on the inside. She held cool water against the split and spat it into the leaves.

Linias brought her a cup after dark. He carried it with both hands.

“You hurt her.”

“Yes.”

He drank his own before offering the cup again. His hands had begun to shake.

Riarhin put hers over one of them until the cup stopped knocking against its companion. He looked down at the joined hands as if he could not understand why they belonged together.

“I was trying to get free.”

“I saw.”

“I heard you this time.”

She nodded. He drank, still shaking, and sat beside her until the pathweaver told them the light was attracting things they would not enjoy feeding.

They put it out.

The darkness was less complete than Riarhin expected. Pale growth showed along a fallen trunk. Farther down the slope, something flashed once beneath the leaves and then again in another place. The pathweaver moved their water vessel away from the glow and laid a cloth over its mouth.

Riarhin sat with Remorse across her thighs. Its scabbard was damp. She wiped it with the inside of her sleeve and listened until she could distinguish the breathing close to her from the movement farther away. Linias had lain down but was not asleep. Harissa shifted every few minutes to keep the wounded leg from stiffening. Coryd had found a place where her back fitted against a root and remained so still Riarhin had to look twice to be certain where she was.

Zemia whispered that she needed to go beyond the camp.

Riarhin rose at once. Zemia said she did not need help, then saw the pathweaver’s expression and stopped objecting. The older woman pointed to a place close enough to the camp that privacy would have to be granted by everyone looking away. Zemia looked mortified. Riarhin turned her back and stood where she could see the lower slope.

Beyond the trees, a light moved as if carried at waist height.

She kept her hand off the hilt. Drawing would make a sound and leave her trying to decide whether she was facing a person before the person had seen her. The light stopped. Its height changed. She could not find a body above it.

Zemia came back, fumbling with a fastening. Riarhin put out an arm to stop her walking into the open.

“What?”

“Wait.”

They stood together. The light moved farther down the slope, then vanished so suddenly that Riarhin could not tell which direction it had taken. Zemia’s shoulder shook against her arm.

When they returned to the others, the pathweaver asked whether they had called out. Riarhin said no. The woman nodded and lay down. She would say no more about it.

Linias raised himself onto an elbow.

“Is she there?”

“I didn’t see her.”

“That isn’t—”

“I know.”

He lay back down. Riarhin took her place against the tree. Her mouth ached where Farieth’s guard had struck it. Each time she swallowed, the split caught. She drank carefully from the cup, keeping its rim below the split. She caught herself thinking of the mouths she had bloodied, as though she had needed this to know what she had done.

Coryd came to sit beside her when the sky between the leaves had darkened further.

“Rest.”

“I’m awake.”

“So am I.”

“I know what to listen for.”

“Tell me.”

Riarhin began, then heard how little of it could be given in a few words. She showed Coryd the part of the slope she could see, the two approaches through which a body could pass without disturbing the low branches, the place where the pathweaver had refused to put a sleeping roll. Coryd repeated the important parts and asked what she should do if something came from elsewhere.

“Wake me.”

“I intended to.”

Riarhin lay down. The root beneath her shoulder was uncomfortable, but moving would put her farther from Coryd. She stayed. Linias’s breathing changed at last. Harissa muttered in her sleep, a complaint without a listener.

Riarhin closed her eyes and saw the grave. She had not thought of it while Farieth came across the boards. Now the cut root lay pale against the earth, and she wanted to be there badly enough to feel the want in her chest. She could not return to the morning before the visit by surviving this night. Ailsa would still be dead when the forest let her sleep.

Coryd touched her shoulder once, lightly, to tell her to turn because she was lying on the edge of her coat. Riarhin turned. Beneath the coat the ground was a little warmer.

In the morning they ate without lighting a fire. Linias found a beetle in his cup and carried it some distance before setting it down. Harissa said he had chosen a long walk for something that would return without gratitude. He said she should know. Their quarrel was quiet enough not to trouble the pathweaver.

Riarhin stood and tested her mouth with her tongue. The swelling had eased. Coryd gave her the sword she had kept beside her through the last watch. Neither said anything about the hours Riarhin had slept.

Part III — The remembered country

Chapter 17 — The people over the baths

The woman who received them had a broad muzzle, short dark fur at the wrists, and a small child asleep against her shoulder.

The pathweaver called her Irset. She listened, with a hand over the sleeping child’s ear, then led Riarhin to a covered opening in the floor and lifted one edge of its woven mat.

Steps descended into darkness. Far below, something moved water from one vessel to another with a regular hollow note.

“You stay above this.”

“We’re looking for—”

“Above.”

Riarhin nodded.

The woman replaced the mat.

They were given two rooms built over the old bathing hall. Light entered through a rib of blue crystal in the wall. It had cracked near the ceiling and been bound with three bands of a dull metal Riarhin did not recognize. Beneath it the stone remained warm.

Harissa put her foot against the warmth and sighed so deeply that the woman smiled.

“Leave room for the child. She sits there after washing.”

The child woke, objected to the visitors, and was persuaded to tolerate them by Linias’s instrument. She plucked one string until her mother took her away.

Outside, an older man whom Irset called Hevrin was replacing roof tiles beside a carved figure whose draped garment flowed into the supporting column. Half the figure’s face was gone. New hooks had been set into its shoulder for hanging tools.

Riarhin helped him lift a tile.

He was not interested in hearing why they had come. When she tried to explain the woman following them, he asked her to keep the tile level. She did. Only after it was in place did he invite her to continue.

“She carries a pale sabre,” Riarhin said.

He glanced towards Remorse.

“So do you.”

“Mine is straight.”

“I can see that.”

She felt foolish and recognized that he had probably intended her to. He asked what the pursuer wanted. Riarhin told him she believed the family dangerous. He considered this while fitting a wedge.

“Are you?”

“I have been.”

He gave her another look, less dismissive this time.

“To us?”

“I mean not to be.”

“People have meant that before.”

Below them his granddaughter called for a piece of string. He told her where to find it. She asked whether she could use the red one. He said no, then relented when she explained its purpose at length.

Riarhin waited for him to return to the subject. He did not. The tile still needed lifting, and he expected her to help with it. He pointed to where it should go. She understood the gesture and still asked him to show her.

From the roof she could see the baths below: pale basins filled with leaves, a colonnade cut across by a heavy later wall, stairs leading to an opening too narrow for the carts that had once approached it. Beyond the trees rose the broken upper edge of a crystal spire.

The man saw her looking.

He pointed farther along the ridge to where a line of stone caught the light between trees. There had once been another roof joining the spire to the baths. The structure was gone, but its supports remained, each carved with a different fold of the same long garment. Riarhin could imagine the walk beneath it crowded with people who expected the roof to last.

“Did it fall in the war?” she asked.

“Which?”

“The old war.”

He smiled without amusement.

“My grandfather repaired one end. His grandfather complained about repairing it. Somebody was always repairing it.”

Riarhin looked again at the missing span. There was no single moment she could use to explain the absence. People had kept it standing until they could not, or until they stopped wanting to.

“Don’t climb that.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

He gave her the next tile with an expression which said he had heard that before.

That evening their hostess’s child spilled a bowl of grain into Harissa’s open chest.

Harissa closed her eyes. The child looked at her mother. Nobody moved quickly enough to rescue either of them from what came next.

“How,” Harissa asked, “did you get that lid open?”

The child showed her.

“I had tied it.”

The child showed her how she had untied it.

Harissa looked at the knot, then at Riarhin.

“Useful household.”

They picked grain from the folds of her clothes. Zemia persuaded the child to sort it into a bowl by giving the task more importance than Harissa thought it deserved. Linias found three grains inside his instrument and accused the child of deliberate malice. She offered to fetch more.

Later Coryd sat with the roof-worker mending a split handle. They had no common fluency beyond what hands could demonstrate. Riarhin could hear them laugh when the first binding slipped. A second attempt held.

She took a cup outside and sat where she could see the tree line. Harissa followed and lowered herself carefully onto a bench.

“You could go to bed,” she said.

“So could you.”

“Mine has grain in it.”

Riarhin smiled.

Harissa looked at the sword beside her.

“How many times have you done this?”

“What?”

“Waited for someone who wants to kill you.”

“Not always in such good company.”

Harissa accepted the evasion for a while.

“Did Ailsa hate it?”

“Yes.”

“Did she ask you to stop?”

“She asked me to come home.”

They watched the dark until the cup cooled.

On the second morning the roof-worker asked Riarhin to help inspect the covered walk. She was light enough to stand on one span while he worked on the adjoining joint.

Below them the old basins lay beneath a skin of leaves. In one, the water remained clear in a perfect circle while the rest had gone green. She asked him why.

He shrugged.

“Never step there.”

“Does anyone know what it does?”

“People used to put flowers in it. They lasted. Somebody put a finger in to get a ring out.”

“What happened?”

“He got the ring.”

The man showed her his own first finger, held rigid while the others curled. He was not the person in the story; she could see that. It took her a moment to understand that the shape of the answer was all he intended to give.

They worked until the roof-worker’s granddaughter came to fetch him. She brought a basket containing three eggs and wanted him to admire one larger than the others. He did, with an attention that made the child’s face shine.

A yellow leaf hung beneath the clear circle’s surface. Another drifted over it. Riarhin kept her hands away.

On the walk back she found Zemia listening to their hostess explain the arrangements for washing. She could speak enough to make herself understood, but the hostess made her repeat the sequence of vessels before allowing her to begin. Zemia did it without embarrassment. Riarhin wondered when she had learned to bear correction so easily, then saw her face when the woman turned away. The expression was tired rather than patient.

On their second evening Irset invited the visitors to eat with the people who lived above the baths. Riarhin came late from the roof and found her place occupied by the child, who had arranged three stones and a spoon in front of her.

“I’m sitting here.”

“I can see that.”

“You can have there.”

She pointed to the warm wall. Riarhin took the offered place before remembering the meal on her first day with Ghere. Across the room Harissa was arguing with Hevrin about a song he insisted she must know. He had given her half a line. She told him she knew six songs containing those words and disliked five.

“That one,” he said when Linias tried a phrase.

“Of course it is.”

Harissa sang it. She made the refrain vulgar in a way that needed no translation. Irset’s child laughed because the adults laughed; her mother covered her own mouth too late to pretend disapproval.

The song concerned a man who made a new window to keep watch over his wife. Every verse supplied a different reason he should have kept the wall. Hevrin objected to the carpenter being blamed. Coryd asked him how much the carpenter charged. Their argument carried on after Linias had finished playing.

Riarhin watched Zemia make room for an elderly man by shifting her own stool. Nobody had told her which place would be needed. She had seen him pause in the doorway and understood. Riarhin wanted to ask whether she had always known such things or had learned by being looked at until she moved.

The child brought over her spoon and stones.

“You take one.”

“Which?”

“This.”

Riarhin took the stone. The child gave her another and frowned when she tried to take the third. Rules were explained only when broken. Riarhin accepted two corrections and lost something that appeared to be important. The child demanded another game.

“Let her eat,” Irset said.

“She can eat and play.”

Riarhin could. She had eaten in a saddle, in rain, standing with a bow in one hand. She took a mouthful while the child redistributed the stones, and heard Harissa say to Coryd that their sister had found an officer she was prepared to obey.

On the third morning Coryd found paid work carrying old roofing slabs to a store beyond the lower court. She asked Riarhin to come because the man paying wanted pairs and Hevrin had refused to lend her his granddaughter.

“He says she has things to do.”

“Does she?”

“She was throwing stones at a bucket.”

Riarhin went. Linias followed, saying he could carry a slab as well as anyone and was tired of having the instrument treated as his only useful weight.

The store lay at the end of an avenue which had once been roofed. Trees rose through the places where columns had fallen, their trunks thick against fragments of pale carving. Other columns still stood, narrower at the base than Riarhin expected, their tops spreading into smooth branches of stone. Blue glints moved along the broken capitals as the light changed. Beneath them, people had laid rough boards across the old paving so barrows would not lose wheels in its seams.

A broad woman with a flat, furred face counted the slabs as they brought them. She wore a leather apron polished at the waist and a cord around one wrist from which three little keys hung. She gave Coryd the first payment immediately, then showed her where the next load must go. Riarhin approved of the payment until she remembered it was not hers to approve.

They worked in pairs, switching when one of the larger pieces needed a different grip. Linias learned to take the weight from Coryd without either of them putting it down. Soon they were moving so easily together that the woman asked whether they had done this before.

“Yesterday we carried a chest,” he said.

“That isn’t a roof.”

“No. It was more troublesome.”

Coryd told him to lift. He lifted. Their steps fell together over the boards.

The sound beneath them changed.

Riarhin had been bringing a smaller piece alone. She stopped with it against her thigh. A low note trembled through the paving. It was too faint to belong to an instrument nearby and too even for the wind. Linias heard it. She saw his head tilt while his feet continued.

“Put it down,” she said.

Coryd looked back. Linias took one more step.

The avenue became longer.

Riarhin could still see the store. It stood in the same place, its open door full of the same stacked wood, but between it and the workers lay a pale stretch of road which had not been there when they began. The trees along its edge had thinned. Something like stars shone in the joints between stones, though the morning was bright. Her eyes tried to follow the line away and could not decide where it ended.

The woman with the keys struck her counting stick against a column.

Everyone stopped.

“Set it down,” she said.

Coryd lowered her end. Linias lowered his a moment later. The slab touched the boards with a hollow knock. Riarhin put hers against the nearest wall. She kept her feet where they were.

Two people came out of the trees at the far side of the avenue. One carried a hooked pole; the other wore a strip of hide across the chest and had pointed ears rising through short, grizzled hair. They looked at the road, then at the workers. The one with the pole spoke to the woman in the apron. She answered sharply and pointed at the stack they had been moving.

Riarhin understood enough to hear an argument about whether something had been uncovered. The woman said no. Coryd, hearing the tone without the words, looked at Riarhin.

“Are they asking about us?”

“About the stones.”

“We haven’t moved the paving.”

“I told them.” The woman had heard. She turned back to the two wardens and said it again, more forcefully.

The grizzled one came along the edge of the avenue, placing each foot on earth rather than stone. He examined the boards beneath Linias. The low note continued, faint enough now that Riarhin might have believed she was remembering it. Linias held his hands away from his sides. His fingers moved once, as if finding a place on the strings.

“Don’t answer it,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t.”

“You were listening for the next part.”

He stared at her. She knew the expression: being told what one had done before one had decided whether to admit doing it.

The warden reached down with his pole and dragged a length of board crosswise over the join between two flagstones. Its end rasped against the pale surface. The woman in the apron flinched and protested. He ignored her. He did not step onto the road.

For several breaths nothing changed. Then the store seemed close again. A bird called from the trees where the distant road had been. Leaves stirred where the bird had landed.

The low note stopped.

The woman began to argue at once. She had paid for the work. Rain would come before the slabs were under cover. She could not have the store closed every time the old paving made a noise. The warden with the pole answered without raising his voice. Riarhin caught the word for beneath, repeated twice.

“We’re not digging,” she said.

He looked at her for the first time. His eyes moved to the sword, then to the hands she held open.

“Keep it so.”

“I meant the roofing.”

He had already turned away. The other warden crouched at the end of the avenue, pressed a palm to the ground, and waited. He stayed there while the argument continued.

Linias looked at Riarhin. “Do we move?”

She looked towards the earth along the columns. No star-light showed there. The warden had used it, but that did not make every place between here and the tree safe. She asked the woman in the apron where the ordinary footpath ran.

The woman pointed impatiently. Coryd picked up her bag. Linias reached for the slab they had put down.

“Leave it,” the woman said. “I’ve paid for today.”

“It’s in the way.”

“Then it will be in the way when I’m allowed to move it.”

They returned by the footpath. At the corner Riarhin looked back. The grizzled warden was still kneeling. The woman stood beside the stack with her hands on her hips. She would be there after they left, with paid labour unfinished and rain threatening her timber. Riarhin wanted to offer another hour when the road quieted. Coryd did so before she spoke. The woman nodded without thanking her and resumed her argument.

Back at the baths, Linias took the instrument out, plucked a string, and put it away again.

“Was it because of us?” he asked.

“I don’t know.”

“We were stepping together.”

“Yes.”

He rubbed his palms on his knees. “I liked it.”

Riarhin sat opposite him. She had liked how easily he and Coryd moved with the weight between them. She had liked it enough to let her attention follow them instead of the ground.

“Tell me if you hear it again,” she said.

“I wasn’t going to play it.”

“I know. Tell me anyway.”

He nodded. Later Coryd divided the payment into three shares. Linias tried to give his back because they had not finished. She put it beside his case and told him he could go back with her when the avenue was open.

For three days Farieth did not come. They washed their clothes. Harissa’s wound stopped weeping. Coryd slept through an entire afternoon and woke furious because nobody had called her for supper, which was still two hours away.

Zemia laughed until she had to sit down.

Chapter 18 — The end of the song

The aqueduct began below the fields and ended above them.

Linias walked its length twice before admitting he could not see how the water arrived at the upper trough.

A boy carrying two buckets told him to sing.

“What?”

The boy sang the first phrase. Linias repeated it, then followed the rest as the boy continued. Water stirred in the lower basin.

An elderly woman came out of a shelter and struck the boy’s shoulder with a folded cloth.

“Who’s at the top?”

He looked uphill.

“Go.”

She waited until he reached the trough, then taught Linias where to end. He had retained the whole song. She made him sing it twice without water before permitting the third repetition beside the channel.

The stream rose.

It gathered itself along the carved groove, climbing past Riarhin’s knee, then her shoulder. Sunlight broke in its moving surface. At the top the boy opened a wooden gate, and water spread into a narrow run towards the fields.

Linias stopped where he had been told. The flow settled.

Harissa applauded. The woman handed her a bucket.

They worked until the vessels were filled. Afterwards the boy showed Linias where a reed had lodged in the run. Linias knelt to reach it and had to be pulled out by his belt when the mud took his foot. Harissa laughed so hard she spilled her drink.

The boy took them to the shade where the field workers ate. Low stones made a circle beneath a tree, with a space on one side for the feet of somebody who needed to sit upright. Harissa chose that place. Riarhin followed the boy to a basket of bread and was stopped by the old woman.

“Bring yours.”

“We have some.”

“Then bring it.”

Linias went for their bag. By the time he returned, everyone else was seated. The old woman had left a place for him and turned a second empty bowl upside down on the ground.

He began to sit there.

“Beside me.”

“But—”

“Beside me.”

He moved. Riarhin saw Harissa begin to smile and then look at the empty place with more attention. The bread went round. A girl with cropped hair shared a dish of beans, and a man whose sleeves were tied above his elbows made room for his child between his knees. They were ordinary people tired enough to begin eating before they spoke.

The stranger came while Coryd was cutting their last hard cheese. Riarhin had heard no steps. He stood in the gap between the stones with his hat in his hand, looking over the meal with an expression of delighted anticipation.

His clothes were dry despite the wet paths. There was a mark across his left boot where a larger foot had stretched it.

“Have I kept you?” he asked.

The old woman put down her spoon.

“No.”

“How kind.”

He glanced at the overturned bowl.

Riarhin waited for somebody to tell him to sit. Nobody did. The child between the man’s knees had stopped chewing. Her father rested his hand against her back.

“Who has brought the bread?” the stranger asked.

“The bread is here,” the old woman said.

“I can see it is here.” He smiled at Zemia. “I asked who brought it.”

“Several of us,” Zemia said.

He came into the circle. The child bent her head over her bowl; her father’s hand went still. Coryd set her knife beside the cheese and watched the stranger lower himself elegantly into the vacant place.

“Then several of you may welcome me.”

“You may eat with us,” the old woman said, before Zemia could answer. “This meal.”

The stranger picked up the overturned bowl. Its unglazed base had left a pale circle on the soil. A few tiny white fungi, crushed beneath it, began to straighten.

Riarhin had seen too much to mistake them for the small creatures they first resembled. She put the bread beside the bowl. The stranger thanked her, using a word she knew as daughter and had also heard used for a cutting taken from a tree. The pleasure went out of the meal.

He ate neatly. He complimented the beans and asked the girl with cropped hair whose kitchen had taught her to use that much salt. She reddened. He asked the man whether his child had always been so quiet. The man moved his own bowl in front of her and said she was hungry.

“And our singer,” the stranger said, turning to Linias. “Won’t he give us something?”

“We’re eating,” Harissa said.

“Music doesn’t prevent it.”

“Poor music does.”

Linias looked as if he might laugh. Riarhin touched his ankle beneath the table of knees and bowls.

The stranger smiled at Harissa.

“Then a little movement.”

She laid her hand on her injured shin. “You’ll have to move yourself.”

The old woman reached for the dish before it was passed beyond her. She replenished his bowl, slowly, making him look at what he was being given. He accepted another mouthful. While he chewed she turned to Riarhin.

“Is the cheese finished?”

“Nearly.”

“Finish cutting it.”

Riarhin took up Coryd’s knife. She divided what remained into small pieces. The stranger watched her hands. He had not looked at Remorse once, which she disliked more than if he had stared.

“Your sister speaks beautifully,” he said.

Riarhin did not ask which sister.

“Her voice is wasted carrying messages. I know places where people would come a long way to hear her.”

Zemia looked down at her bowl.

“She earns her living,” Linias said.

“I’m sure. But does she earn the right living?”

Harissa reached across him for the bread. Her elbow passed between the stranger’s gaze and Zemia’s face.

“I’ve heard that offer. It generally means the wrong bed.”

He laughed, a little too late.

“Does everyone here object to being fortunate?”

The girl with the beans began gathering the empty dishes. The old woman stopped her with a hand on her wrist. The stranger’s spoon paused.

“I haven’t finished.”

“No one is asking you to,” the woman said.

They waited while he ate. Sunlight moved onto Coryd’s foot. The child had grown restless and was pushing against her father’s knees. He loosened his hold. She stood, took two steps towards the gap in the stones, and stopped when his fingers caught her dress.

“Sit with me.”

“I’m finished.”

“I’m not.”

His bowl was empty. The stranger looked at it with interest.

Zemia reached for the last dish and put some beans into the man’s bowl. He began eating them at once. Riarhin heard Linias breathe again.

The stranger lifted a fingertip to one of the small mushrooms beside his boot. It curled towards him. He looked at Zemia, waiting. She kept her eyes on the dish as she put it down.

At last he wiped his spoon with bread and ate the bread.

“A good meal,” he said.

The old woman answered, “You have eaten.”

“And been made welcome.”

“This meal.”

He stood. So did Riarhin, because her body had already decided it would not remain beneath him. For an instant the stones under her feet seemed to slope towards the gap. The stranger took up his hat.

“Perhaps another time.”

The old woman was silent. Riarhin followed her example. He bowed to the company, stepped out of the ring, and walked uphill between the beds. A bean stalk bent towards him and remained bent after he passed.

Only when he had gone behind the shelter did the child begin to cry. Her father lifted her and walked quickly away.

The cropped-haired girl stared at the spoon the visitor had used.

“What do I do with it?”

“Wash it,” the old woman said. “Don’t put it in your mouth.”

Linias looked from one to the other.

“What would have happened if she’d left?”

The old woman began gathering the bowls.

“She didn’t.”

“I saw. I’m asking—”

Zemia put a hand on his arm. He stopped, then pulled free more irritably than he intended. She let him go.

Riarhin helped carry the dishes down to the run. She could feel the fatigue in the older woman’s movements now.

“Thank you,” Riarhin said.

“Next time eat where we put you.”

“We did.”

“Yes. Keep doing it.”

At the water Zemia took the visitor’s bowl without being asked and scrubbed the place where his fingers had rested. Harissa watched her for a moment, then crouched painfully beside her and took the spoon.

Zemia stood beside a covered recess in the aqueduct’s base.

She said a word.

Riarhin heard it clearly and found, when she tried to repeat it inwardly, that she had retained the sound without knowing where one part ended and another began.

Inside the recess a circular piece of pale stone turned.

It faced Zemia.

The old woman seized her elbow and pulled her away.
