Linias stopped speaking to the boy. Harissa put down her bucket carefully. The thing in the recess moved no farther, but Riarhin could feel everyone’s attention gathering around it. She wanted to draw Remorse and had no idea what she would strike.

The old woman said another word, slowly. The pale circle remained facing the place Zemia had occupied.

She tried a third time, altering the beginning. A little dust fell from the edge of the recess. The circle turned back by a fraction.

“Go up to the trough,” she told the boy.

He went without asking why.

Riarhin moved beside Zemia. She could see the pulse in her sister’s throat. Whatever she had done, she had not expected the answer.

“Don’t address it.”

“I didn’t mean to.”

“Then mean not to.”

Zemia put her hand over her mouth.

The stone remained turned towards the place where she had stood. When Riarhin moved there, nothing changed.

They returned by a path between cultivated beds. Zemia walked beside Riarhin, her fingers opening and closing around nothing.

“Did you know that word?” Riarhin asked.

“It seemed to be what it was called.”

“Did you hear it here?”

“No.”

“Before?”

“I don’t think so.”

“You would remember hearing it.”

“I remember saying it.”

Riarhin stopped. Zemia stopped too, watching her face with apprehension.

“Has it happened before?”

“People. Sometimes. I thought I’d met them.”

“The pathweaver?”

Zemia nodded.

“Did Farieth ask you about it?”

For a moment Zemia looked towards the others, who had gone ahead.

“She asked what I remembered.”

“And you told her.”

“Wouldn’t you?”

Riarhin had no honest answer that would make the question easier. Ghere had asked what she remembered. Bea had. Men in the Black Banners had put her at a rise above a road and expected her to repeat every movement below it.

“Don’t try it again,” she said.

“I wasn’t trying.”

“Then tell me if you feel it coming.”

Zemia nodded, though neither knew what that would feel like.

At supper Linias was still pleased with the water. He sang a little of the tune under his breath. The woman laid a hand flat on the table, and he stopped.

Zemia ate almost nothing.

After the meal the old woman called Riarhin aside. She had wrapped the aqueduct’s loose covering more securely and wanted to know whether the visitors would be staying near it again.

“No,” Riarhin said.

“If she speaks, don’t answer for the thing she addresses.”

“Why?”

The woman looked irritated.

“Because it isn’t you.”

Riarhin waited, remembering the bowl and the hearth. This time she had the patience to ask what she needed rather than argue about what had not been offered.

“If I say her name to get her attention, is that an answer?”

“I don’t know.”

“If I touch her?”

“I don’t know.”

“What have you seen?”

The woman described a visitor years earlier who had greeted something in a covered opening. Someone else had answered in mockery, imitating the sound. For the remainder of the day, the water had risen towards that person whenever they passed. They had been persuaded to leave before evening. The woman did not know what happened afterwards.

“Then that’s what you’ll tell the others,” Riarhin said. “If you will.”

The woman agreed. She told the account without embellishment while the siblings listened. Zemia did not attempt to explain away what she had done. Linias asked one question about the mocking answer, then looked at her and stopped.

Later Riarhin found Zemia washing her cup again though it was clean.

“I thought she would teach me,” Zemia said.

“Who?”

Zemia kept her eyes on the water.

“Farieth.”

Riarhin waited for more. Zemia dried the cup and put it away.

“Were you lonely?” Riarhin asked.

Zemia laughed with evident surprise.

“I know people everywhere.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

She leaned against the washing bench. Behind her the last of the water ran down a groove polished by use. The light had gone out of the recess at the far wall; only the warm stones continued to hold the day.

“When people know you remember,” she said, “they begin using you for it. Who promised what, which town paid, whether someone’s wife really said the thing they want her to have said. It’s useful. Then they want to be able to forget it themselves.”

Riarhin remembered men in the company asking her to repeat a command and then disliking her for remembering the part they had ignored.

“Farieth didn’t mind,” Zemia said. “She expected me to remember. She asked what I thought about it afterwards.”

“Did she listen?”

“Yes.”

Zemia straightened and took down another cup.

“You should sleep. Your hands are shaking.”

Riarhin looked at them. They were. She had been holding them together too tightly to feel it.

Chapter 19 — Five more years

A boy practised in the court beyond their rooms each morning. His teacher stood behind him, moving one foot occasionally to change the space in which he worked.

Riarhin watched from the doorway.

She had returned to Ghere with a soldier’s pack and a contempt for exercises she thought she had outgrown.

The Black Banners had taught her how people smelled when they were frightened and how a horse could keep running after it should have fallen. She had slept beside men who died the following afternoon. When Ghere asked her to cross a court carrying a bowl, she laughed.

He waited.

She crossed it. He threw a short wooden rod towards her face. She caught it, spilling the bowl.

He looked at the water on the stone.

“You threw it at me.”

“Yes.”

“Was I to let it hit?”

He took the rod from her and placed it in the bowl.

For a long time afterwards she thought about breaking both.

The next day he used a different bowl. She was prepared for the rod and moved the bowl before catching it. Nothing spilled. Ghere looked at the hand with which she had caught, then at the bowl, and asked her to do it again.

She did it twelve times.

On the thirteenth he called her name before throwing. She turned towards him and spilled the water down her front.

She flung the bowl at his head.

He caught it. For one bright instant she was delighted that he had to move his feet. Then she saw his expression.

She expected him to strike her. He put the bowl down and left.

He did not come the next morning. She practised alone, furiously, and made every mistake he would have corrected. At noon another teacher passed, watched for a few moments and told her to stop before she damaged her shoulder.

“I know what I’m doing.”

“So do I.”

She stopped only after he had gone.

That evening she found Ghere repairing a sandal. She sat opposite him and waited until he acknowledged her.

“I shouldn’t have thrown it.”

“No.”

“You were making me fail.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

He set down the sandal. His explanation concerned what she did when an expected movement became impossible. She understood it. She could have understood it days earlier if he had told her. When she said so, he looked at her with the same bafflement he had shown over the bowl at supper.

“You wanted me to learn it by falling,” she said.

He considered this.

“Did you?”

She stood up and left him with the sandal. In the morning she returned to the practice court. He was waiting. Neither spoke of the bowl again, though he stopped using her name to distract her.

After that, he began taking her farther into the forest between days in the court. Some journeys were work, some training, and she seldom learned which until she had done something wrong. Once they spent a morning carrying a woman who had broken her leg. There was no lesson hidden in it that Riarhin could find. The woman was heavy, the paths narrow, and Ghere had no greater patience for her complaints than for Riarhin’s.

At a steep place they changed ends. The woman asked why. Ghere said Riarhin was better at the lower footing. Riarhin heard the praise and nearly missed her next step.

The woman laughed so hard that she hurt herself and blamed both of them.

They brought her to a house built around a living trunk. Riarhin stood in the doorway while people took the injured woman from them. She expected to be dismissed. Instead somebody gave her a bowl and asked her to hold it beneath the woman’s foot while a binding was cut away. The skin was swollen and hot. Riarhin knew enough from the company to be worried by its colour.

She asked whether they had sent for a healer. The person with the knife said yes, before they sent for carriers. She held the bowl more steadily.

Ghere waited outside while she helped. When she came out, the day had changed around the house. She was hungry enough to shake.

“You could have gone,” she said.

“So could you.”

“I was helping.”

“Yes.”

He gave her food from the packet he had kept. She ate standing beside him, too tired to wonder what his answer had meant. On the way home she told him a story about a man in the company who had tried to walk with a broken foot because he did not want anyone touching his boots. Ghere asked whether they were valuable boots. She said they were stolen. He laughed then, unexpectedly.

Over the next five years she learned movements quickly enough to unsettle people who had expected to spend seasons teaching them. Ghere began bringing others to watch. She wanted him to stop doing that and wanted him to keep bringing them.

On a wet morning in the third of those years, he asked her to keep another student from passing through a gap between two trees.

She did it six times. The seventh, she put the student down hard enough to tear his shoulder. She had seen the opening and used it before he could recover.

Ghere sent the student away with someone to tend him.

“He was going through,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You told me to stop him.”

Ghere waited until she looked at him.

She could remember every part of the movement. She could have done it more gently if he had told her that the boy was not to be hurt. In the company, a man who came at that speed expected to meet something hard. Here everyone seemed to expect her to know which encounter she was in without being told.

Ghere took her arm and placed his fingers where hers had gripped the student.

“I know,” she snapped. “I know where.”

He released her.

For the rest of the day he made her help the boy wash, eat, and dress. She resented this until the boy tried to tie his own fastening and failed. Then she was ashamed of having resented it, which made her cross enough that he asked for somebody else.

Ghere sat beside her after dark.

“You could have told me,” she said.

“Yes.”

She had expected an argument. His agreement left her with nowhere useful to put the anger.

After the boy’s shoulder began to mend, Ghere took her out before dawn with no practice blades. She expected a punishment disguised as work and carried the empty baskets angrily. He let her. They walked until the roofs had disappeared and the ground grew wet enough to keep the marks of their feet.

At a narrow channel he took one basket and crossed. She prepared to follow. He held up his hand.

She waited while he went farther into the trees. His movements became difficult to separate from the settling leaves. At last she could see only the basket, a pale shape moving without an apparent bearer. Then that too vanished.

Riarhin remained beside the channel until she could no longer convince herself that she had been asked to wait there.

She crossed where he had crossed. The water was cold enough to make her calves ache. On the other side she found two clear prints and then nothing. She knew he meant her to follow, which made the lack of a trail feel like mockery.

She went towards the last place she had seen the basket.

A root rolled beneath her foot.

She caught herself against a tree and felt something move in the hollow below the root. It was large enough to displace a pool of rainwater. The surface rose, settled, and became still again.

Riarhin did not move.

From somewhere above her Ghere said her name. She looked up and saw his hand resting on a branch, two fingers turned down. She had been staring into those leaves. He had been there all along.

“Back.”

She began to lift her foot.

“Where it came from.”

She put it down again, felt for the earlier place, and shifted her weight onto it. The hollow remained still. She went backwards until she stood on the bank. Her heart beat so hard she could hear it against the sound of the water.

Ghere came down beside her.

“You could have told me.”

He put the basket down. Inside lay the things they had come to gather. He had done the work while she stood waiting.

“Look.”

“I did look.”

He crouched. After a moment she joined him.

The root she had stepped on was a fallen limb laid across several living ones. Its underside was pale where damp had stripped the bark. She had seen the upper shape and treated it as a continuation of the ground. Beneath it the water had no leaf litter, though leaves lay everywhere else.

She could see it now. She hated how plainly she could see it.

Ghere asked her to describe the place. She did, withholding nothing because withholding would have given him another reason to correct her. He listened and then asked where he had gone.

She pointed towards the basket’s last appearance.

“With my feet?”

Riarhin looked up. A thin limb extended from the tree at the crossing, almost horizontal, with a thicker support above it. He could have taken his weight through his hands and put little on the lower branch. She had watched the basket because it was easier to see than the person carrying it.

She climbed where he showed her. He stayed beneath while she tested the support, then followed far enough to change one handhold. From above, the dangerous hollow was easy to recognize. Beyond it lay another way, difficult to see from the bank and perfectly usable once she knew where to place herself.

They brought the baskets back by that route. Halfway home she stopped and told him she would not do that exercise again without knowing whether a mistake would kill her.

He looked at her muddy feet.

“I was there.”

“That is not an answer.”

He held her gaze for a long moment. Then he put his basket down.

“What would you have me tell you?”

“What I just asked.”

He answered. The answer frightened her more than she wanted him to know. She asked another question, then another, until they stood in growing daylight beside baskets neither of them had picked up. He became impatient once and she made him repeat himself.

Years later she would still look for leafless water beneath roots. She would remember the cold in her legs as sharply as his hand above the hollow. The useful knowledge gave her no wish to thank him for that morning.

One evening she heard him speaking in a room whose window faced the path. Her name was discernible among words whose relationships kept changing as she listened.

Too quickly. Both. Before she knows.

She understood enough to be frightened and waited until he came out.

“What did they say?”

“That you should continue.”

“With you?”

“For now.”

He carried two practice blades. One was newly made, its grip pared smaller for her hand.

She took it.

That blade fitted so well she resented him less for the rest of the day. They practised until dusk. He allowed her a success she recognized as genuine because it startled him: a change of hand and angle that came from a fight beside a wagon, a place where the expected footing had not existed.

He asked her to show it again.

She did, pleased enough to forget caution. The second time he answered it. The third time she altered it and struck his wrist.

He dropped the practice blade.

They stared at each other.

Then he laughed. It was an abrupt, delighted sound, and she laughed too before wondering whether she should be afraid. He picked up the blade with his other hand and made her show him exactly where her foot had been.

That evening they ate together outside. She told him about the wagon, the horse that had bitten a man attempting to help it, the soldier who had fallen asleep sitting up and insisted afterwards that he had been watching the road. Ghere listened with more attention than she expected. Once he asked after a person she had not realized he remembered.

For a little while she thought he understood why she had loved the company. Then he asked whether she intended to remain this time, and the evening became difficult.

“I came back,” she said.

“Yes.”

“What else do you want?”

He turned his cup in his hand. There was a chip at its edge. She noticed the movement, remembered it much later, and could not say why it hurt then.

“To know what you want,” he said.

A group passed on the path beyond them, laughing. One called to Ghere, who lifted a hand but did not rise. Riarhin envied that effortless assurance that the others would still be there when he was ready to join them.

“Do they ask you?”

“Sometimes.”

“And do you know?”

“No.”

She looked at him. He was watching the path, and for a moment he seemed younger than the man who put her on the ground each morning. She had not considered how much of his own place he might be putting at risk by continuing to bring her before people who were uncertain of her.

She could have answered with a hundred things. She wanted him proud. She wanted the place he belonged to. She wanted nobody to ask her to become smaller before they let her stay. What came out was that she wanted to finish her training.

He accepted the answer with an uncertainty she did not want to examine.

In the present Harissa stood beside Riarhin in the doorway, watching the boy.

“He makes him wait too long before correcting him,” she said.

“Perhaps he wants him to discover it.”

“Perhaps he likes standing there.”

The boy stumbled. His teacher caught him before he struck the paving, set him upright, and spoke quietly into his ear.

Harissa folded her arms.

“Well. That’s something.”

Later she explained her own leg to their hostess, refusing Riarhin’s offer to interpret. They misunderstood each other, became irritated, and eventually found a way to show where it hurt.

Riarhin went to fetch the water they asked for.

Chapter 20 — The scholar’s rooms

Eshai, the keeper who had known Farieth, kept folded cloth between the leaves of her books because the insects disliked its smell.

She offered no refreshments until Coryd reminded her that they had walked since morning. Then she apologized and brought a tray with too many cups.

“Farieth corrected the upper measure,” she said. “People were losing the warm water through a fault they thought was in the lower stones. She found it in the wall.”

She pointed through the window. A channel passed beneath the sill, carrying clear water towards the baths.

“She was patient with that work.”

Riarhin looked at the cups.

“Was she?”

“With that work,” the woman repeated.

The room had once belonged to an official of some sort. A figure above the door held a rod and wore a long garment folded over one shoulder. Someone had scraped an inscription from below its feet. A later inscription crossed the scrape; this too had been partly removed.

Coryd laid out the bark.

The woman recognized the scratched route and became unwilling to speak.

“Our sister came to you,” Coryd said.

“She wanted what her father had wanted.”

“What was that?”

“To reach a place whose approaches were closed.”

“Why?”

“He did not tell me.”

Linias asked whether the man had been alone. The keeper said he had arrived alone and left with two others. She would not say who they were. Harissa asked whether that was because she did not know or because she would not tell them.

“Because I promised.”

“He’s dead,” Harissa said.

The keeper looked at her.

“So we believe,” Harissa added, less confidently.

“A promise may concern more than the person who asks for it.”

Riarhin watched the keeper’s hands. They lay lightly on the table, the fingers bent as if she had just put down a small object. She had expected to come here and demand information about her father. Now she found herself defending a stranger’s refusal to give it. The feeling was inconvenient enough that she distrusted it.

“What can you tell us?” she asked.

The keeper looked at the scratched bark.

“That he wanted a route. That the route he wanted was already dangerous. That your sister knew enough to ask a more precise question than you have asked me.”

“Then tell us her question.”

“Which way can be closed from the outside.”

The room seemed to become smaller around the table.

Linias asked whether their sister had been afraid when she came. The keeper considered the question with a seriousness that made him sit still.

“She was angry.”

“At Farieth?”

“At being told she must wait. At the road. At me.”

“Did you help her?”

“As much as I would.”

Coryd drew breath. Riarhin saw the coming accusation and waited to hear whether her sister would make it.

“Why not more?” Coryd asked.

“Because she asked me to open a way I could not close behind her. There are people beyond it whose agreement I did not have.”

“She’s dead.”

“Yes.”

The keeper did not turn away from her. Coryd looked at the table for so long that Riarhin thought she might leave. Instead she asked whether the sister had left anything else.

“A length of thread. She meant to return for the wrapping she had borrowed.”

The keeper brought it from a small box. It was ordinary dark thread, wound around a piece of wood. Coryd took it with the care she had given the waistcoat.

Riarhin found herself thinking of how many things she had left with people she never saw again. A borrowed cup. A horse blanket. A promise to return after the weather changed. She had never imagined those objects becoming the whole account of her arrival. Coryd unfolded the waistcoat’s wrapping and placed the horn fastenings in sight, though Riarhin could not tell whether she intended an appeal or simply needed something to hold.

Eshai fetched the room’s old wrapping from a shelf. It was empty now. The thread Coryd held had bound it once; two little snags still marked where the knot had caught. Coryd compared them without saying anything.

“Did she laugh?” Linias asked.

The keeper looked at him.

“Our sister. While she was here.”

“Yes.”

“What at?”

Eshai stood with the wrapping over one arm. Riarhin could see that she had expected questions about routes, people, dates which might be tied to something useful. Linias waited. After a while the keeper sat down.

“I had put a bowl on a surface which should not be used for bowls.”

“Why?”

“It leans. I know it leans. I still put things there when both hands are full.”

“What happened?”

“The bowl slid. She caught it. She said I had arranged an occupation for anyone sitting beside me.”

Coryd made a small sound. Eshai looked towards her and then away, allowing it to belong to her.

“She mended the foot of the table,” the keeper said. “I had not asked her.”

Riarhin looked beneath it. A pale wedge held one corner level. The resemblance to the bed above Ailsa’s workroom was so immediate that she almost reached for it. Coryd saw where she was looking and put the thread down very carefully.

“May I see the place she slept?”

Eshai hesitated, then led her through the inner door. Riarhin followed only as far as the threshold. The room beyond was small and in use. Somebody had folded clean cloth on the bed. A cup stood by the window. Nothing announced that a dead woman had once lain there trying to decide whether to take a road.

Coryd stood at the foot of the bed. She did not touch the cloth. After a while she asked whether Dovra had been alone. Eshai said yes. Coryd nodded and came out.

Riarhin moved aside to let her pass. She had wanted this visit to produce information about Farieth. Coryd had come to find where her sister had been. For several minutes the keeper let that be the work of the room.

The old footway to the temple approach had fallen. A second way existed, but the woman knew it only as a sequence preserved in an azure sphere. She brought the object wrapped in a dark cloth and set it on the floor between them.

Riarhin knew its colour before the cloth opened.

Near the window a man sorting small tiles looked up. He asked whether the east stair had been restored.

“It is still gone,” the woman told him.

He nodded, then went to the window and measured the distance with his eyes.

“He knows,” she said quietly. “He asks anyway.”

Riarhin looked at the sphere.

“I’ll decide tomorrow.”

A knock came from the outer door. Eshai covered the object before answering. The person outside was a woman with a basket over one arm and wet leaves caught along the bottom of her cloak. She gave the keeper a folded strip, exchanged a few words about the path, and left without looking into the room.

Eshai read the strip twice. Then she put it beside the books and asked Coryd whether she would like more water.

Riarhin watched her pour.

“Was it from her?”

The keeper set the vessel down. “Yes.”

“What does she want?”

“A record she left here.”

“Which one?”

Eshai looked at the strip. Riarhin could see that she was deciding whether answering would break a habit older than their arrival.

“Notes on the inner works.”

“Will you give them to her?”

“Not today.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“It is what I can tell you.”

Riarhin rose. The keeper did not move away from the table. Beyond the window, someone was calling to a person above them to lower a basket. The ordinary interruption made it harder to insist on the speed fear wanted.

“Does she know we’re here?”

“She asks me to send the papers by someone who has not spoken with the visitors.”

Harissa swore. Linias looked towards the door through which the woman had gone.

“She could have come herself,” he said.

Eshai folded the strip. “She has not.”

Coryd put one hand over the wrapping of Dovra’s waistcoat. Riarhin had the sudden, ugly desire to take every paper in the room, the sphere, the keeper’s letters, anything Farieth might want, and put it where she could not reach it. She had seen houses searched that way by people who could explain every reason for doing so.

“Don’t send them,” she said.

“Sit down.”

Riarhin remained standing. Eshai pointed to the chair without raising her voice. At last Riarhin sat.

“The notes refer to work other people did,” the keeper said. “That is why they are here. If she wanted them kept from other eyes, she should not have left them among records which are used.”

“Then she can’t take them.”

“She can ask.”

“Will you refuse?”

Eshai turned the folded strip under one hand. “I will read what she means to remove before I answer her.”

The others waited. She looked at the room’s shelves, the cloth between leaves, the little repairs which kept water from running onto the lowest books. Water had darkened one corner of the lowest shelf. The book above it was propped on two tiles.

“May we see them?” Riarhin asked.

“When I have found them.”

“Today?”

“No.”

Harissa began to speak. Riarhin held up a hand and was immediately ashamed of using the gesture in somebody else’s room. Harissa shut her mouth anyway, giving her a look which promised the interruption had been remembered.

Eshai took the message to a small drawer and put it inside. She kept the key on a cord at her waist. When she returned she asked whether the visitors had eaten that morning. Coryd answered before Riarhin could turn the subject back.

They had eaten. They could wait for the meal. They would not send anyone else to collect the papers. Eshai accepted those answers and fetched another cloth for the sphere.

Riarhin helped her cover it. Their hands met at the edge of the fabric. The keeper’s fingers were cooler than hers and steady.

“I know you are afraid,” Eshai said.

Riarhin almost denied it. She tucked the cloth beneath the sphere instead.

The keeper did not tell her that fear would pass.

“There is still the upper view,” Coryd said.

The keeper looked towards the west wall. “You can see a roof. Seeing it doesn’t put you there.”

“It may show us something.”

“It may.”

Riarhin asked how far the upper view lay. The woman took a stick from beside the door and rose, annoyed by the time this would take but unwilling to describe a way the visitors might misunderstand.

Only Riarhin and Coryd went with her. Harissa had walked enough. Linias stayed to help carry the promised meal, and Zemia sat beside the wrapped sphere without touching it. Riarhin looked back once, then asked the keeper to put the object away until she returned.

They climbed through a series of roofless rooms. Rain had collected in hollows where mosaic tiles were missing. Across the surviving tiles, pale animals ran between feet dressed in garments that fell in heavy folds. One figure still had a face. Someone had removed the eyes with many small blows.

At the top stood a round building split down one side. Its upper chamber had been opened to the air. Through the crack she saw trees, and beyond them a bright segment of wall that seemed too high to stand without support.

A woman knelt at the chamber’s threshold with a wooden case spread open beside her. Small chisels lay in separate grooves. She wore a broad band around her wrist, darkened where sweat had worked into the material.

“Is the turn clear?” the keeper asked.

“Until the shadow touches that.” The woman indicated a pale line in the floor. “Then I stop being responsible.”

“For us?” Coryd asked.

“For the turn.”

The keeper introduced her as Mavren. Riarhin repeated the name and received a short nod. She was not asked for her own.

Above them an arrangement of brass rings moved so slowly that the change became visible only when Riarhin looked away and back. Two pale globes rode the rings. One was scarred, the other smooth and dark along half its face. She had expected a map of the sky. There was nothing above the broken dome that corresponded to either body.

Coryd watched an arm pass beneath another without touching.

“What moves it?”

Mavren tapped the stone in front of her with the end of a chisel. “If you find out, don’t stop it.”

Riarhin walked where the keeper placed her feet. A low opening faced the trees. Through it she saw the bright wall again and a roof beyond, green where water lay. The route scratched on the bark could have approached from the left. She knelt to change the angle.

Coryd caught her sleeve.

“What?”

Her sister pointed down. Riarhin’s next step would have carried her across a narrow band of darker stone. The keeper had stepped over it. Riarhin had thought this incidental.

“Over,” the woman said. “Don’t rest there.”

Riarhin crossed it and put her hand on the window’s edge.

A figure stood on the far side of the chamber.

She had seen that patch of wall empty a moment earlier. Now there was a man before it, his short mantle hanging without any movement in the draught. His face was hidden by the edge of a helmet. One bare leg showed below the folded garment at his waist. His foot occupied the same space as the fallen stone through which it appeared to rest.

“Back,” Mavren said.

The figure turned its head.

Riarhin drew Remorse far enough to loosen it in the scabbard. The keeper put both hands over hers and pushed it down.

“Back where you were.”

Riarhin moved. The figure followed, the pace unchanged as it passed through the low sill. Coryd had already stepped into the threshold behind Mavren. The keeper released Riarhin’s hand and went after her.

Riarhin reached the dark band. The figure’s sword lifted.

She jumped it.

Something struck the wall beside her with a noise like a wet cloth snapped taut. The wall remained unmarked. A loop of hair loosened beside her cheek and drifted onto the floor.

Coryd caught her by the shoulder and pulled her through the door. Mavren placed the blunt end of her chisel on the pale line she had shown them. For three breaths nothing changed. Then the figure stopped, turned, and resumed its measured walk across the room.

Riarhin could hear herself breathing.

“I stepped over.”

“Coming back,” Mavren said.

“I didn’t touch it going in.”

“You put the sword across.”

Riarhin looked at the line. Her hip had crossed it as she leaned to see the roof; the scabbard had extended behind her. Coryd was watching her. For a moment she wanted Coryd to look away so she could be furious with herself in peace. She drew the scabbard close.

“Are you cut?”

“No.”

Coryd examined the fallen hair before believing her.

Mavren sat back on her heels. Her hand trembled as she put the chisel in its case.

“Will it stay there?” Riarhin asked.

“It has before.”

“And if you hadn’t been here?”

“Don’t come when I’m not here.”

The keeper had gone grey around the mouth. She held her stick with both hands and looked at the measuring rings overhead.

“I thought that line was dead.”

“It was,” Mavren said.

The rings continued to move. Shadow passed over the dark globe. Riarhin backed away until she could no longer see the figure and discovered that Coryd had moved with her, refusing to leave the length of an arm between them.

Outside, the roof they had come to see was visible for another moment through a break in the branches. It told her almost nothing. She had risked three people for a roof.

Mavren packed her tools. Coryd offered to carry the case, but the woman shook her head and gave her a basket of stone fragments instead. The request steadied them. They took the basket down between them while the keeper followed slowly.

At the scholar’s rooms, Mavren washed her hands and examined the cut end of Riarhin’s hair with a professional curiosity that she did not enjoy.

“If you need a length of old road looked at,” Mavren said, “send for me. Looking. I won’t promise it will carry you.”

“What would you ask?”

“To see where you went. What you did. If you’ve woken something, I want to hear before I put my hand on it.”

Riarhin agreed. Mavren gave her no token, no object with which to summon her. She told her which house she used when she was nearby, and said she might be away for days.

When the woman had gone, Riarhin sat beside Harissa. Her sister touched the uneven hair by her ear.

“That wasn’t there before.”

“No.”

“Did you find the way?”

Riarhin shook her head. Harissa withdrew her hand.

Coryd went to wash the grit from the basket out of her fingers. Riarhin followed. For a time they stood with their hands in separate bowls.

“Thank you,” Riarhin said.

Coryd looked at her over the water.

“You went first. It doesn’t always mean you saw more.”

“I know.”

Her sister waited until she looked up.

“Next time give me room to see.”

Riarhin nodded. They carried the bowls out together.

Zemia asked whether another reader could take the risk instead. The keeper looked at her, and Riarhin understood that the question had changed the room.

“Would you?” the keeper asked.

Zemia opened her mouth and closed it again.

“She doesn’t have to,” Riarhin said.

“Neither do you.”

Riarhin looked at the covered blue.

She had spent the walk expecting that she would have to. She could leave by the roads they knew, with Farieth behind and perhaps ahead. She could ask these people to continue sheltering them. She could let someone else look into the sphere and receive whatever waited there.

Coryd put the scratched bark away.

“We can wait a day.”

Riarhin agreed.

The keeper covered the sphere and asked them to stay for food. While it cooked, she showed Coryd a series of marks cut into the outer wall. The earliest followed the line of the original room. A later series ignored the doors and ran straight through the place where a basin had stood.

“They wanted more from it,” she said.

“Who?”

She gave a word Riarhin understood as an office and another she understood as a command. Their relation was less certain.

“The accounts speak of the Hyro works. What they had made, what they might make. Requests came before the alterations were finished. More requests followed.”

“War?” Coryd asked.

“Preparation.”

“Is that different?”

The keeper traced a gouge crossing the older marks.

“The people who wrote these thought it was.”

Inside, the man with the tiles had begun arranging a small table for someone who was not coming. The keeper moved one cup back to the shelf. He saw her do it and pressed his palm against his eyes.

Riarhin went to help carry the food. Nobody asked him to explain himself.

Chapter 21 — The blue sphere

The keeper brought the sphere in a basket lined with folded felt. Riarhin had expected a box. The basket was the sort used for carrying small things between rooms, its handle dark where hands had held it. Eshai set it on the table beside a bowl of pears and moved the pears out of reach.

Harissa asked whether the fruit mattered.

“No. I don’t want anyone reaching past it.”

The answer pleased Riarhin more than it should have. She stood near the window with her arms folded, looking at the cloth beneath which the sphere lay. Since the observatory she had been able to imagine the old approach without knowing how its pieces met. There was enough of it to make every gap irritating. The temptation to have it whole was familiar.

Eshai asked her to sit. Riarhin remained standing.

“Who put this one away?”

“I don’t know.”

“Who brought it here?”

“A man who brought three. He died before I came.”

“From this?”

“From a fall. I don’t know what made him fall.”

Eshai did not smooth the answer. She unfolded the upper layer of felt so Riarhin could see the sphere’s size without seeing its surface. It was smaller than the one Riarhin remembered, or the hand in her memory had been smaller. She disliked being unable to settle that. She could remember the wet leaves, the shape beneath them, the blue. Certainty about one thing kept trying to spread to another.

Coryd asked to see the room where they would do it. Eshai opened the adjoining door. There were no stairs in that room, no open hatch, no balcony. Its window faced a wall close enough to touch with a pole. Harissa moved a stool out of the way. Linias took the lamp down from a bracket after asking whether they would need it. The room was light enough without a flame.

Zemia stood in the doorway while they worked. Eshai asked her to move to one side. She did so at once.

Riarhin made Linias repeat what he was to do.

“Speak to you. If you don’t answer, move the cloth between you and it. If that doesn’t work, draw you away.”

“Without touching it.”

“Without touching it.”

Coryd sat by the door. Harissa had cleared the floor. Zemia stood farther back than any of them.

Riarhin knelt.

The keeper placed a folded pad beneath her knees. It was a domestic kindness that made her more afraid. Someone had prepared for this before, had seen a person forget where their weight rested and bruise themselves while attending elsewhere.

“Can you stop it?” she asked.

“I can stop your contact. I cannot take away what you have received.”

“Will it know what I’m asking?”

The keeper shook her head.

“It was made to convey something. You may find what you need within it.”

Harissa moved to where Riarhin could see her without turning. Coryd tested the door and left it open. The man with the tiles had been asked to work in another room; he had gone willingly, but Riarhin had seen him watching the wrapped sphere as he passed.

She put both feet flat behind her. She named the room aloud, its door, the people with her. Linias repeated their names. When he came to his own, Harissa told him that no one had forgotten he was there. He gave her an offended look. Riarhin was absurdly grateful for it.

The sphere did not brighten. Its blue deepened until she could no longer decide how far away its surface was.

There had been rain the first time. She remembered wet leaves against her cheek, a hand she had thought for one wild moment might move, and the same blue beyond it. She had been hungry. She had been angry with Ghere. She had wanted to be somewhere he could not tell her how to stand.

A line crossed the blue.

She knew where it turned.

There was no moment of learning. Her body waited for the turn as it waited for the shallow step outside Ailsa’s workroom, though she had never put a foot here. Something needed to be carried with both hands. She could feel how far apart those hands belonged. When she tried to see the object between them, the knowledge gave her only the interval.

A figure moved beyond the first opening. Riarhin knew she should let it pass. She did not know whether it was a person. Light broke against one edge, blue where the room was pale. Her attention followed the edge until a voice called from somewhere behind her. There was no place behind her in what she knew.

The knowledge arrived with the certainty of her own foot touching a stair. She followed a sequence of openings, the third displaced above the second, a passage narrowing where it should widen. Someone ought to be waiting at the far end. She knew the place allotted to them, the angle of their shoulder, a task unfinished.

Their face would not come.

“Riarhin.”

She had to get to them before the passage changed.

“Riarhin.”

Dark cloth fell between her and the blue. She rose, reaching for a rail which had never been in that room.

Coryd caught her.

The floor came back violently. Her knees struck it. Linias was saying something about the sphere being covered.

“I know.”

He stopped.

She drew the route on a piece of slate. It was exact. She could feel each turn and could not account for how it joined the room in which she sat.

“What else?” Harissa asked.

“Someone waiting.”

“Who?”

“I don’t know.”

“Someone in the room?” Linias asked.

“No.”

He looked at Eshai, then at the drawing. His hand hovered above one of the lines. Riarhin caught his wrist before he could touch it.

Everyone became still.

She was holding him too tightly. His wrist shifted beneath her fingers, then held still. On the slate he had nearly touched the place where something must not stand. There was no reason he could not touch a drawing of it.

She opened her hand.

“I’m sorry.”

He brought his wrist against his chest. Harissa took the slate and laid it face down on the table. Riarhin wanted to tell her that the lines would be wrong when she turned it over. They would not be wrong. They were charcoal on slate. She pressed both palms against the floor until she could feel the unevenness of the boards through her skin.

Eshai asked her to describe the window.

“There’s a wall.”

“What else?”

“A bar. Dust. A little plant in the crack.”

“Can you see the plant?”

Riarhin looked until the small leaves ceased being an obstruction to somewhere else. They were grey with dust, their stems inclined towards the light. One had been eaten along its edge.

“Yes.”

Harissa sat beside her until the feeling of needing to rise diminished.

“Tell me about Ailsa,” she said.

Riarhin looked at her.

“Something ordinary.”

“She hated the step outside the workroom.”

“Why?”

“It was too high when her hands were full. She kept saying we should have it altered.”

“Did you?”

“Eventually.”

“Did she like it?”

“No. Then it was too shallow. She struck her toes.”

Harissa smiled. Riarhin could see the room above the workroom again, not with the alien certainty of the route but through things she had done there: the parcel catching against her hip, Ailsa telling her to turn sideways, the irritated laugh afterwards.

Her hands stopped reaching.

“I’m here,” she said.

“I know. Stay.”

The keeper waited until Riarhin could drink without assistance before showing her the slate. At one turn Riarhin had used a mark the keeper did not recognize. She asked what it meant.

“Someone stays there.”

“To do what?”

Riarhin’s certainty extended to the placement, not the purpose. She felt the same shame she had felt when Ghere asked a question whose words she understood and whose answer everyone else seemed to possess.

“I don’t know.”

The keeper made a note beside it, leaving the mark intact.

Eshai took the slate and compared it with marks in a book. Some matched. Some had no counterpart she could find. She asked Riarhin whether a short hooked line marked an entrance or a place where something turned.

“A person waits there.”

“You said that about this one.”

“Yes.”

“Does the same person wait in both places?”

Riarhin almost answered. The answer was so close that she could feel the shape of it in her mouth. Then she looked at the two marks and found she had supplied the same face to both: no face at all, only the assurance that someone would be there when required.

“I can’t tell.”

Eshai set the book aside.

Linias came back with water. He used his other hand to carry the cup. Riarhin noticed and he noticed her noticing. He put it down without comment, then flexed the wrist she had held.

“Does it hurt?”

“A little.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

She drank. The water smelled faintly of the wooden cup. She could still have drawn the route with her eyes closed. The idea of drawing it frightened her less than the wish to stand and walk it immediately.

Harissa asked Eshai whether they could take Riarhin outside. Eshai said a small enclosed court would be better than the stairs. Riarhin disliked being discussed as someone who might walk into a hole. She had reached for a rail which did not exist. She let Harissa take her arm.

The court contained a trough, a broom, and a plum tree growing at an angle from a raised bed. Someone had tied a supporting cord around its trunk. The cord had bitten into the bark. Riarhin wanted to loosen it. She put her hand on the knot and stopped.

“What?” Harissa asked.

“I was going to change that.”

“Does it need changing?”

“I don’t know what holds the tree.”

Harissa looked up. A branch rested on the edge of a roof. They stood beneath it for a while. A drop of water fell from a leaf onto Riarhin’s sleeve. She rubbed it into the cloth with her thumb.

The man who sorted tiles came back before the evening meal. He saw the covered sphere and paused beside it. The keeper said Riarhin had finished. He nodded and went to the window.

Riarhin joined him there after a while.

“Do you still see the stair?” she asked.

He looked at the space beyond the sill.

“I know where it ought to be.”

“Does that stop you seeing where it is?”

“It isn’t anywhere.”

She waited.

“Sometimes,” he said at last, “I am carrying something when I remember. Then I have put a foot there before I have asked myself where the foot will go.”

Riarhin looked down at the drop. Someone had fixed a low bar across the opening, easily stepped over by a person who wished to, enough to interrupt a body moving without thought.

“Does this help?”

He touched it.

“I asked for it.”

They stood side by side until the keeper called them to eat. At the table he argued about a piece of work with an accuracy and irritation entirely his own. A younger woman had fitted two tile fragments together with the edges reversed. He turned one over and showed her the worn underside. She said she had fitted them that way so the colour continued. He told her the colour was less important than making something she could stand a pot on. She asked whether he meant to stand pots on every surface in the house.

Riarhin ate while they quarrelled. At last the woman put the fragment down in the right direction and reached for the stew. The man passed it without claiming victory.

Later he brought a little stack of tiles to the place where Riarhin sat. He asked whether she could see the difference between two nearly identical glazes. She could. He put them farther apart and asked again. Her eyes found the cooler one at once.

“Hold that while I mark it.”

She held it. He drew a small line on the back with a point of charcoal, then took it from her. They did three more. It was quiet work. Harissa was talking with the younger woman about a place she had danced. Coryd had fallen asleep sitting against the wall.

“Did they all come for you?” the man asked.

“My family.”

He nodded. He did not ask why they looked so different from one another, or whether they had known one another long. When he had finished marking the tiles, he gathered them into a cloth and thanked her.

They slept there that night. Riarhin woke once with her hand held above the bedding, waiting for someone to put an object into it.

She lowered it before the others saw.

Chapter 22 — A little to the left

The tree was wrong.

Riarhin could remember its position without having seen it. The trunk ought to have stood beyond the broken wall. It stood in front.

“The wall has fallen,” she said.

Coryd crouched beside it.

“Not recently.”

“The tree was there.”

“You said you’d never been here.”

Riarhin looked at the roots. They crossed old stone that had settled around them years before.

The pathweaver tied a new knot in her cord and took them a little to the left.

They had not gone far when she stopped again.

Across the path hung a fresh bough, its leaves bound upright around the stem. There was room to walk beneath it. Riarhin had already begun looking beyond it for movement when the woman put down her bag.

“We go round.”

“How far?” Harissa asked.

The woman looked at her leg before answering.

“Farther.”

Coryd went to examine the bough without passing under it. A small strip of green cloth had been wound into the leaves. It bore a faded mark which Riarhin recognized from the stone above the keeper’s door, although here it lay on its side.

“Whose is it?”

The pathweaver gave a name. Riarhin knew the sounds; they had meant a lineage in one account and a place in another. Neither meaning helped her decide whether to be frightened.

“When was it put here?”

“Since I came.”

“Why?”

“They are taking the road.”

Linias glanced back. The phrase had made an ordinary, unwelcome sense to him. “An army?”

The pathweaver lifted one hand. They heard feet.

At first Riarhin thought there were many people coming through the trees. Then the intervals separated. Something walked with a measured tread; something else hurried beside it in shoes that struck twice for every step. Leaves moved above the path although the air had gone still. She could smell crushed stems and the faint, sweet odour of fruit left too long in a closed room.

Coryd backed away from the bough. Harissa stood between the two stronger trees beside the path, setting her bad leg behind her.

Riarhin looked for the shoulders of men beneath the movement. A pale hand appeared among leaves at the height of her face. It carried a pole. Farther up the pole a green hanging stirred and briefly opened, showing a mark she knew she had seen and could not place. The hand passed. A second followed, much lower, belonging to somebody she never saw.

The pathweaver pressed her palm against Zemia’s mouth.

Zemia stiffened. For a moment Riarhin thought she would fight the woman. Then she opened both hands and remained still.

The tread continued across their way. Riarhin counted for a few steps and stopped when the pattern began slipping around the numbers. Linias had his eyes shut. Coryd was looking steadily at a patch of moss near her own feet.

Somebody laughed on the other side of the bough. A voice answered in the old language, using a word for a company assembled under authority. Riarhin heard an instruction concerning those who had remained at a gate. She could not tell whether the speaker meant the people waiting now or people dead before these trees grew.

One face turned towards them. It was as fine-featured as Farieth’s and younger, the mouth parted in an expression of mild surprise. Before Riarhin could decide whether it had seen them, leaves covered it.

The sound diminished. The pathweaver kept her hand where it was until there was nothing to hear but water moving somewhere below them. Then she released Zemia.

“Did I say anything?” Zemia whispered.

“No.”

“Was I going to?”

“I didn’t wait.”

Harissa came out from between the trees. Her face was wet. She wiped it angrily, as if somebody had made her walk through a spray.

“Do they hunt?” Riarhin asked.

“Sometimes.”

“Us?”

“They have a road.”

Riarhin wanted to take the woman by the shoulders and make her give an answer with a beginning and an end. She had felt it with frightened men unable to tell her where an enemy had gone, and with Ghere when he spoke as though she already knew half of what she had come to ask.

She put her hands on her own belt.

“Which way can Harissa walk?”

The woman pointed uphill. There was no path Riarhin could see. Harissa began climbing before anyone offered to carry her.

They crossed a slope of loose stone, moving separately where it would not bear two people. Coryd carried the chest with Linias below her, helping lift it over roots. Riarhin followed the pathweaver until the trees thinned and a great amber surface appeared between them.

At first she took it for sunlight on rock. Then a straight edge emerged, and another above it. A flight of broad steps climbed to a landing that ended beneath open sky. Along one side stood figures in the act of raising a beam. The beam had not reached their shoulders. It hung at a slant, held by hands which had remained beneath it for longer than any arm could endure.

No sound came from that place. Even the birds seemed farther away.

Coryd lowered the chest and stared. In a gap beneath the unfinished beam, Riarhin could see one worker’s face. His mouth was open. It might have been effort. It might have been a word.

Zemia turned her head aside.

“Don’t ask me,” she said.

Nobody had. Linias moved in front of her, blocking the view without touching her.

The pathweaver gave them water. Harissa took three mouthfuls and returned the vessel without her usual objection to being watched. The light on the amber steps deepened. Riarhin could almost hear a hum beneath the silence, but when she bent her attention towards it, she became aware instead of her own jaw clenched tight enough to hurt.

“We are not going there,” the pathweaver said.

“I know.”

She looked at Riarhin until Riarhin stepped back from the gap in the trees.

They had lost much of the morning. Behind them the road remained closed. Riarhin thought of Farieth finding the bough, reading it as readily as she read a mark on a quay. She had no reason to believe it would delay her grandmother in the same way.

At the foot of the slope, the pathweaver loosened a frayed strip from her own pack and offered it to Harissa.

“Across your mouth if the wind smells sweet.”

“Will it help?”

“For a little while.”

Harissa looked towards the others. The woman had no more cloth to give. Riarhin took out a clean handkerchief and offered to exchange it; the pathweaver shook her head.

Harissa tied the strip beneath her chin, ready to raise. She did not offer it to anyone else. Riarhin was glad she had not been made to.

They followed the woman down to the old houses.

They passed a row of doorways whose thresholds had been raised one above another, each later entrance leaving the earlier one partly buried. In one opening a piece of carved drapery protruded from the earth. It belonged to a figure larger than any living person she had seen. Someone had built the new step across its folded stone arm.

The pathweaver stopped to move a fallen branch from the shallow channel beside the road. Linias helped her. Water began moving again, carrying leaves beneath the old threshold.

“What was this?” he asked.

“Houses.”

“All of it?”

“Some of it.”

Riarhin looked through an opening and saw a patch of floor still blue beneath the dirt, the colour deepening where the water touched it. The sphere’s route passed beyond these rooms without attending to them. Whoever had made the account had known them so well they scarcely needed to look.

She wanted to stop and see how somebody had lived there. The desire was unexpectedly strong. It might have been her own curiosity, or something the sphere had left. She told Coryd she wanted to stop, and Coryd asked how long.

“Only a moment.”

They stood together in the ruined doorway. On the inner wall were marks at several heights, worn and recut. Riarhin could not tell whether they measured growth, provisions, or an operation of the room itself. She touched none of them.

At the next rise Riarhin put out her hand for a rail. Coryd caught the back of her coat before she stepped into the empty space beyond the paving.

Neither spoke for a while.

They rested after the failed reach. Riarhin wanted to continue at once, partly because she could see how the others were watching her. Coryd sat on the path until she agreed to eat.

Harissa took the slate and asked Riarhin to describe the route without looking at it. She did. Harissa then asked the pathweaver to describe what lay ahead now. The accounts met at a carved drainage mouth and differed over almost everything surrounding it.

“So we go to the mouth,” Harissa said. “Then we look again.”

Riarhin disliked the simplicity of it. She disliked more that she had needed someone else to say it.

Zemia opened a packet of dried fruit and passed it round. When it reached Riarhin, there was one piece left. She broke it in half and returned the other part. Zemia accepted without trying to give it back.

Linias asked whether they should return to the baths. His voice was carefully casual. Nobody had wanted to be the first to say it after Riarhin had risked the sphere to bring them this far.

“We could,” Riarhin said.

“Would that be worse?”

“It would be more familiar.”

Harissa shifted her leg.

“I am beginning to dislike every answer that doesn’t answer.”

“Then ask a different question,” Coryd said. “Can we reach somewhere to sleep before dark?”

The pathweaver pointed towards the grove. They could. It would have water and a roof, provided they stayed in the rooms she showed them.

“Can she reach it?” Linias asked.

The woman looked at him as if he had asked whether rain could find a low place.

“She knows ways I don’t.”

Zemia lowered her eyes. Riarhin noticed it, stored it with too many other small movements she could not yet use, and felt ashamed of the inventory she was making of her relatives.

They rose. Coryd took the slate from Riarhin and wrapped it before passing it back. The gesture was neither accusation nor reassurance. It stopped the loose edge from cutting through the bag.

The pathweaver watched this exchange with interest, then retied a section of cord. Riarhin asked what had changed.

“Where we stop.”

“Because of me?”

“Because we have stopped.”

Riarhin let the answer stand. She was beginning to tire of every question she asked carrying a hidden request to be reassured.

They reached the grove in afternoon light. One of the trees was stone. Its bark held water in grooves too regular to have been made by weather. Beyond it an arcade extended through the undergrowth, arches opening on shadow.

The pathweaver led them through a service passage to a long chamber. Stone shutters stood within channels on either side. A bronze arm connected one shutter to a low catch; the other end had been worn smooth by hands.

A wooden screen lay across the lower part of the mechanism. Its frame had split where water entered from the roof. Coryd lifted one end while Riarhin drew the other clear of the arm. They leaned it in a shallow recess, leaving the carved inner face turned towards the wall. The pathweaver said it had been falling down for years. She had asked the people who carried baskets to repair it and none claimed it as theirs.

Behind it the bronze fittings were dark with dust. One shone where something still rubbed against it. Linias asked whether the screen had covered the fitting to keep fingers out. The pathweaver said perhaps. She kept a child well away when children came through here. Riarhin looked at the height of the arm and imagined small hands reaching for anything an adult had been told not to touch.

The pathweaver showed them how to hold the arm while another person seated the stone beneath it.

“Do not leave anyone between them when you try it.”

They tried it with the chamber empty. The shutter closed slowly, grinding at the bottom. Linias and Coryd lifted it again together.

Beyond was an outer stair with missing steps. A second narrow passage returned around the chamber to the place where they had entered.

Riarhin walked the loop twice. She asked who used it.

“People who know when to leave,” the pathweaver said, then, seeing her face, gave her a less irritating answer. “We move baskets through at the change. The main arch opens farther in, but not every evening.”

Coryd found marks on the wall beside the bronze arm. Some were at the height of a standing adult’s hand. Others had been made lower, as though the people using the mechanism expected someone seated to reach it.

“Who worked here?” she asked.

The pathweaver did not know. She showed them the wear in the service passage, the way a basket rubbed against one corner unless carried above the hip. Her own people had used it for years.

Linias helped her bring an empty basket through. He chose the obvious turn and struck the corner. She made him try the other shoulder. The basket passed cleanly.

Harissa sat on the low support while her leg recovered from the walk. Zemia asked whether the arm could be held from that position. Coryd tried, found the reach awkward, and shifted the stool they had brought. The change made it possible.

Riarhin tried the arm herself. From the stool she could keep the service entrance in sight; standing, she lost it behind the support. She moved the stool back where Coryd had put it.

They watched until the shadow within the main arch shifted independently of the sun.

Riarhin put her hand in her pocket instead of reaching towards it.

Before returning towards the baths, they changed the meeting place in case anyone became separated. The lower approach had flooded. Riarhin chose the drying shed and made each of them point towards it. Zemia went with the pathweaver to fill the water vessels; beside the spring stood another of the hollows used for travellers’ messages. Riarhin saw her kneel there. She thought she was adjusting the strap beneath the vessel.

Chapter 23 — Her grandmother’s courtesy

Farieth waited across a gap in the old walkway.

Her robe had been cut above the injured leg and tied close. She leaned very slightly away from that side. Riarhin saw the adjustment and felt an ugly relief.

“Your companions are noisy,” Farieth said.

“They’re leaving.”

“Yes.”

Riarhin stood where she could see the service passage behind her. Coryd had taken the others towards the baths. The arranged meeting place was a drying shed beyond the lower watercourse.

“The coast gave you too many doors,” Farieth said. “Too many people to mistake you for their concern.”

“And here?”

“You brought them where you must ask which ground will hold you. Where they will accept a direction they cannot understand.”

Riarhin looked past her at the lip of the walkway. Damp leaves had collected on one side. The other was dry beneath a thin grey skin.

“Ghere made me cross places like this carrying his supper.”

Farieth’s gaze shifted to her feet.

“How long were you with him?”

“Long enough to object.”

She eased her heel away from the grey strip. Far below it water made a hollow sound. Farieth watched the movement more closely than the answer.

Riarhin could have told her the rest: the first years, the company, the return, the five years she had wanted to be allowed to count for something. She let her grandmother have the supper.

“You left,” Farieth said.

“Yes.”

The way she said it seemed to disappoint her grandmother. Riarhin had expected to feel pleasure at that. Instead she thought of Ghere holding a bowl and not knowing what else he could offer her.

“You were wrong about the western approach,” Riarhin said. “The records describe a later wall. It wasn’t there when the passage was made.”

Farieth considered this.

“Who showed you?”

“The woman who kept your books.”

“She kept her own.”

Riarhin nearly smiled.

“The wall was added. The opening you called a fault served the earlier rooms.”

“Yes,” Farieth said after a moment. “That would account for it.”

She looked towards the arches. Her expression changed in a way Riarhin could not read.

A little water fell from the broken roof between them. Farieth looked up, following its course along the underside of the stone. Her attention moved from one seam to another in a way Riarhin recognized from watching Ailsa find a fault in cloth: the visible error leading back to an earlier, less visible one.

“You see where it returns,” Farieth said.

Riarhin did. The water had crossed beneath the walkway instead of falling straight through the gap. A narrow channel took it towards the later wall.

“If they closed that before measuring below—” Farieth began.

Then she stopped. The sabre had not moved from its line, but for a moment the conversation had become one she might have held with the woman who kept the records. Riarhin felt the invitation and wanted to take it. They could stand here, two people with the same troublesome patience for a detail, and follow the water until somebody else’s mistake became clear.

“Then other things may have been misunderstood,” Riarhin said.

“Certainly.”

“Us.”

Farieth’s gaze returned to her. Blood had dried at the edge of the binding. The cloth had been folded neatly despite the awkward place. She had given her grandmother that wound.

“Does it hurt?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes.”

“I can do it again.”

“I know.”

The reply unsettled her. She had wanted pain to have introduced doubt somewhere. Farieth looked at the blade, judged the distance, and remained where she was.

“I saw what one of you could do to the seed.”

“What did you ask them to do?”

The question hung between them.

Riarhin had thought once, as a child, that meeting her father’s people would answer the ordinary embarrassments of being unlike those around her. Here was someone whose likeness lay in the way she measured a surface before placing her weight, in the refusal of her attention to wander when another person spoke. It was unbearable to recognize these things while keeping a sword between them.

“Did you know my mother?” Riarhin asked.

“Of her.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

“No.”

The answer gave her nothing. She had almost asked whether Farieth had watched Bea die as she had waited for Ailsa, and stopped herself because she did not know what she would do with either answer.

“Are there others?” she asked. “Besides those with me.”

Farieth studied her.

“You are not the first to ask.”

“Give me a useful answer.”

“I cannot give you a safe one.”

Riarhin felt a desire to strike simply to stop her speaking that way. She let the point rise a little, then lowered it to the line she could defend.

Farieth looked towards the sound of water below the walkway. Riarhin remembered being told that her grandmother was a scholar. She had imagined a room with books, a woman looking up from work. Here she could almost see that woman in the concentration with which Farieth examined the changed course of the water.

“You used the sphere,” Farieth said.

“Yes.”

“Did it give you what you wanted?”

“Some of it.”

“And the rest?”

“I don’t know what it gave me.”

Farieth nodded with an understanding Riarhin had not expected to find comforting. For a moment she wanted to ask for help. The wish embarrassed her so sharply that she gripped the sword harder.

“Do not follow everything you recognize,” Farieth said.

“You could have told me that before coming to the grave.”

“I had not come to teach you.”

“No.”

A leaf turned in the narrow current below. Farieth watched it lodge against stone.

“The remaining containment will not bear another opening.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.”

Wind moved through the broken arcade. Farieth shifted her sound foot, testing a stone behind the gap. Riarhin noticed and continued speaking anyway.

“Was this person your assistant?”

“Yes.”

“Were they willing?”

“They wished to help.”

For a moment Riarhin heard Ghere explaining her own eagerness to someone who had not asked her.

Farieth put her weight onto the tested stone.

Riarhin stepped back.

Too late to keep her grandmother on the other side.

Chapter 24 — The second entrance

Farieth came around the gap by a ledge too narrow to see from where Riarhin had stood.

Riarhin met her at its end. The first exchange drove her against a pillar. She felt stone through her coat, turned along it, and cut at the injured leg.

Farieth did not offer it again.

She struck at Riarhin’s hand and passed her when she withdrew the blade. For a moment Riarhin thought the next cut would enter her back. Instead her grandmother went down the side stair towards the drying shed.

The shed had not been visible from their conversation.

Riarhin ran.

The stair ended above a channel broad enough to carry the baths’ overflow. A narrow walk followed its edge. Farieth had taken the walk; her robe passed behind a screen of hanging roots and was gone. Riarhin reached the lower turn and saw the wet print of her grandmother’s sound foot on the stone. The next print was lighter. She was still protecting the thigh.

Riarhin could take the same way and reach the shed behind her. She climbed the retaining wall instead.

A root gave beneath her hand. She let it go, found the seam behind it, and pulled herself onto the upper bank. The ground looked level from below. Here she could see the narrow depressions where old drains ran beneath leaves. Ghere had made her carry water over ground like this without spilling enough to darken the basket he put around the jar. She went along the raised edges, placing her feet where the leaf mould was thin. Branches caught at her coat. She ducked under one and felt it drag her hair loose.

The shed roof came into view below her. A strip of cloth moved in its doorway. Linias was standing beside it, his case over his shoulder. He was looking up the channel for Riarhin.

She gave the stopping signal as she came round the watercourse. Linias froze with one foot on the shed’s step. Coryd seized his collar and dragged him clear as the sabre passed through the space his neck would have occupied.

Harissa flung a basket of damp rushes at Farieth. They spread across her face and shoulder. Zemia screamed a word Riarhin did not catch.

“Service way,” Riarhin said.

Harissa moved first. She had one shoe in her hand and the other on her foot. Zemia caught her elbow, missed, and followed. Linias was trying to pull his collar free of Coryd’s grip. Coryd shoved him towards the gate without releasing it until he was moving in the right direction.

Riarhin came down the bank on the side of her boot. She hit the lower walk harder than she intended and brought Remorse across Farieth’s path. Her grandmother gave ground just far enough to avoid the point. Rushes clung to the wet edge of her robe. She brushed one away with the back of her free hand.

“Still here,” Riarhin said.

It came out breathless and angrier than she wanted. Farieth looked at the gate. Riarhin moved into that line, forcing her to look back.

The next exchange was brief. Farieth tested the outside of her blade, withdrew, and cut at the wrist. Riarhin turned her point down and stepped across the channel on a stone she had seen from the bank. Farieth could have followed. To do it without entering Remorse’s reach she would have needed another step, and the next stone was under water. She stopped at the edge.

Riarhin retreated through the gate. Coryd barred it with a pole used for carrying baskets. Farieth struck the pole once, examined the fastening, and began to work at its end rather than waste blows.

Riarhin watched long enough to see that it would hold briefly.

The passage beyond the gate was too narrow for them to run abreast. Linias went ahead with Harissa’s arm over his shoulder. Coryd followed close enough to keep her from falling backwards. Riarhin told Zemia to go next. Zemia was looking through the bars at Farieth.

“Now.”

She went.

At the first bend Riarhin nearly followed the clean stone stairs which rose towards light. The blue sphere had left her certain of the shape above them. Her foot was already turning when she saw the broken edge of the first tread. The upper flight was gone. What she knew would have carried her into open air.

She caught the wall and took the lower passage. Ahead, Harissa had begun to protest that Linias was making her hop too quickly. He slowed without answering. Riarhin reached them and told Coryd to take the next turn uphill.

“Not the stairs,” Coryd said.

“The roots beside them.”

Coryd looked up. Ferns concealed the line until she moved close enough to see where the wall had fallen away. She climbed first, tested a branch, and held it down for Harissa. Linias lifted from below. Harissa swore once, sharply, and got her sound foot onto the root. The loose shoe fell from her hand.

“Leave it,” Riarhin said.

Harissa looked at her. Then she went on.

Something cracked behind them. The gate had not given yet; the noise was too small. Farieth was taking it apart. Riarhin stood at the bottom of the root slope until Zemia had climbed, then followed with the sword held close to her side. Above the passage, wind moved through the trees. It carried the smell of warm water and somebody’s cooking.

The path she found there was an old one, visible by the way roots had grown over places feet once wore bare. It returned to the occupied chambers from above. She did not remember its name. She remembered coming down it in rain with Ghere ahead of her, his shoulders disappearing behind leaves, and how much she had hated him for refusing to slow down. This time she kept Harissa where she could see her.

They left food and two packs in the shed. Linias had the instrument because he had been carrying it. Harissa had nothing but the clothes she wore and one shoe.

At the upper chambers Harissa pulled her arm free of Linias. She stood with one bare foot on the stone and tried twice to speak. Riarhin asked whether she wanted to sit.

Harissa slapped her across the face.

The others stopped.

“You kept talking,” Harissa said.

Riarhin could feel the mark spreading hot along her cheek.

“Yes.”

Harissa raised her hand again, then pressed it against her own mouth.

Coryd brought a stool. Harissa sat, shaking with anger, and let Linias examine her bare foot.

Linias put down Harissa’s foot and came to Riarhin.

“Were you trying to persuade her?”

“Yes.”

“Did she seem persuaded?”

“She listened.”

He looked towards the service passage.

“So did I when you told me to leave the case.”

Riarhin had no answer to the comparison. She wanted to tell him how much she had learned in those few minutes, how precisely the word assistant had fitted into things they had already found. Harissa sat barefoot in borrowed cloth because she had wanted another answer from a woman crossing a gap with a sword.

No one moved to stand beside Riarhin. She had not expected them to. She had expected, absurdly, to be able to explain herself once she had caught her breath. Now the words she might use kept arriving in Farieth’s patient voice.

Harissa let Linias wrap her foot. When he pressed too firmly she struck his hand away, apologized at once, and began to cry. She covered her face before any of them could see much of it.

“I’m tired,” she said angrily through her fingers. “I’m so tired of running for things you find interesting.”

Riarhin lowered herself onto the floor opposite. Her scraped heel had begun bleeding again. She could feel the wetness inside her boot. She did not take it off.

“I’m sorry.”

Harissa shook her head. Linias sat beside her without touching her. Coryd went to fetch water. Zemia stood by the door until Harissa told her to stop looking like that. Then Zemia went after Coryd.

Riarhin stayed. Harissa’s crying subsided in uneven breaths. After a while she lowered her hands and asked for the cloth Linias held. She wiped her face, blew her nose, and said her head hurt. Riarhin gave her the cup when it came. Their fingers did not touch.

Coryd asked which packs they had lost. They worked through the contents. Food, a spare garment, the small pot Harissa insisted could be used for three different things. Zemia offered to try to recover them after dark.

“No,” Riarhin said.

“I can move quietly.”

“So can she.”

Zemia withdrew the offer. She looked less relieved than Riarhin expected.

Their hostess arrived, listened, and said the lower rooms would be closed for the night. The family was to remain above the baths until the household had decided whether they could stay.

Riarhin accompanied her to the household meeting. The people assembled in the workroom had put their evening tasks down in different stages of completion. A half-plaited strap lay across one man’s lap. A woman had brought a bowl she was grinding something in and continued slowly while Irset spoke. Hevrin stood by the window. He did not greet Riarhin.

She described where the pursuit had reached. Someone asked whether Farieth had crossed the lower channel. She said yes. Another asked whether the gate remained on its hinges. She could not say. Hevrin left to look from above, taking another man with him.

“Did you know she would come here?” the woman with the bowl asked.

“I knew she might follow us.”

“Here?”

“I didn’t tell her about the shed.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Riarhin felt the heat return to her cheek. She said she had hoped to keep the confrontation away from the household and had failed. She offered to leave by the upper path before morning if that was what they required. Irset looked towards the closed door.

“With that leg?”

“We’ll find a way.”

“And she’ll wait where we send you.”

No one answered that. The woman set down her grinding stone. They spoke to one another for a while, sometimes in words Riarhin understood and sometimes in associations she could not place. She kept still. The wish to interrupt was strong enough to make her press her hands together.

They decided to close the lower rooms and move the people sleeping nearest the channel upstairs. Riarhin offered to help. Irset told her to wait until the household decided what help it wanted. She promised she would not bring the family through the occupied rooms again.

When Hevrin returned, he said the gate was open. Farieth had laid the pole beside it without breaking it. He had seen no one beyond.

Riarhin thanked him. He nodded once and went to move his granddaughter’s bedding.

On the way back she counted who had heard the change from the old meeting place to the drying shed.

Part IV — What was given away

Chapter 25 — Who heard

They borrowed clothes for Harissa from a woman broader through the shoulders and shorter in the leg. Harissa looked at the reflection in a basin, adjusted the borrowed belt, and said she would never be seen in them again once they were safe.

“You look perfectly well,” Zemia said.

“Then you wear them.”

The household met in the room below while the visitors ate. Riarhin could hear individual voices without following every word. Their hostess spoke more than once. The roof-worker interrupted her and was interrupted in turn. A child complained from the passage and was sent outside.

Coryd set down her spoon.

“They’re deciding whether to put us out.”

“Yes.”

“Can you hear?”

“Enough.”

“Then tell us.”

Riarhin repeated what she could establish. The lower watercourse served three households. One family wanted it reopened at once. Another wanted the visitors gone before the gate was touched. Their hostess argued that sending wounded people onto the road before morning would bring the trouble to somebody else’s door. She did not claim it would remove the trouble.

Harissa pushed her bowl away, then drew it back and finished eating.

“Which one wants us gone?”

“The woman with the two boys.”
