“She gave me these clothes.”

“She can still want you gone.”

Harissa examined the sleeve she had rolled twice at the wrist. She nodded slowly.

The decision came after dark. They could remain until another arrangement was made, under restrictions that inconvenienced the household as well as them. Riarhin thanked the hostess. The woman asked her to reserve thanks until she had done what was required.

Riarhin waited until the meal was over before asking about the drying shed.

Everyone had heard the change. The pathweaver had suggested the spring. A boy from the watercourse had passed while they spoke, but he had been carrying a pot on his head and concentrating on the uneven stones. None of them could swear he had heard nothing.

“What are you asking?” Coryd said.

“How she knew.”

“She could have followed us.”

“She went ahead of me.”

“Then she knows the country.”

“She knew which place in it.”

Coryd pushed her bowl away.

Riarhin asked where she had gone the previous evening.

“To the lower house.”

“Why?”

“For a shoe.”

“For Harissa?”

“No. For the joy of walking there.”

Harissa raised her borrowed shoe and shook it at Riarhin.

Their hostess came in carrying bedding. Coryd asked her to explain where they had been. The woman did, with increasing annoyance as she understood why the question mattered.

She described the lower house and the boxes of footwear kept there for travellers whose own had failed on the paths. Coryd had tried three pairs against a cord cut to the length of Harissa’s foot. When none fitted, she had sat with a knife and softened the edge of one upper by scraping it thin. The woman brought the shavings from a folded scrap to show them, then threw them into the fire with more force than necessary.

Coryd remained standing, her face closed.

Riarhin went after Coryd when she left the room.

“I was wrong.”

“You asked.”

“Yes.”

“You’ll ask again.”

“If I have to.”

Coryd leaned against the warm wall. Her face had lost the easy expression it wore when work was going well.

“Then don’t expect me to make it pleasant.”

Riarhin nodded. Coryd remained against the wall. She had not dismissed her, but Riarhin could not tell whether staying would become another demand.

“I thought you were keeping something from us,” she said.

“I was.”

Riarhin waited.

“I went to ask whether there was work farther down. At the covered walk. Work I could stay for.”

“Why didn’t you say?”

Coryd laughed without pleasure. “Listen to yourself.”

Riarhin leaned against the opposite wall. The warmth from the stone reached through her coat. They could hear the household meeting breaking up below, stools moving, a child being called back from the stairs.

“I don’t like travelling like this,” Coryd said. “I don’t like being told when I can fetch water. I don’t like hearing you come to the door at night to make sure I’m still where you put me.”

“I check everyone.”

“I know.”

Riarhin pressed her shoulders more firmly into the wall. She had believed that doing it equally made it easier to bear. Coryd watched her discover that it did not.

“Were you going to stay?”

“I was asking. There might not have been work.”

“And if there was?”

“Then I would have decided.”

Riarhin wanted to say that Farieth would find her. She had said it in different ways since the household first gathered around Ailsa’s table. Coryd knew. If she chose differently, it would not necessarily mean she had failed to understand.

“I thought you liked being with us,” Riarhin said.

Coryd’s face changed. It was the first thing Riarhin had said which seemed to hurt her more than annoy her.

“I do.”

They listened to footsteps pass on the stair below. Riarhin remembered Coryd putting a cup where Linias could reach it, the speed with which she crossed the first roof and turned to catch Harissa. She had made those gestures into an agreement no one had asked her to sign.

“I should have asked about the work.”

“I might not have told you.”

Riarhin nodded. That was unpleasantly fair.

Coryd drew something from her pocket: a small piece of dark horn with a hole cut through it. It was the missing fastening from Dovra’s waistcoat, Riarhin thought, until she saw that it was unfinished. One side had been shaped; the other retained the rough curve of the material.

“She gave me that before we quarrelled,” Coryd said. “She said I could finish it on the road. As if I needed something else to do with my hands.”

“Have you?”

“You can see.”

Riarhin could. Coryd put it away again.

“I don’t want another person telling me that staying is the proof I ought to give.”

“I wasn’t—”

She stopped. Coryd watched her until the unfinished defence went out of the space between them.

“I’ll ask before I assume,” Riarhin said.

“Ask better than you did tonight.”

Coryd pushed away from the wall and went downstairs. Riarhin stayed until the stone grew too warm against her back.

Back inside, Zemia was dividing the borrowed bedding. She had put Linias’s roll beside hers, leaving the wall for Harissa’s leg. Riarhin watched her do it and saw nothing she had not seen before.

She asked their hostess where an observer could sit without being visible from the paths below.

The woman gave her a long look.

Before answering, she checked the sleeping child in the adjoining room and closed the inner door until only a finger’s width remained. Riarhin watched her do it. The gesture made the question that followed harder to evade.

“Who will you bring to my door this time?”

“No one. I hope.”

“That will need a better answer.”

Riarhin sat down and gave her one.

She explained the false locations, the empty case, the observer who would never need to approach the person looking for it. The hostess asked what would happen if the seeker came up to the roofs. Riarhin showed her the retreat along the covered walk and admitted that no plan could make all danger disappear.

“You suspect one of your own.”

“Yes.”

“Which?”

“If I knew, I wouldn’t need this.”

The woman studied her for some time. Then she sent for the roof-worker and made Riarhin repeat the whole proposal in front of him.

The roof-worker asked whether Riarhin intended to catch whoever came.

“No. I need to know where they go.”

“I won’t follow through the lower trees.”

“Don’t.”

“If they turn towards my house, I leave.”

“Yes.”

He studied the empty case. Its broken hinge clicked when he lifted the lid. He closed it and put it down again.

“Your grandmother,” he said.

Riarhin nodded.

“I argued with mine over a roof she wanted kept. She said it had been there before I was born. I said that was plain from its condition.”

His mouth moved, not quite into a smile.

“What happened?”

“We repaired it.”

They carried the case down together.

He asked for food to be left where he would wait and for someone else to fetch his granddaughter that afternoon. Riarhin agreed.

Afterwards she found Linias sitting by the warm wall with the damaged instrument on his knees.

“Was it Coryd?” he asked.

“No.”

“Was it anyone?”

“I don’t know yet.”

He plucked the bad string. The rasp sounded harsher in the quiet room.

“Zemia thinks you don’t like being helped.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“She said it before. On the boat. She didn’t mean it badly.”

“I expect she was right.”

He seemed disappointed that she did not deny it. She sat beside him until he began to play something which used only the strings that held their pitch.

Chapter 26 — A harmless errand

The case had held sewing tools. Its hinge was broken and the wood swollen at one corner. Their hostess intended to burn it when the weather dried.

Riarhin asked to borrow it.

She told each sibling a different place where she would leave the case with a copy of the route for Eshai to collect. She spoke to them separately, at times when the others were occupied. To Zemia she named a stone bench below the old laundry.

Eshai agreed to collect nothing that day. The route remained with Riarhin; the case held nothing at all. A man who tended the roof agreed to watch the bench from above. Riarhin showed him how to leave without crossing the lower path and told him to go if anyone approached him.

“I have lived here longer than you,” he said.

“Yes.”

He seemed satisfied with that.

Hevrin asked her to walk the retreat anyway. She went ahead of him along the covered walk. A few paces from the corner he put his stick across her path.

“Where would you go now?”

“Over.”

He shook his head.

The roof on the other side looked sound. She crouched, laid her hand on one tile, and felt it rock beneath the pressure. The tile beside it moved too. They were supported on a frame that had come away from the wall.

“Someone could still cross it.”

“You could. I’d go through.”

Riarhin straightened. He had not said it as a reproach. He waited while she looked for the way an older man with a stiff knee could leave.

They found one around the water vessel beneath the eaves. Hevrin would have to turn sideways, but it led to a stair inside his own courtyard. There was a door he could fasten from there. He showed her the bolt and made her push against it.

“I believe you,” she said.

“Push.”

She did. He had set the fastening well. The wood around it had begun to soften. When she told him, he pointed to a new piece resting along the wall.

“I was going to do that today.”

Riarhin offered to help before the watch. He agreed at once. They spent the next hour fitting the new wood while the empty case stood on the floor between them. Twice someone came to ask Hevrin for a tool. Neither person looked at Riarhin beyond making certain she was not standing on what they needed.

She liked that for a while. Then she thought of the laundry bench below them, of a person arriving there because she had set a small, deliberate lie moving through the family, and wished somebody would interrupt her with a question she could answer honestly.

When the fastening was done, Hevrin took a mouthful of water and watched her put the tools away.

“You still mean to do it?”

“Yes.”

“Then leave the hammer.”

She had been carrying it towards the stairs. She set it beside his hand.

Zemia took an empty vessel to the spring after the meal. Riarhin could not follow without abandoning the arrangement with the watcher. She noted the time Zemia left and the time she returned.

Coryd found her at the stair.

“Where’s the case?”

“Below.”

“Which below?”

Riarhin gave the place she had given her before: a shelf beyond the drying room, out of the damp. Coryd nodded and began unrolling a length of cord from her wrist.

“What are you doing?”

“Making it easier to carry. The hinge is bad.”

“It doesn’t need to be carried yet.”

“Then it will be ready.”

Riarhin almost stopped her. Coryd would find nothing on the shelf. She could explain it as a misunderstanding or a change made since they spoke, and watch her sister acquire another reason to dislike being kept outside decisions. Instead she asked Coryd to wait.

“Why?”

“I need it left alone.”

Her sister looked at her for long enough that Riarhin thought she had understood everything.

“Fine.”

She wound the cord back around her wrist. Each turn lay beside the last without overlap. Riarhin watched until Coryd asked whether that too needed to be done differently.

“No.”

Coryd went downstairs. Riarhin remained where she was, listening to her sister’s steps go farther than she expected, then turn into the workyard. A moment later she heard Hevrin’s wife asking for help with a frame. Coryd answered readily. Her voice had lost the flatness it kept for Riarhin.

For much of the afternoon Riarhin worked where she could see the stair. She repaired a strap that did not require quite so much attention and heard every ordinary departure as a possible answer. Harissa went to wash. Coryd went to help the roof-worker’s family carry a frame. Linias fell asleep with his head against the warm wall. Zemia returned with the water and spilled some while setting it down.

Riarhin disliked herself most when she was kind to her.

Zemia had brought an extra cup because she thought the roof-worker might be thirsty. She set it beside the strap Riarhin was repairing.

“Can you take that up?”

“He has water.”

“It’s the warm one. Irset says it helps his knee.”

Riarhin looked at the cup. She could smell the steeped leaves. Zemia had covered it with a scrap to keep the heat in, folding one corner back so it could be lifted without burning the fingers.

“I’ll take it.”

“Is he staying there long?”

The question might have meant anything. Hevrin worked on roofs. People brought him drinks. Riarhin knew precisely what else it could mean and nothing that allowed her to choose.

“Until he’s done.”

Zemia smiled faintly. “He sounds like you.”

She went to fetch the water she had spilled. Riarhin carried the cup to the roof and set it within Hevrin’s reach. He thanked her without turning his head. The steam rose beside his face and vanished into the damp air.

Below them, the bench remained empty.

She took the vessel, wiped the floor, and accepted Zemia’s thanks without being able to meet her eyes for long. Zemia thanked her again when she returned the cloth. Riarhin folded it too carefully before setting it down.

Nothing happened until near dusk.

Hevrin sent word by tapping twice on the drainpipe. Riarhin heard it from the stairs and went up without running.

A young traveller stood below the bench. He was holding a small bundle wrapped in green cloth. He looked under the seat, moved to the far end, and bent to look behind it. Riarhin could see his irritation when he straightened.

The empty case lay elsewhere, inside the locked workroom. There was nothing he could carry back to prove he had come.

Hevrin pointed with two fingers towards the lower path. The traveller had begun walking along it, touching the bundle under his arm as though to make sure that at least was still there.

“I have him,” Riarhin whispered.

“You said no following.”

“You don’t follow.”

He looked at her. She could not mistake his meaning for lack of language. He had heard too many people change the terms of dangerous work once they saw an opportunity.

“Only to the bend,” she said. “Stay here.”

She went down by the inner stair. At the bottom she took off the coat that showed pale against the trees and rolled it beneath the water vessel. The shirt underneath was dark and damp at the cuffs. She moved along the wall until the path bent out of sight, then climbed the low fork of a tree whose upper limbs reached over the adjoining court.

Ghere had once made her spend a morning above a track while people passed beneath. She had been pleased with her concealment until he pointed out that every small bird in the vicinity was watching the same tree. She had stayed there until they ceased to care about her. Now she waited while a tiny bird scolded from the next branch. The traveller stopped below, looked up, and saw only the bird.

He continued. Riarhin moved when the sound of his feet covered hers.

The old washhouse stood beyond a narrowing in the path, its roof broken in two places. A strip of cloth hung across the doorway. Someone inside drew it aside before the traveller reached it.

Farieth’s hand rested against the jamb.

Riarhin stopped with her cheek close to bark. Through the gap she could see the cut robe, the binding beneath it, and the traveller’s face turned upwards as he explained. He shook his head and spread his free hand. Farieth listened. She took the green bundle but did not open it until he had gone.

Riarhin wanted to hear the words. She could move to the next tree, follow the sound down, get the angle of the doorway clear of the broken wall. She knew how to do it. That knowledge felt like permission.

Farieth set the bundle inside. Then her head turned towards the trees.

Riarhin flattened her fingers against the branch. She did not draw them away from a crawling insect. A patch of light had reached the back of her hand; the insect crossed it and passed beneath her sleeve.

Below her, Farieth came out of the washhouse.

Riarhin could have dropped onto the path behind her. She might have reached the injured leg before the sabre cleared its sheath. The ground was poor for landing. A root lay where her foot would come down, and there was a second person in the washhouse whose presence she had not yet established or disproved.

She stayed in the tree.

Farieth looked up once. Her gaze passed over the trunk on which Riarhin lay, then returned to the path. She limped more when she believed herself alone. After a while she went inside and let the cloth fall across the doorway.

Riarhin waited until the light had moved off her hand. She returned by the branches for as long as they carried her, then took the upper bank back to the baths. At the stair Hevrin held out her coat.

“You said the bend.”

“I went farther.”

“Yes.”

He left her with it. Riarhin stood a moment holding the damp cloth, then went to tell Irset that the watching was over and the case could be burned whenever she wished.

Irset did not ask whether it had worked. She asked where Hevrin was. Riarhin told her. The woman went to the upper door, called him, and waited until he answered before returning.

“You went farther.”

“Yes.”

“Was she there?”

“Yes.”

Irset looked at the little passage which led to the room where her child slept. Riarhin had not brought Farieth back. She had watched the path behind her, taken the branches, waited where she could see anyone following. She began to explain that. Irset listened until she had finished.

“You came back another way.”

“Yes.”

“He was waiting for you on the first.”

Riarhin shut her mouth. She had been thinking of the danger she had brought to herself and the danger she had kept from the household. Hevrin had stayed on the roof, waiting for her.

“I should have sent word.”

“You should have come back when you said.”

“Yes.”

Irset took the empty case from the table. Its broken hinge clicked. She laid it beside the wood kept for the morning fire.

“Will you ask him again?”

“No.”

“Good.”

Riarhin went to Hevrin’s house before returning to the family. His granddaughter was eating from a bowl on the step. She moved her feet to let Riarhin pass and asked whether the watching had been a game. Riarhin said no.

Hevrin stood in the doorway. She apologized without telling him what she had discovered until he asked. When she described Farieth in the washhouse, he glanced towards the child and drew the door nearly closed behind him.

“Then do something with it,” he said.

“I will.”

“Before you need another roof.”

He went inside. The child continued eating. Riarhin returned by the open path, where anybody waiting for her could see her come.

She did not tell the family. She sat beside Linias while he tried to close the split seam of his instrument with remaining glue.

“Will it hold?” she asked.

“Until it doesn’t.”

He heard himself, frowned and added, “It needs a clamp I haven’t got.”

Zemia came to take away their bowls. Riarhin let her.

Later she made a small change to the next day’s movement. The others would stay behind the upper court until called; Zemia was to bring the water by the side passage after the second bell from the workyard.

She repeated it back accurately.

In the morning Zemia asked whether anyone needed a message carried to the amphitheater.

Riarhin said no.

She waited until Zemia was out of sight before rising.

Chapter 27 — The hollow behind the steps

Zemia walked quickly until she reached people, then slowed and greeted them.

Riarhin had followed dangerous men through streets where everyone knew their faces. She had waited outside houses long enough to learn whose cough came from which window. Following Zemia made her feel clumsier. Every time her sister turned, Riarhin expected to be recognized by the simple fact of having spent nights in the same room.

At the first bend a woman stopped Zemia to ask about Harissa’s leg. Zemia answered with her whole attention, setting down the water vessel she had borrowed for the journey. She remembered the woman’s boy had hurt a finger and asked whether it was better. The woman held up the child’s hand to show her.

Riarhin waited behind a wall with one palm against damp stone. Zemia’s concern sounded exactly as it had at Ailsa’s table. She had to stop herself from deciding that this proved anything.

When Zemia moved on, she carried a small parcel for the woman. She delivered it at the next house. The detour took her out of Riarhin’s sight, and Riarhin had to cross an open yard to recover the trail. An old man watched her cross. She nodded as though she had a reason to be there. He continued watching until she was gone.

At the lower houses she almost lost Zemia among people taking down yesterday’s washing. She found her by the sound of her laugh.

Riarhin followed at a distance that made her uneasy. In a city she would have used crowds. Here she used walls, trees, and the assumption that nobody had reason to look behind them at that particular moment.

She recognized a strip of soft ground before she reached it. Rain had collected under the leaves, leaving the surface smooth where there should have been the raised edges of roots. Zemia walked around it because the path did. Riarhin, following through cover, would have crossed it at an angle.

She climbed instead.

The first branch would have carried her but would also have shaken the leaves above Zemia’s head. She took the smaller fork near the trunk, bracing her right foot against bark until her left hand found purchase. Remorse lay close to her side. She kept the scabbard under her forearm so it would not tap the tree.

Below her Zemia stopped to retie the strap of the vessel. Riarhin could see the crown of her head. Her sister bent over the vessel with one loose strand of hair held between her lips.

She wanted to call down. They could sit on the step ahead and she could ask. She had asked before. Each answer had been complete enough to let her return to the family with another uncertainty folded away.

Zemia stood and walked on.

Riarhin stayed above the path for the next stretch. Her arms remembered how to distribute the load; her shoulder complained about the time since she had asked it to do so for this long. At a turn she let herself down behind an old wall and stood still until the effort stopped making her breathe through her mouth.

She had not become young again because the trees were familiar. Somewhere ahead her sister laughed, and Riarhin followed the sound.

At the amphitheater Zemia bought thread from a woman beside the entrance. They spoke for several minutes. Then she went down the outside stair and knelt at the hollow.

She placed something inside it.

Riarhin waited until she had gone, then reached in.

The paper was folded around a small stone. She opened it carefully. The hand was the same one she had watched addressing letters at Ailsa’s table.

The others wait in the upper court. I bring the water by the side way after the second bell. Do not come before. You said I could bring them through.

There was no signature.

Riarhin refolded it. Her first impulse was to wait for whoever collected it and learn how far the chain extended. Then she thought of the second bell, the side way, and the people above the baths who had agreed to shelter them.

She had enough to stop the next movement. More knowledge could come later, if it came at all.

She returned the small stone to the hollow without the message. Anyone opening it would find the weight and no words. That might delay them, or tell them they had been discovered. She could not arrange both outcomes to suit her.

Riarhin remained beside the stair until Zemia returned from the stalls.

Zemia stopped when she saw her.

For several breaths neither moved.

“Come with me,” Riarhin said.

“I asked her not to come before—”

Zemia closed her mouth.

Riarhin took her wrist. She did not squeeze. Zemia came without resisting.

They walked past the woman selling thread. She lifted a hand to Zemia and began asking something about the colour she had chosen. Zemia answered before Riarhin could hurry her on. Her voice had almost its ordinary warmth. Then the woman saw the hand at her wrist and let the question end.

Riarhin released her once they were beyond the stalls.

“Stay beside me.”

“I am.”

They passed a place where the path divided. Zemia turned towards the lower way out of habit, or because she meant to go there. Riarhin stepped into her path. Zemia stopped so quickly that her shoulder struck Riarhin’s arm.

“I know,” she said. “I know where we’re going.”

Riarhin could feel how little would be needed to make this ugly in front of people who knew Zemia as the woman who brought water and remembered their children. She put one hand against the wall, leaving the upper way open. Zemia went through it.

At the spring they had to wait for a woman to lift two vessels from the narrow passage. Zemia moved to help. Riarhin said her name. She stopped with her hands half-raised.

The woman looked from one to the other. She lifted the vessels herself. Water spilled over the lip and darkened the earth between them.

After she had gone, Zemia said, “She needed a hand.”

“Yes.”

“I could have—”

“Keep walking.”

Zemia looked at her with a hurt which Riarhin found almost unbearable. She had the folded message inside her coat. Its edge pressed against her ribs each time she turned. She attended to that pressure until they reached the upper court.

No bell had sounded from the workyard. There was still time to prevent the journey described in the letter. Riarhin asked Irset to keep everyone inside, then told Zemia to wait beside the door while she fetched the others.

Linias came first, smiling because he thought they had brought an answer about his repair. Riarhin saw the smile change when he looked past her.

At the upper rooms Linias put the instrument down. Harissa had been sorting the borrowed clothing; she left it across her knees. Coryd came in behind Riarhin and closed the door.

Riarhin laid the paper on the table. She told them where she had found it before asking Zemia to touch it.

Linias looked at the hand. His first movement was towards Zemia, as if someone had accused her in a language she did not understand and he would have to help. Harissa moved the water cup clear of the paper. Coryd stayed near the door.

“Read it.”

Zemia looked at the others.

“It isn’t what—”

“Read it.”

She did. Her voice failed at the last sentence, and Harissa took the paper from her to finish.

Linias stared at the folded edge.

“She might have been trying to keep her away,” he said.

“Yes,” Zemia answered quickly. “I was.”

“By telling her where we were?” Coryd asked.

“I had to tell her something.”

Zemia rubbed the wrist Riarhin had held on the path. A faint line remained where her cuff had pressed against the skin. Riarhin could not tell whether the gesture was discomfort or a request to be seen as hurt. She left it alone.

“Did you tell her about the loft?” Riarhin asked.

Zemia looked at Linias.

“Look at me.”

“I asked her to wait.”

“Did you give her the address?”

“Yes.”

Harissa stood. Coryd caught her arm, and for a moment Riarhin thought they would turn on each other.

“Let me go.”

“Sit down first.”

“I can stand.”

“Then stand here.”

Coryd kept hold until Harissa stopped pulling. Linias had not moved at all. Riarhin asked about the crossing. Zemia said the message had named when they were leaving; she had not known where Farieth would intercept them. Asked about the drying shed, she began explaining the flooded path.

“I was there,” Riarhin said. “I know why we changed it.”

“She wanted to know which route was safe.”

“For whom?”

Zemia stopped.

Linias asked whether Riarhin had followed her all morning. He spoke to the table, looking at neither woman.

“Yes.”

“Before this morning?”

“I watched for a message yesterday.”

“The case.” Coryd’s voice was flat.

Riarhin nodded. She told them about the separate places, the bench, the traveller who found nothing and went to Farieth. She left out neither the man who watched nor the distance she had followed after promising to stop.

Harissa looked towards the window. “You told me the covered steps.”

“Yes.”

“And if I’d gone there?”

“There was nothing there.”

“Except me, if I’d gone.”

She had asked them to leave the case alone. She had trusted that instruction until Coryd nearly disobeyed it by being helpful. Harissa waited for an answer.

“I should have told you not to go.”

“You should have told us what you were doing.”

“I couldn’t tell all of you.”

Harissa glanced at Zemia and closed her mouth. The anger did not leave her face; it changed whom she could give it to.

Coryd asked whether Hevrin had been in danger. Riarhin described the covered watch and the retreat they had repaired. Coryd listened, then looked away. She knew the roof-worker’s house better than Riarhin did by now. She could judge the answer.

Linias still watched the letter. “Is that why you changed the route?”

“Yes.”

“To make her—”

“To find out whether she would tell Farieth again.”

Zemia said, very quietly, “I didn’t want her coming here without knowing where we’d be.”

Linias looked at her then. Riarhin saw the last of his effort to help her find a better explanation falter.

The child appeared at the door. Their hostess lifted her without a word and carried her away. Riarhin waited until their footsteps had faded.

“How did it begin?”

“A name,” Zemia said. “Just a name. And then another.”

Chapter 28 — Until morning

The first was Telvan, the man whose landlady had kept his shoes.

Zemia had known him through a summer of performing together. He had a habit of trying other people’s instruments without asking, and would flatter a player’s technique while demonstrating what he thought it lacked. She smiled before she could stop herself, remembering. Linias looked at her with such naked disbelief that the smile died.

“He wasn’t kind,” she said. “He could be. Sometimes.”

“Was he our brother?”

“Yes.”

“And she knew because you told her.”

Zemia folded the edge of her sleeve back from her wrist. She had done the same thing before helping Harissa wash the wound. Riarhin watched her prepare for an ordinary task in a room where there was none.

Farieth asked where he had gone. She said he was family. Zemia had been pleased to know it. She had told her where he meant to spend the winter and written ahead so he would expect the visitor.

The second was a man called Kelven, who repaired stairways and platforms for travelling performers. He had needed help crossing a border after a dispute with the people who hired him. Farieth supplied a letter. “I could help him,” Zemia said. “I never had money to give people. That time I had something.”

“Did he cross?” Coryd asked.

“He wrote from the other side.”

“When?”

Zemia gave the date. She knew it exactly because a singer she had travelled with had married that day and she had carried the answer in the same packet as her congratulations. Harissa asked whether Kelven had written again.

“No.”

“Did you ask why?”

“I sent another letter.”

“To whom?” Riarhin asked.

Zemia looked at the door.

“To her.”

The lamp had begun to smoke. Linias reached to shorten the wick, stopped because his hand was shaking, and left it. Nobody else moved to do it for him.

“What did Farieth say?”

“That I should stop asking after people who had chosen to go.”

Coryd turned away. She put both hands against the sill and pressed until the tendons stood out beneath her skin.

“I don’t know that he’s dead,” Zemia said.

“No,” Riarhin answered. “We don’t.”

Kelven might be somewhere with a different name, avoiding letters from a woman whose help had frightened him. They would have to look for someone who could warn him without delivering him again.

She asked Zemia where the last reply had come from. Zemia gave the house, the room above a dealer in metal fittings, and the name of the woman who received letters there. Riarhin repeated them once. Linias went for paper.

“Two?” Riarhin asked.

Zemia looked at her hands.

“There was another woman. I never found out whether she went.”

“Where?”

She gave a town, a trade, a description of an inn with a broken stair. Coryd interrupted. The woman at the kiln had once worked in that town.

Zemia bent forward until her forehead nearly touched the table.

“When did you know?” Riarhin asked.

Instead of answering, Zemia described Farieth sitting beside her at a lodging table, correcting the way she addressed a letter. She had been paid badly for a performance and was trying to recover the rest. Farieth showed her whom to ask, what to state first, and which accusation would only give the recipient a reason to throw the paper away.

“The money came,” Zemia said. “All of it.”

She asked whether she could fetch the letters. Riarhin went with her. The others remained at the table. Linias watched them leave with an expression Riarhin could not meet for long.

Zemia kept the packet inside the lining of a bag. She unpicked three loose stitches with a needle from her cuff, then set the needle down where Riarhin could see it. The precaution was so deliberate that Riarhin almost told her it was unnecessary. She did not know whether it was.

“There are other things in it,” Zemia said.

“What things?”

“Letters that have nothing to do with her.”

“Take them out.”

Zemia separated the papers one at a time. A folded page with a childish animal drawn at its edge went onto the bedding. So did a receipt, a letter written on poor paper, a scrap of song in Linias’s hand. She paused over that one. Riarhin waited until she put it down.

The remaining pages were fewer than Riarhin expected. Farieth had not needed to write often. The earliest letter still had the marks where it had been folded and refolded into a smaller space. Zemia smoothed it before handing it over.

Riarhin could read the words. Farieth corrected a direction, advised a form of address, and asked after a cough. Beneath that she had written that she would like to know whether the young man Zemia described had found a better lodging. The request was ordinary enough that Riarhin might once have answered it herself.

“Telvan?”

Zemia nodded.

“The cough?”

“Mine.”

Riarhin looked at the line again. It contained no demand, no threat, no visible reason for a woman who felt alone to refuse the interest. She took the next page. Here Farieth had repeated a phrase Zemia used, praising its exactness, before suggesting another person she might visit on the journey.

“Were you looking for us already?”

“Yes.”

“She didn’t begin that?”

“No.”

Zemia took a breath, let it out, and tried again.

“I wanted to know how many there were. I thought she wanted to know too.”

Riarhin kept the pages separate. One might have been written before the killings began; another referred to a departure Zemia could date only by a later performance. She asked the dates without making the earlier kindness innocent by default. Zemia answered, sometimes precisely, sometimes after looking at the folds or remembering the room in which she had opened a letter.

The last sheet was brief. Farieth had asked for an address and written that she was pleased Zemia had learned to distinguish usefulness from mere willingness. Zemia could not look at it while Riarhin read.

They returned to the table. Linias reached for the packet, then stopped short of taking it without permission. Riarhin set it where everyone could see.

“She told me I noticed people,” Zemia said. “That most who claimed to love them didn’t notice what they needed. She said I could be of use.”

“You were already of use,” Linias said.

Zemia began crying then, quietly and with evident effort to stop. Harissa moved away from the table. Riarhin waited until Zemia could hear the question again.

“When did you know?”

“She told me she had killed him. She said he had been dangerous.”

“When?”

“Before I found Linias.”

Linias stood up. His stool fell backwards. He did not pick it up.

“You told me you had nobody,” he said.

“I didn’t.”

“You had her.”

“That wasn’t—”

“Don’t tell me what it wasn’t.”

His voice had risen. Irset appeared in the doorway, took one look at the table, and shut the outer door without coming in. They heard her speaking to someone beyond it, keeping them away.

Riarhin asked Linias to sit. He shook his head so sharply she did not ask again.

Zemia turned to her. “I thought if I found you—”

“What?”

“She knew about you. She watched where you had been. She said you would become difficult if you were left long enough.”

“And you thought difficult might help you.”

“I thought you could stop her.”

“Then why give her the loft?”

“She said she would leave the others until you came.”

Harissa gripped the back of Linias’s fallen stool. “I was on that roof.”

“I know.”

“No. Say what you told her.”

Zemia drew breath and gave them the message as she remembered writing it. She had asked Farieth to wait until morning and included the yard gate’s position, explaining that the street entrance would be closed.

Riarhin closed her eyes for a moment. In the dark she could see the pale hand below the loading opening.

“You gave her the gate.”

“Yes.”

“And the crossing?”

“The road. The time we meant to leave.”

“Was she going to wait?”

“She said she would hear me.”

“Did you speak to her?”

“No.”

Zemia’s voice had become hoarse. Riarhin could hear her trying to keep each answer small enough that it would not contain what followed from it. She had heard men do that after a raid went wrong: the order given, the thing expected, the thing no one meant. She had done it herself until somebody made her say whose house had burned.

“Did you tell her how we moved?” she asked.

Zemia did not answer at once.

“The signals,” Riarhin said. “The one that sent Linias back.”

“I said you were teaching us.”

“Did you describe it?”

Zemia nodded.

Riarhin remembered Farieth looking before Linias moved. At the time she had allowed that her grandmother might have seen the signal in the loft. She had wanted that to account for it.

Harissa righted the stool with a crash.

“Sit there,” she told Zemia, who was already seated. “Stay there.”

Riarhin stood between them before the anger could find another object. Harissa pushed at her arm, more protest than force, then leaned against the table with her head down.

Farieth had taught Zemia how to make a request that someone could answer, how to keep an address through changes in a household, how to know when a traveller was lying about where they had been. She had praised her memory. She had let her handle things she would not let others touch.

“She said I could learn what was wrong. That I could help her keep it from happening again.”

Harissa laughed once, without amusement.

“Did she say you were different?”

Zemia began to answer and could not.

“She let you handle things,” Coryd said. “What things?”

“Fragments. A stone from a place she’d worked. Papers.”

“What did she ask you to do?”

“To tell her what I thought they were.”

The room went quiet.

Riarhin looked at Zemia’s mouth. She remembered the pale circle turning at the aqueduct, the pathweaver’s sudden refusal of a name.

“Did you say names then?”

“Sometimes.”

“What happened?”

“Sometimes nothing.”

“And the other times?”

Zemia covered her eyes. “She was pleased.”

Linias turned his head away. Coryd remained watching.

“I thought it meant I could learn,” Zemia said. “I thought if she needed me—”

Riarhin waited, but she did not finish. The hand came down from her face. Beneath it she looked tired and ordinary, a woman who had been travelling too long and needed sleep. Riarhin could not make that face belong only to the person who had carried addresses to Farieth. It kept being the person who brought the lamp home, who fed beans to a frightened child’s father, who knew when to leave a cup beside someone rather than insist they drink.

She put the intercepted paper inside her coat. Zemia followed the movement with her eyes.

“You’ll keep it.”

“Yes.”

“For how long?”

Riarhin had no answer ready. She was glad of that.

Linias left the room. He returned with water and set it before her so hard that it spilled. When she reached for his sleeve, he stepped back.

Riarhin made arrangements for someone to remain with her. She began explaining where they would sleep.

“No,” Linias said.

“It keeps the door covered.”

“Put my bed somewhere else.”

Riarhin looked at him. He was holding his own hands together as if they would otherwise do something he did not want.

She moved the bedding.

Zemia drank. Nobody helped her put the cup down when her hands shook.

Later, while Harissa kept watch, Riarhin went to find Linias. He was sitting on the lower step with the case beside him. He had opened it and closed it again without taking anything out.

“I told her about my mother,” he said.

Riarhin sat two steps above him, leaving him room to stand.

“I told her things I hadn’t told anybody. She remembered them. Next time we were in that town, she took me to the street. I thought she had done it because she knew I wanted to go and couldn’t ask.”

“She may have.”

He turned sharply.

“Don’t.”

Riarhin held his gaze, then nodded.

He pressed the heel of his hand against his mouth. She watched his shoulders tighten and expected anger. When it came out as a sob he seemed offended by his own body.

“I was going to bring her to my sister.”

“You have another?”

“My mother’s daughter. She’s got a house. Children. She doesn’t know any of this.”

“Did Zemia have the address?”

“Yes.”

Riarhin waited until he could breathe evenly before asking whether Farieth had it. He did not know. They went back together to ask. Zemia answered that she had not given it, then saw his face and offered to show him every letter she still held.

He accepted. He did not thank her.

Riarhin arranged for a warning to be sent through a route Zemia had not used.

There was still the question of the night itself. They could not put the confession away with the papers and sleep as they had slept before. Harissa would not share the room with Zemia. Linias had already moved his bedding. Coryd said she would keep the first part of the watch if someone explained what keeping it meant.

Riarhin began with the door.

“And if she asks to go out?” Coryd said.

“One of us goes with her.”

“If she refuses?”

The room grew quiet. Zemia sat at the table with her hands around the empty cup. She had been listening as though the discussion concerned a bundle whose place must be settled before people could lie down. Now she looked up.

“I won’t refuse.”

Coryd kept looking at Riarhin.

“If she does?”

Riarhin had held prisoners. She knew the arrangements that would spare a tired guard from having to trust a promise. She looked at Zemia’s wrists and thought of the cord in Coryd’s pocket. The thought came too easily. A loop could be made comfortable enough to let someone sleep and secure enough that every movement towards waking began with restraint.

“No binding,” she said.

“I didn’t ask for it.”

“I know.”

Harissa stood by the window. “Then say what you are asking.”

Riarhin set both hands on the table. “She stays within the household. If she wants to leave, wake me. If she tries to send something, keep it. No one hits her because she says something unbearable.”

Harissa turned from the window. “You can look at me when you say it.”

Riarhin did. The anger in her sister’s face was so bright it made her seem younger. Then Harissa looked at Zemia, and the anger failed to become the blow either woman expected.

“I don’t want to touch her,” she said.

She went out. Irset stopped her in the passage long enough to ask where she meant to go. Harissa answered more courteously than she had answered anyone in the room.

Coryd asked whether the household knew what Zemia had done. Riarhin said she would tell Irset enough to protect her people.

“All of it that reaches them,” Coryd said.

“Yes.”

Zemia put the cup down. “I’ll tell her.”

Riarhin looked at her. She had been prepared to refuse, thinking of the ways Zemia could make a listener understand her need before hearing what she had done. She had watched commanders do the same, and had learned from them. She asked Coryd to sit with them while Zemia spoke.

Irset listened from the doorway at first, then came in and shut it behind her. Zemia began with the message that morning. She tried to explain the postponement. Irset asked whether the woman with the sword had been told which households were occupied. Zemia said yes. She asked whether the drying shed was named. Zemia said yes.

Irset sat down very slowly.

“My child goes there.”

“I didn’t know.”

“You knew people went there.”

Zemia pressed her mouth closed. Riarhin kept her eyes on Irset. If the woman ordered them out, she would have to find somewhere to take them before morning. If she tried to keep Zemia, Riarhin would have to decide whether the shelter had become another kind of custody. The possibilities arrived faster than anything she could honestly offer.

Irset asked for the paper. Riarhin gave it to her. She read it, turned it over, and handed it back.

“She sleeps in the room with the inner door. Someone sits outside. Tomorrow you tell the people from the lower houses.”

Riarhin agreed.

“Her,” Irset said. “She tells them.”

Zemia nodded. Irset rose and opened the door. Beyond it the ordinary life of the household continued: a child being put to bed, a dish set on a shelf, someone asking where a cloth had gone. Zemia watched the opening as if it led somewhere she had never been.

Before writing the warning to his sister, Linias returned to the room where Zemia sat. Riarhin remained at the door. He asked who else had received his sister’s address.

“No one.”

“Did you put it in a letter?”

“No.”

“In a list?”

“No.”

“Say her name.”

Zemia recoiled.

“Not like that,” he said quickly. “The name I gave you.”

She said it. He nodded, then clenched his jaw.

“I kept it because I meant to go with you,” Zemia said.

He closed his eyes.

“Stop telling me what you meant.”

She folded her hands beneath her knees so he would not have to see them shake.

Riarhin moved away before he came out. She could still hear the exchange through the door, but she went far enough that she had to stop attending if she wanted not to know the rest.

Linias asked for help with the direction on the warning, then refused help with the words. Twice he stopped to scratch out a sentence. He folded the third version and tore the spoiled sheets across.

Chapter 29 — What Ghere could not obtain

Riarhin sat outside while the others decided who would keep the first watch on Zemia.

A branch tapped the wall behind her. It had tapped for days. She could have moved it and had not.

Ghere had brought her to the assessment himself. She remembered him checking the binding on the practice blade, although she had checked it already. She remembered wanting to slap his hands away.

On the path she had rehearsed the way she would ask for water if they offered none. A familiar expression, with the pause where Ghere put it. She had been told she sounded impatient when she used the words as she first learned them. When she made the pause longer, somebody had asked why she was frightened.

She decided she would not be thirsty.

The people waiting were neither ancient nor solemn in the manner she had expected. One was finishing food. Another had mud on a knee. The youngest appeared to have come from the water; his hair had left a dark patch down the back of his shirt. They gave her room to set down her things and continued a conversation she could follow only in pieces.

The woman with the muddy knee asked her to take a wrapped object through a series of low openings. It was heavier at one end. Riarhin adjusted her grip and waited for somebody to stand in her way.

Nobody did.

She carried it to the last opening and set it down. The woman came after her, unwrapped it, and checked a thin ceramic tube suspended inside. It had not touched the sides.

“Again.”

This time the young man entered by the opposite end, carrying something equally awkward. Riarhin saw how they would meet in the narrowest place and waited in the opening before it. He passed. She continued.

The woman stopped her halfway through the return.

“What did you see?”

Riarhin described the load, the distance, the place where his fingers would have been trapped if she had tried to squeeze past. The woman asked what she had seen of him.

“His right shoulder is sore.”

The young man looked up.

“From carrying?”

“I don’t know. He didn’t want to bring it forward.”

He rotated it slightly. The movement confirmed what Riarhin had seen. She felt a small, delighted confidence. She had spent years being corrected for answering the wrong question. For once she knew why she had been asked.

They changed the order of the openings. She watched while one support was turned and another moved back. The route narrowed in a different place. When she entered again, the man came towards her sooner, with the load held across rather than along his body.

She turned her own burden upright and placed its heavier end on a support beside her foot. He passed. She lifted it again and finished.

The woman examined the tube.

Ghere stood behind her. Riarhin could not see his face.

The next exercise involved movement without a burden. The man who had been eating took a practice blade and showed her a sequence between three trees. At the second tree he let the trunk interrupt his line, recovered on the far side, and turned across his own former position. Riarhin had not seen the sequence before. She could already feel how it joined movements Ghere had taught her.

She repeated it.

He asked her to do it more slowly. She did, letting them see each place she put her weight. At the second tree the bark shifted under her foot. She changed the angle without stopping, finished, and turned towards him.

He went to look at the loose bark.

Someone said she should not have continued. Someone else disagreed. Riarhin stood with the practice blade held loosely at her side, hearing her own correction discussed as though she had not been the person making it.

“Did you know it would move?” Ghere asked.

“No.”

“Why did you turn there?”

“It was where I could put my foot.”

The answer seemed to please him. She found herself straightening, wanting the others to have heard his tone.

They asked her to watch while the sequence changed. This time the man crossed the opening from the other side and a second person stepped into the space he left. It was an exercise in keeping a passage open while people moved through it. Riarhin watched the first attempt, saw where the timing failed, and waited for them to begin again.

They told her to join them instead.

She took the empty position. The first person came through, then the second. Riarhin changed her pace to fit the delay she had seen. The passage stayed clear. A third person entered without warning and she moved out of the way, leaving a space which closed behind them all.

She had expected satisfaction. The people watching spoke more quietly.

“Again,” the woman said.

They varied the sequence. Riarhin followed. She could remember each previous attempt with such precision that the changes became clear before they were completed. Twice she moved early. The second time the man stopped her.

“You went before him.”

“He was going to turn.”

“Was he?”

“Yes.”

“He could have stopped.”

“Then I could have stopped.”

They tried it. He stopped. She stopped. The distance between them was smaller than she liked but sufficient. For a moment she was pleased in the way she had been when she first struck Ghere’s wrist. Then she looked at the watchers and understood that she had not made the exchange easier for herself.

The youngest asked how she had known where the turn would come. She described the order, the earlier attempts, the slight release in the shoulder before the man moved. He asked her to repeat the first sequence. She did so without the blade, speaking the movements as she showed them.

Before she finished, the woman with the muddy knee lifted a hand.

“Enough.”

Riarhin lowered her arms.

Too quickly, someone said.

She understood that phrase.

Later they spoke about her with Ghere. She stood within hearing. Nobody appeared to consider this unkind. They expected her to follow what concerned her, and much of it depended on things she had never seen.

“Ask me,” she said.

They turned.

“If you want to know why I did it, ask me.”

One did. She answered. Another question followed whose terms she recognized and whose premise she rejected without knowing how to make that clear.

Ghere answered for her.

She hated him then with a force that frightened her afterwards.

One assessor asked whether she could remain without being given the place she wanted. She understood the question and answered too quickly.

“Yes, if you tell me what I’m waiting for.”

The man who had finished eating spoke to Ghere about the hurry of human households. Riarhin had not raised her voice. She had kept her hands still. She looked at them now, lying open beside the practice blade.

“I asked you how long last season,” the youngest said.

The man answered him by name. Their argument changed language halfway through. Ghere began a reply, then stopped when Riarhin moved.

She wanted water. She stayed where she was.

Another assessor proposed that she work with the people maintaining the paths. She had done such work already and liked some of it. Offered now, it felt like being directed towards a door they could close behind her.

“Would that change your answer?” she asked.

“It would change what we knew.”

“Of me?”

“Of you there.”

She wanted a sentence she could carry out of the room whole. They gave her qualifications whose meanings belonged to places and people she had not been permitted to stand among. Her memory kept every word. That evening she would repeat them to herself until the syntax seemed cruelly simple.

The youngest assessor went outside and returned with the broken binding from the load she had carried. Riarhin recognized the object before she understood why he had brought it. One of its supports had a small mark near the end, a darkened place where the wood had once been scorched. She had turned that end away from her hand because she could feel the grain lifting there.

“Would you use this again?” he asked.

“For the same thing?”

He nodded.

“If it holds.”

“How would you know?”

She took it from him. The load had been light enough for the sound wood to bear it even if the damaged part split. She showed him where she would place the strap, taking the pressure farther from the weakness. He looked at Ghere and spoke too quickly for her to follow.

“Tell me,” she said.

The young man began again. There were people for whom the object would always mean its earlier use. He named a place. Riarhin knew the word for it, the shallow holding of water by the western path, but he appeared to mean both the place and something that had happened there. She asked when. He answered by naming a person whose name she had never heard.

“I wasn’t there.”

“No.”

“Then how am I to know?”

The woman with the muddy knee came closer. She took the binding and laid it on the bench between them.

“You put it where he would have to take it.”

“Yes. To let him pass.”

“What if he would not touch it?”

Riarhin looked at the young man. He was holding the damaged end now. She was being asked to account for someone who was absent, whose reasons had not been supplied. She had fought beside men who would not take another man’s knife and a woman who would not cross running water after dark. She could accommodate a refusal if somebody bothered to make it.

“He could tell me.”

“He might think you knew.”

“Why?”

The woman’s gaze went to Ghere. Riarhin heard the breath leave her own nose, louder than she intended.

“I’m here.”

The woman turned back. “You carried it as he would.”

Riarhin looked down at her hands.

The phrase had come at her from several directions over the years. People had mistaken her for someone they had once known when she entered a doorway, then corrected themselves as soon as she spoke. She had taken pleasure in doing a thing so well that she could be mistaken for a person who belonged.

“Show me how I should carry it.”

“There is no—”

“Then ask him to tell me when he doesn’t want it.”

The man who had been eating gave a short laugh. Riarhin looked at him sharply, but he was looking at the woman. He said something about having asked that himself. The woman answered without turning. There was familiarity in her irritation, an argument whose beginnings Riarhin would not be permitted to hear.

For a few moments they ceased discussing her and discussed the absent person. Riarhin stood by the bench, the support still in her hands. She could have set it down. Instead she held it until the pressure of the wood became unpleasant.

Ghere came beside her.

“Let them finish.”

“They’ve been finishing all morning.”

He glanced at her hands. She put the wood down.

The youngest assessor asked whether she would accept the path work if he did it with her. He spoke more slowly this time. There was a house on the western route whose occupants used a different crossing when the leaves fell. They had been inconvenienced by a repair because the people who made it had remembered the wet season and forgotten what the bridge meant when dry. He wanted her to help put it right.

“I could do that,” she said.

“Yes.”

“How long?”

“We would have to see it.”

“After that.”

He rubbed at the dark patch his wet hair had left on his shirt. She liked him suddenly, and wished she did not; she could see that he was trying to offer something he thought she would want.

“After that there would be other work.”

“And the place?”

He did not pretend not to understand.

“I cannot give you that.”

Riarhin turned towards the openings through which she had carried the load. Beyond them a woman was washing a child’s feet in a shallow bowl. The child kept lifting one foot to look at it, dripping water onto the clean cloth. The woman held the ankle and washed around the small, interfering hands.

“I would like to be asked for something I can finish,” Riarhin said.

Ghere moved beside her. She could not tell whether he meant to comfort her or keep her from speaking again. She stepped away before finding out.

Ghere asked for a private word. She said she would stay. He looked at her, and for once she could see that he was tired rather than inscrutable.

“Please,” he said in the language they had first used together.

She went outside.

Through the open window she heard her own name, a pause, and his voice continuing. He did not sound like someone making excuses for a troublesome pupil. He sounded like her brother asking people he respected to look again.

When he came out she almost embraced him. She was already preparing the apology when she saw that he had no new answer.

Outside he proposed more time. Another teacher. Work she could do while the question remained open.

“They have decided.”

“They have decided this.”

Riarhin looked towards the room. Through the window she could see one of the assessors putting away the objects used in the trial. He carried them past the window without looking out.

“What did you tell them?”

“What I have seen.”

“All of it?”

“Yes.”

“You told them about the boy’s shoulder.”

“Yes.”

She took a step back from him. He let the distance open.

“And the wagon?”

“Yes.”

For a moment she did not know whether he had defended her or betrayed her. He had done what she had so often demanded: spoken accurately. She wanted accuracy to have produced the answer she needed.

“And you want me to wait until they mean something else by it.”

He reached for her shoulder. She moved away.

“I tried,” he said.

“I know.”

Knowing had not helped. She packed that night, too angry to be careful and too proud to ask what she should take.

The room had acquired her belongings so slowly that she had ceased to see how much was hers. A spare binding hung from a peg. There were two bowls now, one chipped and one sound. A coat she had meant to mend lay beneath the sleeping cloth. She took the coat, put it down, then took it again because leaving something useful would have made the departure childish even by her own account.

Ghere came while she was rolling the bedding.

“You can leave that.”

“I know.”

“It will be here.”

She tightened the strap too hard. The buckle bit her finger. She could feel him looking at the bundle, counting the things she had chosen and the things she had forgotten. She wanted him to tell her she could not go. She had prepared so much anger for that refusal that his silence left her shaking.

“I’m not asking you to follow,” she said.

“I know.”

“Or explain me to them.”

He sat on the ledge by the door. It forced her to pass close when she fetched the boots, though he had left enough room. For a little while she could not look at him.

“Did you think I would fail?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then why—”

He lifted his hand. She stopped, furious at herself for still obeying the gesture.

“I did not know what they would ask.”

“You knew them.”

“Yes.”

“You knew me.”

He looked down at his hand and let it fall. “I thought so.”

She had been ready for certainty, for one more account of what she needed to learn. His doubt frightened her. She took the boots outside to clean the soles and stayed there until he had gone.

Near dawn she woke to rain on the leaves. She had slept in the clothes she meant to travel in. The room smelled of damp leather and the oil used on the practice blades. Her anger was still there, but it no longer filled every part of her. She could imagine going to the court, putting the bundle down, asking for another teacher as Ghere had suggested. She could imagine waiting a season, then another. She could not imagine asking without hearing herself beg.

She ate the food someone had left by the door. When she lifted the cloth beneath it, she found a fresh binding for the grip of a blade, cut to the width her hand required. Ghere had made it. She knew from the slightly slanted end where he had finished the cut.

She put it in the bundle.

In the morning he stood at the path. His hair was wet. He had not put on a travelling coat. She saw that before anything else and felt relief so sharp it nearly became an insult.

He asked whether she had water.

“Yes.”

She wanted him to say her name. He wanted, she thought, something she could not offer while he stood watching her leave. She went past him. He did not call her back.

Beyond the first turn she stopped where he could no longer see and leaned against a tree. The bark pressed through her coat. She stayed there until she could breathe without making a sound, then went on.

At the baths, Coryd came outside.

“Harissa takes the first watch. I take the next. Linias doesn’t want to be alone with her.”

Riarhin began to explain what she thought Zemia had believed.

Coryd waited until she stopped.

“I heard her,” she said.

Riarhin moved the branch away from the wall. It caught in another and stayed there.

Chapter 30 — The assistant

Before they went to Eshai, Zemia spoke to the people from the lower houses.

Irset brought them to the court beside the washing trough. It was small enough that nobody could stand far from what was said. The woman who had lent Harissa clothes sat with a basket on her lap. Hevrin stood behind her. Two men Riarhin knew only by the work they did remained near the gate. Nobody had brought the children.

Zemia began with an apology. Irset told her to begin with the shed.

She did. She described the changed meeting place and the message left beside the spring. She said she had asked Farieth to wait. The woman with the basket interrupted before she could explain why she thought the request would be heard.

“Did she wait?”

“No.”

“Had she waited before?”

Zemia hesitated. Riarhin saw her looking for the part she could answer without beginning the whole history again.

“Not at the loft.”

“Then you knew.”

“I thought—”

The woman lifted her hand. Zemia stopped.

One of the men by the gate asked whether anyone outside knew the upper paths. Zemia told him which turns she had described and which she had not. He asked her to show him. She stepped towards the gate; Coryd went beside her. They walked as far as the first bend and returned. The man’s face had changed. He began speaking to Hevrin in a language Riarhin understood only in pieces, but the gesture towards the lower gate was plain.

They would have to keep it watched.

“I can help,” Zemia said.

No one answered her. The woman with the basket took a small packet from it and placed it on the wall. Thread, bought at the amphitheater by someone who had remembered what colour she needed. Zemia looked at it and did not reach to take it back.

The packet lay on the wall. A damp patch crept towards it from the washing trough.

The woman went inside. Hevrin told Zemia that if she wanted to help, she should answer when asked and stay where people could find her. He gave Riarhin a look that included her in the instruction.

They waited while the men decided what could be moved away from the lower entrance. Coryd offered to carry a frame. One accepted her help. Harissa took the basket the woman had left and brought it to the doorway, careful not to go in without being invited.

Zemia remained beside the trough. The little packet lay on the wall between her and Riarhin. Water passed through a channel at their feet, making the same sound it had made before any of them arrived.

“May I leave it?” Zemia asked.

“The thread?”

“She may still need it.”

Riarhin looked towards the closed door. “Ask Irset where to put it.”

Zemia did. Irset took it without promising who would use it. Then she told them Eshai was waiting and that the people in the court had work to do.

On the way up, Harissa’s borrowed shoe caught against a stone. Zemia bent automatically to help with the loose fastening. Harissa drew her foot back. For a moment both stayed in that position, Zemia bent, Harissa leaning against the wall with her knee raised. Coryd went past them to fetch the stick Harissa had left at the turn. Riarhin waited without offering to mend the moment.

The keeper would not bring the old papers to them. They had to come to the room where they were kept, wash, and dry their hands fully.

Zemia came between Coryd and Harissa.

The papers were thin sheets that bent like leaf and resisted tearing. Some marks had faded. Others remained so dark they seemed newly made.

Farieth’s hand appeared in the later additions.

Zemia leaned forward and then stopped herself. The keeper moved the page closer so she would not have to ask.

“You recognize it.”

“Yes.”

“Where from?”

“Letters. Corrections.”

The keeper brought out a letter Zemia had written at the baths. The influence was plain when the two hands lay beside one another: the end of a stroke lifted clear, a narrowing of the space before a particular character. Zemia saw it too. She pulled her own letter away and laid it face down.

Riarhin waited for the keeper to object. Instead the woman moved the old paper to the centre of the table and placed a smooth weight on each corner.

“I kept this because she asked for the room to be altered.”

“Did you alter it?” Coryd asked.

“We let her use it as it was. There were objections to cutting through the back.”

“Whose?”

“People who lived below it.”

“What did they know of the work?”

“What she told them.”

The keeper’s mouth tightened. She took a strip of cloth from between two pages and set it aside. Its scent rose sharply enough to make Linias turn his head.

“This is before she went farther in. These are preparations. I did not go with her to Elfreach.”

Riarhin looked at the word on the sheet. She had heard it used for a place that could be reached, for a place that had withdrawn, for a place a speaker did not wish another person to reach. The line beneath it was in Farieth’s hand.

“What came back?”

“A packet. Then she came herself.”

“Changed?”

“She would not sit.”

The keeper said it with a bitterness Riarhin did not understand until she looked at the chair beside the window. There was a cushion worn thin along one edge. It was easy to imagine Farieth there before she became a person whose arrival made other people seek exits.

“She had always stayed to talk,” the woman said. “Even when I did not want her to.”

Riarhin could read the syntax. The keeper helped with the references: a room whose name had changed, an opening identified by what stood beside it before the wall was altered, a word that meant attending and being exposed in the same construction.

The assistant’s place was inside the narrow room.

Farieth’s place was beyond a screen that could be closed from her side.

“Was that added afterwards?” Riarhin asked.

The keeper showed her where an earlier line continued beneath the note. Farieth had referred back to the arrangement when describing the later expedition. Beneath the reference a shorter hand had corrected a measure and drawn a mark connecting two passages.

“The assistant?”

“That is how the writer is identified here.”

“Did you meet them?”

The keeper shook her head.

“Then how can you know which came first?”

She turned another sheet. A request to use the outer chamber referred to repairs the roof-worker’s father had completed before the expedition. Beside it the refusal to cut the wall remained in a different hand. Riarhin followed the sequence twice. There were gaps, but the protection test preceded the interrupted account from farther in.

Coryd asked to see the room.

The keeper collected her stick. She took them along a gallery whose windows had been closed with woven screens, then stopped at a short flight of steps above the baths’ occupied level. Riarhin had imagined descending into the deep. Instead they climbed, passing a woman taking washing off a line.

The woman looked at Zemia, then at Coryd beside her.

“Stay out of the open drain.”

They promised. Nobody asked what the old room had once contained.

It stood behind a wall of flaking pale plaster. The doorway was narrower than the opening that had preceded it; on either side, pieces of an older lintel remained. A figure with robes folded over one shoulder had been cut in half by the new work. Somebody had scratched a small animal onto the figure’s surviving knee.

The keeper lifted a latch and entered first. Dust lay over the inner floor. One corner had been swept recently, leaving a broom mark that ended abruptly at a line in the stone.

“This chamber held the preliminary trials,” she said. “It is not the room in the last account.”

Riarhin nodded, impatient with the distinction until she saw Zemia attending to it.

“They chose this arrangement again?” Zemia asked.

“It is the one she referred to.”

“So she had tried it before.”

The keeper left her to look. The screen’s fittings were old. A second exit stood behind the observer’s position. There was no matching exit inside.

“She knew something might happen,” Harissa said.

“She made provision for it,” the keeper answered.

“For herself.”

Riarhin stepped into the inner position. The observer’s screen hid the shoulders but left the face visible. She could imagine a person there receiving reassurance through an opening too narrow for help to reach them. The floor sloped subtly towards the deeper wall. There were no restraints she could recognize, which told her almost nothing about what could have held a person in place.

Coryd examined the second exit. It opened readily from the observer’s side. From the inner room its fastening could not be reached.

“Could that be for keeping something out?” she asked.

“Yes,” the keeper said.

“Or keeping someone in.”

“Yes.”

Linias took Riarhin’s place while she examined the observer’s opening. He looked unexpectedly small beyond it. A raised strip on the inner floor would keep his feet near the same position even if he moved, and the light fell where it would show his face.

“Could you hear me?” he asked.

“Yes.”

He lowered his voice. Riarhin still heard him clearly. The room seemed to bring it towards her.

“Could they have spoken for themselves?” Coryd asked.

“They could speak,” Riarhin said.

Coryd looked at her, acknowledging the limit. She tried the screen from the observer’s side. It slid easily through the first part of its travel, then caught where age had thickened the lower edge.

“Don’t shut it.” Linias’s voice had changed.

Coryd stopped. She had moved it scarcely the width of a hand.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Don’t.”

She brought it back. Linias came out at once and went to the ordinary window, pressing two fingers against the sill. Harissa followed him but stayed a little way off.

Riarhin found a worn hollow beside the observer’s opening. It had been made by something turned repeatedly while held in place. Beside it three marks remained, one scraped out and cut again. She could not say what had been altered.

Zemia stayed by the entrance. Her eyes went from the inner position to the screen and remained there.

“Did she warn them?” she asked.

The keeper did not pretend to know.

Harissa asked whether anyone had cleaned the room after Farieth left. The keeper said people had tried to use it for storage. Things kept there acquired a smell which did not come out. In the end they had cleared it.

“What smell?”

The woman hesitated.

“Like an animal you cannot find.”

Riarhin breathed through her nose. She could smell dust, plaster, the rain in the open drain beyond the wall. Nothing she could use.

Zemia went to the inner doorway and put one foot over the threshold. Coryd moved beside her before Riarhin could speak.

“I’m only looking.”

“Then look from here.”

Zemia withdrew her foot. Linias left the window and went out. Harissa followed; their footsteps sounded much louder in the passage than Riarhin expected.

On the way back, the woman with the washing asked whether they had found what they wanted. Nobody answered quickly enough. She lifted the basket against her hip and looked at the keeper.

“Put the catch down properly. The children push at it.”

The keeper went back to check. Riarhin stayed with her, watching while she tried the closed door with both hands.

“You knew her,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

“Did you like her?”

The woman let go of the latch.

“When she came she would ask what I had found. Other people asked whether it would be useful. She asked what it was.”

Riarhin thought of Zemia’s account and wished the resemblance had been less exact. The keeper saw her expression.

“I do know the difference between a kindness and what she did in that room.”

Riarhin took the stick the woman had left against the wall and gave it back to her. They followed the others.

They returned to the papers. An interrupted account described the reaction, the loss of two boundaries, and a descent to the remaining vat. It did not say what became of the person in the room.

One section had been written across material prepared for something else. The old lines beneath it showed through in oblique light. The keeper held it near the window and turned it until Riarhin could separate the two hands.

The later hand stopped halfway through a sequence. Below that Farieth’s writing changed in pressure. She had gone over one sign so hard that the point tore the surface. Riarhin could see where the writer had put a finger on the fresh mark and dragged ink towards the edge.

“Is this where she says the seed answered?”

“Reacted,” the keeper said. “Perhaps answered. I would not choose one for you.”

“And this?”

“The boundary failed. Two of them, if these refer to the same measures.”

Harissa held the edge of the table. Zemia was no longer looking at the pages. She watched the keeper’s mouth as each word was supplied.

At the end, Farieth had named the remaining vat and written beneath it a direction for those who might come after. The keeper translated part of that direction, then refused to guess at the rest. Riarhin remembered finding tracks that disappeared in burned ground: the same irritation, the same wish to make an interruption reveal what it had destroyed.

“Was it done to save them?” Linias asked.

The keeper lowered the sheet.

“It says she must enter.”

“To save herself?”

“It says she must enter.”

He nodded sharply, accepting no comfort from the repetition. Riarhin waited until the woman put the sheet down before asking to see the earlier preparation again.

Near the beginning the assistant had written a correction to an ordinary quantity. Beside it Farieth had added a small expression the keeper would not translate neatly.

“It can be used for a child of your child,” she said. “Or for someone you are teaching through another.”

Zemia stared at the mark beneath it.

“Do you know it?” Riarhin asked.

Her mouth moved.

Then she pressed her lips together and shook her head.

“I could say something,” she managed. “I don’t know what it would be.”

“Then don’t.”

Coryd pulled out the chair beside her. Zemia sat.

The keeper covered the page before bringing the next.

This one concerned preparations made before the reaction. Riarhin asked the keeper to go slowly. A protection was to be tested on the observer’s side. The assistant was to remain exposed so that the result could be distinguished from the effect of that protection. There were terms whose exact meanings neither woman would claim to know. The relation of the two positions was clear.

Harissa leaned over the table until her borrowed sleeve nearly touched the page. The keeper moved a weight between cloth and writing.

“She knew enough to keep it off herself.”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

Zemia looked at her.

For the first time since the disclosure, she seemed to want Riarhin to say less rather than explain her better.

“Before?” Zemia asked.

“Before.”

A drop of water fell from somewhere in the roof. The keeper moved the page without looking up and set a shallow dish where it had been. She had evidently been doing this for years.

Linias put his hand flat on the table, well away from the paper.

“What would happen if we went there?”

“To Elfreach?” the keeper asked.

“If being near it is enough.”

Riarhin looked at Zemia. She sat with her mouth closed, one thumb pressed against the other. No one had asked her to keep quiet. Since the mark on the page, she had been watching each person begin a sentence as though they might demonstrate how it could be done safely.

“The paper describes a trial,” Eshai said. “Someone did something.”

“Someone might do something by accident.”

“Yes.”

Linias lifted his hand from the table. “The road moved when Coryd and I were carrying that slab.”

The keeper looked towards Coryd, who described what they had done. She repeated the wardens’ account as closely as she could, stopping where she had not understood it. Eshai asked about the edge of the slab. Coryd showed the measure with her hands.

“That part has responded before,” the keeper said.

“To people like us?”

“To weight. To movement. Once to a support being taken out. I wasn’t there when you crossed it.”

Riarhin remembered the light running ahead of Coryd’s feet, the two bodies adjusting their pace to each other without speaking. She had admired them before she became afraid. She could remember the exact sequence. Repeating it would be easy.

She pushed her chair farther from the table.

“If we could find the part that responds,” Linias said, “couldn’t someone make it stop? The way you cover a—”

“Don’t finish that with something you know how to mend,” Harissa said.
