He looked at her, hurt. She laid a hand over his on the edge of the table. He allowed it there.

Riarhin could imagine the journey he was offering them. Farther in, through places their grandmother knew, to find a thing that might end the hunt. She had come to the Valewood because it offered ground on which she could fight. Linias was looking for somewhere he would never have to.

“What would you need to know?” she asked the keeper.

Eshai rose slowly and went to a chest beneath the window. She took out a narrow frame, wrapped in several layers of cloth, and set it on the cleared end of the table. A piece of blue material lay inside, cloudy at one edge, held by little claws of pale metal. Riarhin kept her hands beside her own cup.

“This came out of a wall they were repairing,” the keeper said. “Before my time. It was wrapped when I received it. Those instructions are on the outer cloth.”

“Is it safe?” Coryd asked.

“Here, like this. As far as those who kept it could establish.”

“And if you take it out?”

“I haven’t.”

She folded back one layer. Writing covered the lining, close and dark, interrupted by a patch sewn over an older tear.

“Read that,” she told Riarhin.

Riarhin leaned nearer. She could make out the directions for setting it down and the account of the person who had carried it. After that the same signs returned in a different order. She had read each of them elsewhere. Here they could mean that the carrier had been changed, or that the person measuring the change had stood in the wrong relation to the object.

“The second person,” she began.

“Which second?”

Riarhin went back to the start. The cloth had been patched where a name or position should have stood. Cut ends of strokes showed beneath the seam.

“I can’t tell.”

“Nor can I.”

Eshai covered it again. Nothing had stirred in the frame. No light had escaped it. Riarhin found herself watching with the embarrassed conviction that something ought to happen to justify the care they had all taken.

“The people who wrapped this had the rest,” Eshai said. “They thought they had made themselves plain. Perhaps they had, to those they expected to open it.”

Linias looked towards the chair by the window. “And Farieth knows more.”

“She has seen more.”

“Would you let her look at it?”

The keeper’s hands stopped on the final fold.

“She has.”

No one asked the next question for several breaths. A voice outside called for the woman taking in washing. She called back that she had heard the first time.

“What did she tell you?” Riarhin asked.

“To keep it wrapped.”

Eshai put the frame away. She closed the chest and held its lid a moment before letting go.

Riarhin wanted something in the room to become simple: Farieth lying about the danger, the account falsified, the object harmless if only somebody dared to test it. The thing beneath the cloth had not obliged her. She looked at the old account of the person left inside the screen.

“We aren’t going farther in,” she said.

Linias’s shoulders sank. He had asked about ending the hunt and been given another place he ought not to go.

“Then what?”

“I have to stop her following us.”

“You tried.”

“Yes.”

“And she keeps coming.”

Riarhin reached for her cup and discovered she had already emptied it. She set it down.

“She’s hurt. She knows we can hurt her. She’ll look for a way that keeps me out of reach while she gets to you.”

Harissa took her hand from Linias’s and sat back. “Then she’ll be looking for somewhere you can’t get between.”

“Yes.”

For a little while they looked at the drawing of the screen, each seeing a different place in which they might be made to stand. Coryd brought the blank paper closer. Riarhin took the pen.

They asked permission to copy the passages. She agreed on the condition that uncertain words remain marked as uncertain and that the copies go to more than one place. Riarhin accepted both conditions. She did not know whether the account would persuade anybody who already wanted to believe Farieth. It would at least exist where Farieth had not chosen the reader.

On the way out Zemia stopped beside the inner room again. Linias waited in the passage. Neither asked the other to move.

Chapter 31 — The ground they may use

Their hostess refused before Riarhin finished asking.

“No fighting here.”

“I know.”

“You brought it to the shed.”

“Yes.”

“People sleep above that water. My child sleeps above it.”

Riarhin laid out the route to the disused approach. She described the separate outer stair, the shutter, the service way. The hostess brought the roof-worker and the pathweaver. They argued over where the old foundations joined the occupied channels.

Riarhin waited.

The roof-worker drew the foundations on a board with a charred stick. He corrected her account of the outer stair: the last turn was steeper than she remembered, but the missing steps could be crossed by someone with sufficient balance. Farieth would have that balance even hurt, if she had time.

“Then it buys us time,” Riarhin said.

“You have been buying time since you came.”

“Yes.”

Their hostess folded her arms. The accusation had not come from her, but she did not contradict it.

Riarhin described what she meant to do before the shutter closed: injure, disarm, force separation. The words sounded uglier spoken over a table while a child played in the next room. She kept them plain.

Coryd added the family’s part. Harissa objected to one placement because it required her to spring twice from the bad leg. Linias asked where he could go if he lost his nerve. Riarhin answered without telling him he would not.

At last they agreed on the chamber beyond the grove. Nothing beneath it supplied the baths. The main arch was to remain untouched. If the family damaged the service way, they would help restore it after they could safely return.

“You may recover here,” the hostess said. “You may not bring her through us again.”

They inspected the chamber together.

Mavren arrived while they were there. Eshai had sent word, and she came carrying the same case Riarhin remembered from the observatory. She looked at the blocked shortcut, the stools, the marks they had placed for the crossing. She said nothing until Coryd asked whether the shutter would hold.

“How far down?”

Coryd showed her. Mavren put a strip of wood into the channel, pushed until it stopped, then drew it out. A wet stain marked one end.

“Clear that.”

Hevrin went for a scoop. Riarhin followed Mavren along the wall. The woman touched no carving and looked only briefly at the stone tree outside. When she reached the main arch, she stopped far enough back that her shadow did not enter it.

“What opens that?” Riarhin asked.

“Things I won’t do for you.”

“I wasn’t asking you to.”

“People begin by asking what.”

Riarhin let her inspect in silence. Behind them Hevrin worked mud out of the channel. Coryd took the scoop from him when the reach became awkward. She had filled half a bucket before Mavren returned.

“Try it empty again.”

They did. The shutter descended farther. Its bottom edge met the floor with a sound Riarhin felt through her soles. Mavren nodded, but when Linias looked relieved she made them raise it and try again.

The second time a pebble caught under one corner.

Coryd swore.

“It came out of the wall,” Hevrin said. He bent to show them the place where the stone was flaking.

“Then look at the floor when you do it,” Mavren said. “I can’t make old stone new this afternoon.”

Riarhin moved Linias’s standing place so he could see the lower edge. It gave him one more thing to attend to while frightened.

“What do you want for coming?” she asked Mavren.

“I told you.”

“To know what happens.”

“All of it. Even the part you think has nothing to do with the way out.”

Riarhin agreed. The woman closed her case.

“I’ll wait by the turn with the people from the baths. If you open the arch, I leave.”

“We won’t.”

“Tell all of them.”

Riarhin did. Harissa asked what would make them want to open it. No one had an answer that pleased her. Mavren waited until she had asked twice, then said there was often an easier-looking way out and she had seen people take it.

Harissa looked down the narrow service passage. “We’ll use the ugly one.”

The bronze arm had to be held down while a stone catch slid beneath its socket. If the arm was released too soon, the shutter rose a hand’s breadth and could be forced. Once seated, the catch could be drawn from the outer side only by reaching through an opening too narrow for a shoulder.

Farieth could leave by the separate outer stair. She could not follow into the family’s service way without returning around the whole structure.

Riarhin walked that circuit with Coryd and timed it by their ordinary pace, then by running. At the narrowest turn Coryd stopped to catch her breath and looked down into the hollow beneath the path.

“She could go there.”

Riarhin crouched beside her. From the chamber it looked like a steep drop choked with trees. From here she could see the low shelves between roots. A person with Farieth’s balance could use them, reach the far support, and climb back to the outer opening before anyone running the whole circuit returned.

“Yes.”

“Can we block it?”

Riarhin considered the weight of the stones beside them. They could roll one into the path. Farieth could step over it. A cord across the gap might catch somebody who had not seen them working. Farieth had time to see.

“Let me try it.”

She passed the wrapped sword to Coryd and went down backwards. The first shelf took her boot. The second had a mat of roots over a hollow; she tested it with her weight through both hands before letting go. Once she was beneath the canopy, she could see a third route, less direct but safer than either of the obvious ones.

Riarhin climbed back by that way. Coryd had moved to meet her without being asked.

“There’s another.”

“I saw your shoulder disappear.”

“You’d have lost sight of her too.”

Coryd looked at the slope, displeased. Riarhin stood beside her until they both stopped searching for something the ground did not offer.

“We watch it,” Coryd said at last.

“From where?”

They tried three places. The first left the watcher visible from the outer stair. The second gave no warning before someone reached the support. At the third, Riarhin could see the last root a climber must touch and the place where their head would appear. She put Coryd there, went down again, and came up as quietly as she could.

Coryd saw her. She said where the movement had first betrayed her. Riarhin tried the other route. Again Coryd saw enough.

“It doesn’t stop her,” Coryd said.

“No.”

“But we’ll know.”

“If you keep looking.”

Coryd gave her a dry look. Riarhin accepted it and took back the sword.

They found a narrower shortcut on the other side of the chamber, where an old timber brace spanned a gap above the fallen stairs. The wood looked sound from below. Riarhin remembered the grainless underside of another root and climbed where she could inspect this one without trusting it. The brace was held by a single fastening at its far end. A climber could take the load through the wall and use it to turn, but only if it remained in place.

Hevrin helped them take it down. He objected when Riarhin proposed leaving it across the gap to break beneath Farieth. Someone else might use it after they were gone. Riarhin looked at the worn places where feet had touched and agreed. They lowered the brace into the hollow with a rope. It struck the ground hard enough to split. The sound travelled through the chamber and came back from somewhere farther in.

Mavren lifted her head.

They waited. No answering movement came. She returned to the shutter and began working faster.

With the brace gone, the second shortcut required a jump from wet stone onto a sloping root. Farieth might still take it. She would have to put the injured thigh beneath her on landing. Riarhin showed Coryd the place and asked her to tell the others that it remained a way in.

Back in the chamber, Harissa had been experimenting with the short crossing without jumping it. She put her good foot on the support, reached for the opposite edge, and leaned until the cloth of her skirt drew tight over her knee.

“I can touch it.”

“Can you take your weight there?” Riarhin asked.

“Let me.”

Riarhin remained below her without reaching up. Harissa shifted, tested the grip, and let her bad leg follow without pushing from it. She arrived on the other side breathing hard and triumphant.

“That once,” she said.

Riarhin tried it after her. It changed the place from which she could cover the crossing. She moved the water, put the little bundle of cloth farther back, and asked Harissa to show Linias where her hand would appear. He held his palm against the wall to remember the height.

Harissa tested the distance between the supports. Her leg objected on the third crossing.

“I can do it once,” she said.

“Then we plan for once.”

Riarhin had packed a plain knife bought at the port to replace the one she had left in the mountain. She drew it, tried its weight, and put it back. It would not survive the uses to which she had put the old knives. She would have to remember that.

Chapter 32 — The invitation she can believe

Zemia wrote the message where everyone could see it.

Her first version asked for mercy. Harissa tore it in half.

“She’ll know.”

Zemia looked at Riarhin.

“Write where we’ll be,” Riarhin said. “Tell her what she can discover by looking. Don’t tell her we’ve become fools.”

The second version named the approach and the evening. It said they would not go farther before speaking with her. That was true. It did not promise to open the main arch or leave anyone alone.

Zemia read it aloud. Her voice was steady in the way a singer’s could be when nothing behind it was steady. Riarhin watched the pen between her fingers. There was ink on the side of the first finger, where Farieth’s way of holding it left a mark. Zemia turned the pen and rubbed at the stain with her thumb.

“Does it need my name?”

“Will she know the hand?”

“Yes.”

“Then no.”

Zemia put the pen down. No one reached for the paper until the shine had gone from the ink.

Coryd carried it to the drop with Zemia beside her. Riarhin watched them leave from the upper gallery. Coryd kept enough distance for Zemia to walk without feeling a hand at her back. At the first turn she let Zemia go ahead, then followed where she could see both hands.

While they were gone, Harissa asked what they would do if Farieth refused to come.

“Leave,” Riarhin said.

“Which way?”

“The pathweaver will take us back as far as the ridge.”

“And then?”

“We keep going.”

Harissa sat down on the low wall. She had been standing too long, refusing the stool because no one else had taken one.

“I could still go back to the amphitheater.”

“Yes.”

“Today.”

“Yes.”

Linias looked up. Harissa held Riarhin’s gaze, testing whether the permission would survive being used. Riarhin felt the plan coming apart in her hands. The short crossing would need somebody else. She could manage without it, perhaps, though the margin would be smaller. She did not tell Harissa that first.

“The pathweaver’s below,” she said. “Ask what passage she can arrange.”

Harissa looked away. “You make it sound as if I’ve asked about a cart.”

“I don’t want you staying because I make leaving difficult.”

“You’ve already made it difficult. You all have.”

She rose and went down the stairs. Linias began to follow. Riarhin touched his sleeve, and he stopped with a resentment she could hardly blame him for.

“She shouldn’t go alone.”

“She’s going to speak to someone in the court.”

“She could keep going.”

“Yes.”

He pulled the sleeve free and sat. After a moment he took out a piece of string and began working a knot out of it. His hands moved much faster than the task required. Riarhin stood by the gallery opening, watching the same patch of path until Coryd and Zemia returned.

There had been no one at the drop. Coryd had put the paper where Zemia showed her and taken nothing away. Zemia asked where Harissa was. Linias did not answer. Riarhin told her.

“She won’t go,” Zemia said.

“Don’t decide that for her.”

Zemia looked down.

They ate a little. Harissa came back while Irset was taking the bowl away. She asked whether there was any left, accepted what Riarhin had not eaten, and sat on the wall again.

“The path is open,” she said.

No one helped her continue.

“She could take me tomorrow. There are people travelling.”

“Good,” Riarhin said.

Harissa gave her a look. “Stop being so agreeable.”

Riarhin could not help smiling. Harissa saw the smile and almost returned it. Then she pressed both hands against her knees and looked at the floor.

“I don’t want to be sitting somewhere waiting to hear which of you got killed.”

“That could happen here too.”

“I know. I do understand a few things without having them made unpleasantly clear.”

She ate another mouthful. Riarhin let her. When the bowl was empty, Harissa asked whether they had tried the shutter again. Coryd answered. The decision remained in those questions; she made no announcement of loyalty for them to admire.

They went back to the chamber before the afternoon light failed.

Linias refused the first role Riarhin gave him.

“If she comes through that opening, I’ll move. I know I will.”

“You only have to hold until—”

“I’ll move.”

Riarhin looked at the opening. She had been measuring it with her own body again, judging the time from what she knew she could bear. Linias stood beside it with his shoulders raised. He could make every movement she asked of him. He could not make himself believe he would remain while Farieth approached.

“Show me where you’d go.”

He pointed to the service passage.

“From here?”

“Yes.”

“Go.”

He crossed in three quick steps and turned in the entrance, already looking to see whom he might have left behind. From there he could see the approach and the bottom edge of the shutter. Riarhin came to stand beside him. The wall protected his left side. It would leave him somewhere to move without running across her line.

“Stay here, then.”

He nodded. The relief embarrassed him enough that he began asking about the floor. She let him examine it instead of telling him she understood.

Harissa took the short crossing. Coryd would bring the catch into place. Zemia would hold the arm, watched by Linias until the shutter descended.

Zemia asked what would happen if Farieth spoke to her.

“She probably will,” Riarhin said.

“What should I say?”

“I can’t tell you that.”

Zemia put her hands around the bronze arm without pressing it. She examined the worn surface beneath her palms.

“She’ll tell me to come away.”

Riarhin waited.

“She’ll make it sound as if everyone else is keeping me here.”

“Will that be true?”

Zemia looked towards Coryd.

“For the message. For going out.”

“Yes.”

“I could go to her.”

Linias made a sharp movement in the passage. Riarhin held up a hand without looking back.

“You could.”

“And she might leave the rest of you.”

“She might tell you that.”

Zemia’s fingers tightened. “I know what you think of me.”

“No.”

“I know enough.”

The bronze creaked as she put weight on it. Riarhin told her to let go. She did, and the sound of it returning to the stop filled the chamber.

“I’m not sending you to her,” Riarhin said. “I’m not offering you for any of us. If you mean to leave, tell us now.”

Zemia shook her head. Then she began to shake it harder, as if the first movement had been misunderstood. Her eyes filled. She turned away from the others and pressed her face into the crook of her arm.

Riarhin stood beside her without touching her. She heard Harissa shift her weight. Linias looked down the service passage and kept looking there. Cold reached through the soles of Riarhin’s boots.

At last Zemia wiped her face on her sleeve.

“I want to stay.”

“Then stay.”

Coryd brought the water. Zemia drank from it, handed it back, and asked Riarhin to show her the signal once more. This time the signal was different from the ones she had described to Farieth. Riarhin did not explain why.

They rehearsed without weapons. The first attempt failed because Coryd reached the catch before Zemia had lowered the arm far enough. Coryd’s fingers nearly went beneath its lip. She snatched them back, furious with herself, and examined the scrape along one knuckle.

“Slower,” Riarhin said.

“She won’t be slower.”

“We will be, until we know it.”

The second failed because Linias saw Riarhin move and mistook it for the signal. The shutter descended while she was still in its path. Zemia hauled it back with a startled cry. Riarhin stepped clear before the edge reached her shoulder.

Linias put both hands over his mouth.

“Again,” she said.

“I saw you—”

“I know. Watch my hand.”

He nodded. On the third attempt he waited. Harissa completed the crossing and sat down at once, unable to hide the pain. Riarhin stopped the practice.

“One more,” Harissa said.

“No.”

“I can do it.”

“I need you able to do it tomorrow.”

Harissa looked at the gap and then at her own foot, as though the two were conducting a disagreement without her. She nodded.

Zemia asked whether she should practise holding the arm longer. Riarhin had almost told her yes before noticing the white pressure marks across her palms.

“Enough.”

Zemia did not release it immediately.

“Enough,” Riarhin repeated, more quietly.

She let go. The arm rose, slowly. Everyone watched it settle against the stop.

Before they left, Coryd asked them to try the way out carrying a person.

Riarhin looked at Harissa’s leg. Harissa lifted one finger.

“Not me. I’d like to walk tomorrow.”

Linias sat on the floor instead, with an expression of exaggerated martyrdom that disappeared when Zemia bent to take his arm. He drew it away before she touched him. The movement was slight and ugly with familiarity; she had known where to put her hand without being shown.

“I’ll do that side,” Riarhin said.

Coryd took the other. They lifted him between them. At the first turn his heel caught, forcing Riarhin sideways against the wall. There would be no room to do this while looking back with a sword in her hand.

“Put me down,” Linias said.

“Wait.”

“You’re turning my knee.”

They lowered him. Riarhin stood with her shoulders pressed between cold surfaces and tried to imagine the same work done while someone screamed. She had carried wounded men through wider places and left blood on both walls. Here the passage narrowed again before the outer bend.

Coryd came back for the rolled cloth they had brought to keep their things off the damp. She passed it beneath Linias’s thighs and asked him to hold her shoulders. He objected that he could not do that if he were unconscious.

“Then we’ll try that next.”

He put his arms where she asked. Riarhin supported his legs. They moved him one turn farther before Coryd stopped, breathing hard.

“He’s too heavy for the whole way.”

“I’m sorry,” Linias said.

Harissa, watching from the chamber, laughed once. He smiled, then lost it when neither carrier joined them. Coryd set him on a ledge and looked down the passage.

“We’ll need people at the far turn.”

“Mavren said she’d be there.”

“To watch. With her case.”

Riarhin nodded. They had accepted help coming in and kept the way out full of people who had not yet agreed to what she was imagining. She went back to the chamber and knelt beside Harissa.

“If I go down, get out of the fight. Don’t come to pull me away from her.”

Harissa stared at her.

“She’ll use it,” Riarhin said. “She’ll wait for you to bend.”

“And how long should I wait?”

“I don’t know.”

“Until you’re dead?”

Linias had come to the entrance. Coryd stood behind him, holding the cloth. Zemia remained beside the arm, watching Riarhin without trying to help the conversation.

“Until she can’t reach you,” Riarhin said.

Harissa turned her face away. Her jaw moved. Riarhin waited, unable to make the request less cruel by improving the words.

“Then get up quickly,” Harissa said.

“I’ll try.”

“No. You’ll do it. I’ve had enough of being sensible.”

Riarhin put her forehead briefly against her sister’s. Harissa caught the back of her coat and held her there, hard enough that the seam pulled beneath Riarhin’s shoulder. Neither spoke. When Harissa released her, she wiped her own face with an angry sweep of the wrist.

“Ask your people to come closer,” she said. “The ones with the case.”

“I will.”

Riarhin went with Coryd to the outer turn. Hevrin was there, waiting to walk back with them. She showed him where they had got Linias and where they had stopped. He examined the width, then the uneven steps below it. He said they could bring a short carrying frame as far as the second bend. Riarhin asked who he meant by they.

“I’ll have to ask.”

“Ask them. Tell them about her.”

“They know about her.”

“Tell them she may still be close.”

Hevrin looked at the cloth in Coryd’s hands. He took one corner, folded it along its length, and tested how much would lie beneath a person’s weight. The work gave him time to consider.

“If we hear fighting at that bend, we go back.”

“Yes.”

“And if you call, we come only when we can see the passage.”

“Yes.”

He returned the cloth. “I’ll ask.”

On the way back Riarhin walked beside Linias. He had stopped making jokes about being carried. At a root across the path he offered her his hand, then withdrew it, embarrassed because she had no need of it.

She took it before it fell. They crossed together.

They returned to the baths for the night. Irset had laid out fresh dressings and told Riarhin where the nearest healer lived. She intended to send for him in the morning, before someone began bleeding. Riarhin paid for the messenger.

Coryd sat on the floor with the scraped knuckle in her mouth. Riarhin crouched beside her and offered a clean strip from the dressings. Coryd took it.

“You could have asked somebody else to do this,” she said.

“The catch?”

“All of it.”

“Who?”

“You tell me.”

Riarhin looked through the open door. Hevrin was taking his granddaughter’s little coat from a peg. Irset stood at the far end of the passage, arguing quietly with the man who would fetch the healer. They had given shelter, labour, a place to return if anyone could return. She could have asked them for more. She could still ask.

“I don’t think they’d agree.”

“Neither do I.” Coryd wrapped the finger, pulled the knot tight with her teeth, and examined it. “I wanted to hear you say it.”

Riarhin stayed beside her for a while. Their old ease did not return because they were preparing to risk their lives together. When Coryd got up, Riarhin moved out of her way.

At supper she sat beside Zemia. Zemia tried twice to begin a conversation and twice stopped. Riarhin passed her the bread when it came round.

Part V — The hunter on their ground

Chapter 33 — Waiting together

Linias played after supper because Harissa asked him to.

The repaired seam gave one note a rasp. He tried to avoid it and spoiled the tune. Harissa told him to stop fussing.

“It sounds like you.”

“It sounds broken.”

“So do you, when you sing high.”

He played the note deliberately. She laughed and put a hand over her mouth as if she had been caught doing something improper.

Zemia sat beyond the lamp. She had once supplied the words when Linias forgot a verse. Tonight she kept her eyes on the table.

Riarhin noticed that Coryd had moved the salt within her reach without looking at her.

When the child had been put to bed, their hostess came to take the empty bowls. Zemia rose to help and was told to remain seated. The woman looked at each of them in turn, as though making certain she would remember who had been at the table.

Riarhin followed her into the washing space.

“If we don’t come back by morning—”

“Don’t tell me to send people after you.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Good.”

Riarhin gave her the address of the workroom and the name of the merchant through whom letters could reach it. She had written a short account already. The hostess took it, read the direction, and put it on a shelf above the damp.

The hostess turned the sealed letter over.

“If you return, you will take this back,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I won’t keep it to remember you by.”

Riarhin nodded.

The woman put a thumb over the seal as though testing whether it was dry.

“Does the letter say what you intend?”

“Enough.”

“Does it say where your money is?”

“Yes.”

The woman nodded. She had clearly dealt with less considerate guests.

Back in the room, Linias was showing Coryd how to loosen the strings if the instrument had to be stored for a long time. Coryd listened without protesting that he would do it himself. Harissa had folded her clothes into the chest with unusual care. Zemia sat beside the lamp, hands empty.

Riarhin almost asked what she needed. The question would have been sincere, but it would also have given her something to do with her discomfort. She sat instead and let the room continue without her management.

After the meal they checked what they would carry. No one brought all their possessions. Harissa left her chest in the room with the warm wall and made the hostess promise not to let the child sit on it.

“She will sit on it,” the woman said.

“Then put a cloth down.”

They reached the chamber before the appointed time. At the turn Mavren waited with Hevrin and two people Riarhin had seen carrying bedding away from the lower baths. One held a bundle of clean cloth. The other had brought a stretcher made from poles and an old door panel. Harissa looked at it, then looked away.

The healer was not there yet. Hevrin said he was coming by the upper walk. He had insisted on seeing another patient first. Riarhin nodded and found she was relieved to hear it. There was a person elsewhere who needed him for something that had already happened.

Mavren asked to see their hands. She was checking for the resin used on one of the blocked shortcuts; it made the grip slide if it warmed. Coryd held out both palms. Linias did the same, a little too eagerly. Mavren gave him a rag and told him to dry them before touching anything smooth.

“You remember where I stop,” she said to Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“I’ll hear things. That doesn’t mean I come in.”

“I know.”

Hevrin looked at the wrapped sword. He opened his mouth, then shut it. Riarhin waited in case he had thought of something about the catch. He bent instead to tighten the binding on the stretcher.

They left the others at the turn. Beyond it, the path narrowed between broken walls and the trees pressed close enough to brush their sleeves. Riarhin went last for a little while, watching the four ahead of her. Harissa favoured the leg only when she thought no one could see. Coryd’s shoulders were level beneath her small burden. Linias kept touching the place where the instrument’s strap normally crossed his chest. He had left it behind.

Zemia turned once to make certain Riarhin was coming. Riarhin raised a hand. She could not bring herself to make the gesture warm, and could not bear to let Zemia turn away without an answer.

At the chamber she examined every place they had left untouched and found it as it had been. She disliked the relief this gave her; she had wanted something to explain the pressure beneath her ribs.

Riarhin placed her water where she could find it without looking. She checked the scabbard’s suspension and moved it slightly so it would not catch against the narrow wall. Her coat lay differently over her hips than the one she had worn in Valora. Years of peace had changed small things about what felt familiar. She had kept practising, but practice seldom required her to listen for another person’s breathing in a dark doorway.

Harissa came to stand beside her.

“Do you think about dying?”

“Yes.”

“All the time?”

“No.”

“I keep trying to decide what I ought to think about. It makes everything sound foolish.”

“You don’t have to choose now.”

Harissa looked at the wrapped sword.

“Have you ever wanted to run?”

“Often.”

“Did you?”

“When I could.”

That seemed to help more than reassurance would have. Harissa went back to her place, where Coryd had put a folded cloth over the edge that would press against her hip. She touched it and gave Coryd a nod.

Farieth did not come at dusk.

The light beyond the outer stair faded from gold to a greenish grey. Insects began calling from the hollow below. Riarhin could place three separate calls and then a fourth, too regular, which might have been water passing through a crack. She did not decide. Deciding too quickly made the next sound harder to hear.

Linias came to ask whether he could eat. She nodded. He took a small piece of bread from the cloth at his waist and chewed so cautiously that Harissa told him he was allowed to have teeth. He gave her the rest. She ate it without caution.

Zemia stood by the arm. Riarhin sent her to sit against the wall until there was reason to stand. She obeyed, lowering herself as though the floor might object to her weight. Coryd kept looking at the catch. At last she went to it, touched the cleared groove with one finger, and returned.

Riarhin knew the desire. She wanted to inspect the blocked shortcut, the outer stair, the supports, the roof. She had inspected them. While she went to one, another would be out of sight. She stayed where she could see her family and let uncertainty remain beyond the openings.

A thin note came from the stone overhead.

Linias stopped chewing.

Riarhin raised one hand to keep him where he was. It was not the note from the avenue. It rose, broke, and began again lower down the wall. Dust fell in a narrow line beneath a seam. The bronze arm trembled against its stop.

Coryd took a step towards it.

“Wait.”

The main arch changed. Light appeared through it from a direction where no light should have been. Beyond the opening lay the beginning of a broad stair, every tread whole, descending towards an afternoon brighter than the one they had left. Riarhin could see pale cloth moving at its foot. She could not see a person wearing it.

Harissa breathed in sharply.

“Was that here?”

“No.”

“Is it open?”

“I don’t know.”

The service passage remained stone, narrow, and dirty. Riarhin looked from it to the lit arch, then back. A smell reached her, faintly sweet, like warm bread with something bruised beneath it. She had smelled it before in the forest and had no wish to find which memory would attach itself to it.

“Look here,” she told the others. She put her hand against the ordinary wall. “At this.”

Zemia was staring past her. Linias moved into her line of sight. He did not touch her. After a moment she looked at him, then at the wall.

There was a sound from beyond the arch which might have been many feet crossing a room. It passed from left to right without growing nearer. Riarhin’s shoulders began to turn with it. She made herself watch the outer stair instead.

Coryd whispered that Mavren had said she would leave if the arch opened.

“She can leave.”

“Should we?”

Riarhin considered the narrow passage behind them. They could take it now, abandon the invitation, go back to the baths while whatever had stirred here settled or did not. Farieth would have heard the same sounds. She might already be moving round to meet them. Riarhin looked at the ordinary doorway and hated having to choose with so little.

“Go to the turn,” she told Coryd. “Tell them what changed. Come back by this way only.”

Coryd left. Riarhin waited with the others. No one spoke. The pale cloth beyond the arch moved again, folding in on itself without a hand to gather it.

Coryd returned before Riarhin had counted as far as she meant to.

“Mavren’s seen it do that. She says it may settle. She won’t come closer.”

“Did she ask us to leave?”

“No.”

“That isn’t the same as telling us to stay,” Harissa said.

“I know.”

Riarhin moved her water and the small cloth bundle farther from the arch. She asked Linias to watch the light and tell her if it advanced across the floor. He picked a stain in the stone to measure it against. It did not advance.

After a while the sound of feet ceased. The impossible stair dimmed until its lower treads were difficult to distinguish. There was still light beyond it. Riarhin let the others sit. She remained standing long enough for Harissa to tell her she was wasting her legs, then sat where she could rise without turning.

Farieth had chosen this forest for them. Somewhere beyond the chamber she was waiting through the same disturbance, finding what use she could make of it. Riarhin listened to the insects begin again.

Riarhin sent Linias to rest first. Then Harissa. Coryd objected when her turn came and lay down once Riarhin threatened to do the lifting herself.

Zemia stayed beside the bronze arm.

“You’ll tire before you need it,” Riarhin told her.

“I’m not touching it.”

“You’re holding yourself as if you were.”

Zemia looked down at her shoulders and let them fall.

Somewhere beyond the outer stair a small stone struck another.

Riarhin raised her hand.

Everyone who was awake stopped moving. She listened through the ordinary sounds: insects, water far below, Linias breathing through his mouth.

Another stone fell.

Not from the same place.

Chapter 34 — The way round

Coryd saw the robe first, above the side opening they had thought too high to use without help.

She did not shout a name. She struck the warning on the stone with the heel of her hand.

Riarhin turned as Farieth dropped.

Her grandmother landed on her sound leg, caught the wall, and came down with the sabre already cutting. Riarhin met it near the guard and let the force turn her rather than trying to hold it still.

Behind her Harissa crossed the gap between supports. She reached the far side cleanly, but her injured leg failed when she tried to push away. She struck the stone with her hip and stayed there.

Farieth changed direction towards her.

Riarhin put the point of Remorse into the space Farieth needed. Her grandmother checked, cut across the point, and drove her back a step. Harissa rolled beyond reach. Coryd caught her belt and pulled.

The first arrangement was gone.

Riarhin had chosen the flat place beneath the opening because it gave her room to turn and put the bad footing in front of her. Farieth had landed beyond it. Now the broken edge lay behind Riarhin’s left heel, and the room she needed was occupied by a woman who had taken the trouble to look before coming down.

She went right.

The sabre followed the expected line. Riarhin changed under it, passing close to a root which had split the paving and grown thick against the wall. Her grandmother checked rather than commit herself to the same turn. Riarhin gained the width of a shoulder and used it to put the root between them.

Farieth cut over it. Remorse rose, turned the edge, and came back towards the unprotected hand. The hand withdrew. The blades separated with a soft scrape.

Riarhin heard a body strike stone behind her and almost looked. Harissa swore. That was enough to tell her who was still moving.

The root offered no protection against a thrust. Farieth knew it. She brought the sabre’s point forward, keeping her injured leg behind her, and made Riarhin give up the easy footing. Riarhin stepped onto the raised root rather than beyond it. For a breath she stood higher than her grandmother expected.

Remorse came down towards Farieth’s face.

The sabre caught it. Riarhin stepped off the root on the opposite side, letting the height become distance rather than trying to force the cut. A fold of her coat tore against bark. Farieth’s blade passed through the hanging cloth and struck nothing beneath it.

They had exchanged places. Riarhin now stood nearer the entrance to the long chamber, but she could not reach it without crossing the line that led to Harissa. Her grandmother turned towards that line at once.

Riarhin went with her.

She did not call her name. She had wasted that hope at the walkway. She put Remorse where Farieth would have to meet it to reach the others, and watched the shoulder behind the sabre instead of the face above it.

Riarhin could hear Linias moving behind her. She had told him to warn and withdraw; she did not look to make certain he obeyed. Looking would have given Farieth the side of her head. She watched the sabre’s guard instead, the small changes in the wrist that preceded its line.

Her grandmother used the robe well. It concealed the beginning of a step and caught against the stone without entangling her. Once Riarhin saw the injured thigh take weight and tried to force more onto it. Farieth turned the attempt into a cut at her knee. Riarhin had to abandon the advantage to keep the joint.

A faint sound came from Zemia. Riarhin could not tell whether it was a warning or a word addressed to Farieth. She felt anger rise and let it pass without giving it a movement. There would be time to ask if they survived.

Farieth spoke her name.

Riarhin did not answer.

“You cannot keep them all behind you.”

Remorse’s point moved towards her throat. Farieth gave ground half a step and used the extra room to bring the sabre around the support. Riarhin followed the movement with her feet, preserving the distance. The blade passed close enough that she heard it cut air beside her ear.

“I don’t need them behind me,” she said, and regretted spending the breath as soon as it was gone.

Coryd had already moved to the side passage. Harissa was beyond the low wall. Riarhin could hear Harissa beyond the low wall, telling Coryd where to put her hand.

“Second place,” Riarhin said.

Linias moved into the service passage. Zemia stepped towards the bronze arm too early and had to flatten herself against the wall when the fighters crossed in front of her.

Farieth did not look at her.

That frightened Riarhin more than a greeting would have.

They circled a low stone support. Remorse moved lightly in Riarhin’s hand. She kept her wrist loose and her elbow close, letting the point offer an approach she could withdraw. Farieth refused two openings. At the third she struck hard enough to take a chip from the support.

Riarhin felt it hit her cheek.

Her grandmother’s injured leg had stiffened, but she had arranged her movement to spare it. She no longer followed across uneven footing when she could make Riarhin come to her.

Coryd got Harissa through the inner opening. The space behind Riarhin narrowed.

She had wanted Farieth in the long chamber. Farieth stood between her and it.

For a moment neither advanced. Riarhin could hear water in the passage beyond the main arch, although the place on the other side had been dry when they looked before dusk. Cool air touched the back of her neck. It carried a smell she remembered from a place where Ghere had forbidden her to stop.

Farieth glanced towards it.

Riarhin saw the invitation almost as soon as her grandmother made it: give ground towards the main arch, leave the apparent space on the other side of the support, let the tired woman take the wider way.

She kept her left foot where it was.

Riarhin stepped as though the main arch were an ordinary wall and waited for her grandmother to alter the offer.

Farieth’s mouth tightened.

The next attack came faster. Riarhin met the beginning of it, turned enough to let the point pass her side, and found the sabre already returning from a shorter angle. She dropped her weight and took the return near Remorse’s guard. Pain ran from her thumb to her elbow. The blade held. She moved before the joined steel could pin her against the support.

A childhood morning came back with bodily clarity: Ghere striking where she had been, the wet ground turning beneath her foot, the lesson she had thought was about being faster. She had never been faster than him then. She had learned to be elsewhere before the speed mattered.

She went around the support instead of away from it.

Farieth followed two paces, then stopped. There were now three widths between them: open ground, the narrow space beside the root, and the service entrance behind Riarhin. Her grandmother chose the open ground. Riarhin let her take it, keeping the support at her own right shoulder.

“Coryd,” she said.

Her sister answered from the passage.

“Ready?”

“No.”

The answer made Farieth move. Riarhin had wanted it to. She brought Remorse up in a short threat to the eyes and went left as the sabre rose. The support interrupted the sweep. Farieth had to come forward to keep the attack alive.

One pace nearer the chamber.

Riarhin’s breathing had grown loud. She could not keep buying paces this way for long.

Chapter 35 — The chamber answers

Zemia said the first part of a name.

Riarhin felt the sound beneath her feet.

The wall to her left lost its shadows. Lines she had taken for cracks opened into a pale pattern, reaching down towards the floor. Beyond them something gave a slow answering pressure.

Farieth turned her head.

Zemia’s mouth was still open.

The sound had not been loud. Riarhin had heard a person make it, had seen the small movement of her sister’s throat. The answering pressure spread along the wall without seeming to cross the distance between them. A line lit beside Riarhin’s knee. Another appeared above the covered opening, showing a depth she had taken for solid stone.

For an instant she saw farther into the wall than the wall should have allowed. There was a narrow space there, and beyond it an arrangement of pale surfaces facing one another. None moved. She had the abrupt, unreasonable feeling that something was preparing to receive a person who knew where to stand.

Riarhin shifted her weight away from it.

Farieth did the opposite. The turn brought her sabre between Riarhin and Zemia. She had ceased trying to draw Riarhin towards the main arch. Whatever she saw in the responding wall had become more urgent than the fighter in front of her.

Riarhin struck before she could advance.

The blow was badly placed, too far along Remorse’s edge to carry the force she needed. It made Farieth check, which was all it had to do. Her grandmother’s eyes came back to her. For the first time since entering, her expression was openly angry.

Riarhin had wanted that anger before. Now she wished the wall would go dark.

Linias caught Zemia’s hand.

“Here,” he said.

She looked at him without recognition.

He used the name she had given him when they first met, the shortened form she no longer offered to strangers. Riarhin heard it between two blows and saw Zemia blink. Linias put her palm against the rough wall where he stood. He was shaking badly himself.

“Feel that. Here.”

Farieth’s attention sharpened. Riarhin saw the intended line before the sabre moved and stepped into it. Steel struck steel. The impact travelled into her shoulder and made her teeth close hard enough to hurt.

Zemia tried to pull air into her lungs. Linias kept speaking, too quickly to be comforting, repeating the place, his own name, the irritating details of a song they had quarrelled over. Riarhin could not hear the words clearly. She could hear his fury at being required to save the person who had brought them here.

“Zemia. Look at me.”

Her breath came out. The next sound was only breath.

Farieth moved towards her.

Riarhin cut across the approach and struck the sabre near its tip. She could not stop the weapon, but she changed its path enough that it passed Zemia’s shoulder rather than entering it.

Coryd pulled the woven cover across the responding wall. It did nothing. Harissa, on one knee, reached for the ordinary wooden screen they had moved aside during inspection. Linias left Zemia long enough to help drag it into its groove.

The pressure diminished.

The screen shuddered in its groove. Dust lifted from the top edge and drifted through the new light. It had covered that opening before the family came; they had moved it because it obstructed their examination of the arm. Riarhin remembered Harissa asking what it concealed and nobody giving an answer worth keeping.

Now Harissa put her good foot against its base and pushed. Linias held the upper frame. They were closing a thing they did not understand with the only thing they had seen close it.

Farieth struck at Riarhin’s wrist. She let her elbow fold, carried the impact past, and made the next turn too wide. A warning from Coryd reached her before the edge did. She pulled back, felt a touch across her sleeve, and knew she had lost a small piece of cloth rather than the arm beneath it.

Behind her the screen settled.

Linias made a noise like somebody whose breath had been held too long. Riarhin could not spare a look to see whether he had been hurt. She moved towards the covered opening, forcing Farieth to choose between reaching it and keeping her own throat clear of Remorse.

Her grandmother yielded the line.

The relief lasted less than a breath. Farieth had given ground towards the doorway Riarhin wanted, and was now close enough to cut off her way into it. She knew what the family was attempting. She had known before she dropped from the wall. Riarhin would have to make the narrow space costly enough that her grandmother chose the wider chamber despite herself.

Zemia pressed both hands against her mouth.

“You see,” Farieth said.

Riarhin could not spare breath to answer. She forced her grandmother back from the screen, using the support to keep the sabre from making its full sweep. Farieth struck her upper arm with the guard. Her fingers almost opened.

She held.

Behind the screen there was a single hollow note. Then water moved somewhere beneath the floor, and the chamber’s ordinary shadows returned.

Zemia lowered one hand.

“I didn’t know it.”

“Stay with Linias,” Riarhin said.

Farieth watched Zemia over the line of Remorse.

Her face contained neither surprise nor satisfaction. That settled something in Riarhin. She would not spend the next breath trying to change it.

Chapter 36 — Finish where you are

Riarhin backed towards the long chamber, making herself the easier body to reach.

Farieth followed this time.

At the entrance Riarhin caught her heel against a raised strip of stone. She could have tried to recover the stance she wanted. Instead she dropped with the stumble, turned around the trapped foot, and let the sabre pass above her shoulder.

Remorse rose towards Farieth’s wrist.

Her grandmother withdrew just far enough. The point scored the sleeve.

Riarhin kept turning. Her right knee brushed the floor, then left it. The movement was one she had practised until she despised it: beginning from a fall without needing the world to give back the stance she had lost. Ghere had used a stick against her ribs whenever she paused to arrange her feet.

Here there was no stick, no teacher waiting to see whether she had understood. The sabre came back along a line that would have opened her shoulder if she rose upright. She went around it with her left hand extended and her body following the point.

Farieth moved back. The injured leg took more weight than she intended. Riarhin saw the hitch and tried to keep it there, pressing the point towards the chest while stepping across the easier retreat.

Her grandmother answered by striking through the threat.

Riarhin had to turn the blade or be cut. She turned it. Farieth used the contact to draw her hand outward and opened the line beneath her arm. Riarhin came up already moving towards the wall, but Farieth’s return caught her side.

For an instant it was only a tug.

Then warmth spread beneath her coat.

She had no room to inspect it. She drew a breath and found she could complete it, though pain spread as the chest expanded. She kept her eyes on the sabre.

The room smelt of dust newly opened from old stone. A chip lay beneath the edge of her boot. She moved off it before committing her weight. Her grandmother saw the correction and attacked during it, making Riarhin pay for having attended to the ground.

She caught the blow on the strong part of Remorse and let the sword slide away before force could pin it. Her right hand touched the wall, giving her the position without requiring another glance. She came around the touch, moving her hips beneath the turn, and found enough space to breathe again.

There had been no room for this with a knife. She would once have tried to enter beneath the sabre and finish before either of them could recover. The old urge was still there, as persuasive as anger. She kept the length of the blade between them.

Farieth’s face had changed very little. Riarhin hated that until she saw the sweat above her lip and the way she guarded the injured leg even when she appeared not to. Her grandmother was working. She could be made to work harder. She moved through the narrow part between the support and the shutter channel. Farieth could reach farther. Here she had to bring the sabre close to her own body to use that reach.

Riarhin drew the port knife with her right hand.

She threw it low, at the sound leg.

Farieth stepped aside. The knife struck the far wall and fell. The step put her injured leg under her weight for a moment longer than she wanted.

Riarhin entered.

Remorse did not hammer against the sabre. Its edge touched, travelled, and left the opposing blade as Riarhin turned her hips. She cut the outer arm and moved away before she could be caught against the wall.

Farieth’s next stroke was shorter.

She had not lost her hold. Riarhin saw her thumb settle more firmly against the grip, compensating for whatever the cut had damaged. Blood showed at the cuff and spread where the robe touched it. The sight did not make her slower in any way Riarhin could safely trust.

They had come near the service opening. Beyond it Zemia stood by the arm. Riarhin could see part of her face whenever Farieth moved. Her sister was watching the fighters with the terrible attention she gave to remembering a thing she could not yet bear to understand.

“Look at Coryd,” Riarhin told her.

Farieth attacked on the words.

Riarhin took the blow nearer the guard than she intended. Her wrist bent. For a moment Remorse’s edge pointed towards her own face. She turned with it, brought her right hand against the wall, and used the contact to stop herself falling into the channel. The wound in her side pulled open. The pain was bright enough to empty the room of everything except the next place to put her foot.

She found it.

Farieth followed, trying to keep her off the line she had chosen. Riarhin gave up one pace, then another, without attempting to win them back. Her grandmother came after her on the open floor. The support was behind them now. There was nothing left between Farieth and the long chamber except Riarhin’s body.

She let the point lower a fraction.

Farieth saw the fatigue. It was real. She came forward to use it, and Riarhin moved sideways rather than backwards, forcing the next step across the edge of the shutter’s channel.

The stones there had been made for feet moving in a different direction. Riarhin had crossed them in practice, carrying a basket because the pathweaver had wanted the work done. Farieth found the change as she committed her weight. She corrected immediately, but the correction used the injured leg again.

Riarhin kept the point towards her eyes until the next turn was clear.

Every breath now pulled at Riarhin’s side. Her shirt moved wetly against it.

“Behind you,” Linias said.

She stepped where his voice directed. Coryd had dragged a loose stone clear of her heel. It was a small thing. It gave her the space for the next turn.

Farieth came forward into the chamber.

Riarhin yielded another pace, then moved sideways through the service opening.

Her grandmother followed towards the line beneath the shutter.

“Now,” Riarhin said.

Chapter 37 — The stone catch

The shutter descended a hand’s breadth and stopped.

Coryd was not yet at the catch. She had stopped over something broken from the side channel as the stone began to move. A flake stood wedged against the runner, its pale edge visible where the old dirt had split.

“Hold,” she said.

Zemia pressed harder on the arm. It did not move. Riarhin saw Coryd put two fingers towards the obstruction and heard herself shout before she had decided to spend the breath.

“Not your hand.”

Coryd drew it back. Harissa slid the little wooden scoop across the floor. It had been left where they cleared the channel, an ugly, split thing which nobody had thought worth carrying away. Coryd caught it, drove its end against the flake, and broke the scoop.

The obstruction remained.

Farieth came forward. Riarhin could no longer give ground without losing the service opening. She met the sabre, turned its edge towards the wall, and brought Remorse back along the shortest line she could find. The point touched cloth. Farieth withdrew before the cloth pulled against flesh.

Behind Riarhin, Coryd struck the flake again with the broken end. A piece fell. The shutter gave a low grinding sound and moved another inch.

“Keep it down,” Coryd said.

Zemia’s breath came out in a sharp, involuntary cry. The arm had shifted beneath her palms. She put her shoulder above her hands and held.

Now Coryd went for the catch. Harissa reached her, took the weight of the stone between them, and lifted with her good leg. Riarhin heard Harissa’s breath stop at the effort. The catch scraped along its channel.

Farieth heard it and looked towards Zemia.

“Release the arm.”

Zemia held it down.

“Until morning,” Farieth said. “You may all have until morning.”

Zemia looked towards Linias. He stood in the side opening, too close to the line if the shutter rose. He could not retreat without losing sight of the arm and the people beneath it.

Riarhin could see the calculation arriving in her sister’s face. There was a familiar way to think through this: another few hours, another chance to explain, perhaps another route. Farieth was offering something she could actually give. The voice had the same patient precision with which Zemia had once taught a boy to tie a knot.

“Zemia,” Farieth said. “You understand me.”

Linias said nothing. He did not plead for her to choose him. Riarhin wanted him to move, but moving would have left Harissa and Coryd without the warning he was there to give.

Zemia shifted her feet farther apart. Her palms slid against the metal. She caught the arm again before it rose.

Riarhin saw Zemia’s fingers change their grip. For a moment she thought she would let go.

She bent her knees and put more of her weight on the arm.

Farieth struck at Riarhin to clear the passage herself.

Riarhin entered beneath the beginning of the stroke. She let the sabre’s rising edge pass outside her shoulder and brought her left hand forward without trying to drive the whole length of Remorse after it. The cut travelled through the forearm above Farieth’s guard. There was resistance, then less of it than Riarhin wanted to feel.

Farieth’s hand opened.

She reached with the other before the sabre struck the floor. Riarhin turned Remorse across the reach. Her grandmother checked the hand and used the movement to close bodily instead. Riarhin felt fingers catch her coat at the wound. Pain took the strength from her right knee.

She let the knee bend. The cloth tightened, tore, and left a strip in Farieth’s grip. Riarhin came up against the wall with the sword between them. The sabre lay close to her boot, its pale edge catching what remained of the wrong light.

She kicked it through the service opening. It struck the lower stone and spun. Linias jumped clear, then planted his foot beyond the hilt so it could not slide back.

Farieth looked at him. Riarhin stepped into her line before she could move.

The catch seated with a sound so small she nearly missed it.

“Out,” Coryd said.

Linias pulled Zemia clear of the arm. Riarhin followed the sabre into the side passage, turned, and put her shoulder against the wall beyond the shutter’s path.

Stone came down between her and her grandmother.

The last thing she saw was Farieth’s injured hand reaching for the narrowing opening.

There was a moment in which Riarhin could have thrust through it. The hand would not have stopped her. She could see enough of the body beyond to know where to put the point.

Then Coryd slipped against her, carrying Harissa’s weight, and Riarhin turned the blade away before it touched either of them. The shutter reached the floor.

She did not know afterwards whether she would have taken it.

Linias was on the far side of Coryd, trying to keep Harissa from putting weight on the injured leg. They had become tangled in the narrow space. Riarhin kept Remorse close, point-down, and took Harissa’s free hand with her right.

For several breaths the work was no more grand than getting four frightened people through a passage built for smaller loads. Zemia had lost one shoe. Coryd’s coat caught on the bronze fitting. Linias was bleeding onto everything he gripped and still saying he was unhurt.

Riarhin wanted to laugh. She wanted to vomit. She held Harissa until Coryd could take her weight and made herself count them again.

Riarhin turned back towards the shutter. Remorse shook in her hand. She lowered the point before she dropped the sword.

Her coat was caught beneath her belt where Farieth had torn it. Warm blood followed the crease. She reached to loosen the cloth and found her right hand would not do the small work cleanly. Her fingers kept closing too hard or missing altogether.

Zemia saw and moved towards her. Riarhin flinched away before she could stop herself.

Zemia stopped.

“Cloth,” Riarhin said. “I need cloth.”

Her sister brought it. Riarhin took it without looking at her and pressed it where the warmth spread. She could hear Harissa breathing through her teeth. Coryd was talking to Linias, asking him to move his foot so the blade could be wrapped. All four voices, even when Zemia only answered a question, came from this side of the stone.

Harissa began to laugh, a breathless and frightened sound, and stopped when Coryd put a hand on her shoulder.

From the other side of the shutter came one measured blow.

Then another.

The stone did not move.

Chapter 38 — What holds

Riarhin checked the catch herself.

Coryd let her look, then pulled her away and pressed a folded cloth against her side.

“Hold that.”

“The outer stair—”

“Linias is watching it.”

“He can’t—”

“He is watching it.”

Riarhin held the cloth.

Through a narrow opening above the channel she could see part of the chamber. Farieth sat against the opposite wall, binding her arm with a strip torn from her robe. Blood had darkened the cloth around her leg. The separate outer door remained open beyond her.

She could leave. She could not reach them by the service way. The other approaches brought her past the places they had left watched.

Linias returned from the lookout.

“Nothing moving.”

The cheap knife remained beyond the shutter, visible against the far wall. Riarhin left it there. They took the sabre. Coryd wrapped it in a spare coat, keeping her hands clear of the pale edge.

Riarhin called through the narrow opening.

“Can you reach the outer door?”

Farieth raised her head.

“Yes.”

“There are steps missing.”

“I know.”

Blood ran between the fingers compressing her arm. She had tied the cloth skilfully. Riarhin could see how much of the work had been done with one hand and her teeth. The binding around the thigh had loosened during the fight. Farieth tried to tighten it while keeping pressure on the arm, failed, and leaned her head back against the wall.

Her eyes closed briefly. When they opened she looked towards the main arch rather than the family’s passage. Riarhin could not see what remained of the impossible light from here. She heard a small sound beyond it, like a weight settling on wet earth. Farieth shifted farther from that side of the room.

“Don’t take the arch,” Riarhin said.

Her grandmother looked up at her. There was a moment in which the warning seemed to trouble her more than the wound.

“I know this place.”

“So do I.”

Riarhin had meant only the smell, the change in the air, the wrong invitation of open ground. Farieth could know a hundred things about the stones which she did not. She watched her grandmother settle the injured arm against her body and prepare to rise towards the outer stair.

“Your papers are being copied,” Riarhin said. “The ones from before the reaction.”

Farieth’s eyes stayed on her.

“The people you asked for,” Riarhin continued. “The room you put your assistant in. The way out you kept for yourself.”

“I did what was necessary.”

Riarhin had expected the answer. Hearing it still hurt.

She stepped away from the opening. There was no further thing she could say that would make leaving easier.

Zemia looked towards the shutter.

Riarhin thought she might speak.

She did not.

They left by the service way.

At the first turn Riarhin had to stop. The cloth against her side was warm through to Coryd’s hand. Harissa sat on a low stone and pulled the sleeve from a garment she had carried beneath her coat. She tore it along a seam and gave it to Coryd without comment.

Zemia offered her own cloak. Coryd took it for carrying the sabre and other things they could no longer hold separately. Linias refused to let Zemia take his injured hand to examine it, but accepted the strip of cloth she put on the wall beside him.

They moved again in a different order. Riarhin disliked being in the middle. It was where they put her. Coryd took the rear for the first stretch; then Linias relieved her.

At the bend overlooking the outer stair, Riarhin made them wait. She could not see Farieth, and the lack of sight troubled her more than seeing her would have. The route they had blocked remained blocked. No one had moved the warning stones. They had to leave on that evidence.

Riarhin took them off the direct path at the fork where the wind came coldest. Coryd began to object and stopped when she saw Riarhin put a hand against a trunk instead of trusting the open ground.

“The higher roots,” Riarhin said.

Harissa looked at the slope. “I cannot climb that.”

“You won’t. We go along it.”

She showed the first two footholds, then let Coryd take the lead. They were low enough to reach from the earth and continuous for most of the way, a harder walk than the path but one that did not pass beneath the open arch. Riarhin had been brought across such ground with wet legs and an empty basket. This time she could explain before someone put a foot where it did not belong.

At the end Linias looked back. From there the direct way appeared to lead into a grove full of evening light. The sky above them was dark.

“Don’t look for us there,” Riarhin said.

He turned. She could see that he wanted to ask how she knew. He waited while she caught her breath.

She counted four siblings. All were breathing. Harissa was furious with somebody, perhaps everyone. Riarhin kept close enough to hear what she was saying.

At the agreed meeting bend Mavren waited with the roof-worker and two others. A short carrying frame rested against the wall. She had brought no weapon. A chisel case hung against her hip, and beside it a folded cloth was tied with cord.

Riarhin tried to tell her about the arm. Breath failed halfway through the first sentence. Mavren held up a hand.

“Did you open the main arch?”

“No.”

“Did anything else move?”

“The recess.”

Mavren looked past her at Zemia. Nobody had pointed. Riarhin disliked the speed with which the look found her.

“We’ll tell you,” she said. “She hasn’t anything else to do there.”

The woman considered the blood spreading beneath Riarhin’s hand.

“Later.”

She took the folded cloth from her belt and gave it to Coryd, then went towards the passage with the roof-worker. Harissa turned to watch them.

“Is she going in?”

“To look,” Riarhin said.

“After all that?”

Riarhin tried to call a warning. It came out too weakly. Mavren turned, saw her expression, and pointed to the place where she had stopped, well before the chamber. Then she knelt and put her hand near the stone without touching it.

Coryd pressed the fresh cloth against Riarhin’s side. The pain made her knees loosen. She let herself be brought away.

The roof-worker left Mavren with one person watching the circuit and brought the other after them with the frame. Coryd helped lower Riarhin onto it. The cloth sagged beneath her hips, pulling the wound until they adjusted the roll beneath her knees. She held the dressing against her side while the two carriers tested the weight.

Roots and the pale undersides of leaves passed beside her face. At the first turn she reached to steady herself. Coryd took the hand and kept it until they were through.

By the time they reached the baths, the healer was there. They lifted her off the frame at the foot of the inner stair. Hevrin offered his arm, and she leaned on him.

They reached the room by a stair Riarhin had climbed easily on the morning they arrived. Now each tread seemed higher than the last. Hevrin stopped halfway without saying he was stopping for her. He looked over the rail, asked someone below to bring the lamp, and waited until Riarhin could move again.

At the bed she tried to sit too quickly. Coryd caught her beneath the arms. A sound escaped her which she would have been ashamed of if she had had room for shame. The healer told everyone except Coryd to leave. Harissa objected. He told her there was another person waiting to look at her leg and that both tasks would go better without an audience.

Riarhin heard them go. Zemia’s step paused by the door. Then it went too.

The healer cut away the wet shirt. The wound hurt more when the cloth left it. Riarhin gripped the edge of the bed while he worked and swore when he told her to turn.

“That is the direction,” he said patiently.

In the next room Harissa was being made to put her leg up again. Linias argued that he had no injury requiring attention. Someone asked him to open his hand. He had torn the palm pulling Zemia from the arm and had said nothing about it.

Riarhin listened until the voices blurred.

The healer asked who could tell him how long ago the injury had happened. Three people answered. He chose Coryd because she gave him a usable sequence instead of reassurance. She described the fight, the walk, the time they had stopped. When she reached the shutter, her voice shook once and steadied.

Riarhin wanted to thank Coryd. The words would not come around the pressure in her throat.

The healer gave her water in a cup with a lip made for drinking while lying down. She had seen Ailsa use such a cup. For a moment she could not swallow.

He waited, then tried again. This time she managed.

Outside, someone was washing the blood from the cloth used to carry the sabre. Riarhin heard water poured from one vessel to another, the same ordinary sound that had accompanied their arrival at the baths. She held onto it until the room stopped moving.

When she woke, the shutter was still in her dream. Coryd sat beside the bed.

“She hasn’t followed,” she said.

Riarhin believed her enough to sleep again.

Chapter 39 — Beds apart

The healer returned in the morning with materials for a small working and a warning that it would not give Riarhin permission to travel that day.

She agreed too readily. He made her repeat the warning back.

The treatment left her tired and hungry, the torn tissue eased but still tender when she moved. Coryd brought food. Riarhin ate all of it and asked for more.

She discovered how much worse the wound had frightened the others when she tried to stand.

Coryd set her bowl down hard enough to spill the last spoonful.

“What are you doing?”

“Getting up.”

“I can see that.”

“I need the pot.”

Coryd’s face changed. She fetched it and helped Riarhin sit on the edge of the bed. Riarhin let her arrange the blanket, then asked her to turn away. Her sister went as far as the window. She remained there while Riarhin breathed through the first sharp pull in her side and wondered whether pride was going to make her fall onto the floor with her clothes around her ankles.

“Coryd.”

She turned at once.

“I need a hand.”

Her sister came back without comment. It was worse, in some ways, than being scolded. Riarhin held her arm and lowered herself carefully. The weakness in her thighs was ordinary, familiar from other recoveries, and humiliatingly complete.

Back on the bed she asked how much blood she had lost.

“Enough.”

“Who said?”

“I did. I carried some of it here.”

Coryd went to empty the pot. When she returned, Riarhin was still sitting where she had left her. Her sister looked relieved and tried to hide it by straightening the blanket.

“I thought you were going,” she said.

“Where?”

Coryd looked at her until she understood.

Riarhin held out a hand. Her sister took it and sat beside her. The palm that had been burnt by the line aboard ship was rough along one edge. She could remember watching that injury treated, the small work of pouring water, her own wish to have prevented what Coryd had already managed. Now Coryd had brought her through a doorway with her side open. She had known this woman for so little of her life that she still sometimes thought her name before speaking it, as if checking that it belonged to the face.

“I’m here,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

Coryd held on until the healer returned and told her to stop bending the patient sideways.

For most of that day Riarhin lay listening to people carry on beyond the door. She could place the room’s activities by sound: a bucket set down too hard, the scrape of a chair, Harissa telling someone that the bandage was tied at the wrong angle. She could not place Farieth. Her attention kept leaving the voices to follow every pause between them.

Coryd returned from the lower court and found her sitting up with Remorse across her knees.

“Put it down.”

“I’m cleaning it.”

“It’s clean.”

Riarhin looked at the blade. It was. She had cleaned it already and could remember each part of doing so. The cloth in her hand had picked up no new stain.

Coryd took the cloth, not the sword. She laid it on the table within reach and went to close the outer door. When she returned she sat by the bed instead of asking another question.

“Did anyone see her leave?” Riarhin asked.

“Not yet.”

“Then she could be—”

“They’re watching the ways she can use. Mavren came back. She looked where you asked.”

“What did she say?”

“That the outer stair is still there. That she wasn’t going down it to find out whether your grandmother wanted company.”

Riarhin laughed, and the pull in her side punished her for it. She set Remorse beside the bed. Coryd waited until she had let go of the hilt.

“Sleep if you can.”

“I’ve slept.”

“Then do something else without standing up.”

She brought the household’s broken basket and a bundle of split reeds. Riarhin looked at it, then at her sister.

“Is this a kindness?”

“It’s a basket. Irset wants it mended.”

Riarhin took it. One side had opened where an old repair gave way. She worked slowly, stopping when bending over it pulled the wound. Coryd showed her where to rest it against a rolled blanket. After that she could keep her hands busy without drawing her shoulders forward.

Irset came in while Riarhin was testing a split reed.

“When do you need it?” Riarhin asked.

“When it’s mended.”

“Tomorrow?”

“Have you promised it to someone?”

“No. I meant—”

Irset took the basket off her lap and put it on the floor. For a moment Riarhin thought she had been found too slow. The woman moved the rolled blanket so she could lean back without pulling the wound.

“Leave it there while you eat.”

“I can finish this side.”

“The food will be cold.”

She brought the bowl over. Riarhin ate with the uncut reed lying beside her spoon. Afterwards Irset stayed to complain about a visitor who had tried to improve the household’s washing arrangement and left three wet blankets for other people to carry. She asked Riarhin whether she liked visitors.

“Fewer than I used to.”

Irset laughed. She was still there when Riarhin put the spoon down. The basket remained on the floor.

By evening one side was bound. Riarhin fell asleep with the uncut end of a reed between her fingers. When she woke, somebody had removed it and put the basket on the floor.

Across the room Zemia sat with a bandage around one palm. Linias brought her water, set it down, and went to the bed they had placed by the other wall.

She watched him cross the room.

“Linias.”

He stopped.

“Thank you.”

He nodded without turning and lay down facing the window.

When the others had gone to eat, Linias came to Riarhin’s bed with his bandaged hand held awkwardly against his chest.

“Does it hurt?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Ask him for—”

“I have.”

He sat on the stool. She waited. He looked at the cup beside her bed, the folded shirt on the wall peg, everything except her.

“I thought about letting go.”

“At the arm?”

He nodded.

“She was heavier than I expected. I was trying to pull her and my hand was torn and she was still holding it. I thought, if I stop, she stays there.”

Riarhin remembered the narrow passage, his hand bleeding on the wall. She had seen him take Zemia’s weight without hesitation. She had not seen what it required of him.

“You didn’t.”

“I wanted to.”

He waited for her to correct him. Riarhin could feel the easy sentence forming: fear made people think things, anger was understandable, the action was what mattered. She had heard versions of it after battles. Sometimes she had been grateful. Sometimes she had known the speaker wanted the conversation over.

“What stopped you?”

He rubbed his thumb along the clean edge of the bandage.

“She looked at me. I don’t know.”

Riarhin moved her cup so he would have somewhere to put his elbow. He set it there carefully.

“I keep thinking she’ll tell someone,” he said.

“What?”

“That I did it. That I saved her.”

“She may.”

“As if we’ve finished.”

Riarhin looked towards Zemia’s bed, empty now, its blanket folded with the neatness she used when she wanted to seem easy to accommodate.

“Do you want me to speak to her?”

“No.”

“Then I won’t.”

He sat a little longer. From outside came Harissa’s voice objecting to the way somebody had tied a bundle. Linias smiled despite himself, then looked ashamed of having done so.

“She’ll be impossible when the leg heals,” he said.

“She already is.”

“She was worse before.”

He told her about a room in which Harissa had refused to dance because the floor had been polished with oil. The man paying her had insisted it was an honour. She had asked him to demonstrate the honour. When he fell, she left with half the payment and his wife’s approval. Linias did the man’s voice, too low and pompous, and found enough pleasure in Riarhin’s laugh to attempt Harissa’s as well.

By the time he left, he could carry the empty cup in his injured hand. He did it slowly, keeping the wrist straight.

Mavren came the following day. She waited until the healer had finished looking at the wound, then took the stool he left.

“You promised me the account.”

“I did.”

“From the beginning.”

Riarhin told her about the light in the arch before Farieth arrived. Mavren asked where the light reached on the floor. Riarhin described the stain Linias had watched. The woman nodded once, making no note Riarhin could see. She asked about the sound. Riarhin imitated it badly enough that Linias, from the other bed, made a correction before remembering who else was in the room.

Mavren asked him to do it again. He looked towards Zemia. She had both hands over her ears.

“Not again,” Riarhin said.
