The woman considered the refusal and accepted it. They continued with words. When Riarhin described the responding wall, Mavren sat very still. She asked where the wooden screen had been found and who moved it. Riarhin answered without claiming that they had known what they were doing.

“Was that a door?” Harissa asked.

Mavren looked at her.

“The part behind it.”

“It may have been a place you were meant to stand.”

“Meant by whom?”

“I don’t know.”

Harissa pulled her blanket higher over her knees. She had been holding the frame shut while Farieth’s blade struck nearby. Riarhin had not asked whether she had seen anything beyond it. She asked now.

“Only the light,” Harissa said. “I didn’t look.”

Mavren approved of that more visibly than she had approved of any other part of the account. She listened to the shutter’s final movement, the catch, the small remaining gap. Coryd showed its height with her hands. Mavren asked whether anyone had reached through it.

“No,” Riarhin said.

“Good.”

“Will it hold?”

“It is holding.”

Harissa began to object to the distinction. Mavren put down her case and leaned forward.

“I will go and look again when it can be approached. I won’t go into a room with an injured woman who has already tried to kill people. Neither will the people whose hands you need to clear the channels. Until then, I can tell you what we saw from outside.”

Riarhin nodded. Mavren sat back.

The wrapped sabre lay beneath the bed. Coryd drew it out at the woman’s request but kept the edge covered. Mavren examined the hilt, the pale metal at the mouth of the wrapping, the place where blood had dried against a fastening. She did not reach to take it.

“Is it what changed her?” Linias asked.

“I can’t tell you what changed a person by looking at something she carried.”

He accepted the rebuke, though it had not been spoken sharply. Riarhin was glad he had asked. She had wondered too.

Mavren wanted the blade stored where a child could not unwrap it. Irset provided a narrow cupboard and kept one key. Coryd kept the other. They put it away together. Riarhin watched the empty place beneath her bed and felt less certain than she had expected to feel without the weapon in the room.

At the door Mavren stopped.

“The path by the missing brace is closed. Someone has put a branch across it. Don’t remove it because you know why it’s there.”

“We won’t.”

“When you’re fit, you can help move the timber.”

Riarhin agreed. The woman left with her case. Harissa looked at the door and said she hoped their next family quarrel could take place somewhere with less expensive furniture.

Later the keeper came with copies of the passages concerning Farieth’s experiment. She read them aloud with Coryd beside her, marking where an interpretation remained uncertain. Zemia supplied the earlier names she had given away, the places, and the contacts through whom she had learned where people were living.

When she could not remember an address because she had never attended to it, she said so. She described the door instead. She corrected the date of one request in a way that made her account less defensible.

The keeper listened.

She had brought another person with her, a narrow-faced woman who bound books and knew the passage of messages through three settlements. Riarhin had objected when she entered. Eshai waited until the objection was finished before explaining that the woman would not be permitted to carry warnings she could not understand.

The binder listened to the description of Farieth without apparent surprise. When Zemia named the lodging house where she had met her, she asked which door faced the water.

“The smaller one,” Zemia said.

“Then I know it.”

“Did you know her?” Riarhin asked.

“I took work there. She found a buyer for it.”

Harissa set down the cloth she had been folding.

“Read the paper,” she said.

“I mean to.”

The binder did. She asked Eshai three questions about the marks, then read it again. Riarhin watched for the familiar movement by which someone decided that a person they liked could not have done what the evidence required. Instead the woman put the copy on the table and rubbed a finger against an old ink stain on her thumb.

“Who is in danger now?”

They could not give a complete answer. Zemia knew places where people had once lived. Coryd knew a man who refused further questions. Linias had a maternal sister whose children were not part of Farieth’s line and who might still be put at risk by an address. Riarhin had a house above a workroom and no idea how far the warning about Farieth had travelled since she left it.

The binder drew a stool close enough for her knees to touch the table. She asked who would be willing to put a name to what they had seen.

“All of us,” Harissa said.

“Each of you separately.”

Harissa looked towards Zemia. “Some of us have seen more than others.”

“Yes. I need to know which.”

Riarhin watched the woman divide a sheet into spaces with the edge of a narrow board. She could feel her patience thinning. Her side hurt. The person whose kindness the binder remembered was somewhere beyond the shutter, able to walk if she could bear the wound, already calculating what the family would do next.

“She tried to kill us,” Riarhin said.

“I have heard you.”

“Then write that.”

“I mean to. Which of you saw the attack at the loft begin?”

“Linias,” Coryd said.

“I heard it,” he said. “The first part. I saw her when she came past the stair.”

The binder made a mark. Riarhin could hear how the account would travel: heard, saw afterwards, believed. She had spent much of her life being believed because people could see what happened if she was crossed. Lying injured in a room full of people who had already paid for trusting her, she wanted to demand that someone carry her certainty for once.

Eshai shifted the lamp so the binder could see better.

“You can ask me about the papers,” she said. “I will give you a copy of the refusal to alter the room. The person who wrote it is still living.”

“And the later hand?”

“I recognize it. I have other work in it.”

The woman nodded. Riarhin leaned back against the rolled blanket. The pull in her side eased.

“Who will receive them?” she asked.

“The people who can carry the warnings farther than I can.”

“Will they act?”

“Some will.”

“And the others?”

“Some will want to know why a woman who helped them is being accused by people they have never met.”

Harissa made an impatient movement. The binder put down her pen.

“One of them has a daughter who lived because Farieth found somebody to come. I’m going to ask that woman to turn her away from her door. I will ask. Don’t make me go with an account that falls apart at the first question.”

Riarhin looked at the blank spaces on the sheet. There would not be enough room on one page for everything they had to distinguish. She asked Coryd to pass the remaining paper.

They began with the landing. Linias described where he had set the instrument and why he had been nearer the stair than the others. He stumbled over the part where he went back for it. The binder asked whether it had blocked his view. He said yes. She wrote that down without looking reproachful, and he managed the rest more easily.

Riarhin gave the position of the blade when she first reached Farieth. The woman asked which side of the landing held the window. Riarhin drew it. She added the beam, the narrow place beside the chest, the door the landlady had told them not to use. The room returned so clearly that she could smell old cooking fat on the timber. She had to stop when she reached the edge from which Harissa fell.

Harissa took the pen.

“This way,” she said, correcting the direction of her own movement. “My skirt caught there. She cut after I turned.”

The binder watched her draw. “Did you see where she was looking?”

“At me.”

Harissa spoke with such force that Linias turned towards her. She handed the pen back before her grip could begin to shake.

They worked until the light changed. At the end the binder read the account to them, leaving the mistakes visible where they had been corrected. Riarhin wanted the page made clean. Then the woman put a finger beside Linias’s first description and read his correction immediately after it. Anyone receiving the sheet would see how they had arrived at what remained.

“Keep that,” Riarhin said.

“I intended to.”

The binder began another sheet for the separate notices. Riarhin asked whether the landlady at the loft would be warned too. The woman added a mark beside the landing’s name and asked who could carry a message there without mentioning where the family slept now.

The binder asked for separate messages.

“Not a list I can lose.”

Eshai nodded and brought more paper.

Zemia began suggesting who should carry which message. The binder stopped her.

“I need what you know. I’ll decide who I ask.”

Zemia lowered the pen. The work continued. Zemia answered each question in turn, supplying landmarks when she could not give an address, admitting when a description came from someone else. Once she began a name and put a hand over her mouth.

The binder waited until she could give the name the person used.

When Zemia gave the town where the third sibling might have gone, Coryd asked her to describe the inn again. Zemia did, adding the colour of the stable door and the fact that one side of the upper landing smelled of cooked onions. She had not gone upstairs. She said that twice when the keeper began to infer something from the arrangement.

Linias asked whether the letters she had sent could still be found. Some might. Others had been answered through people whose whereabouts she did not know. He pressed her until Harissa told him to stop asking the same question in different words.

“I want her to be sure.”

“She isn’t. Making her say it differently won’t make her sure.”

Zemia kept her eyes on the table. She did not thank Harissa for the intervention.

Riarhin asked the keeper to mark what came from Zemia separately from the older material. The woman nodded. She had already begun doing so.

“Who should receive this?” she asked.

They chose people who could warn the households without publishing the living siblings’ present location. The records of the earlier experiment would remain with more than one custodian. Farieth would find people who knew what had happened, though none could promise what they would do with the knowledge.

Harissa refused to travel with Zemia after they recovered.

Zemia said she understood.

“I don’t need you to understand,” Harissa replied. “I need you not to arrange it.”

Riarhin kept quiet.

Afterwards she found Harissa in the court, trying to make the borrowed clothes fit without cutting them. Riarhin held the cloth while she pinned it.

“Do you think I’m cruel?” Harissa asked.

“No.”

“You hesitated.”

“I was thinking of something I could say that wouldn’t tell you how to feel.”

Harissa pushed a pin through too hard and pricked her thumb. She sucked it, annoyed.

“I liked her. I still keep wanting to tell her things. Then I remember that telling her things is how she does it.”

Riarhin held the fold without answering.

“That’s the part I can’t bear. I have to keep remembering.”

She took the cloth back and checked the line. Riarhin went to fetch the mirror she asked for. By the time she returned, Harissa had stopped crying and was angry that the mirror was so small.

That afternoon she wrote home. She said she had been hurt and was being cared for. She described the warm wall, Linias’s damaged instrument, and the child who had sat on Harissa’s chest despite the cloth placed over it.

She did not know how to describe the family yet.

She wrote their names.

Writing them made her want to send something more useful than a page full of injury. She asked Irset whether she could buy a little of the household’s weaving. The woman brought three pieces, named a price for each, and refused to let Riarhin pretend the most expensive was the one she had liked first.

“That one is for a covering,” she said. “You wanted something a person can carry.”

Riarhin bought the smaller piece. She wrapped it with the letter, then unwrapped it because the paper would rub the edge. Irset watched her begin again.

“Who is it for?”

“Ailsa’s daughter. The house.”

“The person who died?”

“Yes.”

Irset sat down. She had not sat for any of the other questions Riarhin had asked that day.

“How long were you there?”

“Seven years.”

“Before that?”

“I knew her in Ubral.”

The woman nodded, accepting the place without trying to make Riarhin put the whole history between them. From the adjoining room came the sound of the child attempting to be quiet and failing.

“I don’t know if I should go back,” Riarhin said.

“When you can walk?”

“When it’s safe.”

Irset looked at the bandage. “Write before you do. Let them decide what they can bear.”

Riarhin had been trying to decide for them from a room so far away that the first letter might not yet have reached the workroom. She took the page out again and added a question about returning. It was harder to write than the account of the wound.

Irset waited until she had finished. Then she called the child in and made her show Riarhin the reed animal she had been building, which had too many legs to stand but was meant to fly.

Chapter 40 — A place at the table

By the time Riarhin could walk to the lower court without stopping, Coryd had found work mending the covered walk above the baths.

Harissa had begun teaching in the court on mornings when her leg let her stand. At first she taught from a stool, moving her hands to show a turn and trusting the pupils to supply the rest. That went badly. She got up to demonstrate, hurt herself, and sat down angrier than before.

On the third morning she asked Riarhin to do the movement while she watched. Riarhin tried it, keeping the weight clear of the place where her side pulled.

“No,” Harissa said.

“It’s the turn you showed me.”

“It’s the turn before you stab somebody.”

The children laughed. Riarhin looked at her feet and discovered Harissa was right. She had finished in a position from which she could move in either direction at once. The dance asked her to remain a little longer where another person could see her.

She tried again. Harissa watched, chin in hand, then told the pupils to follow the second version. A boy with heavy fur along his forearms copied the hand perfectly and nearly fell when he added the feet. Riarhin caught him. Harissa told her to let him find the balance before putting him down.

He found it. She let go.

After the lesson Harissa remained on the stool, rubbing the edge of her bandage. Riarhin brought water. They watched the pupils repeat the turn without permission, altering it into a contest over who could go lowest before losing balance.

“I was teaching something else,” Harissa said.

“They’ll come back to it.”

“Eventually.”

The furred boy won and looked towards her to see whether she had noticed. Harissa lifted one hand in reluctant acknowledgement. He beamed.

She intended to return to the amphitheater once her leg allowed it. She had been invited to teach a dance and had agreed only after making clear that she would also perform. Linias wanted a proper repair for the instrument. A man in a settlement farther west might do it, if he could be persuaded that the wood was worth saving.

Zemia had written the warnings under supervision. There were replies to wait for, and she had agreed to remain until the first arrived. The hostess assigned her work that did not involve carrying messages. She complained about the weight of the water vessels once, caught herself, and was told she was allowed to complain if she kept carrying them.

Riarhin put the slate on which she had drawn the sphere’s route into a wrapping beside her other papers. One turn still troubled her. Beyond it she remembered the place of a shoulder and the expectation that somebody would be there.

She covered it.

The boy who had learned the turn came to fetch her for Coryd. There was no emergency; he said so at once, which meant someone had told him to. Riarhin followed him to the covered walk. Coryd sat astride a beam with a mallet hanging from a cord at her wrist. Beneath her Hevrin held the end of a replacement board.

“Can you stand there?” Coryd asked.

Riarhin looked at the place indicated. It was flat, sound, and close enough to a rail that she could support herself without making a show of it.

“Yes.”

They had a corner of the covering to lift, too light to require strength, too awkward for the two people already holding other parts of the work. Riarhin took it. Coryd fitted the board, Hevrin checked its end, and Riarhin lowered the covering when asked. It was done in a few minutes.

“Was that all?”

“For now.”

Coryd came down. She had a splinter in one sleeve and a dark line across her forehead where she had wiped it with a dirty hand. Riarhin offered the cloth from her pocket. Coryd took it, wiped her face, and gave it back with an apology for the stain.

“I wanted to ask about going west,” she said.

“To the repairer?”

“With Linias, perhaps. He’ll need someone to help carry it if that hand stays sore.”

Riarhin nodded. The small separate journeys had begun arranging themselves while she lay in bed. She had known they would. Knowing did little to prepare her for the first one spoken aloud.

“Do you want me to come?”

Coryd looked at her side. “When you’re fit.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“I know.”

They stood beneath the new board while Hevrin tested its fastening. Dust fell from above. Coryd moved Riarhin half a step by touching her sleeve, then withdrew her hand.

“I don’t know yet,” she said. “I like working with you. I don’t like having to explain every time I go through a door.”

Riarhin looked towards the upper rooms. Zemia was carrying water across the court. Irset called to her to use the smaller vessel and stop trying to finish in one trip.

“I don’t want to keep doing that,” Riarhin said.

“Then don’t.”

It was harder than the words made it sound. Coryd knew that; she had been watching the same doors. Riarhin took the stained cloth and folded it into her pocket.

“If you go, leave an address where I can write.”

“I will.”

“Not because I’m asking where you are.”

Coryd’s mouth moved. “I know how letters work.”

Riarhin laughed. Coryd let the brief ease remain between them, then went back up to help Hevrin with the next board.

The pathweaver brought a packet from the amphitheater two mornings later. It had followed the places where they said they would be and arrived after they had ceased being there. One outer covering bore a stain from somebody’s supper. The inner letter was dry.

Riarhin knew the hand before she saw the signature.

Ailsa’s daughter had written closely at the top and begun leaving more room between the lines as she went on. She complained about the lamp. It worked, but the angle was wrong for fine work and the man who sold it had said it was the angle of the table. Her father had moved the table. Now they struck their knees on the chest which had been moved to make room for it. She had included a little drawing with an arrow showing exactly whose knee suffered.

Riarhin laughed. The pathweaver, who had stayed for water, looked up.

“Good news?”

“An argument about a lamp.”

“Then perhaps.”

The rest concerned work. A customer wanted the blue cloth cheaper because Ailsa would not be making the garment. The daughter had sent him away. Her father thought she could have accepted a lower price for the cloth alone. They had quarrelled. The letter did not say who had prevailed. Instead there was an account of the son coming home with a fish nobody knew how to cook, which had settled the question of what they would do with the rest of the evening.

Near the foot of the page the writing grew small again.

I wear the dress when I want. I haven’t altered the pocket. I thought you would ask.

Riarhin smoothed the paper against her thigh. She had not asked in the letters already travelling towards them. She had asked about the shutter, the guard, whether anyone strange had come to the lane. She could have supplied a description of every fastening in the house. The dress had continued without her supervision.

There was a second page from the son. He had started a new piece rather than use the back of his sister’s letter. He was going on a short voyage, there and back, with a man whose name Riarhin knew from the harbour. His father had agreed. He had remembered what Riarhin said about finding out where his things would be kept before agreeing to a berth. The place was dry. He had checked after rain.

At the bottom he had written that he hoped she would be home when he returned.

The letter had been sent before the family’s first arrival at the baths. Nothing in it could answer the page she had written from bed. She held both sheets and tried to calculate how far the son might have travelled by now. He could already be home, complaining of the food and displaying a purchase his sister considered foolish. He might be leaning over a rail under the same morning sky.

Coryd came to sit beside her.

“Are they well?”

“They were when they wrote.”

“May I?”

Riarhin gave her the son’s page. She had written to the daughter about Coryd’s work aboard ship. The two letters had probably passed each other, each carrying a person’s small competence towards someone who would be glad of it.

Coryd read the account of the dry berth twice.

“He checked after rain,” she said.

“I told him to.”

“He did it.”

Riarhin nodded. Coryd returned the paper. Below them Irset’s child was hauling the finished basket across the court, stopping whenever one corner caught. Riarhin could see the fresh binding she had put in. It would be tested sooner than she had hoped.

“What will you tell them?” Coryd asked.

“I’ve already told them I was hurt.”

“About us.”

Riarhin looked at the two sheets. The daughter had asked whether Harissa had taught them to dance yet and whether Linias would remember the tune he played upstairs. She had drawn a little wave beneath Zemia’s name, because Zemia had been the one to assure her that weather at sea was often less troublesome than people made out. Riarhin could not decide whether the mark meant kindness or a joke.

“I don’t know.”

Coryd looked over the rail. “Tell them I’d like to see the workroom again.”

“Would you?”

“I liked being there.”

Riarhin folded the son’s letter. She was afraid of showing too much pleasure and equally afraid of making her sister regret saying it by showing none.

“There’s room,” she said.

“I know. I slept in it.”

Coryd touched her shoulder as she rose. Riarhin stayed where she was until the court emptied for the midday work. Then she took out paper and began by asking the daughter to draw the room with the table where it stood now. She described Harissa’s lesson, the furred boy, the complaint about her own way of turning. She wrote enough about Zemia to require another sheet, and left it unsealed while she considered what belonged to other people to tell.

On the son’s page she wrote that she would like to hear how the voyage went. She crossed out a question about whether he had been careful. In its place she asked what he had seen.

A reply came to the first inquiry on a wet afternoon. It had travelled only from the settlement beyond the amphitheater, through a woman who knew the potter. The dead sister’s child was alive and had received the warning. No address was supplied.

Coryd read the message twice. Then she took the waistcoat from its wrapping and folded the note inside it.

Zemia asked whether there was anything she could do.

“No,” Coryd said.

Zemia remained standing a moment longer. Coryd folded the waistcoat around the note and tied the wrapping. She did not look up until Zemia moved away.

Riarhin asked whether Coryd would send a reply.

“Yes. To say we won’t look for him.”

“The child?”

“He is grown. He has had enough people coming through doors asking who he is.”

Riarhin nodded.

Coryd pressed the knot flat with her thumb.

“I wanted her to have someone left.”

“She does.”

“I wanted to meet him.”

Riarhin sat beside her. They watched Zemia carry the empty water vessels into the court.

Zemia returned to the work she had been given. Riarhin watched her measure thread for a repair with the same care she had once given an introduction. She measured a second length against the first, cut it, and waited for Irset to show her where it belonged.

Linias eventually asked whether she remembered the name of the instrument repairer farther west. She did. She wrote it down rather than offering to contact him. He took the slip, looked at it, and put it inside the case.

Riarhin began her own letter to the keeper of the sphere. She asked for no further contact with the object yet. She wanted to know whether an ordinary account existed of the outer approach before the later wall was added. The keeper could answer when she had time. She put a second sheet beside the letter for the keeper’s reply.

She sealed the letter and left it for the next traveller.

The sabre remained in the locked cupboard. They had not decided what to do with it beyond keeping it out of Farieth’s hand. Remorse stood where Riarhin could reach it, clean and sheathed.

Outside, the child was trying to coax a sound from Linias’s instrument. He had loosened all but one string for her. The single note rose repeatedly through the window.

Harissa called for mercy.

The child struck it harder.

Linias told her that volume and music were separate accomplishments. She asked him to demonstrate the difference. He did, using the one string, and she listened with an attention that made him play longer than he intended.

Zemia came into the court carrying the repaired blanket. She stopped at the sound. Linias saw her, hesitated, and continued. She sat on a low wall well away from him. Nobody invited her nearer. Nobody told her to go.

Riarhin stood at the top of the steps while the little tune repeated with small changes. Ailsa would have asked whether she meant to stand there all day. Riarhin could hear the impatience so precisely that she almost answered aloud.

Harissa shouted that if the music continued somebody had better bring her food.

Riarhin laughed, and her side hurt. She held it until the pain eased. Then she went down to help carry the meal.

They could not fit everyone at the same table. The hostess suggested moving a bench. Harissa needed room to stretch her leg; Linias chose the far end before anyone assigned him a place. Zemia stood with a bowl in each hand, waiting to see where she was wanted.

Coryd took one bowl from her.

“That goes here.”

Zemia set the other down where Coryd pointed. She took a seat near the end, leaving a space beside Linias.

Riarhin sat opposite her. For a moment the sunlight on the cups brought back the room above the workroom, Ailsa leaning across to take something from her plate, the familiar offence of it.

She wanted her so suddenly that she closed her eyes.

When she opened them, Harissa was holding out the bread.

Riarhin took it and passed it on.
